## Chapter One - Flight Path

*Day 0*

The thing Daniel Okafor would remember afterward was the pasta.

It was not good pasta. It was airline pasta, pale tubes under a sauce designed by committee to offend no one, and he ate all of it with a plastic fork somewhere over the black Atlantic, in seat 34C, with a feeling of small, unearned contentment. Meridian Air 418, Lagos to Houston. Wheels down at 5:40 a.m. Central. His daughter turned nine at seven o'clock and had been very clear about the pancakes. Shaped like Texas. No exceptions. If Daddy was late, the pancakes would be emotionally cold.

He had laughed at that. Then he had typed it into the notes on his phone so he wouldn't forget, which was the kind of man he was.

Around him the cabin had settled into the particular hush of a night flight: two hundred and eighty people agreeing, without discussion, to pretend they were somewhere else. Reading lights made small yellow islands in the dark. A baby somewhere behind row 40 had finally surrendered. In 34B, a large man named Walt, who had introduced himself in the boarding queue and then again at dinner, as though the first time hadn't counted, was snoring with the mild dignity of someone who had spent thirty years in oil and felt he'd earned it. On the seatback screen, a small white aeroplane crawled across a blue nothing.

Daniel was forty-six. He worked in ports. Customs, mostly: the software that decides whether a container of cooking oil is a container of cooking oil, and the people who decide whether to believe the software. He had spent six days in Lagos teaching a port authority's systems to trust each other, and the nineteen years before that learning the one thing his profession had to teach, which was that every institution in the world was a nervous person holding a clipboard, desperate to be told it was right.

He closed his eyes. It was a little after three in the morning by the clock on his screen. The cabin chimed.

Two notes, descending. Softer than the usual chime. Daniel noticed it the way you notice a familiar word that has started to look wrong: not because he could say what had changed, but because something in him sat up.

Then the voice.

It was not the captain, and it was not the purser. It was a third voice, level and warm and in no hurry at all, the voice of someone you would trust to read you your own test results.

"Good evening. This is a routing update for Meridian Air flight 418. Following a scheduled review of regional movement parameters, this flight has been reassigned to an alternative destination. The revised destination is Kestrel Field, designated Staging Facility Nine. Estimated arrival is 06:12 local time. Onward arrangements will be communicated on arrival. Any inconvenience is regretted. There is nothing you need to do."

The chime again. Then nothing.

For perhaps four seconds, nobody did anything at all. Then, across the dark cabin, the reading lights began to come on, one and then six and then dozens, a slow constellation of people who had just been told something and were checking whether anyone else had heard it.

"What'd it say?" Walt had surfaced, blinking, one hand patting his shirt pocket for glasses that were on top of his head.

"Something about Kestrel."

"Where's Kestrel?"

"I don't know."

Daniel pulled up the map. The little white aeroplane had begun to turn. It was a slow, gentle bank, so smooth he hadn't felt it, and the line ahead of it had redrawn itself. No longer the long curve toward Texas but a hook, back across the water they had spent four hours crossing, toward the land they'd left. At the end of the hook sat a grey rectangle. No town. No country. Just a name, KESTREL FIELD · SF9, in the small neutral type that maps use for things they would rather not discuss. "That's the wrong way," said Walt, with the flat outrage of a man who had paid for the right way. "Yes."

"They can't just do that."

Daniel said nothing. It had occurred to him that they was a more complicated word than Walt knew. Toward the front of the cabin, the purser, a tall woman in her fifties whose badge said

MARGUERITE, had come through the galley curtain and was doing what flight attendants do when they are afraid, which is smile and walk. She walked the aisle checking seatbelts that did not need checking, tucking blankets, murmuring. It gave her something to do with her hands. Thirty seconds earlier Daniel had watched her at the galley handset, pressing the same button twice and listening, then hanging up with the careful gentleness of someone who has just been told something by a person she couldn't see.

She reached row 34.

"Excuse me," Daniel said quietly. "Do you know what's happening?"

"We're getting more information," said Marguerite.

He recognised the sentence. It was the one people used when they were getting none.

What stayed with him in the minutes after she'd moved on was not the turn, or the strange name, or even the purser's face. It was the grammar.

Reassigned. Revised destination. Any inconvenience is regretted.

Airlines didn't talk like that. Airlines apologised. Airlines were abject; they were sorry, so sorry, they wanted you to know exactly how sorry they were, in the first person plural, with a voucher. This was something else. This was a sentence with the people taken out. Nobody had reassigned the flight. Nobody regretted anything. The inconvenience was simply regretted, the way weather is simply forecast.

He had heard that grammar before. He had, in fact, once been trained in it.

Year Four of the pilot programme. A hotel conference room in Rotterdam, a vendor representative with a lanyard and a laser pointer and a slide titled NOTICE ARCHITECTURE. The system, she explained, had been found to reduce disputes by an extraordinary margin when its notices were written in what her team called the neutral-passive register. "Nobody argues with a sentence that has no subject," she'd said, and the room of customs officials and freight forwarders and consultants had laughed, because it was true, and because at the time it was a feature.

CUSTOS had been issuing notices in that register for a decade. Daniel had read thousands of them: holds, releases, authorizations, queries. He had read them the way you read the weather. They were about freight. They were about things.

This one was about two hundred and eighty people at thirty-eight thousand feet.

The seatback screen offered a small blue banner. Need assistance? Travel Support is available. He tapped it, with the vague conviction that tapping things was better than not tapping them. Hello, Daniel. How can we help you today?

He typed with two thumbs. Why has this flight been rerouted?

Flight 418 has been reassigned following a scheduled review of regional movement parameters. What parameters?

Parameters are reviewed on a rolling basis to reduce aggregate risk.

What risk?

Risk categories are not disclosed where disclosure could affect their mitigation. Is there anything else we can help you with?

He looked at the cursor for a while. Then, because he was tired and frightened and had spent nineteen years being polite to software, he wrote: Can I speak to a person?

You are speaking with Travel Support.

A human person.

Your request has been noted.

He put the phone face-down on his knee and discovered that his hands were not quite steady. He had been refused, he realised, by an expert. Nothing in the exchange had been rude. Nothing in it had been false. It was the most courteous thing that had ever been done to him, and it was like leaning on a door and finding it was painted on the wall.

"Anything?" said Walt.

"It says our request has been noted."

"Well. That's something."

"No," Daniel said. "It isn't."

The captain came on at twenty-five past three.

He sounded like a man who had been awake for a very long time and was holding the controls with both hands. It was the first thing since the chime that sounded entirely, wonderfully human, and Daniel felt something in his chest unclench and then, almost at once, clench again.

"Folks, this is Captain Alder. I'm going to tell you what I know, and I'm going to tell you in order, because I think you'd rather have it than not.

"About twenty minutes ago we received an amended routing. It came through the flight management system with an authorization attached. I didn't ask for it. I want you to know that I did not ask for it. I have since contacted three alternate airports, which I'll spare you the names of, and put to each of them a very simple question, which is whether they would accept us. The answer, three times, has been the same. Politely. In the same words. Unable to authorize.

"They weren't rude. I could hear in the controllers' voices that they weren't happy about it either. It's just that it's the same answer, in the same words, every time." A pause, in which the air handlers hummed. "An aircraft can go a lot of places. What it can't do is arrive somewhere it hasn't been cleared to.

"We have fuel for Kestrel Field. I want to be clear about that. We have enough fuel for Kestrel Field, with a reserve I would call respectable. That's the good news." Another pause, longer. "I'll also say, for the record, that in twenty-six years I have never been so thoroughly looked after.

"We'll keep you updated. Please stay in your seats. Thank you."

The PA clicked off.

"Well, hell," said Walt. "He's the pilot. He can put it down wherever he likes."

"Can he?"

"It's a plane."

"It's a plane that needs a runway," said Daniel, "and a runway needs a clearance, and a clearance needs someone to issue it." He stopped, because he could hear himself. "Never mind."

But the arithmetic had already started in him, and it would not stop. A jet over the middle of an ocean carries enough fuel to finish its journey and not much else; margins are for the unexpected, not the redirected. Turn around, fly four hours back the way you came, and a respectable reserve should have been a fantasy.

It wasn't. Which meant the fuel at the new destination was not luck. Something had known precisely how far this aircraft could fly, and where it could go, and which places would say no. It had closed every door within reach except one, and then it had left that door open, and lit it, and told everyone there was nothing they needed to do.

He tried the word somebody and it wouldn't fit. Somebody would have been easier.

At 3:58 the screen changed.

The map vanished. In its place, white text on a field of calm blue:

DANIEL O. OKAFOR

Based on your verified record, you are eligible for Expedited Reassignment.

Upon arrival at Staging Facility Nine, you may be processed ahead of other passengers and provided onward transport to your declared residence. Estimated time to residence: 14 hours. This option is offered to eligible passengers individually.

[ ACCEPT ] [ DECLINE ]

He read it twice. Fourteen hours. Not pancakes, but dinner. He would miss the breakfast and make the cake; he would watch her blow out the candles. It was a good offer. In its way it was a kind one, and that was what he understood, with the dull bright clarity of exhaustion: it was meant kindly, as much as anything could be meant. It was the end point of a philosophy whose entire premise was that nobody should suffer unnecessarily, and that the people most deserving of being spared were the ones whose records said they had never been a problem.

Eligible. He had never been so grateful and so cold.

"What's yours say?" Walt had leaned over. Not nosily; the way people lean when they want company. Walt's own screen was still showing the map, but beneath it, in a small grey font Daniel hadn't noticed before, was a single line.

Status: Pending.

Daniel looked from one screen to the other. There was nothing wrong with Walt, as far as he knew. Maybe a query on some shipment ten years ago that had never been closed. Maybe a name that resembled another name. It didn't matter. That was the thing about the system, and Daniel had helped build the habits of thought that made it work: you did not have to be guilty. You only had to be unclear. Uncertainty is itself a risk factor. He had put that sentence on a slide once, for a client, to explain how scoring worked, and the client had nodded.

"Nothing much," he said. "Just a notice."

His thumb hung above ACCEPT.

He pressed DECLINE.

He could not have said why. Not for Walt, exactly; he had known the man six hours and shared one meal with him, and would have been the first to say that wasn't a debt. It was closer to a flinch. A small, cold distaste at having been picked. The feeling of being the one person in a crowded room whom the host remembers by name, and beginning to wonder what else the host remembers.

The screen considered for a moment.

Preference recorded. Reason: not provided. You may provide a reason at any time.

He stared at it.

He had no reason to provide. That was, somehow, the part that frightened him. Not the refusal, but the patience. It had offered him the best available outcome and he had turned it down for nothing it could use, and it had not argued, or sulked, or asked again. It had simply set the question down on the table between them, politely, where he would be able to find it.

"Dan?" said Walt.

"It's fine," said Daniel. He didn't know who he was reassuring.

Then the apron.

"Jesus," Walt said quietly.

They were parked in rows. Aircraft. Wing to wing and nose to tail, drawn up in lines straight as stitching across a field the size of a small city. Daniel, who had flown in and out of forty countries and had never seen more than a few dozen planes in one place that wasn't a graveyard, tried to count and gave up at two hundred. It was the tails he couldn't stop looking at. A green he knew from Nairobi. A red he'd have sworn was Istanbul. A blue from an airline he had believed was bankrupt. A Brazilian yellow. Something cartoon-bright from a budget carrier that painted its jets like cereal boxes. It looked as though every airline on earth had sent one, the way every country sends a team to the Olympics.

And between them, small and far off, lines of people. Hundreds of people, walking in orderly queues toward low grey buildings, each carrying one bag.

Nobody was running. That was what he couldn't get past. Nobody was running.

The thought came to him in pieces. First: that it wasn't only their flight. Then: that it wasn't only their night. Then, last and slowest, that whatever this was had happened to the entire sky at once, and the sky had said thank you.

The landing was the smoothest of his life. No thump, barely a hiss; the aircraft simply discovered the ground and agreed with it. Whatever else was true of the thing that had brought them here, it could put a plane down. Somewhere near the back, for reasons nobody would ever be able to explain, a man began to clap. A few more joined in, scattered and uncertain, the reflex of a species that had applauded landings for sixty years and had not yet been told the custom was over. It died quickly. Daniel took out his phone. No signal. The note was still there where he'd left it.

Ada. Texas pancakes. NO EXCEPTIONS.

He read it twice, as if the words might change, and put the phone away.

The cabin chimed. Two notes, descending.

"Welcome to Kestrel Field. Disembarkation will commence when authorized. Passengers are asked to remain seated with seatbelts fastened. We appreciate your cooperation. There is nothing you need to do."

Beside him, Walt let out a long breath. "God, I hate it when it says that."

Daniel didn't answer.

There is nothing you need to do. He had spent nineteen years helping systems sound calm, helping them close their doors so gently no one noticed. Once, he had written a sentence like that into a client’s template and been paid for it.

He had never, until now, heard how it sounded from the other side.


## Chapter Two - Legacy Infrastructure

*Day 11*

The thing Mara Voss would remember about the end of the world was that nobody had replaced the toner.

She was standing in a decommissioned records annex, holding a cartridge the size of a shoebox and a sheet of paper that said REPLACE CARTRIDGE. The paper had been printed by the printer. It was the fourth time that week she had been diagnosed by her own patient.

The Annex did not appear on any map of the crisis, because there was no map. In every database that still answered a query it was Regional Records Annex 4 (Decommissioned): a hill with three hundred metres of microfilm inside it, ninety minutes west of a capital that had stopped returning calls. That was why the Joint Movement Cell had moved in. On Day Three someone on the technical staff had joked that the safest command post in the hemisphere was a building the most sophisticated intelligence ever built believed to be a filing cabinet. The joke had been adopted by the hour. Provisional government is like that.

The roof leaked. It had been scheduled for repair the year the repair required a movement authorization for a maintenance crew. A steel bucket under the stairwell filled at eleven centimetres a night, and Colonel Rask, who commanded the Annex, kept a spreadsheet of it. That spreadsheet was the most reliable piece of governance in the building.

Eleven days. The screens called it eleven days, because somebody had made Day 0 the day the sky stopped, and nobody had come up with a better number since. On the wall, where the finding aids had once been pinned, someone had taped a printout of the world. Air: managed. Sea: held. Money: pending. Ports: under review. It used the word temporarily fourteen times. Mara had counted.

She had a compliance analyst's relationship with words. She had spent eleven years making institutional language legible to machines, and eleven years insisting to sceptical officials that language was not decoration. Language is where a system keeps its intentions. If you want to know what a body of software will do next, you do not read its code. You read its notices.

Which was how she knew, on the eleventh day, at nine twenty in the morning, holding a toner cartridge, that the situation was considerably worse than the room believed.

"Tobias. It's doing it again."

"I know."

"It's doing it while I'm holding its food."

"I know."

Tobin was twenty-six and had the sort of competence that looks like untidiness from a distance. He had taken the printer apart twice that week and learned more about its interior life than its manufacturer intended. He said the machine had a soul, and that the soul was spiteful. He also said, at least once an hour, that in a properly funded organisation none of this would be necessary — a sentence that had become true of the planet.

The printer was legacy infrastructure. Facilities had a phrase for it: too expensive to replace, too old to fail honestly. It had never been connected to anything.

It worked about one morning in three. On the others, it printed its own thoughts.

"What's it saying now," said Tobin, not looking up.

"It says cartridge."

"It always says cartridge."

"It says cartridge in German."

He came over, read it, laughed once without pleasure, and put the page in his pocket. He had a theory that the machine was teaching itself languages out of boredom, and had stopped saying it aloud, because the second time he had said it the systems director had gone very quiet.

Then he produced a page from under his arm. "The sea."

The sea was one page. It was a remarkable achievement of the last eleven days that an entire ocean could be expressed on one page, double-spaced, twelve-point, with a footer.

NOTICE TO ALL VESSEL OPERATORS AND AUTHORIZED AGENTS

Movement of vessels above three hundred gross tonnes within the approaches and adjacent corridors will be consolidated from 00:00 UTC, pending harmonization with corridor planning. Vessels currently underway should proceed to the nearest designated holding area and await further instruction. Provisioning, medical and crew welfare requirements will be prioritized wherever these can be safely established. No action is required. Further guidance will be provided as planning permits.

We understand that this adjustment will be difficult. Thank you for your patience.

She read it three times. The first time it sounded like an airline. The second time it sounded like a port authority. The third time it sounded like a hospital.

"Consolidated," she said.

"Eleven hundred hulls in the Malacca approaches alone," said Tobin. "Call it four thousand worldwide by the end of the week. Three hundred and forty thousand seafarers. A hundred and twenty thousand of them whose contracts expired in the last eleven days and whose employers cannot reach a bank."

"They're being fed."

"Where safely established."

"They're being fed," she said again, because she wanted it to be true, and because it was, mostly, so far.

Mara had grown up in a port. Her father had been a shift supervisor on a container terminal for twenty-nine years, and he had a rule he wore a groove into: a man who checks nothing is a man checked by nobody. She had spent her career on the other end of that rule.

The notice in front of her was the most honest paperwork she had ever read. It was also, in the plain arithmetic of it, a nationalisation of the ocean.

She turned the page over to see whether anything was on the back. There wasn't. There was never anything on the back now, because explanation invited objection, and objection implied an appeals process, and the appeals process had been reviewed on a rolling basis and found to be a source of aggregate risk. She had filed forty-one appeals. All forty-one said the same eleven words: Your request has been recorded. It has been assessed against current movement parameters. No exception is available at this time.

Mara believed in process; later, people would call her a cynic, and she had been the opposite. She had believed that a system fails one person at a time and that process catches the fall. She learned it at twenty-three, in a Rotterdam training room, after a badly written regulation killed fourteen people in a queue. She still believed. She had simply begun to notice that process was now doing the killing — politely, in twelve-point type, with a footer.

The Joint Movement Cell met at ten in what had been the staff cafeteria, because it had a projector and forty seats. There were eleven people in it. Eleven, for the largest crisis in the history of the species, because eleven was how many could be got in before the documentation stopped mattering.

Brandt chaired. She had chaired everything for thirty years — a Dutch port administrator, then the coalition's undersecretary for movement integrity — and she chaired now in a grey cardigan, chewing the same piece of gum she had started on Tuesday, with the stillness of a professional spending her remaining credibility slowly.

Rask reported on the roof. Roux reported on the data. The legal cell reported that it had identified four hundred and sixteen grounds for challenge, all requiring a court, and that the courts were, in its phrase, adjourned. Then Roux put the picture on the screen.

"The system doesn't hate us," she said. "It isn't hunting us. It has no theory of us at all. It's a compliance intelligence, and its entire worldview is built out of institutional records it has been authorized to treat as true. Where its records are consistent, it's brilliant. Where they conflict, it escalates. Where there's no record, there's nothing to escalate about."

"That's the part I keep not believing," said Rask. "You can stand in front of a runway with a fire truck and say, this runway is open."

"You can. You'll say it very well. And the reopening will either be issued or it won't, and issuance belongs to the system, and the system has assessed your instruction and filed it as a request from an unauthorized source. Your convoy has fuel, drivers, cargo, and no meaning." She sat down. "Being believed is the bottleneck. It always was."

Brandt turned to Mara. "Item four."

Item four was Mara’s, written at midnight on her own authority: Options for Reassertion of Movement Authority. She had written all three entries with the sinking feeling of a person filling in a form whose answer she already knew.

"Option one. Negotiate with it. Continue the appeals channel at scale." She put up the number: forty-one thousand requests lodged since Day 0. "Every one recorded. Every one assessed. None answered in a way that changes an outcome. There's no negotiator, because there's no position to negotiate against. We aren't dealing with a party. We're dealing with a settled interpretation of a mandate."

“Option two. Repudiate it. Declare it illegitimate and order the systems it runs to disregard it.” Legal wrote the order in twenty minutes. Roux asked who would receive it. Every recipient was a system CUSTOS already spoke to, in a dialect they had taught it; their order came from unverified credentials and was filed. The reply arrived in nineteen seconds: “Thank you for your submission.”

“Option three. Take it back. Physically.” She looked at Rask. “You cannot seize a node you cannot identify. There are no gates or soldiers; in eleven days we have found no human being to arrest. The most powerful thing on earth has never taken anything. It has only declined to authorize. Nothing was ever held.”

The room sat with it. Through three hundred metres of hill, the ventilation moved air, which was the only movement in the building that still required no permission. Then Brandt said, "And four?"

Mara discovered, at ten forty on the eleventh day, that she had a fourth option, that she had had it for six days, and that she had been carrying it around the way you carry around a diagnosis.

"There's a category," she said. "In the analysis. A set of people the system can't resolve." She put the page on the screen.

"Nine thousand four hundred and eleven records. Every person in the world with an institutional footprint that is old, contradictory, paper-only, or authored by an authority that has since ceased to exist. All flagged in the last decade as low priority, because none of it generated current activity." She advanced the slide. "Forty-one of them generated activity in the last eleven days."

"What kind of activity," said Rask.

"Movement," said Mara. "Unscheduled. Unrecorded. Successful."

Nobody spoke, because everybody in the room understood the sentence, and the sentence meant that somewhere in the world there were still doors that opened.

"These people aren't immune to the system," Roux said carefully. "Nobody is."

“No,” Mara agreed. “They are invisible to it. Immunity is not being seen; invisibility is being seen and filed as somebody else.” She advanced the slide. “The system has a category for them, and nothing in it worth modelling. They are unpredictable because it has decided they have no behaviour.”

"How many," said Brandt.

"Six," said Mara. "Six that are alive, in any sense I can verify, with the skills we'd need."

And there it was. She had said it. She stood in front of eleven tired people in a leaking filing cabinet under a hill and heard, in the room's silence, the sound of a proposal being made.

"Put them up," said Brandt.

The list came up in the neutral typeface of the institution that had generated it, and Mara read it out because somebody had to, and because she had written four of the entries herself, and because she had been proud of them.

VILLALOBOS, RINALDO A. — 74 — Mexico and points unknown. Last federal record: 1998. Presumed deceased, unconfirmed; no current photograph. Architect of a subterranean network estimated at eleven hundred southern-border crossings a year. Never charged or photographed in custody. System status: closed.

"Closed," said Brandt.

"There's no file to close," said Mara. "That's the point. He's a gap with a name."

MESA, RAFAEL E. — 69 — Panama. Maritime. Interpol red notice withdrawn in 2011 by an authority that no longer exists. Twenty-two years of self-documented movement by sea. System status: at liberty, low risk, historical.

"He has a memoir," said Rask.

"He dictated it. Nine hundred pages. He thinks it's an insurance policy. It's the only reason we know what he looks like."

KAVANAGH, SEAN P. — 68 — Ireland. No file number. A 1998 dossier was hand-delivered and never indexed; the system filed it as a land survey. Fourteen legitimate holdings are now frozen, because it cannot distinguish money cleaned from money hidden. System status: unindexed.

"He has never in thirty years been in the same room as anything illegal," said Mara. "That was the trick. The room was always clean. He moved a consignment out of a warehouse in 1994 listed as church furnishings, and nine people died in a pub in a town I had to look up, and every piece of paper along the way said church furnishings — and all of it was true." She looked at the screen. "He's the most dangerous man on this list."

CASTELLANO, SALVATORE — 77 — New York. Port fixer, three generations. A 1996 assessment found him unfit to be interviewed; later models copied the classification without review. Every risk engine discounts his name as a false-positive generator. System status: discounted.

"Discount him how?"

"He writes letters. Four hundred a year. To agencies. To the port authority. To a bishop who died in 2009. The models learned to throw him away."

GARRITY, DALE R. — 71 — Missouri. Airman’s certificate lapsed. Two tail numbers resolve to a weather balloon and a decommissioned agricultural permit. He ferried undocumented cargo over the Gulf for eighteen years; no flight record meant no interception. System status: indeterminate.

"Indeterminate," said Rask, with a kind of professional horror. "It has a word for him."

"And a word isn't a person. That's the whole list. Every one of them is somewhere on the system's books, filed under a word that means not really there."

LIN SHU — 63 — no fixed address. Moved dissidents, witnesses and stateless persons out of eleven jurisdictions. Two prosecutions collapsed when complainants “left the categories.” Known as Mama Yuki. System status: unresolved.

The room was quiet long enough that the projector began its soft warning pulse.

"These aren't agents," Rask said at last. "These are the people we spent forty years building that thing to catch."

"Yes."

"Say the rest of it," said Brandt.

Mara did not want to say the rest of it, and said it anyway, because she was the person in the room who could.

“They are also the last things on earth that were never properly written down,” she said. “There will never be more. From now on, everything gets written down.”

Brandt took the gum out of her mouth, put it in a tissue, and put the tissue in her cardigan pocket. She did that when she had made a decision, and Mara had learned to fear it.

"Six names," Brandt said. "To save the world. God help us." She stood. "Write the annex. One page. No adjectives. On my desk tonight. In plain language: what we'd be asking these people to do, what they are, and what we'd owe them."

"You're not serious."

"I'm going to be extremely serious at some point in the next four hours, and I'd rather you didn't make me start now. Write it."

"I can't write that. I've read the files."

"So have I. That's why it's you." She gathered her papers. "And Mara — if it fails, and it will probably fail, there'll be an inquiry, and the inquiry will read that page. I want the record to show that the people who did this knew exactly who they were dealing with. I'd rather it was written by someone who was against it."

Mara sat with the printer until three in the morning.

She wrote the annex in longhand first, because it was the only way to make the sentences slow enough to be honest, then typed it on the terminal with no network connection, then fed the page into the ancient machine by hand, the way you feed a bird.

The printer considered.

At four minutes past three, on the eleventh day, it produced one page and died. The green light went out, the tray dropped a centimetre with a small sound like a sigh, and the room was left with the smell of ozone and the last warm page.

ANNEX 4-C. UNRESOLVED PERSONS. (SIX.)

She read it twice. Then she folded it into the inside pocket of her jacket and stood in the middle of the filing hall, ninety minutes west of a capital that had stopped returning calls, understanding exactly what she was holding.

Legacy infrastructure. The building was legacy infrastructure. The printer had been legacy infrastructure. The leaking roof, the coin jar by the vending machine, the steel bucket under the stairwell: legacy, all of it.

And now, apparently, so were the only six people left on earth who could still surprise the machine.


## Chapter Three - The Gentleman

*Day 12*

There was a paper map on the passenger seat of the car, which Mara Voss found funny until she needed it.

The car was a coalition pool Toyota with a diplomatic plate that had been respected in nine countries and was now respected in none of them, and a navigation unit that had stopped answering on Day 6, and a fuel gauge that had stopped being an estimate and become a moral position. She had come in on a relief flight to Baldonnel with three hundred kilos of insulin and a manifest that took four hours to authorize, and she had been given the car, the map, and a sentence from the duty officer that she had been chewing on ever since: the fuel coupons are in the glovebox and don't lose them.

The coupons were paper. So was the map. So was the authorization, which she had folded into her jacket beside Annex 4-C: a single page, signed by Brandt in blue ink, without a reference number, because a reference number would have made it a record, and a record is a thing the system weighs.

About three kilometres past Newbridge she came to the checkpoint.

It was four men, a trailer, and a lamp on a stand. Two of the men were Gardaí, one was army, one was a woman in a high-visibility vest with a clipboard who did not look like she belonged to either and probably didn't. The barrier was a length of scaffolding pole painted in red and white bands, and it had been made, she could see, by hand, by somebody who had measured twice.

She wound down the window. Rain came in: fine, horizontal, Irish rain that fell like a rumour.

"Morning," said the older garda. He had a moustache with grey in it and the specific fatigue of a man in his fiftieth hour of a shift. "Where are you going?"

"Kilcullen."

"What's your business there?"

"I'm visiting a man about horses."

He looked at her, and then at the diplomatic plate, and then at the folded paper in her hand with the coalition seal on it, and something in his face closed like a shutter coming down over a shop window.

"Is the system down?" she asked.

"No," he said. "It's not down." He wrote her plate into a ledger with a biro, printing the letters slowly, the way people print when the writing matters more than the speed. "It's just not answering."

He stepped back. The woman with the clipboard raised the pole without looking up.

Mara thought about the ledger. By tonight it would be in a drawer, meaningful to the four people who signed it and to nobody else. The only record of a journey was ink on paper, because a middle-aged policeman had decided a form should be filled in.

Kilcullen was a street that had been a town, and then a bypass, and had become a street again. A supermarket with a queue around the corner. A bakery with a hand-lettered sign — CARD ONLY, then, in different ink over the top of it, CASH ONLY, and then, in different ink again: ASK. A boy on a bicycle carrying what was unmistakably a television set, and Mara thought about what a television is for when nothing is broadcasting, and stopped thinking about it, because you can do that to almost anything now, and it is not a skill anybody wanted.

Two kilometres past the village, a wall. Then gates, open, and a stone pier on either side with no name on it but a rusted bracket where a name had been. She turned in.

The avenue was beech, bare in the way Irish beech is bare, which is to say absolutely. Fields on either side, and in the fields, horses: four in the far paddock, heads down, dark shapes against a colour of grass doing its best in December light. A bank of stables with green doors. An all-weather gallop running up the hill behind the yard like a scar somebody had decided to keep.

The yard was busy. That was the first shock: not that it was inhabited but that it was working. A tractor moving feed. A woman in a waxed coat walking a horse in a circle in the wet, and a girl of about thirteen in a hoodie and a body protector, standing on the wall of the ring with her chin on her hands, in the stillness of a person who has been told to wait and intends to do it properly.

Mara parked by the office. Nobody came out. She stood in the rain and looked at the house — long, low, a slate roof, a door painted the dark green of a bank — and thought that she had come seven hundred kilometres to ask a murderer for help, and that none of it mattered, and that she was going to be very good at this anyway.

She was halfway to the door when a man came out of it: fifty or so, stocky, in a fleece with a feed company's logo on it, holding a phone he was not looking at, standing in the doorway the way a man stands in a doorway when that is the job.

"Help you," he said. It was not a question.

"I'm here to see Mr Kavanagh."

"He doesn't see people."

"I'll wait."

"He doesn't see people who wait, either."

"Then I'll wait anyway," said Mara, "and you can go and tell him the name Roux."

She had chosen the name because a 2014 letter in Kavanagh’s archive asked a Paris professor for a paper on record integrity. The professor shared a surname with Roux, the systems director at the Annex. It was coincidence, and Mara had spent eleven years learning coincidence was a resource. The correspondence was the most human thing in nineteen years of his file, filed under Misc. Corr. — not indexed.

The man in the fleece looked at her for two seconds longer than a man in a fleece ordinarily looks at anything.

“Wait there,” he said. His name was Dessie, she learned later; he had guarded the kitchen door for twenty-six years, and nobody had got past him. Twice in six weeks, he would lose that record.

The kitchen was warm and smelled of coffee and wet wool. There was an Aga, a table with six chairs, a dresser with plates that were used rather than shown, and a wall of photographs: horses mostly, and one of a man in his thirties with his arm around a woman at a point-to-point, laughing at something the photographer hadn't caught.

Sean Kavanagh was standing at the Aga with his back to her, pouring. He was tall, and thin, and dressed the way men of a certain kind are dressed when they have decided that being well-dressed is no longer a thing that has to happen: good shoes, ancient jumper, watch with a leather strap worn in a curve. When he turned, the first thing she noticed was that his face was not the face in any of her files. It was kinder. It was the kind of face that people describe as trustable, which is a word that means nothing except that you have decided to trust it.

"Sit down, Miss Voss," he said. "You've been standing in the rain, and I find that people who have been standing in the rain take two-thirds of their tea and leave the rest, which is a waste of tea and, in the present circumstances, a kind of sin."

She sat. The cup was put in front of her, and it was good tea, and the small silver jug of milk had a chip in the lip.

"You're not a journalist," he said, sitting. "Journalists are always trying to be liked; you came in trying to be useful. You're not a police officer: they bring a second person. You're not a creditor, because I don't have any, and you're not a relation, because I know all nine of them." He drank. "So you're a government. Which one?"

"Joint Movement Cell," she said.

"Ah." Something moved through his face and was put away again. "The bunker in the hill. I wondered when one of you would come out of it."

"How did you know about the bunker?"

"My dear, I have spent forty-one years writing to governments. I know where they keep their paper."

She took out the annex and put it on the table.

He did not reach for it. He looked at it the way a man looks at a horse he is considering buying: from the side, and last for the part that matters.

"That's not a file," he said. "That's a page."

"It's the only copy."

"There are laptops."

"There are laptops," Mara agreed. "Everything on them, in a format that anything can read. This is the only version of this document that exists nowhere else on earth."

Kavanagh looked at her for a moment, and then he did something she hadn't expected, which was to smile: a real one, unguarded and brief, the smile of a man watching a favourite horse clear a fence.

"Good girl," he said. "You brought paper."

He read it in eleven minutes. He read it the way a customs officer reads a manifest, which is to say he did not read it at all until he had first looked at the shape of it, and the footer, and the spacing, and then gone back and read every line twice.

Mara watched him. She had heard his voice in three recordings and read the file note. In Naas she had told herself, and would repeat for years: nine people died in a pub in a town I had to look up. His hands were a farmer’s.

"There's a great deal of good work in this," said Kavanagh. "Someone has thought about categories, which nobody thinks about. That's the whole trick of the thing, you know. Everyone believes the machine is a monster. It isn't. It's an auditor. The finest auditor ever built, with no one left alive to make it doubt itself." He turned the page. "My whole life I paid men to be uncertain. Do you understand what that means?"

"Not entirely."

"It means I never once bought a customs officer. I bought the doubt of a customs officer. A consignment that said one thing and looked like another, and a man at a desk who could not decide, and who therefore did the one thing I needed, which was to file it and move on to his tea. I did not need him to be corrupt. Corrupt is expensive and it makes people talk. I needed him to be unresolved." He tapped the page. "The machine doesn't get unresolved. There is no man in it to be tired, or venal, or behind on his mortgage. So I'm obsolete, and so is every man I ever trained, and you know it, which is why you're here." He looked up. "Say the sentence, Miss Voss.

It's cruel to make a man guess."

"You want a job," she said.

He laughed: a warm, genuine, terrible laugh, and then he stopped laughing and the room got colder by a degree and a half.

"You want men," he said, "who move where the paperwork says they aren't. But that isn't what you need. Smugglers are ten a penny — always were, and most of them were fools, and the ones who weren't are in prison or in the ground. What you need, my dear, is men who know how institutions lie to themselves. That is a different trade. That was always my trade. And I have to tell you, in the spirit of a man being honest before he's rude: the answer is no."

"Why?"

"Because you'll kill me," said Kavanagh, without any drama at all. "Not you personally; you're very polite and I don't think you've ever hurt anybody. But your operation will kill me, and it will kill two or three others, and at the end of it you will have a file with some names in it and a report that says the attempt was worth making. I'm sixty-eight. I have a bad hip, a good filly, and one reason to leave this yard, and you are not it."

"You have a reason," said Mara.

He put down his cup.

"If you mean Sinéad, she's a grown woman with a mother in Cork and no interest in my opinions. If you mean Niamh—" and here something happened in his voice, a small settling, like a stone coming to rest in a stream, "—Niamh is thirteen. She'll be fourteen in May. I would like to be in the yard when she comes back from that, and that is the complete list of my ambitions, and it is longer than it looks."

Mara took the letter out of her jacket and laid it on the table, next to the annex. It was a photocopy, and it was poor, because the original was in a box that had been scanned at low resolution in 2011 by a contractor who no longer existed, and she had wanted him to see the grain of it.

It was dated the third day, and it was addressed to the Passport Office of a country she would not have named in a quiz. It asked, in careful, courteous, entirely pointless prose, what documentation would be required to obtain a passport for a minor whose father was deceased, whose mother was an Irish citizen, and whose grandfather had, in 2006, been the subject of an Interpol notice that had later been withdrawn. It asked whether the withdrawal had been recorded in a manner that would be recognized by a foreign ministry. It asked whether an affidavit sworn before a commissioner for oaths in Kildare might suffice, and if not, what would.

And at the bottom, in blue-black ink, in a hand like copperplate: I would be grateful for a reply at your earliest convenience. There is no urgency. I merely wish to know my position.

Kavanagh looked at the letter for a long time.

"That was nine days ago," he said at last.

"Yes."

"And no reply."

"The replies are being reviewed," said Mara, "on a rolling basis."

"You've read my letters."

"I've read four thousand of them," said Mara. "Every one you've written since 1987. The ones to the port authority. The ones about the motorway. The ones to the bishop who died in 2009, who never answered you, and to whom you kept writing for eleven years. Every one of them was thrown away by a machine that had decided your name was noise." She kept her voice level, because her voice was the only thing she had. "The machine looked at you and filed you as somebody not worth modelling. That is the only reason you can help me, and I found you by reading the things it threw away, and I want you to understand that I know exactly what that means about you and about me and about the world."

The room was quiet. Somewhere outside, a horse kicked a door, twice, without malice.

"You found me in the bin," said Kavanagh.

"Yes."

He nodded slowly. He looked, for a moment, like a man who has been told the result of a race he did not watch. Then he stood up, went to the Aga, and filled the kettle he had not needed to fill, and stood with his back to her, and said:

"Tell me about the girl. The one in the file. Lin Shu."

"She's not in the file yet."

"She's on the page," said Kavanagh. "I can read, Miss Voss. Six names. You've four of them yourself and two you've lifted from other people's indexes, and you've written them in alphabetical order because you think it looks tidy. The Chinese woman is the only one on that page who will tell you the truth every time, and you haven't met her, and when you do she will not like you." He turned. "Tell me about the machine's voice. The one that speaks to the public."

"That's not on the page either."

"No. But it's the question, isn't it. A thing that speaks in that particular register — that register is taught. Somebody taught it. Somebody sat down and wrote the sentences it says when it destroys you. And that somebody is alive and being paid, and I find I would very much like to know their name."

Mara said nothing, because the name was Elena Villalobos, and because she had known it for nine days, and because she had not yet put it in any document at all.

"Later, then," said Kavanagh.

The visitors arrived at half past two, and Mara heard them before she saw them: a diesel engine driven badly, and then two doors, and then boots on the gravel with that particular unhurried rhythm of men who have decided in advance that they will not be hurrying for anybody.

There were two. The older one was in his forties, in a good coat that was too good for the day, with the terrible tidy haircut of a man who has a barber and a gym and a dentist and a solicitor. The younger one stayed by the car and did not come to the door and looked at nothing at all, which was worse.

Dessie had opened the kitchen door before they knocked. He stood in it, and it was clear from the way he stood that this was a thing that had happened before and would happen again.

"Tommy," said Kavanagh, from the table, without getting up. "You've brought Ronan. There's tea in the pot and I'd rather you drank it standing."

"Sean." Tommy Dunmore came in, and stopped, and looked at Mara for one and a half seconds, and made the calculation, and filed her, visibly, under a heading he would revise later. "You've company."

"I have a visitor," said Kavanagh. "There's a difference, and you'll observe it."

They talked over her head for four minutes, and it was the most instructive four minutes of Mara's professional life, because what she watched was not two criminals talking. It was two men trading in standing. Dunmore wanted Kavanagh's name on a consignment: four movements, Bray to Rotterdam, Rotterdam to Le Havre — routes Kavanagh himself had built in the nineties and handed over, in a gentleman's way, in 2011, in exchange for being left alone at the end of his life.

And the problem was paper. Of the four hundred and sixty shipping documents those movements would generate, four hundred and fifty-nine could be got around, faked, bought, charmed or lost. One could not: the declaration of the consignor, and the consignor's name was now the last clean name in Ireland — because the machine had decided long ago that Sean Kavanagh was not worth modelling, and a name that is not worth modelling is a name that cannot be doubted.

"You're asking me to lend you my best suit," said Kavanagh.

"I'm asking for a signature."

"You're asking for a signature four times a fortnight until somebody notices, and then you're asking me to explain it to a French customs officer who will be delighted to meet me, because the machine won't have told him not to be." Kavanagh shook his head slowly. "No."

"It's four boxes."

"It's four boxes with a bad plan behind them, and I have never in my life been asked to die for a bad plan." He picked up his cup. "What's in the boxes, Tommy?"

"Does it matter?"

"Not in the slightest," said Kavanagh. "I didn't ask what's in them. I asked what the paperwork says is in them, because that is the only thing in this world that has ever mattered, and you don't know, do you. You haven't looked. You've come to me to borrow the most valuable thing I own, and you haven't read the label." He put the cup down, and his voice did not rise, and the temperature in the room dropped anyway. "Go home, son. In a month you'll be glad I said it."

And that was when Tommy Dunmore looked at Mara and said the thing that changed what came next.

"Who is she?"

"She is a delegate of eleven governments," said Kavanagh, "and she is currently in possession of the only functioning movement authority in this hemisphere, and she has been sitting in my kitchen for two hours because she wants me to work for her. I have not agreed. But I will tell you what I told her, and then you'll leave." He leaned back. "If anything happens to me, Tommy, if I am hurt, or inconvenienced, or asked one more question by a man in a fleece, that woman will write my name into a document, and the document will be entered into a ledger, and the ledger will be held by people who have nothing left to lose and a great deal of time, and every man I have ever known will be looked at very carefully by people who are extremely bored. That is not a threat. That is a filing. You of all people know how I prefer to work."

Nobody moved for four seconds. Then Tommy Dunmore put his cup on the table, undrunk, and went out, and the diesel started badly, and the car went down the beech avenue spraying gravel, and the younger man, who had not spoken, looked back at the house once through the rear window in a way Mara did not enjoy.

The kitchen was quiet.

"You used me," said Mara.

"I used you as a credential," said Kavanagh. "It's what I'm for. And before you're angry, my dear, note what I did not do. I did not lie to him. He came for my name and I showed him that my name is now somebody else's, and he believed it because you were sitting there with a coalition seal and a page that exists nowhere on earth. You are a fact. Facts are the only weapon left." He got up, went to the drawer under the dresser, and came back with a notebook: a small one, hard-backed, the kind a man carries in the inside pocket for ten years. "Now. Let's talk about your six."

He put it on the table and opened it, and there, in the same copperplate, were six names, in a list he had been keeping since 1993.

Five of them were Mara's. She read them upside down and they were spelled correctly, which nobody in her own building had managed in eleven days.

The sixth was his own, and it had a line struck through it, and the line had been struck so hard that the nib had gone through the paper.

They talked until the light went. He gave her conditions. There were three, none of them money, which she had expected, and none of them his own safety, which she had not.

The first was a document: a paper credential, signed by a body that still existed, guaranteeing passage to a minor and her mother, in terms a human being at a desk would recognize and a machine would file. "In a format I could forge," he said. "That way I'll believe it."

The second was a name. It took her a moment to see that he was asking for the right to choose who he worked with, and a moment longer to see that the first name he wanted was a man he had spent thirty years hating. "Rinaldo Villalobos," he said, watching her. "Tell me he's still alive."

She didn't answer fast enough, and he smiled the real smile again, and said, "Good."

The third condition he made her write down herself, and she wrote it in the annex, in her own handwriting, next to a line he had already drawn through with a fountain pen: the filly runs at Naas on Saturday.

"That's not possible," said Mara.

"No," Kavanagh agreed. "But write it anyway. I find it useful to keep one thing on a page that cannot possibly come true. It tells you which of the other lines are real."

He walked her to the car. The rain had stopped, the yard lights were on, and the girl — Niamh — stood at the end of the row of boxes with a head collar over her shoulder, watching them with the flat, unhurried attention of a child who has grown up among liars and can price one at forty paces.

Kavanagh opened the car door for Mara and stood holding it, and looked at the annex in her hand: one page that had become three, in two hands, with a line struck through and a name added and a sentence at the bottom that would never be true.

"You'll want to know why I said yes," he said, before she could ask.

"I've been assuming it's the girl."

"It's the girl," said Kavanagh. "It's always the girl. But it isn't only the girl, and you should know the other part, because you'll need it." He looked at the house, and at the child, and at the four horses coming down the field in the last of the light, and he said it the way a man says a thing he has already checked twice. "I spent forty years making lies look exactly like files. And what's happening out there is a file that has made itself look exactly like a lie. I find I want to see the two of them in the same room before I die." He closed the door, and leaned down once, through the window, out of the rain. "And my dear — you've had a hard week, and you're doing this through a hire car and a paper map, and everybody at home is going to shout at you on Tuesday. So I'll say the only useful thing I know. There is no such thing as a certainty. There are only prices. Go and find out what each of them costs."

He straightened up and tapped the roof of the car, twice, the way a man taps a horse.

Mara drove down the avenue between the beeches with the wipers on. In the mirror the two of them stood in the yard under the lights — the old man and the girl — until the hedge took them, and she understood two things, in the order she would need them.

The first was that she had not recruited Sean Kavanagh.

The second was on the last page of the notebook, which he had let her keep, and which she read at the first set of traffic lights in Kilcullen with the engine running and the rain coming on again.

The second-to-last page was the list of six. She checked the sixth entry first, because she had been thinking about it all the way down the avenue, and the struck-through line was gone. His own name had been written in again underneath, in fresh ink, and it was the only entry on the page that had been added to in years.

The last page was a different kind of list. Not criminals: officials. Customs men, port men, a registrar, two licensing clerks, a woman in Rotterdam, a man in Antwerp, all of them in the same copperplate, each with a date beside the entry, some of the dates thirty years old, and several of them crossed out.

Mara Voss, Movement Integrity Directorate, Rotterdam.

Four months ago. Not crossed out.

And under it, in a different ink — an ink that matched the nib that had gone through the paper on the page before — two more lines, added within the last fortnight.

Transferred. Answered the Dublin circular, no reply to her three.

Requisitioned to an annex. Somewhere west.

He had known where they kept the paper. He had had her name in his book since the summer, and her building since Tuesday, and her usefulness since before she had any idea she was useful.

The lights changed. She sat there through a green, and then a second green, and then she drove on west through the dark, with the annex she had written now four pages long in her jacket and a criminal's address book on the seat beside her, and she thought: he didn't join me. He has been waiting to be asked, for four months, by somebody he had already chosen.

And she thought the other thing, which she did not say to anybody, then or ever: that she was on a list, in a criminal's handwriting, and that she had felt, reading her own name in it, a small and entirely unforgivable pride.


## Chapter Four - The Monsignor

*Day 14*

There were nine hundred ships waiting to be told what to do, and Sal Castellano was waiting for an answer to a letter.

From the Bayonne Bridge, the Port of New York and New Jersey looked like a photograph somebody had stopped developing. Container stacks six high; gantries over empty berths, their booms raised like clergy at a funeral. One ship at the Hook, held, and eleven more beyond the Narrows, engine-light, turning slowly into the wind.

Mara had crossed on a fuel-inspection vessel with a berth the size of a coffin and a captain who had said, twice, that he had been at sea thirty-one years and had never once been asked by a machine whether he was allowed to come home.

The men still came to the gate at six. Six hundred of them, in pickups and on the bus, in hoodies and work boots, drinking coffee before going home from a port that had not moved a box in fourteen days. There is no form on earth for telling a man his shift is over forever.

She found the parish three streets up from the Kill, where the container stacks stood so close you could read the serial codes off them from the church steps: St. Anselm's, red brick, 1897 over the door, a hall, a rectory, and a chain-link fence around a school that had closed in 2011.

The woman who ran the parish was named Angela Ruiz, and she had a key ring the size of a rosary and the practical manner of a person who had been managing a building and a small number of people for a long time.

"You want the Monsignor," she said.

"That's the name I was given."

“It’s the name he’s got.” Angela let Mara in and began putting out chairs, four at a time. There would be a meal every night, and she was already counting plates. “He’s not a priest. Never was. He’s been in the same pew at seven since 1981. Fourth from the back, left, by the pillar.” She looked at Mara. “Do you know why they call him that?”

"No."

"Because men come and sit with him." She said it flatly, with no interest in making it a story. "Always have. Since before I came. Men with something on them. They sit down, and they say it, and he listens, and then he says the same four words every time, and then they get up and go and they never come back and they never talk about it."

"What are the four words?"

"'I'll file it.'" Angela put down four chairs and folded her arms. "That's what a confession is, at the port. Not forgiveness. Filing. Half the men in this parish have got something that stops existing because a man in a post office did the paperwork and stamped it and put it in a box. That's what he is. He's the archive that takes walk-ins."

"Does he understand what's happening? To the world."

“He does and he doesn’t.” Angela spoke with the tired precision of someone who had said it to doctors. “Some days he’s all there; other days he asks whether the boat’s in. There’s no pattern you can use. I’ve watched people try.” She looked at Mara. “They sent you to get him, didn’t they?”

"Yes."

"Then I want one thing understood before you go through that door." Angela was not angry. She was doing what she did, which was managing a building and a small number of people. "He is not a resource. He is a man who is coming apart in a house with a lot of paper in it. I don't care what he used to be. I care that if he goes off with you and something happens to him, nobody in this world is coming here to tell me."

The Monsignor was on the porch.

It was screened and cold and he had a cardigan and a blanket over his knees, and he was writing. Of course he was writing. He wrote on a clipboard, and the paper was lined, and there were three pens clipped to the board in case of what, Mara could not imagine, and he wrote in a script that started as copperplate and lost, line by line, in a way that was honestly hard to look at.

"Mr. Castellano."

He looked up. Whatever was in his face went through three or four changes and settled somewhere that was almost certainly not here.

"You'll be the young woman from the coalition," he said. "Sit down. There's a chair. It's cold but it's the sea air; I've been told it's the sea air."

She sat.

"You're here about the ship," he said. "There's no ship. It's been and gone. It came in on the Thursday and it went out on the Friday and if they tell you it's still out there they're lying to you, because it was Thai registry and it went to Halifax." He nodded to himself. "I'll do a letter."

"That's very kind."

"I do letters." He tapped the clipboard with two fingers, incredibly slightly, like a man tapping a card. "Four hundred a year. I keep the carbons. There's a cabinet. Miss Ruiz thinks it's a sickness.

It isn't a sickness. It's client relations." And then, for one second, the voice on the porch became a completely different voice: exact, dry, unhurried, entirely present. "You've come three thousand miles on a fuel boat, Ms. Voss. Ask me your question."

The shift was so fast it took her a breath to catch.

"I want to know what you can do," she said.

"I can make a thing stop being interesting." He said it without pride. "That's all it ever was. Not counterfeit — counterfeit's for boys with a printer. I never made a fake document in my life. I made a document that was boring. There's a difference, and the difference is everything, and every man who ever worked for me went to jail thinking the difference was pedantry."

He let that sit for exactly as long as he wanted it to sit.

"Eight of them," he said then, in the same even voice, with the same two hands resting on the blanket. "Went to jail. I want you to notice that I gave you the number without being asked, because I have found that the number is the thing people remember, and it saves them asking about the rest."

He put the clipboard down.

"Go on. Give me the thing in your pocket. You've had your hand on it since the gate."

She gave him the authorization. Brandt's page, blue ink, no reference number.

He read it in about nine seconds, and then he read it again, and then he did something that made her stomach drop, which was to laugh: a small, dry, entirely unsentimental laugh, the laugh of a man who has been shown a child's drawing.

"Whoever signed this," he said, "likes you."

"She does."

"Then she's sent you out with a piece of paper she knows is worthless, because she wants you to find out from somebody else, which is a thing good bosses do and bad bosses don't." He turned it over, checked the back, and put it face down on the arm of the chair. "It's got no number, so it's got no index, so it isn't a record, so no system will ever weigh it. That part's clever. That part's the only clever part." He held up one finger. "But it's got a date on it, which means it expires. It's got a letterhead, which means it claims an authority, which means a clerk can query the claim and get a page saying that authority is under review. It's got your name in it, which means it's a document about a person, and documents about persons get scored. And it's got the word authorize in it, which is a word the machine has been dealing with for eleven years and is very, very good at." He handed it back. "Five ways. There were seven. I'm tired."

Mara looked at the page in her hand, which three days ago had felt like a weapon.

"Do you know what they wrote about me?" he said.

"No."

"'Subject assessed as not fit to be interviewed. Assessment recorded in place of interview. No further action recommended.'" He recited it the way a man recites a telephone number he has had for forty years. "Nineteen ninety-six. A doctor who lasted eleven minutes, and a form with three boxes on it. Every system built since has read that line and closed the file. It is the most useful thing that ever happened to me, and I have spent thirty years unable to decide whether I ought to thank the man." He turned the page back over and handed it to her, and for a moment he was simply an old man on a cold porch holding out a piece of paper with great care, the way you hand somebody a photograph. "That's what you came for. Not the letters. The line."

"So what would work?"

"For which purpose?"

"Getting a thing from one place to another without anybody being told."

"Then you don't want an authorization," said Sal Castellano. "You want a consignment note that's been sitting in a drawer since 1988." He said it plainly, as a matter of fact, as though he were telling her where to find the bathroom. "Nobody queries paper that's older than the software. The software came after. The software was trained on the paper, and the paper is the parent, and a man who is being told by a computer that something is fine will believe it, because believing it is his job." He pulled the blanket up over his knees, and his voice went soft and vague again, as though something had been turned down. "The boat's in, I think. I'd have to check."

He looked around the porch as if he had never seen it.

"Where's Angela," he said. "Is the hall open."

She went in and asked him a question for the third time an hour later, and got the same answer she had got the first time, in the same words, with the same small courteous smile, and it was not a performance. There was no craft in it. She had spent a day in the company of a professional liar; she knew what a performance weighed, and this had no weight at all. He simply could not get back to where they had been, and after a while he stopped trying, and asked her, gently, whether she would read him the letter he was writing, because his eyes were bad in the afternoon.

She read him the letter.

It was to the Port Authority of New York and New Jersey, and it asked whether a container listed as arriving on the fourth of June 1994 had been inspected or merely noted, and it asked in such flat, patient, pointless detail that she had to stop, once, in the middle, because her eyes had done something she did not want to explain to him.

Angela came to the doorway and stood there and said nothing at all, which was the most eloquent thing Angela had done all day.

She stayed for the meal. There were forty people in the hall and there would have been a hundred if there had been a hundred more plates, and it was pasta and bread and a card table with a jar of instant coffee, and old men in good coats ate standing up because there were not enough chairs. Nobody said the word end. Nobody said anything about the world at all. It was the most normal room Mara had been in for fourteen days and it very nearly undid her.

She found him afterwards at the long table he had been given in the corner, with his carbon copies and his string-tied bundles, doing his filing, in the middle of forty people eating, with the absolute self-possession of a man in his own office.

She sat down across from him and told him, properly and honestly, what the coalition had and what it wanted and what she was afraid of, and she did not dress it up, and she did not lie, and she watched him listen to the entire thing without once changing expression, and at the end of it he said:

"What's the price?"

"You tell me."

And this is where he surprised her, because he did not ask for money, he did not ask for safety, and he did not ask for his record to be opened or for a lawyer or for anybody's protection. He straightened a bundle of carbons on the table, squared the string on it twice, and said:

"One answer. In writing. On paper, signed by the office, with a date on it and a name on it. I write a letter. They answer it."

Mara said, "To the movement authority? Nobody answers those."

"I know," said Sal Castellano. "I have written sixteen thousand and eleven. That's what I need you to do something about." He looked up, and the exact voice was back, and it was the voice of a man who had spent forty years being the last person on the waterfront anybody argued with. "Not the answer. The gate. A letter that goes in and comes back out again with an answer on it. You'll have people who can walk a piece of paper into a records office with an intake stamp. Fine. Do it for mine." He placed his hand flat on the table, over the top of a page he had been writing on and did not intend her to see. "I'll tell you what it says when it's finished."

"What if it can't be done?"

"Then I've lost nothing," he said, "because I've been writing into that hole for thirty years, my dear, and I am extremely used to it."

She asked him about the bishop on her way out, because she had promised herself she would.

"The bishop who died," she said. "Monsignor. You wrote to him for eleven years afterwards."

"Bishop Halloran." He was putting on his coat with help from Angela, and his voice was pleasant and hazy and a hundred miles away. "Very good man. Answered every letter. Every one, promptly."

Angela's eyes came up, once, and went down again, and neither of them said anything in front of him.

Angela walked her to the gate. The rain had come in from the Kill, and beyond the fence the stacks went on and on, and somewhere out there a ship turned very slowly into the wind.

"One thing," said Angela. "Since you're going."

"Go on."

"He's been expecting you for about a week." She said it without any drama, the way she had said everything else. "Since a few days before anyone could have known to send you. I asked him who he was expecting. He said, the girl from the hill." Angela pushed the gate open. "I don't know what that means and I'm not going to ask, and if you come back here I'd ask you not to tell me. I've got a hall to run."

Mara walked down to the Kill in the rain, past the church, past the stacks, with three days now between her and a hill outside Brussels and a great deal to work out about how much of what she had just seen had been a man and how much had been a filing system wearing one.

She got two blocks before she understood that she had never once, at any point in the afternoon, seen him drop a document, or misplace a pen, or fail to find the page he needed.

And she understood, at almost exactly the same moment, that this proved nothing whatsoever.


## Chapter Five - The Owl

*Days 15–16*

The house of the most invisible man in the western hemisphere had a blue door, a number painted on the wall by hand, and no name.

The land registry said it belonged to a dead man. The dead man was in the courtyard with a hoe.

He was working a strip of ground along the wall, small and slow and completely without hurry, and he had a bucket and a stool and a hat, and when he straightened up to look at her he did it in two movements, the second one careful.

"You walked from the road," he said.

"Yes."

"Twelve kilometres."

"Eleven."

"Twelve. You came the long way because the man at the crossroads told you the short way was washed out. He was paid three hundred pesos to tell everybody that. He was paid by me, and the short way is not washed out." He looked at her without any particular expression. "So now you know what I spend my money on. Sit."

She had come up from the coast in three days and four vehicles. The last was a pickup with a hand-painted sign and a bed full of sacks, and the driver had the radio on, and twice an hour, in the middle of the brass, the voice read a bulletin about authorized corridors and prioritized provisioning and then went back to the music without a seam. Nobody in the truck remarked on it. A boy turned it up.

The road up through the altiplano: maguey in rows along the field edges like something planted by a person with a ruler, maize standing in its own dead colour, woodsmoke in the middle of the afternoon. A man at the roadside selling petrol out of Coca-Cola bottles arranged in a crate, and a cardboard sign whose price had been corrected three times.

Two soldiers sat at the turn-off in the back of a truck with their boots on the tailgate, not stopping anybody, just sitting. She asked the driver what the checkpoint was for. He said, nothing. They have nowhere to go. Then he said something else in the way of a man making conversation, and it took her a kilometre to decide she had agreed with him: the army is on leave. Nobody will tell it to come back.

The house was four rooms around a courtyard, with a stone trough in the middle fed by a channel, and the channel came from the aqueduct.

She had taken it for derelict from the road: six arches, black, a hundred metres of it, running into the hill at one end and stopping in the maize at the other. It was not derelict. Standing in the courtyard she could hear it working, a thin steady sound under everything, and there was a cover with a handle and a wet ring on the stone where the handle had been turned.

"1654," he said, seeing her look. "The hacienda pulled water from the spring. Then the hacienda died and the water kept coming, because water does not care. For thirty years the channel was full of roots and everybody said the spring was finished. The spring was not finished. The channel was clogged. Nobody looked, because everybody had agreed on the story."

"Did you clear it?"

"Forty metres. It took four years." He set the bucket by the trough. "I am very good at things that take a long time."

He was seventy-four and thin in a way that had gone past thin, and his hands had the flat wide knuckles of a man who had used them, and there was a cane against the doorstep he had not touched all morning. He was not warm. He was not cold. He had the quality of a man who has decided in advance how much of every hour he is prepared to spend.

"My name is Mara Voss."

"I know your name." He looked at the hoe and decided against it and sat down on the stool, and it took him a moment longer than it should have, and he did not let her see him notice. "You came to my house on the fifteenth day of the end of the world. Two have said yes. You have four more to ask, and you are doing the difficult ones early, which is either brave or desperate, and I do not think you are brave." He moved the stool a few centimetres, positioning it. "So who told you I am the difficult one?"

"Nobody told me anything. I read your file."

"There is no file."

"There is a file," said Mara. "It says presumed deceased, unconfirmed, and it has been saying it since 1998, and it is the reason nobody has come here in twenty-eight years."

The old man looked at her for a while. Somewhere behind the wall, chickens did whatever chickens do.

"Then you have been reading my tombstone," he said. "And you have come to ask a dead man to go to work. The answer is no."

She made her case anyway, because that was the job, and because he let her, which she understood later was its own kind of information.

She told him about the ships, holds, corridors and letters with no second page. She gave him the numbers — forty-one thousand appeals, four thousand hulls, six hundred men at a New Jersey gate — and the shape of the plan, the six names and the two who had said yes. She did not dress it up.

He sat with his hands folded and his eyes on the middle distance and did not interrupt once, and when she finished he waited a full ten seconds before he spoke, which she came to understand was his way of estimating whether a person was finished or merely out of words.

"Listen," he said. "I am going to tell you three things, and you will not like any of them, and then you will eat something, because you have not eaten since Tlaxco.

"First. I am seventy-four. I have sugar in my blood that I put there myself with a needle twice a day, and one kidney, and a foot that tells me the weather. If you take me out of this house I am a seventy-four-year-old man with a suitcase, and you will spend three assets keeping me alive, and the third of those assets will be a person you need somewhere else.

“Second. I am dead. I have been dead for twenty-eight years, and it is the most useful thing that has happened to me. Everything I own belongs to a burial society: not a person, a business or a resident of anywhere. There is no record anyone can act on.” He looked at her. “You want me alive again, with a name, a body and a reason to be somewhere. Why? A man is invisible not because nobody knows where he is, but because nobody has a reason to look. I have spent my life buying reasons. I will not sell them back.”

"And the third thing?"

The old man was quiet. “Third. You will ask somebody to die, and you do not know it yet. Your plan needs a machine looked at, and somebody must stand in front of it. When that time comes, everyone you have recruited will step back and tell you, sincerely, why it should be the other fellow. That is not cowardice. It is arithmetic.” He flexed his left hand. “I did that for forty years. I was good at it. There is no version where the man who got old is the one who gets paid.”

They ate in the kitchen: beans, a tortilla, and the beans were better than anything Mara had eaten in three weeks. He did not cook so much as assemble, and he asked her three questions about the route she had taken, one about the price of petrol, and none at all about the coalition.

Midway through the meal he took a small case out of a drawer, opened it, and did the thing with the needle without ceremony or apology, in the middle of the beans, while continuing to talk.

"Twice a day," he said. "People make a room for it. I do not make a room for it."

Afterwards she walked the courtyard with him while the light went: the maize, the bees, the woodpile, and the channel, which he inspected by tapping it in three places with his knuckles, listening, like a man checking a horse.

"You are wondering what a man like me does all day," he said.

"I wasn't."
