“You were. Everybody does. They expect a room with maps.” He looked at the channel. “I plant maize I do not need and repair water nobody else uses. In June the fireflies come to the forest above Tlaxco, and every man in the state drives up to see them. I do not.” He had built four hundred kilometres of tunnel; now the hole he had repaired was the only one he knew still worked. “I am not a sad old man in a house. I am finished, and I bought that. I am not selling it.”

The boy came at dusk.

He came through the gate without knocking, which meant he had been allowed in before. He was maybe nineteen, in a football shirt and a good jacket, and he stopped when he saw Mara and then decided she did not matter.

"Don Rinaldo."

"Say it."

"My cousin. Néstor." The boy's hands went in and out of his pockets. "He is in the north four days now, in a house in Sonora, and the man there says two more days and two more days, and now he wants more money, and my aunt has sold the goats. My uncle asked me to come and ask you."

"I cannot help your cousin."

"You don't know yet."

“I know the house, the man, the price. In four days they will put him in a truck and take him to a place sold as a door that will not be a door. The norteamericano system will file him as an event not authorized to exist, and he will stay filed.” El Búho’s voice stayed flat. “Your aunt will have lost the goats; the man will still be in the house, and in six weeks another four will go.”

"So what do I tell my uncle?"

"You tell him no."

The boy's face did something. It had not been long since he had discovered that shouting worked on some people. "You had men. You sent men."

"I sent men. I buried some of them, and I paid for it, and the mothers still come here sometimes.

Tell your uncle no." He picked up his cane — not to lean on, simply to hold — and took one step toward the boy, and his voice did not change at all. "And don't come back into my courtyard without knocking."

The boy left at a walk that was trying not to be a run, and looked back twice on the road.

Mara watched him go and understood that she had just seen two things at once, and that they were the same thing. The refusal had been a mercy. The mercy had been delivered with the exact instrument that had made men afraid of this person for forty years, and it had not been turned off, and it was not going to be.

"How many?" she said.

"Nine." He set the cane back against the step. "Nine that were mine. Everybody thinks a man like me counts the money. Nobody counts the boys." He went in and shut the door on the cold, and she heard the bolt, and then the scrape of it again as he thought better of it, and then he opened the door and said, "The coffee is on. It is bad. Come in or don't."

She stayed the night. The room was made up: a bed, a chair, a small window with lace on it, and nothing else. The sheets were clean and there was a fresh bar of soap on the dish, which meant somebody had known.

She lay awake a long time in a dead man's house in the mountains, then got up at two for water and passed an open door on the far side of the courtyard, and looked, because the doorway was lit.

The room was clean. A bed with a coverlet turned back, the way you turn back a bed for somebody. A shelf with a lamp on it. No dust anywhere, and nothing else in the room at all, and the smell of a room that gets cleaned every week and used by nobody and has been getting cleaned every week for a very long time.

She went back to her own bed and did not think about it, in the way that a person does not think about something for four hours.

In the morning the radio was on in the kitchen and it was reading a bulletin, the warm level unhurried voice, and El Búho was standing with his back to the room and a cup in his hand.

—provisioning at the designated holding point will be prioritized where conditions permit. Vessels currently at anchor should remain at anchor. There is nothing you need to do.

The voice stopped. The music came back. And the old man stood there with his cup, not drinking, and did not move for the length of a verse and a chorus, and Mara watched his shoulders and did not say anything.

Then he reached over and turned the radio off.

"That voice," he said.

"What about it?"

"Nothing." He put the cup down. "It is a good voice. It is a very kind voice. That is the thing about it."

He did not say anything else for eleven minutes. She timed it, because she had already begun, without deciding to, to treat everything in that house as evidence.

He walked her up to the road, and it took an hour. He used the cane for the hill, did not apologise for it, and did not slow down, which she understood by the end was the courtesy.

"Who else?"

"Kavanagh. And a man in New York."

"Kavanagh said yes." He stopped and looked at her, and something crossed his face that was either recognition or weather. "Did he tell you why?"

"He wants to see whether a file can beat a lie."

"That is not why."

"That's what he said."

“Then he lied to you, or to himself — with that man, it is the same act twice.” He started walking. “Kavanagh lost a son. He did not tell you; in his trade you are worth nothing once someone knows what you want. He will work for you until the job requires him to say the boy’s name. Find out the name. It will be worth more than his signature.”

"He asked for you," said Mara. "That was his first condition. Before money, before anything. He made me write your name into the page."

The old man walked on for a while without answering, and when he did his voice was exactly the same.

"Then he wants to look at me one more time while we can still both stand up," he said. "Men like us do that at the end. It is not affection. It is inventory."

"Who else should I ask?"

"Who is left?"

"There's a man in Panama."

The old man walked four or five paces. "Mesa," he said.

"Yes."

He said nothing for ten or twelve paces, and then, in exactly the same tone of voice, "Tell him I am dead."

"All right."

"No." He stopped and looked back down the hill at the roof of his own house, and his jaw moved once. "Tell him I am alive. Tell him I am very old, and very comfortable, and that I have not thought about him in years." He started walking. "An angry man is easy to steer. Never give a man an insult he can carry. Give him one he has to answer."

At the road there was a bus that went down to Apizaco and on down to the coast, and it was late, and they stood and looked at the empty road together in the last of the cold.

"You are going to ask me one more time," he said.

"I am."

"Then ask."

"Come with me."

“No.” He said it as he had in the courtyard. “There is one person who would be damaged if I became interesting again. I will not make her interesting. Ask me a second time and I will go inside; you will have lost me, and the hour, and you will remember both.”

Mara looked at him. He was seventy-four, the day was cold, he had a bad foot, and he had just told her twice that he was finished.

"All right," she said. "Then I need one thing that isn't you. I need a route, and I need to move eighty kilos of equipment south in eleven days without anybody being able to tell me not to."

"South through where?"

"Michoacán, down the coast, then across at Huixtla. The river's low this time of year."

The old man looked at her for a moment. Then he looked out at the road with the very faint, very private satisfaction of somebody who has just had a small bet confirmed, and he said:

"Not Huixtla. The water's up. They've had three weeks of rain in the Chiapas highlands and the gauge at Escuintla has been above the marker since Thursday."

And Mara Voss stood on the shoulder of a mountain road at eight in the morning on the sixteenth day of the end of the world, with the maguey in rows behind her and a dead man's house below her, and understood that she had just been told the water level of a river four hundred kilometres away by a man who had no intention of coming.

She got on the bus.

At the next rise she looked back, and he was still standing there, eight hundred metres below and behind her, small in the cold, not waving — watching the bus go down the hill the way a man watches a thing he intends to think about later.


## Chapter Six - Floating

*Days 17–18*

There were two hundred and six ships at anchor in the bay of Portobelo, and one of them was moving.

Mara counted them from the water taxi, because counting was the only thing she could do with her hands. Tankers, bulkers, three container ships in a diagonal line, a car carrier the size of a municipal building, a yacht with a helicopter pad and no helicopter, all of them head to wind, turning slowly and together like a field of sunflowers on a clock.

Out past the point, the sea went on being the sea. In the bay, two hundred and six ships had been told, politely, in the passive voice, to wait.

The coast was what was left of a Spanish fortification and a village that had grown in the ruins of it and never quite admitted to it. Black cannon on the sea wall, bolted through the trunnions so nobody could steal them. A customs house with a tree growing out of the roof. Pelicans on the pilings, looking like failure while being extremely good at their job.

The one moving ship was a launch of about eleven metres and the wrong shape for the bay: too much power in the stern, not enough superstructure, paint laid thick over a dozen older colours. It came out from behind the car carrier at eleven knots with no lights on and cut across the whole anchorage the way a man walks through a library — briskly, without knocking anything over, and without the slightest interest in the audience.

"Whose boat is that?" Mara asked.

The water-taxi driver did not turn around. He was seventy and had the shoulders of a man who had spent his life hauling on things.

"That one don't have a name on the paper," he said.

"Does it have a name on the side?"

"On the side it says Ana María." He spat into the water. "But that is not what it is."


*• • •*

The dock was concrete, bad, and had a sign at the end of it in a language that was not Spanish and turned out to be the private shorthand of one family. A canopy of faded canvas, three plastic chairs, a fan made of a car radiator and a belt, and a boy of about fifteen sitting on a bollard with a ledger.

The boy looked at Mara and wrote something in the ledger. Then he looked at her again and wrote something else.

"I have an appointment," she said.

"You have nothing," said the boy, without hostility. He turned the ledger around and showed her: two columns in pencil, one of them names and one of them times, and the next blank line had her time already written in it, in a hand that was not the boy's.

Mara looked at the handwriting for a while. It was beautiful. It was the handwriting of a man who had once been taught to write by nuns and had never once in his life been in a hurry.

"Don Rafael is on the terrace," said the boy. "When you go up, don't lean on the rail."

"Why not?"

"It isn't attached."


*• • •*

The terrace was the roof of a house built onto the fort, and it had the best view of the anchorage any man in Central America could reasonably have bought, and the heat came off the stone in a way that was almost companionship.

Rafael Esquivel Mesa was sitting in a wheelchair.

It was a wicker wheelchair, white-picked rattan, a high curved back and fringed canopy, made in Havana in 1958 for a hotel lobby. He wore an open white shirt, a medal on a gold chain and pressed trousers; he held a black cigar that was not lit and never would be. Sixty-nine, he looked eighty. His eyes belonged to a man underestimated for fifty years.

The chair was being pushed by a young woman in a nurse's tunic. Mara assumed she was a nurse, and was still assuming it when the young woman went past her with a tray and turned out to be the cook.

"Miss Voss." He did not offer his hand. He offered the view. "You have come at the correct hour, which almost nobody does. Sit down. Not that chair. That chair is broken and it is broken in an interesting way."

She sat.

"Two hundred and six," he said.

"Two hundred and six what?"

“Ships. You counted a hundred and ninety-one on the way in, stopped, started again, and got two hundred and six.” He waved the cigar at the bay. “The last came from Guayaquil with eleven thousand tonnes of bananas; by Thursday they’ll be very ripe, and nobody can do anything. This is the greatest anchorage in the world, mi amiga. I’m the only man with a front-row seat.”

"You know how many I counted."

"Of course."

"How?"

"Because you are the sort of woman who counts," said Mesa, "and because the boy wrote it down, and because when he wrote it down I had him bring it to me." He tapped the arm of the chair. "You are going to ask me now about my legs, and I am going to tell you that they are perfectly fine, and I am going to mean it, and you are going to disbelieve me because of the chair. That is the arrangement. Let us proceed."

"I'm not going to ask about your legs."

"No," said Mesa. "Good. You are the first one who hasn't." He looked at her with an entirely new and much less comfortable attention. "The Irishman said yes to you, and the woman who moves people will say yes to you, and Rinaldo said no."

"I haven't asked Lin Shu yet."

"You will. She will say yes, and she will hate all of us, and she will be right." He rolled the cigar between two fingers. "And Rinaldo said no, and you are here because you think that means something about you. It doesn't. It means something about him, and I could have told you six weeks ago and saved you the bus fare."

"Then tell me."

"Because a man who is comfortable cannot be sold anything," said Mesa, "and Rinaldo has been comfortable since the day he died. Sit. You have not sat properly. Sit the way a person sits."


*• • •*

He talked for an hour and ten minutes, and about forty minutes of it was the memoir.

The memoir was real. Mara had the file: nine hundred pages, dictated, typed by a woman in Colón over four years, bound in eight volumes by a man in Panama City who thought he was binding medical textbooks. It had a title — The Man Who Kept the Sea — a dedication to the author's mother, and an epigraph by the author.

Mesa kept a set in a glass case on the terrace, and had his nephew bring out volume four and made Mara look at a page.

"Read that."

"I've read it."

"Read it aloud. Please."

She read it aloud. It was a passage in which the protagonist, described in the third person throughout, took a launch out of Cartagena in 1986 with four hundred kilos of contraband and a woman who is at no point named, and outran two patrol boats in weather that the author describes for nine pages, and there was a sentence in the middle of it that went: He was not afraid, because he had made a private arrangement with the sea, and the sea, unlike every man he had ever known, kept its word.

Mesa listened to the whole thing with his eyes half closed, like a man at a concert.

"That is not an accurate account," he said, when she had finished.

"No?"

"No. There was only one patrol boat, and I was extremely frightened, and I was sick twice." He took the book back and put it in the case and closed the lid, and then he straightened the case by about a centimetre, which it did not need. "Nine hundred pages. Every one of them a lie of a specific kind. Do you want to know what the book is for?"

"Yes."

“It is not for me. I know what happened; I was there. It is for them. Someday a boy in a clean shirt will want to know who Rafael Mesa was. He can read an intelligence file — smuggler, fifty-one years, nine suspected bodies — or this book. He will think I am a legend, a maritime hero, and his file is only paper.” He smiled. “That is not vanity. That is preparation.”

"It's a forgery."

"It is a memoir," said Mesa, with tremendous dignity, "and I dictated every word of it myself, which is more than most men can say about their lives."


*• • •*

She gave him the message at four, in the flat gold light, because she had decided she was not going to be able to reach him any other way and because she wanted to watch.

She had rehearsed a version of it. What she said was:

"Don Rinaldo asked me to give you something."

The change was immediate and small. The cigar stopped moving.

"Did he."

"He said: tell him I am alive. Tell him I am very old, and very comfortable, and that I have not thought about him in years."

The terrace was quiet. Out on the water, the car carrier swung a degree or two round its anchor chain with a sound like a bell being rung in a distant building.

The nurse-cook came out with a tray and looked at Mesa's face and went straight back inside again, and that, Mara understood later, was the most important information she received all afternoon.

"He said that," said Mesa.

"Word for word. He made me repeat it."

"He made you repeat it." Mesa looked out at the bay. He was doing something with his jaw that was not quite chewing. "Twenty-nine years. Twenty-nine years, and the man sends me a message, and the message is that he is comfortable." He laughed, once, and it was not a good laugh; it was a sound with a floor under it. "You know what he is doing."

"I think so."

"Say it, then. If you are going to work with men like us you should say it out loud."

"He's making sure you have to answer it," said Mara. "That's what he told me. He said, never give a man an insult he can carry. Give him one he has to answer."

Mesa sat very still.

Then he put his hands on the arms of the wicker chair, and pushed, and stood up.

It took a long time and it was not elegant and nothing about it suggested that it would ever get easier, and at no point did the young man who had brought the book move to help him, which told Mara everything about the household. When Mesa was finally up he stood with his weight on the left leg and the heel of the right hand braced on the back of the chair, and he looked down at her from a height of about four inches higher than he had been looking at her before, and that four inches appeared to matter to him enormously.

“He would. In 1993 I had three hundred tonnes of coffee that did not exist in a Colón warehouse, and a buyer coming in the morning. Rinaldo kept me waiting six hours, then asked, ‘Who is the buyer?’” Mesa gripped the chair. “He let my brother die. Twenty years later he asked me that, and never thought the questions were related.”

"Tell me about your brother."

"No." He said it fast and flat and the theatre was gone out of him entirely. "Not today, and not to you, and not in that chair. Someday a room full of expensive lawyers will ask me about Tomás, and I will lie to them the way I have always lied to them, and it will be a beautiful lie, and I will enjoy telling it." He looked at her. "You get the version with the truth in it when you have earned it, which will be never, or when I am dying, which is a different department."

He sat back down. It took longer than getting up and he did not bother to hide it this time.

"Now," he said. "You may tell me what you want from me. I warn you that the answer is no, and that the no will be elaborate."


*• • •*

She gave it to him plainly: the plan, the six, the state of the machine, the names on the page. He interrupted twice, both times to correct her, and neither correction was wrong.

When she finished, Mesa said: "And in your plan, who goes into the difficult room?"

"There's more than one."

"There is always one that everybody is talking around. I have been doing this since before you were born, and I have been negotiating about the difficult room since I was younger than you are now, and I can hear it in the way you leave the sentence open." He lit the cigar at last — not the black one; the young man had brought another, smaller one — and smoked it with the profound and unearned satisfaction of a man who has decided a question is a compliment. "In the difficult room, who goes?"

"Probably Rinaldo."

Mesa began to laugh.

The laugh went on much longer than the joke deserved, and it was not entirely a laugh by the end of it, and the young man with the ledger looked at the floor.

“Rinaldo. In the difficult room.” Mesa wiped his eye. “He will look at the problem for six hours, decide which door to keep closed, and sleep afterwards. He is the best in the world at it. It is not a skill but a deficiency in the shape of a man; God help whoever is on the other side of the door. If you have to send somebody to die, send him. He will come back. He always does.”

"Does he always come back?"

"He came back from Tomás," said Mesa. "Somebody should have shot him for it, and nobody did, and he built himself a house in the mountains and a spring that runs clean, and he goes to sleep at nine o'clock like a farm animal, and he is comfortable." He said the word like it was a diagnosis. "Yes. He always comes back."


*• • •*

He took her out that night, because he wanted to and because he could, and because — she understood this only afterwards — it was the only way he knew how to answer a question.

Getting him aboard took forty minutes. The launch lay alongside the steps; a bosun’s chair, rigged by the young man and two others, lifted Mesa from the stone and set him on deck like a crate of eggs. All the way across he talked about the wind, a girl in Valparaíso, and the man on the hoist, whom he first corrected and then praised. He never looked down.

And out on the deck, when he was set down and the harness came off him, he looked up at the lights of two hundred and six frozen ships and said, "Mi amiga. This is the first time in my life I have had the sea to myself."

They went out past the breakwater with the lights off.

It was not a demonstration. He had a schedule, and the schedule existed whether she was aboard or not, and she had been allowed to watch a working thing.

Ninety minutes out, the launch slowed to a walking pace and a small boat came alongside out of the dark, no lights, and two men passed four plastic drums of diesel across the gap and took away a canvas bag, and nobody spoke, and it took six minutes. A kilometre further on, another. Then, at about half past one, a third rendezvous, and this one was a man who came aboard and sat with Mesa in the wheelhouse for eleven minutes and went away into the dark, and when he was gone Mesa was in an extremely good mood.

"That one," he said, "is a harbourmaster. Whose harbour I will not say. He has just told me that on Thursday the corridor at the western entrance will be closed for six hours for maintenance, and that the maintenance vessels have been authorized, and that he has been told to expect them, and that he has been told their names." He spread his hands. "Miss Voss. What do you do with that?"

"You write down the names."

“I write down the names.” Mesa was delighted. “Not the ships: the names they expect. If I know the authorized names, I know the names I can be.” He tapped the wheelhouse window. “Two hundred and six ships, each on a list. I have one boat with eleven names and change its transponder like a hat; nobody has noticed, because nobody looks for a boat authorized to be there.” He sat back. “That is my trade, worth more now than ever. I am not saying yes for Rinaldo. I am saying yes because this is the best thing that has happened to me in two weeks.”

That was when they saw the other boat.


*• • •*

It was a fibreglass panga, about nine metres, low in the water, dead engine, drifting out of the shipping lane into the edge of the bay. The launch's light caught it at two hundred metres and held it for four or five seconds.

There were people in it. Twenty, perhaps more, sitting and lying in the dark in a hull floating forty centimetres too low. One of them was standing and waving with both arms. One of them was holding up something that might have been a child or might have been a bag.

The young man at the wheel turned his head. "Don Rafael."

Mesa looked at it for perhaps six seconds, which was longer than he looked at most things.

"Reduce speed," he said. "Hold this course."

"Don Rafael—"

"Hold this course." He said it without heat, and the young man held the course, and the panga slid past on the port side at about the distance where you can see faces and not details.

They passed within a hundred metres of twenty-two people, and one of them was a woman holding a child, and Mesa did not look away from them. That was the part that stayed with Mara: he did not look away, and he did not turn his head, and he did not stop, and he watched them the whole way past.

Then he said, "Write it down. Time, position, hull, number. Twenty-two or twenty-three, say twenty-three."

The young man wrote it down.

"Why," said Mara. Her voice came out in a way she did not recognise.

"Because when the Guardia picks them up tomorrow morning and they say a launch passed them in the dark and did not stop, somebody will be asked to check the logs of every vessel in the bay." Mesa was settling back into his chair, and he spoke without any defensiveness at all, which was the horror of it; he was explaining his reasoning to a colleague. "And every log in that bay will be silent, because every one of those two hundred and six ships has a master with a career and a computer. And my book will have the entry in it, in pencil, with the time and the position and the number of souls. And it will be in my book, which is paper, which nobody can search, and which says we were there and we saw them and we did not stop."

"That's worse."

"That," said Mesa, "is true. And true is the only thing on earth that cannot be checked." He turned to her fully, and in the dark of the wheelhouse his face was not theatrical at all. "Mi amiga, I want you to understand something, because you are going to spend the next month writing down what these men are, and I would like the version of me in your notebook to be accurate. I have moved eleven thousand people across water in my life and I have never once been paid to rescue anybody. I have been paid to move cargo, and sometimes the cargo has had a name, and sometimes the cargo has had a mother, and I have never in fifty-one years lost a consignment. Not one. Do you know what that costs?"

"No."

"No," he agreed. "You don't. That is the entire distance between us, and it is why you will never be good at this, and it is also why you are the right person to be doing it." He nodded to the young man, who pushed the throttles forward again. "There were three hundred and eleven on this route last week. I have the numbers. When the fuel runs out at the holding points, it will be four hundred. They are not going to drown, mi amiga. They are going to arrive, and the machine will file them as an event that is not authorized to exist, and there they will stay, in a warehouse outside a town you have never heard of, with a number and no page." He looked back at the bay.

"That is the machine your government built. I would like you to know that I have read its notices, and I think it is a very poor sailor. It has never once been afraid."


*• • •*

She slept aboard and went ashore at seven. He came down to the dock in the chair to see her off, which she understood was not something he did.

"I have a condition," he said.

"Go on."

"Two. One small and one large." He had a cup of coffee he was not drinking. "The small one is that the Chinese woman is told, by you, before the first meeting, that the sea route that brought four hundred thousand people out of the last war in the last century ran under my flag. Do not soften it. She will despise me either way, and I would rather be despised for the true thing."

"And the large one?"

Mesa looked out at two hundred and six ships.

"When it is over," he said, "somebody will write it down. There will be a report. There is always a report, and it will be sealed, and in fifty years a boy in a clean shirt will open it and read the names of the people who did this." He held up one finger. "I want mine in it. Spelled the way it is spelled on my mother's papers. Rafael Esquivel Mesa. And I want the line to say I commanded the sea." He said this with no irony whatsoever, and with a small, awful dignity, and it was the first entirely honest thing he had said all day. "Everything else, they can put in an appendix. That line, in the report, for the boy in the clean shirt. That is my price. It is the only price I have ever charged that I cannot laugh at."

Mara said she would see what she could do.

"Do not see what you can do," said Mesa, and for a second the old voice came through, the one that had run eleven hundred men and a coastline. "You are a delegate of eleven governments. You will do it, and if they refuse you will write it in yourself and tell them afterwards, and if they object you will remind them what they asked for." He subsided. "Mi amiga. Write it properly."

The young man started the car. Mesa rapped twice on the roof of it.

"And one more thing," he said. "For Rinaldo."

"Tell me."

"Ask him one question. Ask him whether he ever told her."

"Told her what?"

Mesa smiled — the full theatrical smile, the one from the photograph in the file, forty years younger and just as unreadable — and waved the car on, and Mara went up the coast road with her head turned, watching him in the wicker chair on the concrete dock between two hundred and six ships that could not move and one boat that could, until the customs house with the tree in it took him.


## Chapter Seven - The Cowboy

*Day 19*

The strip was eleven hundred feet of grass on a ridge in the Ozarks, and it had been mown.

That was the first thing Mara noticed, and it should not have mattered. Someone had mown eleven hundred feet of grass on the nineteenth day of the end of the world. The fields were going to brush; the fence sagged; but the strip was mown, with whitewashed tyres and a hand-painted windsock.

The hangar was corrugated steel, sixty years old, with a sliding door that had a chain and no lock. Beside it, a trailer with a porch nailed onto it and a satellite dish pointed at a sky that was not answering.

Mara parked. Nothing happened for about ninety seconds.

Then a dog came round the corner of the hangar and stood in the middle of the gravel in her way, and regarded her with the flat and total indifference of an animal that had decided some years ago that the world could come to him.

He was a big yellow dog of no particular breed, and old, and one eye had gone milky at the top, and when he eventually turned his head toward the house he did it in a way that suggested he could hear about half of what was going on and had no intention of being told the rest.

"Senator," said a voice from inside the hangar. "Who is it?"

The dog did not move.

"He don't answer to that," said the voice. "He answers to Rusty, because that's what the woman who left him called him, but I named him Senator, so we're at an impasse."

Dale Garrity came out of the hangar sideways, wheeling a drum of aviation fuel in front of him and holding a cigarette in the same hand, and he was seventy-one years old and had the shoulders of a man who had spent his life lifting things into aeroplanes, and he walked with a hitch on the left side that he had clearly decided was nobody's business.

He looked at Mara, then at her car, then at the sky.

"You're the government," he said.

"Yes."

"Which one?"

"All of them," said Mara.

Dale Garrity looked at her for another second, and then he did something she was not prepared for, which was to smile with his whole face: a wide, unguarded, delighted, completely uncomplicated smile, a child's smile on a man who had been waiting a very long time for somebody to knock.

"Well, hell," he said. "Come look at her."


*• • •*

The aeroplane was a Cessna 185 on floats and wheels, older than most of the men on Mara's page, and the most beautiful machine she had ever seen in her life.

She had been looked after the way some men look after a car and no men look after an aeroplane: not restored — maintained. Every panel had been off her at some point and put back. In the cockpit the yoke grip was worn shiny on the left side only, and on the panel, in a corner where the manufacturer had left a square of bare metal, somebody had punched nine small holes in a pattern, by hand, at intervals, over decades, one for each time he had done something he was not going to talk about.

"She's had two engines," Garrity said. "One in '94, one in '09. She's had four sets of floats and she's lost one. She's flipped once, on a sandbar in the Yazoo, in '96, in weather I had no business in, and I have got the crack in the left wing root to this day, and I have never fixed it because I want to remember that I was stupid once and got to keep the aeroplane."

"The registry says your tail number belongs to a weather balloon."

"That's right."

"And the other one belongs to an agricultural permit that was withdrawn in 2011."

"That's right too."

"So what does that make this?"

"Says it right there on the panel." He tapped the instrument panel with one knuckle, on the little plate. "It says I'm a balloon."

Mara looked at the plate. It was riveted on and hand-stamped, and it read: AEROSTAT — UNMANNED — SCHEDULED LIFT.

"I've been a balloon for fourteen years," said Garrity, with enormous satisfaction. "Best thing that ever happened to me. Thursday before last the whole country shuts down, and I'm in the air within the hour, and a machine that can read a truck's tyre pressure from orbit looks at nine thousand feet of Missouri sky and sees an aerostat — a scheduled lift — and files me. No flight plan, no clearance, no nothing, because I have spent fourteen years being the least interesting thing in the sky." He put his heel on the cigarette. "Nobody intercepts a balloon. Intercepting a balloon is a waste of a good afternoon. That's not me being clever. That's every other man in my trade being too proud to be boring."


*• • •*

He would not sit down to talk. That was a thing about him Mara understood within an hour, and would write up later, in the kind of handwriting that means somebody is trying to be fair.

Dale Garrity did not sit down all of the first day. He cleaned a carburettor while she asked her questions and sorted a wall of logbooks by decade while she read the annex aloud, and every time she got to a part that mattered he found a reason to be up a ladder.

He took her through the hangar instead, which was a museum of a specific and self-aware kind. A young man in a hat, standing on the float of an aeroplane, squinting. A panel of instruments from an aircraft that no longer existed. A letter of commendation from a state senator for the rescue of three people off a flooded road in 1983 — and next to it, unframed, taped to the wall, the same man in the same year outside a federal courthouse, laughing for the camera, hands in his pockets, because the jury had hung.

"Your file says eighteen years," said Mara.

"Twenty-six."

"Your file says eighteen."

"My file is about arrests," said Garrity. "I got arrested twice. First time in '84, second time in '07, and between the two of them there's twenty-six years and a lot of very good flying, and the dear lady who wrote up my file didn't have any of it, because there wasn't anything to have." He wiped his hands on a rag and looked at the aeroplane with real fondness. "You know what's in a logbook, Miss Voss?"

"Hours."

"Hours, fuel, weather, route, and my signature," said Garrity. "Forty years, and there is not one line in any of them that says what I was carrying. Forty thousand hours, all of it honest, and there is no crime on earth in a man not writing down something nobody ever asked him about." He tossed the rag onto the bench. "Most fellows in my trade lie about where they went. I never did. A lie is a loose thing. It rattles. Mine's an omission, and an omission is bolted down."

It was the only precise thing he said all day without a joke attached to it, and Mara wrote it down.


*• • •*

The question of what he had been doing for nineteen days took a while to get at, and the answer came out sideways, the way everything with him did.

She asked him directly at about four, standing in the hangar doorway while he adjusted something on the bench.

"So what have you been doing since Day 0?"

"Flying."

"Where?"

"Wherever." He kept working. "Wednesday I ran four cases of insulin from a man in Springfield to a clinic in a town you've never heard of, because the clinic's supply was in a truck that got routed to a staging facility eleven hundred miles away, and the truck is still there, and the insulin is now a compliance matter." He said compliance matter in the flat voice of a man doing an impression of somebody he hated. "Friday I took a woman and two kids across to her sister, because the kid had a heart thing and the hospitals are doing what they're doing. Sunday I did nothing. Monday I flew up the river and looked at things for four hours, because there was nothing to carry and I wanted to be up."

"That's not legal."

"Neither's the aeroplane."

"Does it work? What you've been doing."

"It works because it's not legal," said Garrity, and he turned around for the first time. "I want you to understand the actual difference between me and every other pilot in America. Everybody else is stuck on the ground because their aeroplane is a thing that exists. It's on a register, it's got an owner, it's got an airworthiness certificate that expired, it's got a medical, it's got a forecast and a corridor and a slot. Their aeroplane is a record, and the record is what's been frozen. Mine is a record of a balloon that nobody's ever going to look at because looking at it is boring." He shrugged. "So I go. And nobody notices, because noticing me would mean somebody had to do paperwork about a weather observation."

Mara said nothing for a moment.

"That's the most useful sentence anybody has said to me in three weeks," she said.

"It is," Garrity agreed. "And I've been sitting here for nineteen days waiting for one of you people to come out and say it to me, and nobody came, and I have to tell you I was getting a little hot about it."


*• • •*

He took her up at five, and she said no twice, and then said yes, because he had asked in such a way that saying no had started to feel like a comment on something.

It was a good hour. She was fair enough to give him that. The ridge fell away and the Ozarks went out flat and blue in every direction, the lakes in the folds of it the colour of a knife, the little towns putting their lights on one by one, and not one aircraft anywhere in the sky. Dale Garrity flew with two fingers and a wrist, and the aeroplane seemed to do the thing before he got there.

"You see why I do it," he said, on the intercom.

"I see why you do it."

"I've got eleven hundred hours in this valley alone." He banked her gently over a bend in the river. "There's a fella in '01 wanted four hundred kilos out of a strip down there that isn't a strip, it's a hill, and I put it in on the fourth of July with the ground coming up like a wall. I have thought about that landing twice a week for twenty-four years." He laughed, and the laugh was real. "You know how many men have ever asked me about it?"

"How many?"

"Not one. I'll tell you what they ask about." He banked her back. "They ask about the money."

They came back to the ridge with the sun gone and the wind up from the southwest, the windsock straight out and flapping, the strip a grey line in the last of the light, the tyres at the near end visible and the trees at the far end not, particularly.

And on the approach, at about two hundred feet, Dale Garrity's right leg stopped working.

It did not stop because he was old, and it did not stop because of anything anybody could point to. It stopped the way a leg stops when a back has been holding aeroplane fuel and four hundred hours of stick for a man of seventy-one who refuses to sit down, and it stopped all at once, and Mara watched the rudder pedal under his right boot go soft and stay soft.

The aeroplane yawed. Not much. A few degrees, then a few more.

He corrected with the yoke, and his face did not change, and his voice on the intercom stayed exactly where it had been.

"Little bump there," he said.

The strip came up wall-eyed and low. They were left of the centreline, and a fence post went past the right window that had no business going past the right window. He worked the yoke and the throttles and the aeroplane did what he told it. They touched down on the left main first and it was not smooth, and the tail came down hard enough that Mara's teeth met, and they rolled, and the far end of the strip arrived and went past, and the aeroplane stopped about eleven feet from where the trees started.

The engine came back to idle. The prop did a slow turn and stopped.

For four or five seconds, neither of them said anything.

"That's the wind," said Garrity. "Coming up the ridge like that, it'll push you left every time."

"Yes," said Mara.

He unbuckled and got out, carefully, right leg first, which she had not seen him do before. On the wing strut he stood with his hands on his hips and looked back down the strip at the tyre marks, and she watched him take the four or five seconds it takes to put your body back in the box where you keep it.

"Miss Voss," he said.

"Yes."

"I'd take it kindly if you didn't write that down."

Mara got out and stood on the step and looked at the eleven feet of grass between the nose wheel and the first tree.

"I have to write it down," she said. "It's the only honest thing I've got."

Garrity was quiet. Then he nodded, once, slowly, and looked out at the dark coming up over the ridge. "Well. That's a shame," he said, with no self-pity in it at all, which was somehow much worse.

And then, because he was who he was, he said: "She'll take eight hundred feet and a crosswind and a load, and I've put her on worse." And the smile came back, and the four seconds went into the box, and he walked her up to the house.


*• • •*

The trailer was a mess and the coffee was good.

The dog lay by the woodstove and did not move for anything. On the wall, taped up, was the airspace advisory, printed off before the printer stopped, and Mara read it while the coffee brewed.

NOTICE TO ALL AIRSPACE USERS

Scheduled and unscheduled movement within designated corridors is suspended pending harmonization. Exemptions will be provided for medical, humanitarian and maintenance categories where such movement can be authenticated in advance. Meteorological assets are excepted from this notice.

Somebody had drawn a circle around Meteorological assets are excepted in ballpoint pen, and written beside it, in a hand that was doing its best to be steady, That's me.

"They don't even know they did it," said Garrity, from the stove. "They wrote me a permission slip by accident, because I've been nothing for fourteen years. It's the funniest thing I ever saw."

"Is it."

"No," he said. "It scared the living hell out of me, if you want the truth. I sat here on the eighth day with that bit of paper in my hand and I thought: they could take this away. They could take away the balloon, and I have got nothing else, Miss Voss. There is no other thing that I am." He came over and put the mug down in front of her, and his hand was not quite steady, and he looked at her with the whole face for the second time that day. "So whatever it is you came to ask. If it's flying, I'm in. You didn't need the sales pitch."

"You haven't asked what it is."

"Don't care."

"You haven't asked what it pays, or what it costs, or who's going, or where."

"Nope."

"Why?"

Garrity sat down — actually sat down, for the first time in nine hours — and looked at her across the table like a man finally getting around to the thing he had been avoiding all day.

"Because I'm seventy-one," he said. "I've got a back that goes out if I look at a bucket, a knee that needs doing and won't get done, an airman's certificate that lapsed the year they shut the tower at Jefferson City, and a heart that's fine, thank you, because I run and I don't drink and I've never eaten anything green in my life." He took a pull of coffee. "And twelve days ago a woman in Springfield asked me how I'd got in, and I said I flew, and she looked at me like I'd told her I walked on water. That was the best feeling I have had in eleven years, and I have been sitting here ever since trying to work out how to have it again." He put the cup down. "So no. I don't need the pitch. You've got the only thing I want, which is for somebody to need me to fly."

Mara looked at him for a while. It was the single most transparent interview she had conducted, and it was the one that worried her most.

Every other man on her page had told her what he was protecting. Villalobos had refused her three times. Mesa had wanted his name in a report and four hundred thousand displaced people told about. Kavanagh had wanted a passport for a girl and a name said out loud. Castellano had wanted one letter answered.

This one had nothing. This one wanted to be seen using his hands.

That was wonderful, and it was the most dangerous thing she had found in nineteen days, because a man who is paid in relevance will pay for relevance, and the people who pay for it are never the ones who get hurt.

"One thing," she said.

"Shoot."

"Your answer can't change in the middle of it. If we're three weeks in and it turns out this is going to kill somebody, you don't get to change your mind because it stopped being fun."

Dale Garrity looked at her for a second, and for the first time all day he did not smile.

"They all say that, first time out," he said. He reached down and rubbed the dog's head, and the dog did not wake. "I've been in four aeroplanes when they were going to crash. Four. And I'll tell you what I did in every one, which is I flew the aeroplane, because there was nothing else. So I won't promise you I won't change my mind. I'll promise you something better." He looked up. "I won't tell you you're going to be fine. That's what the others do. That's the whole thing they do." He picked his cup back up. "Now. Let's talk about your dog."

"What about my dog?"

"About who's going to keep him," said Garrity. "Because I'm not putting him in a kennel, on account of there not being any kennels, and I'm not asking my daughter, on account of her views on me, so if I come away with you, that is an item, and I want it on your list."

Mara wrote it on her list. And that night, in a motel outside West Plains that had a generator and no television, she wrote the day up in the annex honestly, including the eleven feet of grass and the words right leg, approximately forty seconds, no correction available, and sat looking at it for a long time before she closed the book.

Because the second-to-last item in it now read: dog — Senator, male, yellow, 11, one eye. Must be placed before departure. No kennels.

And because the item above it read: Garrity, D. R. — in. Will fly anything. Will not admit to a limit.

In the morning she flew out of Springfield on a coalition courier with a manifest that had taken six hours to authenticate, and somewhere behind her and to the west, a seventy-one-year-old man with a bad back was mowing eleven hundred feet of grass for the last time.


## Chapter Eight - The Railroad

*Days 20–21*

The airport was the first place in three weeks where Mara had felt the sheer size of what had happened, because the airport was working.

Suvarnabhumi on Day Twenty was clean, lit and empty. The signs still spoke in four languages, the escalators ran, and nine people queued at the currency desk for a service that could no longer be performed, asked to remain in the designated area.

Mara had a corridor authorization. It had taken nine hours to obtain and it worked.

That was the part she could not explain at home: the machine was not broken or hostile. It had simply become the only institution left that could still say yes.

Outside, Bangkok was still Bangkok: towers, flyovers, mango sellers, hot asphalt, jasmine and drains. What had gone was the traffic. Without it, the city was like a man without a voice — the same face, everything wrong. Three trains stood on the stopped overhead rail, their lights on; people had taped signs to the windows and photographed them, with nowhere to send the photographs.

Mara got out of the taxi in a district she could not have named and walked eleven minutes in the heat to a shophouse with a laundry in it.

The sign said SABAI WASH. A washing machine, a folding table, a plastic chair, and a woman of about forty with a phone, and behind her, through a beaded curtain hung for customers, a corridor that went further back than the building had any business going.

"Closed," said the woman.

"I'm expected."

"No."

"I have an appointment."

"No." The woman did not look up from the phone, which had no network. "Everybody has an appointment. There is no laundry."

Mara stood under the awning and did not leave, and the woman let her stand there, and at about the nine-minute mark a much older woman came through the beads with a plastic basket of somebody else's shirts and looked at Mara for two seconds and said, "Take her to the back. And her bag."


*• • •*

The back was three rooms and a yard, and in the yard a covered well, a mango tree, forty kilos of rice, and a Toyota pickup that had been taken apart and put back together by somebody who did not care how it looked.

Lin Shu was sixty-three, small, and looked like a woman who had run a shop for forty years. Grey hair cut short as a boy's, reading glasses on a cord, sandals repaired twice. She was weighing rice into plastic bags on a dial scale, with the unhurried accuracy of a person doing arithmetic in her head, and did not stop while Mara talked.

She let Mara talk for nine minutes without one interruption. That was the first thing.

And then she asked four questions, one at a time, with a small pause between each, and none of them were about the plan.

"Who paid for your flight?"

"Coalition funds."

"Who paid the man who drove you from the airport?"

"Nobody. It was a metered taxi."

"How much?"

Mara told her.

"You paid eleven. It is nine. Every driver has put the tourist price on this week. You did not ask, and he did not cheat you badly, and you did not notice either." Lin Shu tied off a bag of rice. "The man in Dublin told you I would not like you. He was right, and it is not about you. I do not like people who arrive by the front door."

"And the fourth question?"

Lin Shu stopped, and looked at her properly for the first time, and the glasses came off and went onto the cord.

"What did they tell you to say to me before you asked me for anything?"


*• • •*

So Mara said it, standing in a yard in the heat, because she had promised an old man in Panama that she would say it unsoftened, and because she had worked out somewhere over the Bay of Bengal that the only thing she actually had to spend in this house was the truth.

"Rafael Mesa told me to tell you, before the first meeting, that the sea route that brought four hundred thousand people out of the last war in the last century ran under his flag. He said not to soften it. He said you would despise him either way, and he would rather be despised for the true thing."

Lin Shu put the last bag of rice on the table.

For about four seconds nothing happened on her face at all, which Mara would learn was how the woman did everything.

"I know," she said.

"You know."

"Everybody in this work knows. It is not a secret, it is a ledger entry. He ran the boats and he was paid and the boats came, and four hundred thousand people are alive, and eleven of them are my family." She sat down on a stool by the table and began, without any ceremony, to fold the top of a rice bag over. "He is a grown man playing at confession. He said it to you so it would arrive in the room before he did — so that when I meet him I will already be angry about the right thing and I will not go looking for the wrong one."

Mara stood in the yard holding nothing.

"What's the wrong one?"

"There is always another thing." Lin Shu squared the bag, twice. "Ask him. Ask him what he did not stop for."

Mara said nothing.

She said nothing for a long moment, in a yard in Bangkok, with a fan knocking somewhere inside the house, and what she was not saying was that she had been on the boat.

She had stood four metres from Mesa in the dark wheelhouse and watched twenty-two people pass in a hull sitting forty centimetres too low, one holding something that might have been a child. He had them logged in pencil, in a book nobody could search; he watched without turning his head, then explained himself as if explaining a trade.

She did not say it. She stood there with it and did not say it, and she understood that she had just failed a test that Lin Shu did not know she was administering.

"All right," said Lin Shu, to the rice. "You told me. That is more than the last three did. Come inside and sit down; you have been walking all day and you will get sick and then I will have to look after you."


*• • •*

She would not discuss the coalition in the house. That was a rule, and she did not explain it, and the woman from the front room brought tea and then went out to sit in the shop and watch the empty street.

Instead they walked.

They walked because Lin Shu had four errands and saw no reason to do them later. The four errands took three hours and went through a market, a hospital, a temple and a bus station, and by the end of it Mara understood she had been shown the entire machine.

In the market, a woman sold noodles from a corner with no premises, no lease and no stall: two pots, a stool, a child doing homework on an upturned crate. Lin Shu talked to her for four minutes in a language Mara did not recognise and came away with a folded paper.

"Her husband is in a holding area near the airport," she said, walking. "There are four thousand people in that holding area and eleven hundred of them are in a building with a roof, and the rest are under tarpaulins, and the tarpaulins were supplied, which is a thing to understand. They were supplied. Somebody planned for the tarpaulins. Somebody is doing this well."

"How do you know where her husband is?"

"Because I asked her what he was wearing and what he had in his pockets and what time the bus left, and it will be in a ledger somewhere written by a man who is paid to write ledgers, and a ledger is a thing I can find." Lin Shu crossed the road. "Nobody is hiding these people. That is the mistake everybody makes. Everyone thinks the people are hidden. The people are filed. There is a difference, and the difference is everything people are not looking at."

At the hospital she sat for twenty minutes in a ward with a man of about eighty who was mending a chair. He was not sick and not a patient. He had been in a bed in a corridor for four years because no country would admit he was theirs, and a nurse had given him the chair-mending job, and Lin Shu had brought him a bag of screws.

Nobody asked him his name. He had a card. The card had been issued by a mission that had closed in 2014 and it had a number on it and a photograph of a much younger man.

"Six countries," said Lin Shu, on the way out. "He has been in the categories of six countries. Every one of them says he is somebody else's."

At the temple, she gave money to a boy at a gate, and did not go in. At the bus station, she stood for a while and looked at the board, which still had times on it, and then she turned around and went home.

That was the whole thing. Nobody was smuggling anything, forging anything, or hiding.

"You want to know what I do," said Lin Shu, on the way back, in the middle of a sentence about a bus timetable. "Everybody who comes to me wants to know what I do, because they think it is a trick and they think they can buy the trick. So I will tell you and you will be disappointed, and then you will think about it for a month.

"It is very hard to hide a person. Almost impossible. You people from governments think it is all false papers and tunnels; it is not. A person is seen every day. A landlord sees them, a driver sees them, a nurse sees them, a policeman sees them and decides not to look. So you cannot hide a person. What you can do is make the person they see boring."

She stopped at a corner and watched traffic that was not there.

"A woman going into a hospital is boring. A woman going into a hospital who is also a stranger with no papers is a problem, and a problem gets written down, and a thing written down is a thing that is findable. So I spend my life making people into women who are going into hospitals. That is forty years."

"People have to be willing to see them that way."

"Yes. That is the work. Not the paper. The willingness." Lin Shu started walking again. "Underneath it, it is the same transaction as your friend in New York. He makes a thing boring to a clerk. I make a person boring to a neighbour. He does it with a file. I do it with twenty minutes of conversation and the price of a bowl of noodles."

"And when the neighbour is not willing?"

"When the neighbour is not willing," said Lin Shu, without slowing down, "I move the person before the neighbour has finished deciding. That is the whole art. Knowing how long a person has."


*• • •*

She refused that night, sitting in the back room with the fan on, in about ninety seconds, and it was the cleanest refusal Mara had received.

"No."

"May I tell you why we need you?"

"You may tell me. I will not change my answer, and I would like to know how you argue, because that will tell me what you are."

Mara argued well; she had twenty days of practice and believed much of what she said. The machine could act only on what it could resolve; it trusted categories, and the people it had filed as nothing could move unseen. She said they needed Lin Shu not for her network but as proof that people could still move without permission. She told her about the six names, the annex, the hill and the governments that had signed a page that did not exist.

Lin Shu listened to all of it and then said: "You are asking me to make the last railroad into a weapon."

"Yes."

"Whose?"

"Not ours. There isn't an ours. There's only the thing that's happening."

"That is what everyone says." She poured the tea. "Four thousand people at the airport. Four hundred thousand at sea. My people are not in the second category. My people were never in any category. They are the ones who were already gone. And you have come to tell me that if I help you take the machine apart, the world will go back to the way it was."

"No. I don't think it will."

Lin Shu looked up.

"Good," she said. "Then you are not a fool, and I am sorry for you."

She said no again, with reasons, and the reasons were better than Mara's. The coalition had built the machine and would build the next one. Her network was three hundred people in five countries and existed for one purpose; used for anything else it would not exist afterwards, and the people who needed it would be the ones who paid. And she had a list — the whole of her life's work, everyone she had ever moved — and she had never given it to anybody, and she was not going to begin on the twentieth day of the end of the world.

"It is not a no to you," she said. "It is a no to the thing you are carrying. Come back if you are carrying something else."


*• • •*

The call came at ten past eleven, and Mara was still in the house, because Lin Shu had given her a mat and a blanket and told her the roads at night were a thing she did not want to explain to Brussels.

The woman from the front room came through the beads at a run, which was the first time in the whole day that anybody in that building had hurried.

What had happened took forty minutes to assemble, and Mara got it in fragments, in three languages, from people on phones that did not work to people standing in a temple a hundred kilometres away. Thirty-one people had been sheltering in a temple compound in a province on the northern border, for between four months and nine years. Most had no country. Some had a card from a mission that had closed, or from an agency that still existed but did not answer, or nothing at all. Lin Shu had been moving them four at a time all year and had moved nine.

At some point in the last six hours, three buses had come to the temple gate.

Nobody had ordered them. When the monk asked who had sent the buses, the man showed him a page with a reference number, a destination and a phrase the monk could not remember: consolidation of unresolved persons for provisional accommodation. The man had been polite. He had been paid to be polite. He said there was nothing anyone needed to do.

And the thirty-one were gone, and the record, so far as anybody could tell, was that nothing had happened at all.

Mara sat with a phone that had no signal and did the only thing she could do, which was write down every word the monk said, and then ask Lin Shu for a pen that worked.

"Can you find them?" said Lin Shu.

"Not tonight."

"Can you find them tomorrow?"

"I can try. The coalition can query. We can put an inquiry into the system that issued the page, and if the page has a reference, the reference has a movement record, and the movement record has to say where the bus went, if the bus had an authorization—" She stopped, because she could hear herself doing it, doing the thing she had done for eleven years: describing the shape of a system as though the shape were a promise. "I don't know."

Lin Shu looked at her for a long moment.

"Say that again."

"I don't know."

"Yes." Lin Shu stood up. "The last three people who came to this house to ask me for something told me they knew. One of them was from your coalition. He sat in that chair and told me that his office would find my people, and he said I know in that voice, the voice with no furniture in it, and I put him out of my house." She picked up the kettle. "You do not know. Good. We will start from there."


*• • •*

She gave Mara her terms at two in the morning, and they were not conditions so much as a contract between a woman and a country, and Mara wrote all four in the annex in ink.

"First. I come to your meeting and I say what I think of every man in that room, in front of him, and no one is permitted to be surprised. Tell them. Write it to them. They should arrive knowing that the woman from Bangkok is going to look at them and tell the truth about them in their own language."

"That's not—"

"Second. I am not an asset. I am not in your column of things. When I speak, it is not a report. It is what I think, and you will not use it to manage anybody, and if I find out that you have, I will leave and I will take three hundred people with me who will never answer a phone again."

"All right."

"Third. You will not tell them I have forgiven anything." She poured water. "I have not. I will work beside those men as long as there are people to move. That is not cooperation, it is arithmetic, and if any of them tries to make it a nice thing, I will remind him in public of what he is. Including yours. Especially yours."

Mara wrote it down.

"And fourth?"

Lin Shu set the kettle down.

"You said you can query the record. The movement record of the buses." She said it without emphasis, the way she said everything. "When this is finished — when the machine is gone, or dead, or whatever your people are going to do to it — there will be a moment when the records still exist and can be opened by somebody. Before that moment closes. I want a list."

"A list of what?"

"Of everybody in the holding areas." She looked at the wall, which had a calendar from a bank that no longer existed. "Every name, every number, every person who was consolidated for provisional accommodation and stayed under a tarpaulin for two weeks and was then moved somewhere nobody wrote down. I do not want the machine stopped. I want the list. That is my price. It is the only one I have."

Mara said, "That could be three hundred thousand people."

"Three hundred and forty-one thousand, in the holding areas, by my count, and I am low, and I am often low." Lin Shu sat down again. "I have a list of my own, from forty years. Three thousand four hundred and eleven names of people I moved successfully. And another list, of sixty, who are the ones I did not move in time, and I know every one of them, and I can tell you all sixty names tonight."

She did not offer to.

"Sixty is what I live with," she said. "I do not need sixty-one."


*• • •*

In the morning she reopened the laundry. That was her answer to everything, and to the twentieth day as well: she put the sign out, filled the machine, and sat with the woman from the front room going through somebody else's shirts. When Mara left at nine, Lin Shu did not get up, did not shake her hand, and did not wish her luck.

"Tell them I am coming," she said, to the shirts. "Tell them I am not coming to forgive them. Tell them I am coming because the alternative is that I stay here and do nothing while people are being taken off the board, and I have watched that happen before, and I am too old, and I know myself too well."

Mara got into the car.

She had four hours at the airport before her corridor slot, and used them writing up the chapter in the annex the way Brandt had asked on the eleventh day: what they are, what we are asking them to do, what we owe them.

She wrote up Lin Shu in eleven lines. She wrote up the rice, the chairs in the corridor, the sixty names, the four conditions, the temple, the page with the reference number on it.

And she did not write the boat.

That was the other thing she did at the airport, in a chair by a gate with no aeroplanes on the other side of it: she sat with the fact that she had been on the boat, and had stood in a yard in Bangkok and not said it, and that Lin Shu had then made her a contract and given her a mat and a blanket.

Mara was, by then, twenty days into a job she had invented on her own authority in a converted filing hall under a hill. She had four names on a page and a fifth on the way, and she had already begun keeping the second list, the smaller one every person in this trade keeps, the one with your own name on it.

She wrote one line at the bottom of the page, and shut the book.

Not told. Will have to be told. Not yet.


## Chapter Nine - Roll Call

*Day 23*

The meeting was scheduled for ten o'clock, and by twenty to ten Mara Voss had lost control of her own building.

It had happened in stages. At eight, a van arrived at the north gate with a seventy-one-year-old American in the front and a yellow dog in the back. The American got out, looked at the hill, said, "Well, that's not a runway," and spent twenty minutes walking the service road with his hands in his pockets, looking at the sky, before anybody could stop him.

At half past eight the systems director found a man in a cardigan sitting in the microfilm vault with a suitcase of carbon copies, assumed — reasonably, on the evidence — that he was a visiting archivist, spent nine minutes explaining the finding aid system to him, and came away saying he was the politest person she had ever met and she could not remember telling him anything sensitive and was not entirely sure she hadn't.

At nine, a van came up from the coast with a wheelchair in it and a man in it who would not be lifted out until a specific person was named and the vehicle was reversed eleven feet so that the chair could be unloaded on the correct side, with the sun not in his face.

At ten past nine a woman arrived in a coalition car with two plastic bags of food, asked the guard where the kitchen was, went to the kitchen, and stayed there.

At twenty past nine an Irishman walked the last two kilometres in from the bus stop with an umbrella, signed the visitors' book — the visitors' book — in the copperplate of a man taught by nuns, and then, because nobody had asked him to, went into the filing hall and started reading the finding aids.

At a quarter to ten Brandt came out of her office, stood at the top of the stairwell and looked down at her command post, which now contained four internationally wanted criminals, one yellow dog, a wheelchair, two hundred kilos of paperwork and a woman cooking. She said, to Mara, in a voice of great and controlled calm:

"You have twenty minutes to tell me what you have done to this building."


*• • •*

The rules were negotiated in eleven minutes and written by Brandt on the back of a printout, because the machines were off. No names spoken above a whisper in the corridors. No photographs at any point, by anyone, of anyone, including the building. No member of staff to be told who the visitors were; the visitors were consultants. No record of the meeting. No exceptions, no minutes, nothing that could be scanned.

"Consultants," said Kavanagh, when it was read to him. "My dear woman, that is a worse insult than anything in my file."

"Sign here."

Kavanagh looked at the sheet, and then at Brandt, and signed it — slowly, with a fountain pen from inside his jacket, in a hand so beautiful and so unlike his own that it took Mara four seconds to understand what she had just watched.

"That is not your signature," said Brandt.

"No," said Kavanagh. "And in eleven days it will be a consultant's signature, on a page, in a book, in a building the machine has never been able to read, and I will have been here and it will not know." He put the pen away. "You people keep asking us to be paper. That is the whole of my advice and I have given it to you in the first minute and none of you will take it."


*• • •*

They met in the microfilm vault, because it was the biggest room with the fewest windows, and because El Búho had specified it without ever having been inside the building.

That was a thing Mara had elected not to think about. He had sent one instruction four days earlier, through a pickup driver and a laundry in Belgium: the room with the film, not the room with the screens.

Three hundred metres of shelving. Forty kilometres of microfilm. Ten metres under a hill. Four strip lights worked; the rest did not, and nobody wanted to find out why.

Garrity refused to sit in a chair facing a camera. There was no camera. He moved the chair anyway.

Roux came in with a laptop, and Mesa told her from the doorway, without looking at her, that if she brought that into the room he would leave — and mean it. Mara said, "Leave it outside." Roux, who had run a coalition systems directorate for six years, left it outside and stood in the doorway afterwards with the expression of a person who has just been told something true about herself.

The dog lay down on the cold concrete in the middle of the floor and went to sleep with the entitlement of an animal that had decided this was a reasonable place to be.

Lin Shu arrived with a tray, put it on the table, said nothing to anybody, and sat at the end with her back to the shelving.

Sal Castellano was helped in by a coalition orderly he had recruited in ninety seconds on the way down and who was already, visibly, his. He sat at the corner nearest the door, opened his suitcase, took nothing out of it, and closed it.

Mesa arrived last of those who had arrived, in the chair, pushed by the young man from Panama who had not been on any list and would not be on any list, and whom Mara could not now un-authorize.

And Kavanagh stood for the man in the wheelchair, which Mara noticed, filed, and did not understand for another four hours.

The clock in the vault said two minutes to ten.

Mara stood up at the head of the table with a page in her hand that was three weeks old and now covered in the handwriting of five people, and said: "Thank you for coming. Can we begin."


*• • •*

Nobody sat down for eleven minutes.

She lost the room one door at a time. Garrity studied the microfilm; Mesa moved his chair, then moved it back; Kavanagh opened his notebook; Lin Shu poured tea for two people, neither Mara. Sal Castellano, in the corner, spoke the first of the six sentences he would say that day.

"You have a leak in the north corner," he said. "Two metres up. It has been running since the spring."

Everybody looked at the north corner. The wall was dry to the eye. Rask, who had been standing at the back with his arms folded, said, "There's no leak."

"There is a leak," said Sal, and closed his eyes, and appeared to go to sleep.

"Can we," said Mara, "begin."

"He's right," said El Búho.

He had come in without being heard, which nobody could explain afterwards, and he had done it with a cane and a limp, past a gate, three corridors, a stairwell and two armed men, one of whom he had greeted by name.

He stood in the doorway and looked at the room, and did not come to the table. He looked at the shelving, and the forty kilometres of film in it, and said to nobody in particular, "They kept all of it. Sixty years. They never threw it away."

Then he walked slowly to the far end of the table, took the worst chair — the broken one at the back with the wobble — sat down, put his cane against the leg, and put both hands flat on the table.

"Rinaldo," said Mesa.

"Rafael."

"You are dead."

"I know."


*• • •*

Mara got four minutes of her agenda. That was all.

She got through the geography, which they understood better than she did, and the threat picture, at which Mesa laughed once, at the wrong moment, and did not explain. She got as far as the word operation when Kavanagh put his pen down and said, "Miss Voss. Before you go further. Have you told these men what you told me?"

"Told them what?"

“That you found every one of us in a bin.” Kavanagh smiled. “She read our letters and the agencies’ letters and picked us because a machine decided we were not worth the electricity.” He turned to Mara. “Say that out loud, to these men. It is the only power you have over them. Spend it honestly.”

The room waited.

Mara looked at the page in her hand. Then she put the page down and told them the truth, unredacted, in order, in about ninety seconds.

She named the nine thousand four hundred and eleven records and the six labels — closed, at liberty, unindexed, discounted, indeterminate, unresolved — then read her own annex: Villalobos, a gap with a name; Kavanagh, the most dangerous man on the page; Mesa, self-documented and knowable; Castellano, unassessable; Garrity, an honest, empty logbook; Lin Shu, uncharged because her complainants had left the categories.

Nobody interrupted. That was the first interesting thing that had happened to Mara in four days.

She finished. Then she said the other thing, which she had not planned.

"You should also know," she said, "that I am the reason this meeting is happening and I am not in charge of it. There is no one in charge of it. The coalition can authorize money and food and corridors. It cannot authorize the one thing that matters, which is that you do as you're asked. Nobody in this room works for anybody in this room. I have been aware of that since about two minutes past ten."

Kavanagh, of all people, laughed: a warm laugh, completely without malice, which meant nothing.

"Good girl," he said, for the second time in her life.


*• • •*

Then Lin Shu stood up, and put her cup down, and said, "I have a condition."

"I know," said Mara.

"You wrote it down. Good." She looked around the table, once, slowly, without hurrying, and every man stopped doing whatever he had been doing, including Sal Castellano, who opened his eyes.

“I said I would tell each of you what I think, and nobody may be surprised. Mr. Kavanagh. In 1994 a consignment went into a warehouse in your name; the papers said church furnishings. Nine people died in a pub in a town I had to look up. One was the brother of a man I moved to Vancouver in 2008. He has a shop and grandchildren. He will never know I know whose paperwork killed his brother. I decided that. That is what I think of you. Sit down.”

Kavanagh was already sitting. He stayed sitting, looked at the table, and said nothing at all, and something in his face closed like a door being pulled to.

"Mr. Castellano."

Sal opened his eyes.

"I have thought about you for two days," said Lin Shu, "and I cannot tell you what you are. I have met men who pretend to be confused, and men who are confused, and men so far into pretending that they have lost the address of the house they started in. I cannot put you in any of those three boxes, and I am not going to pretend that I can." She paused. "That is all I have to say to you, and it is the first time in forty years I have had nothing to say to a man at this table."

Sal Castellano inclined his head, gravely, like a man accepting a compliment he had not expected, and said, "Thank you, Miss Lin. I have your letters."

The room went still.

"I beg your pardon," said Lin Shu.

"From the mission in Jakarta. 2011. Two writers, one typewriter, forty-one pages, every one signed with a single letter, and you chose M, because the man you were writing to had a mother named Maria and you thought it would help." Sal Castellano was looking at the table, his voice level and unhurried. "It did not help. He never opened it. It went into a box in a records office in a basement on Jalan Surabaya. I know the box. I am the only living person who knows the box, and I am telling you because you have had nothing to say to me, and I find I would rather you were interested."

Lin Shu did not move for four seconds.

Then she said, "You are not a confused man."

"No," said Sal Castellano, and closed his eyes again, and said nothing further for the rest of the meeting.


*• • •*

"Mr. Garrity."

"Yes, ma'am."

“You are going to die doing this.” Lin Shu’s voice held no cruelty. “Your leg is failing. You are seventy-one; you will get into an aeroplane and may not get out. Worse, you will be doing it well, and it will feel better than anything in eleven years. That is why you will not stop when you should. The man behind you will be watching.”

Garrity sat with it. He looked at the table, and then he looked at the dog, and then he did the thing he did, which was to smile.

"Well," he said. "That's a hell of a thing to say to a fella."

"It is."

"Can I tell you something?" He leaned back. "I've had four aeroplanes try to kill me. Four. And not one single time did it feel good. Not the day out of Yazoo, not the night in '07 with ice on the wings and the tachometer gone." He put his hands behind his head. "So you're right, ma'am, and you're wrong, and I'll take it, and you and me can talk about it on Thursday."

"Mr. Mesa."

Mesa had been waiting for it, and he had arranged himself for it, and he performed his preparation so openly that even Mara could see it: the chin coming up, the hands on the arms of the wicker chair, the theatrical settling.

“You moved four hundred thousand people out of a war,” said Lin Shu. “That is true, and I will not take it from you. You were paid and moved those who could pay, or were worth carrying. Three thousand others — stateless, undesirable, unaccompanied, paperless — could not go out or in. You were on the water. You knew where they were. There was one island in particular.”

"I did not go to that island," said Mesa.

"No," said Lin Shu. "You did not go to that island. And there are four hundred and eleven people alive today who were on it, and eleven of them are mine, and every one of them knows your boat's name, because they watched it pass."
