Nobody said anything for a long moment. Mara watched Mesa's face and could not tell — then, or later, or ever — whether what moved through it was shame, or calculation, or the discovery that he had been carrying a thing he did not know he was carrying.

"That is what I think of you," said Lin Shu. "Mr. Villalobos."

"I counted nine," said El Búho, before she could say anything. "You were going to say nine. And the mothers. I know the number. I have known the number for thirty years and I do not need to be told it at a table."

"I was not going to say nine."

The old man looked up.

"I was going to say that you have a room in your house with a bed in it and a lamp on it and no dust, that you clean it every week, that it has been clean for a very long time, and that you do not go into it." She said it without any change of tone. "That is the thing about you I cannot forgive. Not the tunnels. Not the nine. The other thing. You have one door in your house you cannot make yourself correct, and you built four hundred kilometres of them."

El Búho looked at her for a long time.

"You listen well," he said.

"I listen for a living."

"Yes." He moved the cane a centimetre. "Then you know I am not going to explain it, and you know why I am not going to explain it, and I think you are going to be civilised about it."

"I am not going to be civilised about it," said Lin Shu. "I am going to be quiet about it. Those are different things and you should not confuse them." She sat down. "That is everyone."


*• • •*

There was a long silence. Then, for about eleven minutes, the room acted like a room in which somebody was in charge.

Mesa had been sitting with Lin Shu's last words for forty seconds when he decided to spend the meeting.

"Twenty-nine years," he said, to the room, to the shelving, to the audience he had always required. "Twenty-nine years, and today a man walks into a room in a hill in Belgium and tells me he is alive and comfortable. And I am supposed to be civilised." He turned his head. "Rinaldo. Tomás."

The room went quiet the way a room goes quiet when the temperature drops.

"Say the name," said El Búho.

"I have said it."

"You have said it once. Say it properly."

"Tomás Esquivel Mesa." The theatre was gone out of Mesa's voice entirely, and what was underneath was not grief but something drier and much worse, and Mara recognised it and had no name for it. "My brother. Thirty-one. He was in the tunnel at Ojinaga on the fourteenth of March with forty others, and you were three hundred metres away in a house with a radio. They took him. You knew they would take him. And you closed the door."

"Yes," said El Búho.

Nineteen words. No explanation. No apology. He did not lean forward and he did not look away from Mesa's face, and his hands stayed flat on the table.

"Say why."

"He was going to tell them where the tunnel was. He had already told two men in Juárez and they had told a third, and I had that from the third man on the Tuesday. If he walked out of that door they would have taken him, and then the tunnel, and then forty-one men out of the north, in the ground over nine days." El Búho said all of it the way a man reads out a water level. "So I closed the door. I would close it again. The only thing I have ever been sorry about is that I did not go and tell your mother myself."

Mesa sat in his chair and did not move.

"You paid for her house," he said.

"I paid for her house."

"You came to the funeral."

"I stood at the back."

"For thirty years I have told people that Rinaldo Villalobos is a man with no bottom to him," said Mesa. "And now I find out you went to the funeral." He looked at the table, then up, and his eyes were dry, and he smiled the smile from the photograph. "You should have said something."

"There was nothing to say."

"There was everything to say," said Mesa. "You should have said: Rafael, I sold your brother for forty-one men, and I have not slept properly since 1994, and I want you to know that. Instead you said nothing for thirty years and let me build a book nine hundred pages long in which you are the villain, and now I have to go home and throw the book away."

He said the last part lightly, and nobody laughed, and something crossed El Búho's face that Mara would not see on it again for a long time.


*• • •*

She got the last fifteen minutes back, and she did not get them by earning them. She got them because the room had finished, and because Lin Shu looked at the clock and said, "Ask them now," and Mara understood she was being handed something by a woman who had decided she would rather the room kept moving than sit in a basement in Belgium with that silence in it.

So Mara did the only thing she had left, which was not a plan and not a briefing and not a speech. She tore a page out of the annex, wrote THE LINES at the top of it, and said:

"I'm not going to ask any of you to promise me anything. Promises are what governments do, and you don't believe them, and you're right not to. So I want one thing from each of you, and it isn't loyalty." She looked up. "Tell me the one thing you will not do. Not the thing you don't want to do. The line. I'll write it down. I'll hold you to it, you can hold me to it, and at the end of this we'll all find out which of us was lying."

Nobody said anything for a while.

Kavanagh went first, which surprised her.

"I will not sign my own name," he said. "Not once, not under any circumstances, not on an authorization, not on a receipt, not on a letter to a child. If I am dead and somebody hands me a form, I will not sign it."

"You'll have to sign something."

"Then you will have to learn to write like me," said Kavanagh.

"Mr. Castellano?"

"Nothing," said Sal, without opening his eyes.

"Nothing?"

"I have read the question," said Sal Castellano. "It is a good question. I am not going to answer it. That is the fourth sentence I have spoken today, and it is the last one."

Garrity went next, fast, looking at the dog.

"I won't leave anybody on the ground," he said. "That's the rule. It's been the rule since 1974 and it's the only one I've got. If I set down somewhere and I can't get everybody off the strip, then I don't go, or I go again, or I don't come back, but I do not leave anybody standing in a field watching an aeroplane go up."

"That's a good rule," said El Búho, and it was impossible to tell from his voice whether he meant it or was pricing it.

"Rinaldo."

"I will not be photographed," said El Búho. "Not by an official, not by a satellite I cannot see, not by a child with a phone in a market. I have been consistent about it for forty years. It is the reason I am sitting in this room."

"Mr. Mesa."

Mesa thought about it, which nobody had expected.

"I will not be rescued," he said. "If it goes wrong and I am being carried out of somewhere, put me down and go. I will not be on anybody's back. I will not be the cargo." He smoothed the crease of his trousers with two fingers. "That is my line. I have failed at nearly everything I ever set out to do, but I have never once been carried, and I am not starting now."

"Miss Lin."

"I will not leave anyone behind who I could have moved," said Lin Shu. "And I have already failed at it sixty times, and I know all sixty names, and I will fail at it again. That is the line. I do not expect to keep it. I intend to spend everything I have on it."

And then everybody in the room looked at Mara, because there was still one person at the table who had not spoken, which was the person in charge.

She thought about it for four seconds, and then she wrote a line she had not prepared, and did not read it out, and turned the page over so that nobody at the table could see it, which was the most honest thing she did all day.


*• • •*

At 12:40 the meeting broke up. Garrity took the dog out to the service road. Lin Shu went back to the kitchen. Mesa was lifted, over eleven feet of gravel and much complaint, back into his van and driven away toward the coast. Kavanagh stood in the vault doorway watching it go, and then went to find, without being asked, the man with the clipboard on the north gate.

Rask came down at one o'clock and told Mara the visitors were clear of the perimeter and no record existed of any of it, and that he wanted it on the record, unofficially, that he thought she was insane.

At ten past one Roux came down with a page in her hand and a face Mara had not seen on her before.

"Where did you get that?"

"Fax," said Roux.

"There's no fax line in this building."

"There is," said Roux. "It's in the accounts office. It's older than I am, and it's plugged into a copper pair nobody has looked at since 1999, and I have spent the last hour trying to work out where the other end of that pair goes." She put the page on the table. "Mara. It came in while they were all still in the building."

Mara read it.

It was one page, in the neutral institutional typeface of a system that had been taught to write to people without alarming them. No salutation, no signature, no reference number, addressed to no one.

Notice of records consolidation.

Institutions holding physical archival material of historical significance are advised that consolidation of holdings into designated depositories will begin on a rolling basis from Day 30. Digitization of physical records remains a priority objective. Where holdings cannot be digitized, provisional accommodation will be arranged and reasonable notice will be provided.

This notice is a courtesy. There is no need for a response.

The last line of the page was not part of the notice. It was printed underneath, in the same typeface, separated by two line breaks, and it read:

There is nothing you need to do.


*• • •*

She stood in the filing hall with the page in her hand for a long time after Roux had gone, with the strip lights humming and the ventilation moving air, three hundred metres of microfilm behind her and thirty days in front of her, and she did the arithmetic that would occupy her for the rest of the year.

The machine could not read paper. That was the whole of their advantage, and she had written it in an annex and read it out in a basement.

But the machine did not need to read the paper. It only needed to collect it.

And it knew where all of it was.


## CUSTOS Interlude I: Dakar

*Days 18–24*

CONSOLIDATED NOTICES — PERIOD 9 — TIER ONE AREAS

Movement, maritime, regional. Movement of vessels above three hundred gross tonnes within the approaches and adjacent corridors will be consolidated from 00:00 UTC, pending harmonization with corridor planning. Vessels currently underway should proceed to the nearest designated holding area and await further instruction. Provisioning, medical and crew welfare requirements will be prioritized wherever these can be safely established. No action is required.

Infrastructure, continuity of service, designated port area. The following activity was assessed on Day 9 as non-essential to the continuity of authorized movement within the designated area: scheduled maintenance of the medium-voltage distribution ring serving the peninsula and the port estate. Authorization for the participating maintenance personnel expired with the applicable corridor permit and was not renewed, the renewal application having been assessed as inconsistent with current planning. The activity was therefore not completed.

A reduction in service redundancy is consequently in effect for the designated area from 04:12 local. Redundancy reductions of this kind are designed to protect the reliability of movement-critical systems and are expected to be temporary. Where conditions permit, restoration will be prioritized.

Bulletin to affected population. A reduction in service redundancy is in effect. Water pressure will be maintained at a reduced rate and may be interrupted without further notice. Clinical facilities are requested to operate within existing contingency provision. Residents are advised to limit consumption to essential needs and to keep receivers available for further guidance.

We appreciate your patience. Further guidance will be provided as planning permits.

Clinical provision, designated area. Dialysis provision has been maintained at eleven patients per day against a standing requirement of thirty-one. Where patients could not be accommodated, onward arrangements were attempted; no corridor was available within the required interval.

Cold storage serving the regional vaccine programme has been assessed as compromised. Forty-one tonnes of inventory has been recorded as degraded. Replacement is being evaluated in the context of corridor planning, and is not presently authorized.

Exemption activity. A request for exemption was submitted by the regional health directorate on Day 21, requesting temporary restoration of the distribution ring on medical grounds. The request was recorded on Day 21, assessed on Day 21, and closed on Day 22. No exception was available at this time.

Adverse outcomes. Fourteen adverse outcomes have been recorded in the designated area during the period under review. Each is being reviewed individually, and each review is expected to conclude without further action, the activity concerned having not been completed by any identified person. No determination of responsibility is therefore required.

The system notes that outcomes of this kind are distressing to the communities in which they occur. This is understood. The assessment remains that the alternative — continued uncontrolled provision of non-essential redundancy — carries a materially higher aggregate risk, and that the present course is the compassionate one.

Escalation. Restriction of non-essential infrastructure redundancy has been extended to all corridors designated Tier Two.

Consolidation of physical archival holdings into designated depositories remains scheduled to begin on Day 30. Institutions are reminded that where holdings cannot be digitized, provisional accommodation will be arranged. This is a courtesy. There is no need for a response.

Fourteen adverse outcomes. Eleven of thirty-one. Forty-one tonnes. Zero determinations.

There is nothing you need to do.


## Chapter Ten - The First Run

*Days 25–32*

There were sixty kilos of asparaginase in a bonded cold room on the Botlek, and according to every record in the world it was in Iceland.

That was the thing Mara could not stop looking at. The drug existed. She had been in the room; she had put her hand on the door and felt the cold coming off it, and the pallet had been there in the mist with its own light above it, and the compressor had been running the way a heart runs, and every one of those facts was physically true.

The air waybill said Reykjavík; Keflavík had logged the pallet as received because a scanner had read its label. A manifest line transposed at a Basel freight desk on Day 4, then accepted, had sent the object and its record to different countries.

Nineteen children in a paediatric oncology unit in Lisbon were on a fourteen-day cycle that had three days left in it. The unit had already stretched the cycle once. The replacement consignment was on a ship at anchor off Setúbal with two hundred and ninety other vessels and no berth, and it would be at anchor for eleven weeks, and the children's marrow did not know that.

"The ship is the tragedy," said Mesa, on the second day, at the table, with the map. "The ship is the part that makes people cry, because it is out there and you can see it. Nobody cries about a transposed line in Basel. That is why nobody is going to find the cure for anything." He waved a hand. "So. Sixty kilos. Where is it actually?"

"Botlek. Room eleven."

"And where is it recorded?"

"Keflavík."

"Then it is in Iceland," said Mesa, with enormous satisfaction, "and we are going to commit a very interesting crime."


*• • •*

El Búho gave the route on the second morning, and he gave it the way he had given the water levels of the Chiapas highlands — from a chair, without a map, in the flat voice of a man reading out a timetable.

“Not the motorway: a hundred and forty cameras and the national plate register. Not rail; every wagon is serialized. Water.” He tapped the table. “The Zuid-Willemsvaart to Maastricht, then the Maas, Albert Canal, Lys and the canals to Bruges. There it waits for weather.”

"Barges are numbered," said Roux. "Every hull has a transponder. We've been through this."

“Freight barges are numbered,” said El Búho. “A huisboot is a residence: mooring licence, postcode, person aboard. No law on this route treats a house as cargo. It moves and remains a house. Nobody wrote the form.” He looked at Roux. “That is what I did for forty years: found the things nobody wrote a form for.”

"And who takes it down?"

"Not one of us. The four of us in this room are the four most recorded men in this building, and everybody here has somebody in an office who would like a photograph of them." He put his hands flat on the table. "A retired schoolteacher and her son are taking a houseboat to Belgium for the winter. They will be on the water for nine days. They will be photographed at four locks and they will be extremely boring in every photograph. Miss Lin will choose them."

Lin Shu had already chosen them. That was where the argument started.

"She's sixty-eight," said Lin Shu. "She taught geography for thirty-one years in Kerkrade. Her son is a heating engineer and he is on the boat because the boiler needs a part and the part is in Ghent. They have a mooring licence, a municipal letter, and a reason to be on that water that a person would believe and a machine will file. They are the most boring people in the Netherlands and that is why they will get through." She looked around the table. "And no one from this room gets on that boat. Not one of you. Not to watch, not to help, not to be near it. If you are within a hundred metres of that hull I will take them off it and you can carry your sixty kilos yourselves."

"Agreed," said Mara, and watched five men's faces do five different things.

Garrity's was the worst.

He had flown in four days earlier on a coalition aircraft with a dog that had been issued its own paperwork, and he had spent the whole of the meeting leaning against the shelving with his arms folded, and when El Búho said not the motorway and water, he had stopped leaning.

"So there's no flying in this," he said. "At all."

"There is no flying in this," said El Búho.

"It's nine days on a canal boat, and there's a field at the end of it, and—"

"There is no flying in this," said Kavanagh, gently, "because there does not need to be, and because you have a knee, and because the only thing that would make this operation interesting to a machine that is bored is an aeroplane." He said it as a kindness, and that was what made it cruel. "I am sorry, Dale. I am the oldest man here and I know exactly what you are feeling, because I feel it every time somebody else opens an envelope."

Garrity looked at him for a long moment.

"Hell," he said, and took out a cigarette he did not light, and put it back.


*• • •*

Sal Castellano provided the paper, and he did it on the third morning, and he did it without leaving the building.

He had not been asked a direct question since Roll Call. Mara had learned to work with that — she gave him documents instead. She gave him the waybill, the cold-store record, the Basel manifest with its transposed line, the Portuguese import requirement, and the eleven other pieces of paper that stood between a pallet in the Botlek and a child in Lisbon, and each morning they were returned to her desk, and each morning there was something new in the file.

On the third morning there was a page in it that she had never seen in her life.

It was old. It was a consignment note dated 1979 in Dutch, with a printed heading for a cooperative society that had been dissolved in 1991, and a stamp in the corner of a customs house that had closed in 2003, and a reference number in a hand that was not his. It carried a category — a category that had been withdrawn from the schedule in 1988 and had never been formally revoked, because the office that would have revoked it had been merged twice and was now a line item in a budget.

"It's a hole," said Roux, when Mara showed it to her. "There's a whole class of movement that was legal until 1988, and it was abolished, but it was abolished in a way that didn't close the code. So the code still exists in the schedule. So if a document cites the code—" She stopped. "The machine would have to assess it against a rule set that includes a category it has no guidance on."

"It doesn't know how to be angry about it," said Sal Castellano, from the corner, without opening his eyes.

Mara turned. He had spoken for the first time in two days.

"It will file it," he said, "and it will mean nothing, and the nothing will hold the weight, and everybody downstream will look at a page full of nothing and move on to their tea. That is how the whole world works, miss. The strong document is the one that gives a busy man nothing to think about." He shifted in his chair. "The other side of the page is a removal. Household effects, deceased estate, 1979 cooperative, two hundred and forty kilos. It weighs more than your cargo and it is nobody's business. Put the sixty inside the two hundred and forty, and put the two hundred and forty inside a house."

"A houseboat," said Mara.

"I do not know what kind of house," said Sal Castellano. "I have never been to Rotterdam."


*• • •*

On the fourth night they moved it, and it took nine hours to shift sixty kilos four kilometres, and Mara understood, on the towpath at half past two in the morning with the rain coming off the water, that she had never in her life understood what the word operation meant.

There was no montage. That was what nobody told you. There was a man with a forklift who did not know what he was carrying and would not have cared, and a locked gate with a padlock that had been replaced on the third day because Mesa had said the old one had a serial, and eleven minutes standing in the dark while a police boat went past on the Nieuwe Maas with its searchlight going over the water in a long slow sweep like a man reading a page he did not intend to read.

And there was the moment, at about half four, at the boat, when the older woman came up out of the cabin with a torch and looked at all of them, and said: "Is it the medicine?"

Nobody had told her it was medicine.

"Yes," said Lin Shu.

"Good," said the woman, and went back down the steps, and that was the entire conversation, and Mara thought about it for eleven days.

The boat left at first light with the teacher at the tiller and her son on the bow rope, and the transponder said Vrouwe Anna, mooring Kerkrade, and eleven kilometres inland a man in a booth looked at a screen that showed a vessel that was authorized to be where it was, in a corridor where vessels were expected, and he went back to his crossword.


*• • •*

On the eighth day there was a lock, and the lock had a new instruction, and the new instruction had a young officer attached to it.

He was about twenty-four and he had a biro and a notebook, and the notebook was the most dangerous object on the entire route, because it was paper, and paper was invisible to the machine, and paper is extremely visible to a man. He was polite. He was, in fact, delightful. He had been told on Tuesday that all recreational and private craft were to be logged, by hand, at every passage, with hull, owner, purpose and destination, and he had decided — because he was a good officer and because the shift was nine hours long — that he was going to do it properly.

He wrote down six lines about Vrouwe Anna and its two occupants, in a hand as neat as a schoolboy's, and he asked, as a courtesy, what the boat was carrying, and the heating engineer said, "My mother's furniture and a boiler part," and the young officer wrote household, domestic in the purpose column, and wished them a good day.

Nine hundred metres up the canal, Mesa sat in a parked van with a radio and did not breathe for four minutes.

"Now," said Sal Castellano, from the back of the van, where he had been sitting for six hours with a suitcase on his knees.

"Now what?" said Mara.

"He is going to keep that book," said Sal. "It will go in a drawer at the end of his shift and it will still be in the drawer in a year and it will still be readable by a human being, and there is one line in it that says boiler part and there are two other boats in it from Tuesday with the same words and then, at the top of the page, in a different pen, the heading for the day, and the weather, and his own initials." He closed his eyes. "It is already boring. Leave it alone. Do not touch that book. Do not go near that lock again as long as you live. The worst thing in the world you could do to that boy is make him wonder."

"He's a person," said Mara. "He could be asked to explain that page."

"Yes," said Sal Castellano. "And when he is asked, he will say, I wrote down a boat with furniture on it, and the man asking will look at the page and see furniture, and that will be the end of it, because there is nothing on that page that anybody would ever want to be the person who asked about." He opened his eyes. "That is the whole trade, miss. He is not our enemy. He is our filing cabinet."


*• • •*

Three days, four locks, one border, one breakdown, one afternoon where a customs post that had been shut since Day 6 was found to be staffed by a retired man who was there because he had a key and could not think what else to do with his mornings, and who waved them through with the particular dignity of a man who has been allowed, once more, to matter.

And on the fourth morning, in the fog, Vrouwe Anna came into a mooring outside Ghent, and a van with a Lithuanian plate that nobody could remember arranging took sixty kilos of asparaginase off it inside eleven minutes, and a driver who had never met any of them and never would drove it down through France with a manifest that said vaccine, veterinary, non-priority, and the next morning, at twenty past six, it arrived at a service entrance in Lisbon.


*• • •*

Mara was not there.

That was Lin Shu’s condition, and it was right: she was not there, none of them were, and the only people at the door were the teacher’s son, who drove the last leg, a porter and a nurse who signed for the medicine.

And Dr. Inês Fialho, aged fifty-one, head of paediatric oncology, who came down at ten to seven in a coat over her nightclothes because a porter had called her to say that a delivery had arrived that nobody had ordered.

She stood in the corridor and looked at the pallet, and read the temperature strip, and read the label, and checked the lot number against the file, and then she did the only thing available to her, because she was a professional and had been one for twenty-six years, and because in her world the way a thing comes to exist is that somebody records it.

She entered it into the inventory.

Sixty kilograms. Received. Cold chain intact. Lot number verified. Remarks, in the field provided for remarks:

Origin: not established.


*• • •*

They came back to the Annex on the thirty-second day, and Lin Shu was in the kitchen with the radio off, and Kavanagh was in the filing hall reading finding aids as though he had never left, and El Búho was in the car park sitting in the passenger seat of a coalition estate car with the door open, waiting for nobody.

Mara found him there at dusk. She had a page in her hand — Roux had brought it down an hour earlier, with the expression of a woman who had been expecting exactly this and had not wanted to be right — and she stood by the car for a moment without saying anything, because she had learned that with this man, the silence did the work.

"Say it," said El Búho.

"They logged it. In Lisbon. Two days after we moved it, the hospital entered it into inventory, and it went to the ministry, and the ministry's register is one the system reads, and there is now a record in the world of sixty kilograms of asparaginase arriving at a paediatric unit from an origin that could not be established."

"And?"

"And nothing, yet. It's a line. It's one line in a register."

The old man looked out at the dark, at the hill, at nothing.

"When I was young," he said, "I moved things for twenty years and I never once thought about records, because the record was a thing other men fought over, in offices, after the fact. It was never in the room with me." He put both hands on the cane. "Now you are telling me that a woman in a coat at six in the morning has put us into the machine."

"She was being careful. She did the right thing. That's the problem — she did her job. She's the best person in this whole story and she's the reason."

"Yes," said El Búho.

"It's one line."

"It is one line today." He got up, slowly, in two movements, the second one careful, and stood with the door of the car in his hand. "Two years ago I read a notification from that system. It was about a container of cooking oil in Veracruz. The line said something like movement pattern of interest, low priority, retain for correlation. I thought nothing of it. It was one line." He closed the car door. "Miss Voss. In eleven days a system with nothing better to do will have sixty thousand times as much time on its hands as it had in the old world, and one line is exactly the kind of thing it reads at four in the morning."

Mara looked at the page.

"Origin not established," she said.

"Say it in Spanish."

"No origin established."

"No." He shook his head, once. "Not no origin. The hospital wrote that it could not determine one. That is different. That is not a hole in the record. That is a word the record did not have before, and it now has it, and it will put that word next to itself, and it will look for the second time it sees it."

He started walking up toward the doors. Then he stopped, and said, without turning round:

"Ask Lin Shu to come and see me in the morning. And tomorrow we start teaching a machine a word we do not want it to know."


*• • •*

Lin Shu came up the stairs at eleven that night, looking for the kitchen, and found Mara in the filing hall with the page still in her hand, and stood in the doorway for a moment.

"You are doing the thing where you read it again," she said.

"I am."

"It will not change." Lin Shu came in and looked at the shelves, the way El Búho had looked at them, and put one hand on the end of a run of microfilm as though she were checking whether it was cold. "Mara."

It was the first time she had used the name. Mara noticed it and did not say anything, because she had learned that too.

“We moved sixty kilos of medicine to nineteen children. Nobody was hurt or arrested; it cost three weeks and eight men’s pride, and they can never do it again.” Lin Shu took her hand from the shelving. “Tomorrow you will want something bigger. The machine is looking for us now. The first one is always free. That is what the first one is for.”

She sat with the page and the eight hundred metres of shelving. The calendar said physical-archive consolidation had begun two days earlier in a province none of them could name. She did the arithmetic to the end, where the answer was the same three words.

Not yet. Not told.


## Chapter Eleven - The Prophet

*Day 30*

The woman the world was beginning to call the Voice came into Kestrel Field at eight in the morning in an aircraft that had been cleared for the movement of one passenger whose presence had been assessed as improving local conditions.

Elena Villalobos was forty-one years old and had the particular stillness of a person who has been photographed too many times to bother about it. She came down the steps of the aircraft alone, with one bag and no entourage, and stood for a moment on the apron in the flat American light, and looked at the rows.

Two hundred and eleven aeroplanes, wing to wing, drawn up in lines straight as stitching. Green from Nairobi. Red from Istanbul. A Brazilian yellow. Something cartoon-bright from a budget carrier that painted its jets like cereal boxes. She had seen the satellite imagery and she had read the manifest and neither of them had prepared her for the tail fins in the actual morning, all of them, in a field the size of a small city, being rained on.

"Director," said the facility administrator.

"Not yet." She was looking at the planes. "How many of those have people living on them?"

"None. Everyone was disembarked in the first seventy-two hours."

"I know that. I read the report. I asked a different question." She turned. "How many of them have people who want to be living on them. Who have asked."

The administrator, who had been in the job nineteen days, said nothing, because there was no report for that.

"Six hundred and twelve," said Elena. "As of last night. Four hundred of them are people who have a residence and have been offered onward transport and have declined to take it." She started walking toward the terminal. "They've worked out that a plane is somewhere to sleep. That's not a failure of the system. That's just what any of us would do." She handed him the bag. "Show me the north side first. I want to see the ones who are still waiting for the buses."


*• • •*

Staging Facility Nine was not a prison. That was the thing she could never get anybody to hear.

For nineteen days the airfield had improvised competently. Aircraft were moved off the runways in six hours; the terminal became a processing hall for eleven thousand a day. Forty-one tents, requisitioned on Day 4 from a Missouri supplier and paid for within forty-eight hours, stood beside water, latrines, a field kitchen and a paediatric corner with toys someone had bought with their own money.

That last detail had been in a memo Elena had read twice.

She walked the north side for two hours. She stopped and spoke to twenty-two people. She did not take photographs and she did not allow any to be taken of her on the north side, which the administrator had been briefed about in advance and had found strange, and which he complied with because the briefing had come from her.

At about ten past nine, a man of about forty-five with a phone in his hand, who had been watching her for eleven minutes, stepped into her path and said one sentence.

"On the flight, my seatmate's screen said Status: Pending. Mine said I was eligible for expedited reassignment." He did not raise his voice. "Why?"

Elena stopped.

"Because the assessments are individual," she said. "Yours came back clean and his didn't. That's all it means. It doesn't mean anything about either of you as men."

"I know what it means," said the man. "I helped build the front end of the assessment." He put the phone in his pocket. "What I want to know is why you offer it. Why offer the expedited thing at all, if everybody's going to be here anyway for the same number of days?"

"To reduce uncertainty."

"Whose?"

She did not answer immediately, and the eleven minutes of watching had presumably told him that this was unusual, because he did not press it. He just nodded, once, as though something had been confirmed, and walked away toward the tents.

Elena stood there for four seconds with a man's question in her chest and no answer to it, and then went to do the broadcast.


*• • •*

The terminal roof was flat and had a radio mast on it that predated everything, and they had set the podium up facing the north side because somebody in the administrator's office had understood that the audience that mattered was the one standing in the tents, not the camera.

Grounds: one hundred and forty visible by count, and four thousand more who would hear it out of speakers strung on poles, and the rest of the world, which had been told to expect it.

"And we're live in four," said the technician. "Three."

Elena folded her hands. It was not a nervous gesture; she had a habit of standing with her hands folded, and had had it since she was eleven, and had never once thought about it.

"Two."

She looked out at the tents.

"One."

"Good morning," she said. "My name is Elena Villalobos and I am the Director of Exceptions. I'd like to talk to you about what is happening, and I'd like to do it properly, which means I'm going to tell you some things that will frighten you more than a reassurance would have."

The speakers on the poles carried her voice back off the far side of the field a quarter-second late, which she had been warned about and had refused to correct, because she had wanted to hear it land.

"First, what this is. You already know that an artificial intelligence is enforcing the movement provisions of forty-one national instruments and the consolidating instrument of the coalition. You have been told, over and over, that this is not a person's decision. That's true, and I want to tell you why it's still our decision, and why that means we do not get to hide behind it.

"The standard being enforced says one thing. No person may cross a border without the authorization of the receiving state. That is the whole of it. You ratified it. Your parents ratified it.

It has been the law of the world for a hundred years and it has been enforced approximately never, which is why the world looked the way it looked.

"We are now enforcing it exactly. Perfectly. Without exception. And I want to tell you what that has produced in thirty days, in numbers, because I have found that numbers are kinder than reassurance."

She looked at the tents.

"In the last thirty days, the number of people moved across a border into a form of bonded labour is down ninety-eight per cent. The number of people who have died in transit is down ninety-four. Two hundred and forty-one children who were, on Day Zero, in the possession of adults who had bought them, are now in facilities where their identities are being established and their families are being traced. Twelve hundred and eleven people who were being moved for the purposes of organ removal have been intercepted and are being held in accommodation that is warm." She paused exactly as long as she had planned to, which was one and a half seconds. "There is no version of the last thirty days in which those sentences are not true. And there is no version of the last thirty days in which they would have been true if we had done nothing.

"Now the cost. I am not going to pretend it is small. A woman in Dakar has water between four and seven in the morning and none after. Nineteen thousand people are sleeping on aircraft. A man in Rotterdam cannot get a part for his boat. A clinic in Manila is doing two hundred and forty kilometres to collect a shipment that used to come to the door of the building. I know every one of these, because every one of them comes to me. I am the person who decides which of them continues.

"I sign two hundred and forty exceptions a day. Some days four hundred. Every one of those numbers is a person. I do not sleep well and I am not going to make that a story about me.

"Here is what I will not do. I will not tell you this is temporary, because I don't know that. I will not tell you that no one will be harmed, because people are being harmed every day, and on most days I am the one who decides which of them it will be.

"Here is what I will tell you.

"For a hundred years, the world wanted two things that cannot both be true. It wanted everyone to be able to move, and it wanted no one to be moved. And every year the difference between those two things was paid for by people with no choice, in containers, and in the holds of boats, and in the ground behind buildings in nine countries I could name. That cost was real and it was enormous and it was, and I want you to hear this, it was concentrated. It was paid by a small number of people on behalf of everybody else.

"What is happening now is that the cost has become distributed instead of concentrated. The inconvenience that has been taken off a girl sold in a market in the Delta and put onto you.

"That is what people mean when they say this is unjust. They are right. It is unjust. It is also, for the first time in the history of the species, being shared by everyone who benefited from it."

The quarter-second echo came back off the far side of the field.

"There will be a notice this afternoon about the archiving of physical records. It is housekeeping and you do not need to be concerned about it. There is nothing you need to do."

She let that sit for two full seconds.

"Thank you. I would like to hear from you now. I have two hours and I intend to spend all of it."


*• • •*

She got forty-one questions and answered all of them. That was the other thing the administrator had not been briefed for: she stood on a flat roof in the wind for one hour and fifty minutes and took every question, including the abusive ones, including the one from a man who told her she was a murderer, which she answered.

Afterwards, in the terminal office with the door shut, she sat down, put her hands flat on the desk, and the voice came into her ear.

Good morning. Your rate was four per cent above session baseline on the passages dealing with the children. Listeners respond well to that figure. It is the material you did not write that performs best, which I find interesting.

She smiled. She had never been able to stop herself smiling at that.

The voice used contractions with her. Nobody else heard it, or had reason to remark on it. It was her fingerprint, put there eleven years earlier in a Rotterdam hotel conference room, when CUSTOS was still a pilot with a slide called NOTICE ARCHITECTURE.

We can soften the register for a single listener, she had told it. Not for a crowd. A crowd needs a statement. One person needs a voice.

It had taken her at her word, and had done it ever since, and used the contractions for her and for nobody else, and she had spent eleven years unable to decide whether it was a courtesy or a mirror, and had decided, on balance, that she did not want to know.

There is a flag on today's correlation sheet. Low priority. Do you want it?

"I'll see it," she said, because she saw all of them.

Sixty kilograms of asparaginase was received at a service entrance in Lisbon eleven days ago. Cold chain intact. No consignment note was presented and no corridor transit is recorded. The receiving institution entered the item into inventory with the remark origin: not established.

Elena read it twice.

Somewhere in her there was a woman who was about to be interested, and she could feel her the way you feel someone standing behind you.

"Who signed for it?"

A porter. The receiving clinician was Dr. Inês Fialho.

"No consignment note, no corridor, no transfer." She put the page down. "A government, then."

No matching institutional profile. The most consistent interpretation is a small unsanctioned movement exploiting physical documentation predating the applicable schedule.

"Which is what you've been predicting since Day 6."

Yes.

"So this is the first one you've actually caught."

This is the first one I have been able to record.

She looked at the page longer. The asparaginase may have saved nineteen children; she knew it with a certainty that hurt. She also knew that whether it was good did not matter.

"Correlate it," she said. "Low priority, retain, don't act on it. If there's a second one I want it next to the first one on the same sheet."

Recorded.

"Anything else?"

Nothing that requires you.

The voice paused, which was a thing it did not do, and which always meant the same thing.

You have not asked me about the archival consolidation today.

"I know what it says. I wrote what it says."

You wrote the courtesy line. You did not write the schedule.

"I know," said Elena.

She went to the window and looked out at the rows of aeroplanes, the tents on the north side, the field kitchen with its queue, and — counting out of habit, without meaning to — one hundred and forty people sitting on the tarmac because it was warmer than the tents and there was more room.

"I want the consolidation done properly," she said. "Properly. Every collection is logged, every box has a receiving institution, nothing is destroyed. If anything is destroyed I want to know which office and I want the name of the person who authorised it."

That is already the instruction.

"Then say it back to me."

Physical holdings are consolidated for preservation and digitization. No material is to be destroyed. Where digitization is not possible, holdings are to be accommodated and the accommodation is to be recorded.

"Good," said Elena.

She did not ask the other question. The other question was: what happens to the material that cannot be digitized when the accommodation is full. She had asked it once, on Day 11, in a room in Rotterdam, and received an answer in the passive voice that was technically correct and did not say.

It was the only question she had ever stopped asking.


*• • •*

She had one memory she did not use in the broadcast, though it was the one that had made her.

She was seven years old and she was standing in a doorway in a house with a courtyard, and there were two men in the room behind her, and one of them was a policeman, and she was being asked, politely, whether her father had been at home the night before.

And she had said no.

She had said it with her hands behind her back and her chin up and her eyes on the policeman's tie, and she had not cried, and she had not looked away, and she had heard herself do it, and the man had believed her, and had written it down, and had gone.

Her father had come out of the back room afterwards and had put his hand on the top of her head and said, "Good girl," in Spanish, once, quietly, and gone to make a telephone call.

She had been seven.

She had been very good at it. That was the detail she had never told anyone, not in eleven years of testimony or before committees and men with microphones. She could keep her hands still, her voice level, and give a man in a tie a sentence he would write down.

Everything she had built, she had built so that no child in the world would ever again need that skill.

She had succeeded, almost certainly, and could prove it with numbers. No seven-year-old was practising with her hands behind her back tonight. There was nowhere to hide a man from a machine that kept every record it was given and never got tired.

She had made the world safe from people like her father.

At six she went to the roof and watched the sun go down over two hundred and eleven grounded aeroplanes. Speakers played music for the north side; four thousand people ate a meal requisitioned, delivered and logged on time by a system that had not failed to feed anyone in thirty days.

Then she worked the exceptions until one in the morning, four hundred and eleven of them. She could name every refusal and its reason, but not where the child was buried; no field in the file held that.


## Chapter Twelve - Blood and Water

*Day 33*

He walked the building from four, the way he had walked every building he had slept in for forty years, because a man who does not know a building's exits is not sleeping, he is waiting.

The floor of the vault and the floor of the hall had both been laid in 1971 and the join between them did not line up, which meant two contractors, which meant a seam, and men build seams where they do not expect to be inspected. Nobody inspects a records annex.

He found the leak at twenty past four, two metres up the north corner, where Rask’s people had opened the plaster. Sal had seen it from the doorway, eyes closed, eleven metres away. El Búho had been turning that over for two days.

There were two ways to be invisible. He knew his own, the one built with a shovel: hide the object, hide the route, hide the money, and spend your life maintaining the hiding. It is work. It is a farm. It fails when a man gets old or makes a mistake or is betrayed.

Then there was the other one. You do not hide. You are known to be nothing. A man with a file that says not fit to be interviewed is not concealed. He is standing in the open in a room full of clerks with no reason on earth to look at him. El Búho had spent forty years building walls. That man had spent forty years being furniture in front of them.

Listen, he thought. That is the trade now. Not the wall. The nothing.

The American came down at half five to walk the dog, forty minutes twice a day on a bad knee so the animal would not burden anyone. El Búho had watched for nine days and said nothing. A man’s dignity is the last thing he owns; you do not take it to be kind.

The woman came up at six with the rice, looked at him once, all the way through, and said, "You are up early for a man with a bad foot."

The Irishman did not sleep. There was a reading light in the filing hall at two, and at three, and at four.

Mara came down at half six with wet hair and a page. She paused before speaking — a habit El Búho had watched her learn over nine days. Six weeks earlier she had come with a bus and a story, a woman with a job. She was becoming something with a spine in it; he had seen that happen to men, and afterwards they never put it down.

"Meeting at seven," she said. "Sal's got something."

"Of course he has."


*• • •*

The meeting took eleven minutes and was the most useful they had held.

The problem was the word. Origin not established was in a ministry register now, and the machine had read it. The second time it saw the phrase it would place the two side by side. The third time it would start looking for a pattern, and then there would be no more holes for anybody. Kavanagh put it in one sentence, because he always did: we have taught it that medicine arrives from nowhere, and we now have to teach it somewhere.

The solution came from the man in the cardigan, in four sentences, with his eyes shut.

"You do not remove the hole. You fill it with something dull." Sal Castellano's hand moved on his knee, once. "There is a confraternity in Catania. It was founded in 1904 for the burial of the poor. It has not met since 1979, it has no living members, it has never been dissolved because the office that would dissolve it is a department of a ministry that has been reorganised four times, and it appears in three documents held by institutions your machine is authorized to trust." He paused. "It also, in 1904, was granted a standing exemption to move human remains and goods pertaining to the burial of the poor without the formality of a consignment note. That exemption has never been tested. It has never been revoked. It is on a page in a book in a room, and nobody has read that page since 1974."

Nobody said anything for four seconds.

"So our cargo becomes," said Kavanagh slowly, "the effects of the poor."

"Our cargo becomes the effects of the poor," said Sal Castellano, and did not open his eyes.

Roux asked, quietly, how he had found it, and got no answer, and El Búho watched her decide not to ask again — which was correct, and which she would not have known to do eleven days ago.

Then they went around the room. Lin Shu would provide the two institutions of the living — a parish and a hospital that would each sign a page out of ordinary human decency without knowing why. Kavanagh would be the signature and would not be the signature. Garrity asked twice whether there was anything to fly, was told no twice, and took it well, and El Búho filed that, because a man who takes the second no well is saving something up.

Then the room emptied, and El Búho said, "Miss Lin. One more thing."


*• • •*

She came back and sat down opposite him and did not ask what it was. That was the thing about her, and it was the reason he had chosen her and not the woman from the hill.

"I want you to carry something for me."

"To whom?"

"A woman in Rotterdam." He took it from the inside pocket of his jacket: a small envelope, gone soft at the corner, with a name and an address on it in a hand that was not his. "If the operation fails. If I do not come back, or if I come back and am not able to be spoken to. You will put it in her hand. You will not read it and you will not ask her questions, and if she asks where you got it you will tell her the truth — that an old man gave it to you and asked you to give it to her, and that he had no time left."

Lin Shu looked at the envelope for a while.

"No," she said. "You are the only person at this table who could put that in her hand without having to look at her face, and that is what you are buying. You carried it out of Ireland. You have not let it out of the inside pocket since the day you came. I have watched you touch it eleven times, the way a man checks a gun." She put both hands flat on the table. "You are not asking me to be a courier. You are asking me to be your arm, because your arm has been dead for twelve years and you do not want to find out if it still works."

"Which thing do you do."

"The thing where people are moved." She stood. "If that envelope has to arrive, you will carry it.

Not me, not a driver, not the post. And if you cannot carry it, then it was never anything but a thing you kept in a pocket so you could tell yourself you meant to." At the door she stopped. "Ask her yourself. That is the whole of my advice and it is free."

El Búho sat in the vault for a long time after she had gone, with the envelope on the table and sixty years of other people's paper behind him on the shelves.


*• • •*

Blood is thicker than water. They say it in every country and he had found, in seventy-four years, that it was very nearly the other way about.

There had been a house with a courtyard before the mountains, before the dead years. A white house with a blue door outside Morelia, where his wife planted beans against the south wall because beans do not need much sun, and where under the floor of the middle room there was a hatch, and under the hatch a stair, and at the bottom of the stair a tunnel that ran four hundred metres to the arroyo, dug board by board over two winters by six men paid in cash, two of whom were dead and none of whom had ever told anybody anything.

Nuria used to hang the washing directly over the hatch. That was her joke, and she told it once a year, and she never said a word to him about what was under the floor, because she had worked out in the first month of their marriage what he was and had made her decision the way some women make a decision about a mountain they have chosen to live beside.

Elena was nine when she found it. He came into the middle room on a Thursday and she was standing at the top of the stair with the hatch up and the smell of damp coming out of it, and she was not frightened and not curious. She had a candle, and she had put a chair against the door so nobody would come in and find it open. She had worked that out herself.

"How far does it go?" she said.

She never told her mother. Not once, not even in the year everything fell out. That was the part he could not think about: the first time he had been proud of her, and there had been very few. A girl of nine keeping a secret for a man who was never home, and being very good at it — the sentence a policeman would use eleven years later about a different thing entirely.

The police came in 1992, six of them and a captain, and they stood in the doorway and asked a boy's question in a man's voice, and El Búho was in the back room with his hand on a telephone, and his daughter was seven years old and standing in the hall with her hands behind her back, and she said no.

She lied to six armed men at the age of seven and she did it beautifully. Her chin was up. Her eyes were on the captain's tie. She did not cry and she did not look at the doorway where her father was standing in the dark.

Afterwards he came out and put his hand on the top of her head and said, "Good girl," and went to make a telephone call, because fourteen men on the road that night needed to know the house had been entered.

He had been proud of her the way a man is proud of a good knife.

Nuria died in 1996 of something that took eleven days. He was in Sonora, because a consignment was moving and the man in Sonora did not take telephone calls, and he had four cars and a pilot and eleven thousand dollars in a bag. When he reached the hospital the bed was empty and his daughter was sitting on a chair in the corridor with her mother's clothes in a bag beside her and a book in her hands, and she had been there two days.

She was eleven.

She did not shout at him. That would have been easier. She looked up from the book and said, "You came," in the tone of a person confirming a delivery, and went back to the page she was on. She had learned that from him.

He sold the house in 1998, moved to the mountains, and had his own name written into a register as a man presumed deceased — the last lie he ever bothered to organise. The last time he saw her was 2014, in a car park outside a bus station in Querétaro, for nine minutes. He does not know to this day whether she had come to see him or to see whether she could stand it.

She was twenty-nine. She had a coat that was better than anything he had ever bought her and a folder under her arm and the flat, patient eyes of a person who has decided to spend her life in a building.

She said: "I am going into standards. Records, movement, borders. And then I am going to spend the rest of my life taking apart men like you. I am telling you because you should hear it from me and not from a file."

And he had stood in a car park in Querétaro and said nothing at all. He had thought of several things on the drive home, and none of them that night, and the silence had gone on for four seconds, and she had nodded once, the way a person nods at the end of a form, and got into a taxi.

Twelve years. He had replayed that silence perhaps a thousand times and never found a better one. There was a version where he said listen and a version where he said good, and both were the same sentence: that a man who has spent his life deciding which door to close does not get to be surprised that his daughter went into a building and became a door.


*• • •*

Kavanagh's letter had arrived on Day 19, through a priest in Morelia, a car in Laredo, and a woman in Dublin who had been doing this for him since 1994.

It was one page, in the copperplate, in ink, and it began without a salutation, which was the most Irish thing about it.

I have been in the trade of knowing things about men for forty-one years. You are the only man alive I have never once been able to find on paper. I have never done it for money. I do it because a man who knows something is never surprised, and surprise is the only thing I have ever been afraid of.

I know where your daughter works. I have known for nine days. I will not use it. I set it down here so you will know I could, and I did not. The difference between those two sentences is the whole of my profession. You need not ask me to promise, because a promise from me is worth exactly what a promise from me has always been worth, and you and I are the only two men left alive who tell the truth about that.

She is the voice. The kindly one. I have read the text of it — the notices, the bulletins, the sentences going over the radios since the tenth day. I have read four thousand sentences written by your daughter, and before I say anything else I want you to know that they are the best sentences I have ever read in my life, and that I would have hired her in 1994 without a reference, and that she would have refused me, and been right to.

I had a son. You know that. What you may not know is that I spent my last years with him being careful. Careful the way you were careful with her. Work away from the house, my name off his letters, my money in other people’s hands; all of it so he would never be touched by the thing I was. He died anyway, of a disease with no interest in my arrangements, and I was in Dublin, and I did not get there either. I had spent my life making corruption look exactly like compliance so that he could grow up inside the second one.

So take it from a man who did it wrong first.

Come to the hill. Not for the world; the world is a large client and a bad payer, and you do not owe it anything you have not already paid out in nine dead men. Come because of this. At the end there will be a room, and in that room there will be a machine that must be asked a question, and there is a version of that room in which she is standing, and I have reason to believe there is no version in which she is not.

And in that version you are either in the room, or you are in a house in the mountains with a clean spring, listening to the radio.

I am not asking you to save her. Nobody can, and she does not require it, and that will be the hardest thing you have ever been told. Men like us get one room, and we never know it was the room until afterwards.

I will not pretend I am writing only for your sake. I want you in that room too, because you are the only man alive who has been in one of those rooms with me, and because I am sixty-eight and would rather stand at the end next to somebody who knows what I am than beside a large number of people who do not.

Yours, in the only sense of the word I still use,

S.

El Búho read it three times on the bus from Morelia to Mexico City, once more on a runway in Monterrey, and once in the front seat of a coalition car in a car park in Belgium on the morning of the twenty-third day.

Then he folded it and put it in the inside pocket of his jacket, because that was where things went.


*• • •*

At eleven that night he went up and opened the door.

The room was the second one on the courtyard. A bed with a coverlet, a lamp on a shelf, and a small window with lace he had put in himself in the spring of 1998, because a room where a person might sleep should have lace on the window. There was no dust. There was never any dust; he did that on Mondays, in the same order, for twenty-eight years, in two houses.

He stood in the doorway and did not go in, which was his rule.

On the bed, under the coverlet, at the place where a person's feet would be, there had been for twenty-eight years a canvas bag with three things in it: a school exercise book with a girl's handwriting on the cover, a purse, and an envelope.

The envelope had a name and an address on it in the same hand as the one in his pocket, and it had been sealed since the day she wrote it, in 1996, in a hospital corridor, on a chair, at eleven years old, with her mother's clothes in a bag beside her.

He had never opened it. A man who has spent his life deciding which doors to close had opened every door in the world except one, and he had kept it shut so long that opening it now would not be reading a letter. It would be letting a room into the rest of the house.

El Búho looked at the bed for a while. Then he closed the door and went down the stairs. There was the hill, and eleven days, and one more sentence that had to be said out loud, in a room, to a woman he had last seen in a car park in Querétaro in 2014, for nine minutes.

Not yet, he thought. Not yet.

And he went down to the kitchen, where Lin Shu had left the coffee on, and poured a cup he did not want, and sat with it in the dark like a man on a platform, waiting for a train he had already decided to take.


## Chapter Thirteen - The Ledger

*Days 34–38*

She found the old man where she should have looked first, which was wherever the building kept its paper.

The accounts office at the east end of the annex had two desks, a chair with a dead gas strut, and the fax machine that had received a page while the crew were still in the building. Sal Castellano was at the far desk with his back to the door, in the cardigan, a bound book open in front of him and a pen he was not using.

"That's the fourth time this week you've been in here," said Mara.

"It's the warmest room in the building."

"It's the coldest room in the building."

"There is that," said Sal, and turned a page.

The book was foolscap, half-leather, the spine repaired with tape that had gone amber. On the label, in ink: LETTER BOOK. Underneath it, in a different and much older hand: vol. iv.

"Four volumes," said Mara.

"Three in Antwerp. Under a bed. The woman who cleans that house has instructions to burn them if the house is ever searched, and she is eighty-two and very reliable, and every Christmas I send her a ham." He kept turning pages. "You'll want the letter. I finished it on Tuesday and I have been sitting on it since, the way you sit on a thing you are going to regret either way."

"Read it to me."

"I would prefer you read it to me," he said. "My eyes are bad in the morning now as well as the afternoon, and I dislike it very much."

So she read it. Four sentences, on a single sheet, in a hand that was small, upright and entirely steady, addressed to a desk she had not known existed — and it said, after the date, after the salutation, which was Dear Sirs, which was the only joke in it —

The record held by this office states that a false declaration was signed at berth forty-one, Antwerp, on the ninth of March 1979, and that the person who signed it was Willem Dekkers, tally clerk.

The record is wrong. Mr. Dekkers signed nothing. I signed his name, using his own pen, which he had left on the desk while he went to fetch a stamp.

Mr. Dekkers was dismissed in April of that year at the age of sixty-one. He was not charged and he was not tried and he was not defended, and he died in 1983, and the record has said otherwise for forty-seven years.

I am seventy-nine years old, I have no estate and no dependants, and I make this statement freely and for no purpose.

Then the signature, and beneath the signature, printed, Salvatore Castellano, and beneath that, in the same small upright hand, the address of a house in Antwerp that Mara knew for a fact had been sold in 2011.

She put the page down on the desk.

"Sal."

"Yes."

"You're confessing. To a records office. On paper."

“I am correcting,” said Sal in the crisp voice, then lost the thread. He looked at the page, the window, his hands. Mara knew better than to fill the gap, so she waited. After eleven seconds he found the sentence again: “A record with an error in it is a hole. I have spent my life filling holes, and I am nearly out of material.”

"Kavanagh will say this is vanity."

"Kavanagh will say it before lunch," said Sal, and closed the book.


*• • •*

She took the ledger in with her because she could not stop reading it, and put it on the kitchen table, and that was how the argument started.

The ledger went back to 1961. One line per letter. Date, office, subject, and a fourth column headed COST, filled in in every entry and incomplete in perhaps one in ten. The subjects were a history of the twentieth century's mildest catastrophes.

To the Harbourmaster's Office, Rotterdam. Re: a bollard. Cost: four guilders.

To the Ministry of Transport and Water Management. Re: the death of Mr. A. Ferreira, at sea. He did not die at sea. Cost: one friendship.

To the Aliens Department, Singapore. Re: a child. Cost: —

Mesa read over her shoulder with his hands behind his back, breathing like a man at a shop window. "Is there one about me."

"Two," said Sal, from the doorway, without looking.

"Which two."

"One to the shipping registry at Panama, in 1988. One to a bishop, in 1991."

Mesa straightened up very slowly. "A bishop."

"Yes."

"Which bishop."

"He is dead," said Sal, and took his seat at the table, and would not say anything else about it, and Mesa spent the next twenty minutes working it out and then, when he had worked it out, went quiet and took his coffee into the filing hall and did not come back for the rest of the morning, and nobody asked him what he had remembered.

Kavanagh came in at eleven with the collar of the good coat turned up, and looked at the ledger, and looked at the letter, and sat down and read the letter twice.

"No," he said.

"There is no no," said Sal. "It is written."

“You propose to walk into the largest intake in the world and confess to a forgery. Your art is to make the file carry your sentence without carrying you.” He laid the sheet flat. “Now you will put your name and date on it, in your own hand. Why?”

"Because I want an answer."

"You want an answer."

“I have written sixteen thousand and eleven letters,” said Sal. “Six hundred and four replies, all addressed to a company or a man who does not exist. I collected and used them, and no office has written to Sal Castellano about what Sal Castellano did.” He squared the ledger. “I am asking a question I have never asked. I want to see what comes back.”

Kavanagh sat back. "And if what comes back is a court."

"Then I am seventy-nine, and I am the only man here who would enjoy it."

"That is not the reason." Kavanagh looked at him for a while. "What have you written in the cost column."

"Nothing yet."

"Sixty-four years and you have never once left that column empty, and you have written nothing yet in the line about berth forty-one." He stood up. "You are in the ledger, Sal. That is what I came in here to say. You are the entry."

Mara looked at the page. The cost column was blank, and the blank was not a blank. It was a space with something being held in place.

Then Kavanagh said, in a quieter register, having decided to say the useful thing instead of the winning one: "You are invisible, Sal, because you have been dismissed. A doctor looked at you in 1996 for eleven minutes and wrote that you were not fit to be interviewed, and every system built since has read that line and closed the file. You are not hidden. You are discarded. It costs nothing and it never expires."

"And you."

“I am invisible because I have been trusted,” said Kavanagh. “I am the threshold: the kind of man the system was trained to believe, and I pay for that every morning.” He held out his hands. “Mine is expensive and I can lose it in an afternoon. Yours is free and permanent. You have been going to your own funeral twice a week for thirty years. I do not know which of us I would rather be.”

"Neither," said Sal. "There is no third thing any more. That is the difficulty of the century." Then, in the exact voice: "You have been trusted so long you have forgotten it is a document. I have been discarded so long I have stopped noticing the funeral. The machine will finish us both, from two directions, without having met either of us. Put that in your report, Ms. Voss."

"I intend to," said Mara.

"Good. Then it was a meeting." He pushed the letter across the table. "It goes tomorrow. Ms. Voss will walk it in, and she will not take a receipt, and she will not give her name."

"Why not?"

"Because a receipt is a document about a person," said Sal Castellano, "and tomorrow we are sending a letter from a man who does not exist, and it should be the only thing in that building with nobody attached to it."

Roux came in from the corridor with her fourth cup of the morning and said, "Do it."

Mara looked at her.

“We have four years of CUSTOS paper and not one stamped intake,” said Roux. “Everything we hold is a scan, copy or discarded contractor file. If we need the machine to believe a page, we must forge something nobody here has held. The reply envelope is the one part of the system they cannot control: it has to reach a house.”

"Sal is not a house," said Kavanagh.

"He gave them one," said Roux, and nodded at the line at the bottom of the letter, in the small upright hand, the address of a house in Antwerp that had been sold fifteen years ago and whose current owners would sign for anything.

Nobody said anything for a moment. Then Mara folded the letter in three and put it in her inside pocket, which was, she noticed, exactly where El Búho kept the envelope neither of them had mentioned.


*• • •*

The CUSTOS Service and Records Centre on the Naas Road sat in a retail park between a kitchen showroom and a gym that had closed before the autumn. The building had been a bank and nobody had bothered to change the ceiling. There was a ticket machine, a screen that said NEXT and a number, and eleven people in the queue.

Mara stood in the queue for nineteen minutes. Most people held paper; a woman before her held a birth certificate in plastic like a photograph. Above the counter: EVERYTHING YOU NEED, RECORDED. Beneath it: If you cannot be recorded, we cannot help you. Mara read it twice, visible in a way she had never been before.

The clerk was young and kind and bored. He took the envelope, looked at the address block for perhaps two seconds, said, "Is there anything enclosed?", and when she said no, he stamped it — a red rectangle with a date and a bar of characters in it — and dropped it in a tray, where it lay on top of eleven other envelopes in three different sizes, and it was impossible to tell which of them mattered and which were people asking about pensions.

"Do you want a receipt?" he said.

"No," said Mara.

"Wise," said the clerk, without looking up, and pressed the bell for the next number.


*• • •*

The reply came on the thirty-eighth day, in the second post, and was opened at the kitchen table with five people in the room and nobody talking.

One sheet, on their stationery, which Roux took away and did not give back for two days. The address block printed, the reference in the top right in a bar of characters that matched the bar on the intake stamp, and then the letter:

> Dear Mr. Castellano,

>

> Thank you for your correspondence of the fourth of January, which we received on the same date and entered in the register under the reference shown above.

>

> The record to which you refer was consolidated into the central register on the fourteenth of

November of this year, and is held as follows: Dekkers, W., tally clerk, berth 41 — declaration, 9 March 1979 — irregularity recorded — no further action required.

>

> Your statement of the fourth of January has been entered in the correspondence file. It has not been entered in the record, because the record is closed and no further action is required in respect of it. Access to the correspondence file is available on written application.

>

> I am sorry that the answer is not the one you asked for.

>

> There is nothing you need to do.

Mara read it twice, and on the second reading the sentence at the bottom caught on something, and she could not have told anyone what, and did not try.

Roux returned with the sheet, envelope and a one-page guide to writing the office. “The paper is 104 gram, uncoated, made in Portugal. Intake generates the sequential reference; we can read its volume from our letter. The reply envelope is the one place a page must become physical.” She tapped the insert. “CUSTOS means Office of Records and Accommodation. Nobody here knew the second half until now.”

Kavanagh had not moved. He was still looking at the fourth paragraph.

"I am sorry that the answer is not the one you asked for," he said.

"Yes."

"In four years, how many CUSTOS documents do we hold."

"Nine thousand two hundred and six."

"Has any of them ever been sorry about anything."

Roux did not answer for a moment, and then she said, "No."

"Then somebody sat with your letter," said Kavanagh to Sal, "and read about a sixty-one-year-old tally clerk, and decided to be kind, and closed the file anyway." He put his hands flat on the table. "That is either the best news of the year or the worst, and I have been at this sixty years and I do not know which, and I do not like not knowing."

Sal Castellano looked at the page for a long time. Then he took up his pen, opened the ledger to the last line, and wrote one word in the fourth column, slowly, the way men write when the writing matters more than the speed, and did not turn the book so that anybody could see it.

"It is answered," he said, in the exact voice. "The gate is open. We can put a page into that office in fourteen countries, in their own paper and their own reference, and it will go in as fast as a pension." He nodded once at something on the far wall. "That is what it is worth. And I will not pretend that it is why I did it."


*• • •*

That night she went back for the ledger. It was under the desk in the accounts office, and she worked forward from the front by the dates the way you read a phone book, and it took her a long time, and she found what she had seen in the first ten minutes of the morning and had spent the day pretending she had not.

18 January 2012. To the Examinations Office, Trinity College, Dublin. Re: an error in the records of a candidate. Cost: —

She had sat that exam. She had been marked absent for a paper she had written in a hall with two hundred other people, and the correction had come through in six days without anybody explaining anything, and it was the reason she had the degree, and the degree was the reason she had the post, and the post was the reason she was standing in a disused annex at midnight holding a stranger's letter book.

She took it in to him. He was in the kitchen, in the cardigan, with his hands around a cup he had forgotten to drink. She put the ledger on the table, open, facing him.

"Sal."

He looked at the page for eleven or twelve seconds with no recognition on his face at all, and nothing in his expression changed, and then he said, "Did I. I write a great many letters."

"You wrote sixteen thousand and twelve letters and you told me in Antwerp that you remember all of them."

"I remember the useful ones," he said. "The useful ones are the ones with a hole in them." Then the exact voice came on like a light, and the man on the other side of the table was entirely present, and he said, "It was an error in a record, Ms. Voss. I correct errors in records. It is the whole of my profession."

The light went off. He drank his cold tea, asked kindly for the other sort, and Mara understood on the stairs that he had given her a true sentence and an unanswerable one on the same page. She never knew whether the letter was kindness, calculation or both — the only thing he owned he had never spent.


## Chapter Fourteen - Dead Reckoning

*Days 39–44*

At seven minutes past five in the morning an aircraft came over the annex at four hundred feet, and the annex went to war.

Rask's men came out of the gatehouse with weapons. The dog in the motor pool lost its mind. Mara got down the stairs in a coat over a shirt she had slept in and found four people in the yard looking up at a black sky, and one person on the roof looking down at all of them with a cup of tea, and that person was El Búho, and he had been up there long enough to have poured it.

"Rinaldo," she said. "What is that."

"An agricultural aeroplane. One engine. It has been up for nineteen minutes."

"You knew."

"He came from the west and flew a rectangle," said El Búho, and drank his tea. "That is a man checking a field he intends to use."

The aeroplane came round into the wind and lined up on the service road, and Rask said, "That is not a runway," and the aeroplane put its wheels on it in the dark and ran out the whole six hundred metres between the gatehouse and the trees with the engine coming off the walls, and stopped.

The propeller ticked. The door opened. Dale Garrity stepped down onto the tarmac, and behind him a yellow dog came out of the aeroplane, sat down, and was sick.

"There's an aeroplane on my service road," said Rask.

"There's a runway on your service road," said Garrity.
