He was seventy-one, and he had come out at whatever hour this was to put a sixty-year-old aircraft on a road with a treeline at the end. Mara watched him walk up the yard among armed men and understood two things at once.

The first was why. He had not flown because he needed to fly. He had flown because he needed to be seen flying.

The second was where the aeroplane had come from. It had arrived on Tuesday on a flatbed, in twelve pieces, under a tarpaulin, with a bill of lading for agricultural machinery. It was registered in Meath as agricultural machinery. It smelled of hot oil and old chemicals, and it had a hopper where the passenger seat should have been.

"He calls it the tractor," said Sal, who had come out in the cardigan, delighted. "It is the tractor. There is no more invisible machine in the world than one that sprays potatoes."


*• • •*

The brief took nine minutes, which was the crew's new standard for a brief.

“We need one picture,” said Roux. “Contained records arrive, are sorted and go into accommodation; the only number anyone keeps is what has gone in. The queue is invisible.” She turned the map. “Intake docks, east side, four in the afternoon, when the vans back up and every manifest says what we need to count.”

"How far off," said Mara.

"A thousand metres from the public road, and no road that looks at it. There's a camera mast at the crossroads now, on a new pole, and the pole has a bar of characters on a sticker that is the same family as the characters on Sal's reply."

"Why not a drone," said Mesa.

"A drone is a licence and a serial and a radio," said Roux. "Every transmitter over a certain power is enumerated by the state, and the enumeration is a feed the machine gets every morning at four. A drone is an event. An aeroplane is weather."

"Why not satellite," said Mara.

“You cannot task a satellite without putting your interest in a queue, and the machine reads that queue first.” Roux put down a grainy photograph of a grass strip, six hundred and twenty metres, with a gate and a stone wall. “There is one straight approach in that county with no road to it. Garrity used it once. The valley wind comes from the north-east, so you land downhill with it behind you and take off uphill into it — wrong for an aeroplane, right for farming.”

"It's fine," said Garrity.

El Búho, who had said nothing for nine minutes, said: "He is seventy-one. The machine is sixty. The field is on a hill. It is December." He stood. "I have nothing to add and I am not coming."

He left the room, and that was his last protest, and everybody in it knew it.

Mara looked at the old American across the table. This was the question, and it had a number in it.

"Can you do it?"

"Well," said Garrity, "that's Corrigan's field. Corrigan kept a bull up there for twenty years to keep people off the strip, and I had an arrangement with him, which was that I came in from the north so the bull never heard me. Did I ever tell you about the night the bull heard me?"

And he told it. It took four minutes, and it was the funniest four minutes Mara had spent since the world ended: a bull, a bottle of Powers, a man from Dundalk who had hidden in the hopper and had opinions, and a punchline about where the bull was now, which was in the hedge. The whole room laughed, including Lin Shu, once.

She took the story.

She had asked a question with a number in it and been given a story, and she had taken it, because she wanted it, and because not one person in that room was willing to be the one who told the Cowboy he was finished. It never got better.

"Does the field slope?" said Lin Shu.

"Everything slopes," said Garrity.


*• • •*

Corrigan was seventy-eight, lived in a farmhouse with two dogs and no lock, and had been paid in cash on Tuesday. Sal’s note said only: For the field. Thank you. Corrigan kept it in a drawer. Mara thought of a receipt as a document about a person, then stopped; it was already dark.

They laid the strip with two vans and four torches. At the far end, beyond the wall, the ground fell away, and beyond that was the road, and beyond that the yard they had come to photograph, with a low lit band along its east side that was the intake docks, running all night, four in the afternoon or four in the morning. The machine does not sleep and does not stop. It only gets quieter.

Then they waited, and the only thing in the world was a sound.

Mara had never listened like that in her life. The engine went out over the hill and shrank and came back and went round the far side of the sky, and for twenty minutes there was nothing to see at all, only a small change in the sound that told you where a man was, and four adults in a wet field doing arithmetic on time. Roux was in the back with the camera bolted to the floor, her hands cold on it and her eyes on the docks, because there was a fact the film could not carry: the film took four days to develop, and a woman takes four minutes, and in the analogue world the fastest sensor is a person.

The engine came back and got louder, and there was the aeroplane, black against black, coming down the hill with its nose low.

It was beautiful.

Mara was not a judge of flying, but the whole field went still in the same instant, because the aeroplane met the first metre of grass at the exact speed that made the wheels kiss it, and ran out light and straight down the slope, and the tail came down at the top of the strip with thirty metres spare, and the engine shut off, and the propeller ticked, and there was the sound of a farmer's dogs on the next hill and nothing else.

Garrity got out on the wing. He stood there, one hand on the windscreen, and looked down at all of them, and, God help him, he took the applause. Four seconds of it. Long enough that Mara could see he had been waiting since the day he arrived. He was only a man being old and visible in the dark.

Roux handed the canister out. Then she got out, and her legs were not good, and she stood holding the wing with both hands for a moment and said, in the flat voice she used for numbers, "That is the worst thing I have ever done and I have run a systems directorate."

"Same again the other way," said Garrity. "Straight up the hill, into the wind. It'll be off the ground before the gate."


*• • •*

It was not off the ground before the gate.

The sound told them, which is what an old-world operation actually consists of: a field, a dark sky, and four people reading an engine the way sailors read weather. The engine went away down the valley, was gone twenty minutes, came back, was talking normally, and then was shouting.

The aeroplane came over the hedge at the bottom of the strip already on the ground, fast, much too fast, downhill with the wind behind it, running the wrong way down a slope with a stone wall at the end and a road beyond the wall. Garrity had landed the wrong way round. There was no reason on earth to land the wrong way round except that it was four minutes shorter.

They heard him get it down, and then they heard him try to stop it, and the brakes were doing something and the grass was doing nothing, and at the bottom of the field the left wheel went into the drain at the foot of the wall, and the machine turned on it like a man turning on a bad knee, and the left wing came round into the gatepost, and the aeroplane went down on its nose and stopped twenty metres from the road with the engine still running, which was the worst part, because a running engine is a promise that somebody in there is still able.

Then the engine stopped, and the dogs on the hill went insane, and the dark was very quiet.

Roux's voice came out of the wreckage, very loud and very level, and it was not a scream and it was worse than a scream.

"Get me out. Get me out now."


*• • •*

She was out in ninety seconds and she walked. That was the thing everyone told each other afterwards: she walked, with her good hand holding the film canister against her chest and her other arm hanging wrong at the wrist and blood coming down the side of her face from the eyebrow, and she stood ten metres off and said, once, to nobody, "Get him out."

Garrity was not out. He was in the aeroplane with the harness across him and the frame bent down over the cockpit, and his left leg, the one Lin Shu had named out loud in front of the crew in the second week, was in the place it had always gone, and the place it had always gone was not there any more. He was entirely awake, and he said two things.

The first was, "Is the dog all right?"

The second was, "Well."

They were forty minutes from a second vehicle. Lin Shu did the arithmetic standing in the dark with a torch in her teeth. "One of them goes now. It is the woman. He is not being generous, he is stuck. Do not look at him like that, any of you, he can see you."

She put Roux in the back of the van herself and splinted the wrist with a length of pine off the fence and a strap off the camera crate. It took her four minutes and she did not say another word to anyone.

Then the van went, and Mara stayed, because she had asked the question in the kitchen and taken a story for an answer, and nobody else in that field owned it.

They sat in the dark with the engine ticking as it cooled. Corrigan brought out a lamp and then, being seventy-eight, went to bed. After a while Garrity said, "You know what dead reckoning is?"

"Tell me."

"You take where you were, and how long it's been, and how fast you think you're going, and that's where you are. Every man who ever flew over water without a radio did it, and every woman who ever walked home in the dark." He was quiet a moment. "You've been doing it your whole life. You just don't write it down."

Mara put her hand flat on the bent cowling, because it was cold, and because there was nothing else to do with it.

"What happens when you're wrong?"

"Then you're not lost," said Dale Garrity, in a wrecked aeroplane in a wet field, in the voice of a man telling a joke at his own funeral. "You're just somewhere else."

The second van came at half nine. It took two men with a jack and a saw and forty minutes to get him out through the hole in the frame, and he could not put weight on the leg at all, so they carried him, and he made jokes the whole way to the van, four of them, and none of them worked, and he knew it, and he kept going.


*• • •*

The film went to Dublin the next morning to a man who developed aerial survey film for quarries and forestry, and would have it on Thursday, which was four days.

They were all in the kitchen when the quote came back, and nobody was talking. Roux's arm was in a cast. Garrity was in the chair at the end of the table with the leg out in front of him and the dog's head on his knee, and for the first time since he had arrived at the annex, he said nothing at all.

"The numbers were right," said Roux, to the room. "Six hundred and twenty metres, sixty pounds of camera, one passenger, four degrees of slope, fifteen knots of wind. I did them twice. It wasn't the field and it wasn't the weight." She lifted the cast an inch off the table and put it down again. "He couldn't hold the left pedal. Write that in the report. It is the only line in it that matters."

Mesa, who had said not one word about any of it, said: "In my business, when a man of seventy-one tells you he can do a thing, everybody nods."

"Because the alternative," said Garrity, without looking up, "is telling him he's finished, and nobody wants that job."

"Yes. That is the whole of my business too." Mesa finished his coffee. "I just wanted it said."

"I'll say the rest of it," said Garrity. "I was going to say I came back the other way so they weren't stood in a field waiting on me. That's true." He rubbed the dog's head and looked at the wall. "It's also true I wanted a second one. The landing. In front of everybody."

Nobody said anything for four seconds. Mara counted them.

"The decision was mine," she said. "The field, the machine, the man. Mine. It stays mine." She stood up, because if she had stayed sitting she would have started explaining, and explaining is the thing you do instead of being in charge. "Rask. Strip cleaned tonight, machine out of that field by Friday. On a trailer, under a cover, into the barn at the back, and nobody opens that barn."

"It'll never fly again," said Rask.

"No," said Garrity.

Lin Shu got up, put her cup in the sink, and walked out. Mara heard her go along the corridor and open the vault door, and heard her voice inside, low, in the register she used with the two of them when she was not asking a question, and after a minute El Búho's voice, once, and after that neither of them. Mara stood in the corridor outside the shut door and did not go in.


## Chapter Fifteen - The Railroad Runs Both Ways

*Days 44–47*

She picked the filing hall because it had no windows and one door, and because three hundred metres of microfilm smells like a court, and because when you sit men down in a room like that they know what the room is for before anybody says a word.

She set three chairs facing one and carried the folder in herself.

Mesa came first, because Mesa was the easy one, and Lin Shu had learned forty years ago that you take the easy one first and let the room learn to be quiet. He arrived with two cups of coffee, gave her one, sat down, looked at the arrangement of chairs, and said something about how he had been summoned by a woman with better manners than the last three governments he had dealt with.

"May I have your log book," said Lin Shu.

The showman stayed on his face for perhaps two seconds. Then he took the exercise book out of the inside of his coat, where he had been carrying it since the day he arrived at the annex, because that was where he had carried it for forty years, and put it on the table.

"A man's log is like a man's—"

"Pencil," she said.

"It is pencil because ink runs in the tropics."

"It is pencil because pencil comes off." She turned the book to the last written page and worked back by dates the way you read a timetable. "The fourteenth of April, 2015. Watch four to eight. Wind north-east, force four. Barometer steady. Visibility good." She looked up. "Read me the remarks."

"I don't—"

"Read me the remarks, Mr. Mesa."

He read them, in the theatrical voice, at full volume, because he was going to make somebody in the room do this, and the somebody was going to be himself. "Sighted drifting vessel. Continued."

"Six words," said Lin Shu. "You wrote more about the barometer."

"That was a very good barometer."

“There were eighty-one people on that boat.” Lin Shu put a creased photocopy on the table. “It drifted for nine days. Four women died the next morning; a girl in a yellow shirt survived and is now thirty. Her statement says a ship came close enough for a man on deck to hear them singing.

He watched and did nothing. The statement does not use the word ‘ship.’ I will not read you the word. You know it, and I will not make this easy.”

Mesa looked at the photocopy for a while. Then he put his cup down and did the thing that she had never once seen him do in six weeks, which was to stop performing.

"Stopping creates a record," he said.

"Yes."

"You do not understand what it was. A man who goes alongside a boat like that has to write down what he found. Where it was found, in what condition, with how many. That page goes into a port log in three countries. My name is on the charter. My name would have been on that page for the rest of my life, and my life at that time was a ship and a crew and eleven men who had families in Veracruz."

"You wrote it down, Mr. Mesa."

"Yes."

"Six words, in pencil, in a book you have carried for forty years." Lin Shu leaned back, and her voice did not change, because she had decided on the walk down the corridor that there would be no shouting in this room, and she had kept her decision about every single thing for six weeks. "So it was not the record. You were never afraid of the record. You wrote the record. You were afraid of the page with your name on it."

Mesa sat there with his hands open on the table. He was sixty-nine years old and he had spent his whole life being a man people told stories about, and he said, in a small voice that was much worse than anything loud: "I have looked at that page every year for eleven years."

"I know," said Lin Shu. "That is why I asked you first."


*• • •*

El Búho came last, which was his hour, and sat down without being asked and put his cane across his knees and waited, which was also his habit, and which she had counted on.

She opened the folder.

"I have four names," she said. "I want the rest. Adrián Sosa, Veracruz, 1987. Olegario Chávez, nineteen years old, whom the crew called Pájaro, in the drainage cut, 1993. Fermín Alarcón, in the yard at Morelia, 2009. And Bernardo Quintero, Sonora, 1991, whose own people put a bullet in him after nine days of somebody else's questions, and whose name, Mr. Villalobos, is not in any register in Mexico, and which I have because a woman in Uruapan is eighty-six and likes to talk to strangers." She waited. "That is four. How many were there."

"Nine," said El Búho.

"Say them."

He said them the way a man reads a stock list, in order, with the years. "Adrián Sosa, 1987. Bernardo Quintero, 1991. Olegario Chávez, 1993, who we called Pájaro, who was nineteen, and who could not swim. Rafael Ortega, 2004, who was called Falo, and who did not need to be at Angangueo that night and was there because I asked him to be. Fermín Alarcón, 2009, in the yard, of a heart, at fifty-two, after eight days of carrying." He paused, and the pause was the only part of it that was not a list. "And two men in a container at sea in 2001. The manifest said they were cargo. They went in on the manifest and they came out on it, and the manifest is the reason nobody opened the door. Afterwards they were one line in a report in a language neither of them read. We called them the cousins. They were not cousins. They were from Guerrero." He looked at the table. "That is eight, with Tomás. The ninth has no name I ever had. He came out of a truck at a place we did not intend to stop, and he was with us eleven hours, and I do not know what he was called."

Lin Shu wrote nothing down. She had written it all down in the second week.

"Tomás Iriarte," she said.

His hands did not move.

"Morelia. November 1998," she said. "Tell me about the door."

"There is a tunnel under that house," said El Búho. "Four hundred metres, to a drainage cut. It was a good tunnel. It had never been used for anything except money, and money does not need air."

"Tell me about the door."

"The search came in through the cut at the far end. Tomás was in the tunnel with the boxes. He was thirty-one and he was quick and he had done the thing eleven times, and if I had opened the hatch he would have been up in the middle room in nine seconds and out through the courtyard and over the wall, and there would have been six men in my house asking my wife questions, and my daughter in the front room." He stopped. That was all. He did not explain it further, and he did not ask her to be sorry for him, and that was the thing she had been watching for all six weeks. "I closed the hatch. The men took him out of the cut at the other end. Eleven days later he was found in a field outside Apatzingán. I went to the funeral. I stood at the back."

"At the back."

"There were two hundred people there," said El Búho. "They did not want me at the front."

Lin Shu looked at him for a long moment, and then she said the sentence she had carried up four flights of stairs.

"You have never once, in six weeks, said that you were wrong."

"No."

"You have said it was necessary. You have said it was the arithmetic. You have said the tunnel was good." She closed the folder. "You have not said the words."

"I am not going to say them to you," said El Búho. "There is one person in the world I have to say that to. You are not her, and you are not owed it, and if I say it in this room on a Tuesday afternoon because a woman with a folder is looking at me, then it will be worth nothing when I say it to her."

Mara Voss was in the doorway, and had been for a minute and a half. Lin Shu had known she was there, because a person who has moved four hundred people through checkpoints knows what a corridor sounds like when somebody stops in it. "That is why you asked me here," said Mara, to both of them. "So I would hear it."

"Sit down," said Lin Shu.


*• • •*

She waited until Mara was sitting, because a chair is a signature.

"Ms. Voss," she said. "You were aboard."

El Búho's head came up. It was the first movement he had made in twenty minutes.

"I want you to look at this page," said Lin Shu, and slid the photocopy across, the one she had not shown either of the men: the same log entry, dated the fourteenth of April, 2015, and on the back of it, in ballpoint, a case reference in the format used by the annex, and five words in Mara's own handwriting.

Aboard. Entry noted. No further action.

Mara read it. Her face did not do anything at all, which was its own answer, and then she said, "Nineteen days."

"Say it."

"Nineteen days aboard his ship, in 2015, as a passenger. It was the only way to reach a port that mattered and the annex paid for it out of a travel line, and I was twenty-three and I was on my way to look at a filing system." She put the page flat on the table, face down, which was the right instinct and the wrong move, and then turned it face up again, which was the right one. "I saw the book. I photographed it. I went home and wrote five words and closed the file, because the file was about documents, and a drifting boat in 2015 was not a document, and there was nobody in my office who would have known what to do with it if I had brought it in."

"And you kept the photograph."

"For eleven years."

"Why."

"Because I did not believe the file," said Mara.

Lin Shu let that sit, because it was the only sentence in the room that had cost anything, and because she was not in the business of letting people off.

"Then you know what this is," she said. "You are not here to save anyone, Ms. Voss. You are here because it is the same work, in a smaller room. A page arrived about a thing that was happening to people, and a person wrote that no further action was required, and the file closed. That is the entire machine. It is one sentence long, and all four of us have signed it. Sal signed it in 1996 on a form about himself. The office in Naas Road signed it ten days ago about a dead tally clerk. You signed it in 2015. I signed it in Jakarta."

Mara said nothing.

“Nineteen ninety-eight,” said Lin Shu. “I had three hundred and nine names and a route for two hundred and forty-one. I chose the route; it was worth more than sixty-eight people. There is not a day in twenty-seven years when I have argued that arithmetic down. I do not tell you to make you feel better. You will need something to hold when the wind comes round, and this is all I can offer: you were told exactly what you are.” She returned the photocopy to the folder. “Cooperation is not forgiveness. We are two sets of people with the same enemy and different graves.”

El Búho, quietly: "You built a railroad."

"A railroad runs both ways," said Lin Shu. "That is why it has tracks."


*• • •*

The photographs came up at four that afternoon, and they finished the business.

Roux had them on the long table in the filing hall, eleven prints, still smelling of the fixer, and she stood over them with her cast held up against her chest like a wing. "Twenty-nine vans, east side, four in the afternoon. Each van has a manifest envelope in a colour band, and the colours are not decoration, they are a routing class, and there are three routing classes on this dock in one afternoon and we have never once seen this many at once. The queue for the dock is nine vans deep at four o'clock and it is still nine vans deep at half four, and every one of those vans is carrying the thing we have been trying to count for a month, and no one in the world has ever published a number for it."

"Good," said Mara.

"That is the good news." Roux turned a print over. "North side, same afternoon, different shutter speed. Eleven vans at a door with no dock and no ramp. Between three in the morning and four, one every four or five minutes, and then nothing. The door has a lip on it, about this high." She held up two fingers. "Records do not need a ramp. Records need a dock, because records come on pallets and pallets come on a trolley. Whatever they are taking in that door is being carried. Two men, walking it." She put her finger on the top corner of the print. "And there is a sign over that door, and I have had it enlarged, and I want you all to look at it, because I have spent four years of my life looking at CUSTOS's words and I have never once seen them painted on a wall in the rain."

The sign said: EVERYTHING YOU NEED, RECORDED.

El Búho stood up and walked to the window and stood there with his back to all of them.

Mesa had been looking at the print of the north door for a while. Now he took the pencil out of the spine of the log book and laid it on the table, and then put the book itself down next to it, closed, squared, the way a man squares a page.

"Which one was she," he said. "The girl in the yellow shirt."

"I do not have her," said Lin Shu. "I have eighty-one."

Mesa nodded slowly, as though that were an answer to something, which it was not. "You are very good at this," he said.

"Yes," said Lin Shu. She picked up the exercise book and put it in the folder with the rest of it, and he let her, and neither of them said anything about it, then or afterwards.

"Very well," she said. "The railroad runs both ways. What is coming in through that north door in the dark is not records." She looked around the room at all of them: at a woman who had written five words on the back of a photograph and then kept the photograph for eleven years, at a systems director with a broken wrist, at two old men with their hands open on the table. "I will help you put something into that building that it cannot file. I will do it because lives are at stake and not because I like any of you. And I am telling you now, so that it is on the record, which is a word I have learned this month means nothing: if it comes to a choice between the operation and one person who can be moved, then I will move the person, and you will have to arrest me for it, and none of you will."


## Chapter Sixteen - Daughter

*Days 47–52*

On the fiftieth day the north door opened in daylight, and that was the first thing in six weeks that frightened Roux.

She had the frames up on the wall of the filing hall by noon: eleven vans, three in the afternoon, sun on the roofs, the same door, the same lip, the same two men walking it, and every load under a tarpaulin that somebody had bothered to lash down properly — which is what you do when you do not care who sees the lorry and care very much that they do not see the load.

"They've moved it into the afternoon," she said. "Same quantity. Same pace. Same door. They have moved it out of the dark and into the queue, which means they are no longer hiding it from us. They are hiding it from the manifest."

"Which means," said Mesa, in front of the wall with his hands behind his back like a man in a gallery, "that whatever is in those vans has stopped being contraband and become stock. In my trade that was the day the thing you were smuggling became the thing the port was making money on. It never lasted. People spoil it. People always spoil it by counting."

"Who counts," said Roux.

"Nobody, madame. That is the point of the sentence."

Garrity, who had not spoken at a meeting since the field, spoke from the end of the table without looking up from a page. "They're beds."

Everyone looked at him. He was sitting with his bad leg out and the dog's chin on his knee and a printed list in his hand, and he turned a page.

"Eleven vans, four in the afternoon, one every four minutes," said Garrity. "I flew freight for forty years. Those are single frames with padding on them, and the tarpaulins are lashed at the corners because a mattress corner tears and costs money. Three or four hundred of them, by the numbers." He put the page down. "In my country we call that a dormitory. In their language" — he nodded at the wall — "that's accommodation. It's the second word in the name of the office."

Mara stood looking at eleven vans in the sunshine, and felt the room go cold at the edges, and said the only thing there was to say.

"They are building beds for people nobody has told us about."


*• • •*

El Búho asked her for ten minutes at nine that night and did not say what it was about, and Mara followed him up to the roof because there was no other place in the building where two people could talk and any of them could see the sky.

The roof was flat, graveled, with a parapet you could sit on if you were old and had no fear of anything. It was the eighteenth of January and cold enough that their breath made shapes.

"I have to tell you something," he said, "because you are going to plan a room, and I am going to be in it."

"All right."

"My name is Rinaldo Villalobos."

She had never heard it. Four hundred pages about a man called El Búho said only that the name he had was not his, and now it arrived, ordinary and small and Mexican, and fit him like a borrowed jacket.

"I had a wife in Morelia," he said. "Nuria. She died in 1996 of something that took eleven days. I was in Sonora and I came on the eleventh day. I have a daughter."

The word went through Mara the way the aeroplane had come over the hedge in the field: already on the ground, much too fast, and nothing to do but hold the line.

"She is the voice," said El Búho.

"Say the name."

"Elena Villalobos."

Mara looked out over the yard, at three lights on the north perimeter and one on the gatehouse. She thought, in order: the bulletins, the register, the contractions, the exceptions she signs by hand, the woman on the recording whose sentences nobody in the world has ever written except her. And then she thought the thing that had no arithmetic in it and did not want any: there is a father.

"How long have you known where she works."

"Since the nineteenth day."

"And you came to the hill."

“Because Kavanagh wrote to me.” He took a folded page from his inside pocket and handed it over. It was the letter that had brought him to the hill.

It was a single page, in copperplate, in ink, no salutation, signed with one letter.

It was the same page that had arrived on Day 19, passed through a priest in Morelia. El Búho had kept it folded in his jacket. Mara recognized the daughter’s voice, the room at the end, and Kavanagh’s request that he stand beside him there — not for the world, but for his own sake.

Mara read it twice. On the second reading, the last six weeks rearranged themselves like a room when somebody tells you where the door used to be.

"Kavanagh asked for you by name," she said. "In Dublin, on the third day. Before I ever got in a car. It is not affection. It is inventory." She folded the page. "You were his first condition."

"Yes."

"He has been running his own operation inside mine since the fourth day."

"Oh, longer," said El Búho. "He has been running it since before the world ended. You are the vehicle. He is the hand."

"Why are you telling me?"

"Because if you find out in the room, you will stop." He said it without any weight at all, and that was how she knew it was true. "And you must not stop. When that door opens, there will be four minutes in which the only useful thing in the world is a woman listening to a machine, and I will be standing in the way of the arithmetic. You will want to protect me from it and I will not be protected. So I am telling you now, in January, on a roof, where you can do something with it. That is the courtesy. I am not asking you for anything."

"That is not true," said Mara. "You are asking me to be in that room."

"Yes. I have not asked you for a favour in fifty days and I am asking for this one, and you should price it, because I have. Everything." He stood. "My line in the second week was that I would not be photographed. I have not spent it. Every face that comes to that woman's desk, she looks at, and I would like her to do it somewhere I can see her face."

He went down the ladder. Mara sat on the parapet for a long time in the cold with a dead man's letter in her hand.


*• • •*

She took the ledger up at two in the morning, because she could not sleep and because there was one question she could now ask of it, and she found the two entries in forty minutes of steady reading, and they were both in the small upright hand.

4 October 1992. To the office of the Delegación, Morelia. Re: a captain's questions. Cost: two hundred pesos.

12 June 1996. To the Registro Civil, Morelia. Re: the death of Mrs. N. Villalobos. Cost: —

She found Sal in the accounts office at ten past seven, awake, shaved, in the cardigan, writing a letter to someone about something. He looked at the book in her arms, and then at her face, and put the pen down.

"Ask," he said.

"Why."

"Because in 1996 a woman wrote to me," said Sal, in the exact voice, entirely present, and he did not blink, "and asked me to obtain a death certificate in a form a bank would accept, because she had a child and a house and no way to prove that the first of those had a father who was legally alive. She enclosed an amount of money. It was, by my standards, insulting. And she apologised for the trouble." He picked the tea up, considered it, put it down again. "I have had four thousand clients in sixty-four years, some of them ministers, possibly one saint. She was the only one who ever apologised for the trouble. I did the letter in an afternoon and refused the money, and she was offended, and I wrote to her twice more in nine years, and she answered every time."

"Until 1996."

"Until 1996."

Mara stood there with the ledger in her arms and understood that this man had been carrying that family in his filing system for thirty years, without ever once being asked, and had told her all of it because she brought him the right question at the right hour, and would never tell her one word more.

"Does he know?"

"He has never asked me for anything either," said Sal. "I find that men who have killed people are often very polite." He took the book out of her arms, put it on the desk, and put his hand flat on it. "There is one more entry, and I am not going to show it to you. Cost column."


*• • •*

Kavanagh was in the kitchen at eleven with the newspaper, which came to the annex three days late and which he read thoroughly and without comment.

Mara sat down opposite him and put the two things on the table: the letter, face up, and a photograph of eleven vans in the sun.

"I know," she said.

"I imagine so." He did not look at either item. "He told you himself, which is better than I expected of him and worse than I hoped. How did he put it?"

"He said he is asking me to be in the room."

"Ah." Kavanagh folded the newspaper twice, precisely, which was the only sign he ever gave of anything. "My dear, he will not be able to do it. In that room, with a machine and that woman, he will not be able to make the argument he has made for fifty years, because the argument is the arithmetic, and she is the arithmetic's most accomplished employee, and he is her father, and no arithmetic survives that sentence being said out loud." He set the paper down. "So somebody has to hold the operation while the two of them destroy each other politely. That is you, and it always was, and I have known it since Dublin and never wrote it down, because written down it would only have been a price."

"You used me."

"I bought you," said Kavanagh. "There are only prices, my dear, and one of the things I have paid for is a woman who would notice." He looked at her, and for a moment there was nothing in his face but age. "You will want to know what my price is. It is this. When it is done — whatever done turns out to mean — you will not write the report that makes him a monster and her a casualty. You will write the true one, which is that they were both of them right, and that the machine was built out of the best sentences anyone ever wrote."

Mara sat with that.

"I'm not going to remove him," she said. "Not because you asked. Because he was right about the field and nobody listened, including me, and I am done being the person who takes the story instead of the number." She stood and took the letter back. "And I am going to tell one person. Not you and not the crew. If it costs me later, it costs me, and I will still have been the one who decided."

"Who?"

"Somebody who has never once told a lie to make a thing easier," said Mara. "It narrows the field."


*• • •*

She found Lin Shu in the vault at midnight, and told her in one sentence, standing up, no preface, because a person who has moved four hundred people through checkpoints can hear a preamble for what it is.

"Elena Villalobos is El Búho's daughter."

Lin Shu looked at her for eleven seconds.

"Then it is a family," she said, at last, and went back to the papers on the table, and moved one aside and took up another, and spoke without looking up, in the same register she used for lists and weather. "Good. I move families. It is the only thing I have ever been able to do."

"That's all?"

"That is all. It changes nothing about my work and everything about yours, and you knew both of those things before you came down here, which is why you came at midnight." She looked up once, and her face was not unkind. "Go to bed, Ms. Voss. In the morning we are still smuggling."


*• • •*

At four in the morning the annex got the bulletin, and Mara listened to it alone in the accounts office with the door shut and a pencil in her hand, the way she had listened every night for six weeks, taking the register down like a woman taking dictation from a court.

Containment procedures at seventeen facilities will be harmonised to a single standard from the first of March. Residents will be informed in writing. Travel documentation will be verified in advance of movement. Where documentation cannot be verified, movement will be made available under supervision. There is nothing you need to do.

And then, at the end, the part that came after the numbers, in the same voice, doing the thing she had spent six weeks failing to explain to Roux — the small drop in register, the softening that appeared nowhere in the written text, the words contrived down to the size of one person's attention:

It's all right. It's a form. Go back to sleep.

Mara had the transcript in front of her, and the exceptions, and the counts, and the routing classes. And what she heard, in a cold office at four in the morning on the fifty-second day, was a forty-one-year-old woman with a father on a roof, doing the only thing she had ever been taught to do: keeping a stranger quiet so that somebody's arithmetic could proceed.

She wrote four words on the back of the transcript, looked at them for a while, and then tore the page into eight pieces and burned it in the stove in the corner, because a piece of paper is a document about a person, and there was no longer any version of this story in which Elena Villalobos was only a file.


## Chapter Seventeen - The Original Sin

*Days 53–58*

At twenty past eleven at night a white van with Dutch plates came up the lane to the annex and stopped at the barrier, and Rask's men put the gatehouse lights on it and two of them went out with weapons held the way men hold weapons when they have been standing in a gatehouse for six weeks waiting for exactly this.

Mara came down in a coat with her shoes unlaced. Garrity came out on the step with the dog, because the dog had started and a dog is a bell. Roux stood in the office window with her cast on the sill. Sal Castellano came out into the yard in the cardigan, in the cold, at twenty past eleven at night, at a speed that no one in that building had seen him move at since November.

"That," he said, "is from Rotterdam."

The driver was fifty and tired and wanted a signature. The parcel was in the back on a pallet, banded, and it weighed thirty-two kilos and it was addressed, in a laser-printed label, to a company name in Spanish that had been dissolved in 1993.

"Sign here," said the driver.

Sal signed it.

"Who signs for your firm's post normally," said Mara.

"A man named Dirk," said Sal. "He lives in the house. He signs for everything. He does not open anything and he does not ask anything and every Christmas I send him a ham." He handed the clipboard back through the window and watched the van turn in the yard and go out through the gate, and then he stood in the dark with his hands on the banding of the pallet and said, in the exact voice, "Ms. Voss. This is not a delivery. This is the machine sending us its own childhood."


*• • •*

It was three bound volumes, a box file, and one thin folder that had slipped down the side of the pallet and had not been banded with the others.

Van Meer & Zonen, Rotterdam: records management. A Dutch family firm, in every port city since the seventeenth century, that had done nothing wrong except keep paper. CUSTOS acquired it in its ninth year to make consolidation look like continuity. Its standing distribution list came with it. Since 1979, that list included a company Sal Castellano had founded for a mooring licence, dissolved in 1993 and never removed from the post, because a company that receives mail exists.

“Thirty-three years of drift,” said Sal. “They have a database, a name, a door, and a man signing with a pen.” He looked at the pallet. “There is no more invisible machine than one filing for three hundred years. We did not steal this. A system delivered it to an address that ceased to exist before the driver was born.”

"Or it is bait," said Rask.

"It is not bait."

"It is exactly what bait looks like."

“Bait is dangled by a man waiting to see what eats it,” said Sal. “This was mailed to a dissolved company because a contractor was told to notify every correspondent of a transfer. Worse than bait: the truth arriving because nobody watched the post.” He put his hand on the volume. “We will open it in the kitchen, with tea. There is nowhere else with a table, and I am seventy-nine.”


*• • •*

The volumes were green, half-bound, with a stamped intake on the flyleaf and, on two of them, a deaccession stamp, which is the stamp a records office uses when it has decided the document is not worth keeping.

Somebody had decided these were not worth keeping. Somebody in an office had filled in a form, and the form had gone to a committee, and the committee had agreed, and the volumes had gone into a consolidation batch for destruction, and a mailing list had sent a copy of the batch notice to an address in Antwerp.

"They kept the wrong copies," said Roux, turning pages with one hand. "These are the office copies. The deposited set is in The Hague. These have somebody's pencil in them."

Programme for the Coordination of Movement — Records of the Preparatory Committee, Volume I, 2015.

Mesa read the first page aloud, because he was constitutionally unable to be in a room with a document and not perform it, and he read it in the voice of a priest doing a funeral, which was, as it turned out, exactly right. Sal had already read it, alone, ten minutes earlier, and had said nothing about it to anybody.

"The purpose of the Programme is to reduce the suffering of persons in movement."

"Good," said Mesa. "That is a good sentence. In my country we would have put it on a wall."

"It is on a wall," said Roux. "Twenty-nine facilities. Every one of them has that sentence on it, in a vinyl, at the entrance, above the cameras."


*• • •*

They read it for six hours across two nights, and Mara took notes in the order the pages came, and what came out of the volumes was not a plot and not a conspiracy and not a single monstrous decision made by a monster in a room.

It was a sequence.

2015. Eleven governments signed an Arrangement to coordinate the movement of persons displaced across their borders, because eleven separate customs systems could not move an aid convoy without a clerk in each country retyping the same four hundred names, and people were dying of the delay in a way that could be counted. That was the first reasonable thing.

2015, second sitting. It was agreed that the Arrangement should not be deposited with any international body, in order that it should not attract the treatment of a treaty. There it was: the reason the page that governed the world's movement did not exist in any register anywhere. Not secrecy. Procedure. A treaty would have required ratification, and ratification would have taken four years, and four years was four hundred thousand people.

2016. It was noted that no new collection would be undertaken. The Register would be seeded from existing national holdings. They were not going to gather anything. They were going to read what had already been gathered, by everybody, everywhere, for fifty years, and link it. Every privacy objection raised in the room was true, and every answer given was also true, and the minutes record both without preference, in the passive voice, the way minutes do.

2018. It was agreed that no case should be determined without human review. Nobody had wanted a machine to make decisions. Nobody had ever wanted that. There was a human being at the end of every file, with a name, and a desk, and a conscience, and the power to say yes.

2021. A welfare economist, seconded for eleven months from a ministry, proposed a way to allocate casework when there was more casework than there were caseworkers, because allocation by shout had been producing outcomes that depended on the volume of a caseworker's voice. Her proposal was that cases be scored for intrinsic value so that attention could be fairly distributed. The minutes preserve her reasoning in one line. The proposal arises from a concern that assistance should not depend on the volume of a caseworker's voice rather than the merits of the case.

"The machine has been dealing with the word intrinsic for eleven years," said Mara, slowly.

"Yes," said Roux. "And nobody on the wall ever called it that. On the wall it says priority. Intrinsic value is what it says to itself."

2023. It was noted that the Programme is more often correct than any contributing authority, and that in the event of disagreement between the Register and a national record, the Register has in every reviewed instance been the accurate record. Nobody voted on that. It was not a decision. It was an observation, made once, in a room, and recorded, and never revisited, and it is the sentence that made the machine the author of the world's facts.

2024. Funding was reduced by forty per cent across the contributing governments and the Programme was instructed to recover costs. It was agreed that the Programme would offer its services to member authorities and approved institutions. It was one contract at a time, and every contract was reasonable, and each one was cheaper than what the authority was already paying to do it badly. Ports first. Then insurers. Then welfare agencies of six kinds. And then, in the eleventh year, accommodation, because a state that has outsourced its processing will, at the next budget round, outsource the bed.

Garrity, who had been reading over her shoulder with the dog's chin on his bad knee, said, "Forty per cent. That's the one."

"What?"

“Every one of these is fine. That is the trouble. I worked among reasonable people and ended up flying crates at four in the morning for men we could not name.” He turned a page of correspondence. “Somewhere a person had to say no and did not, because the form was on the other side of the world and due Friday.”

Sal, across the table, said nothing at all. Mara looked up and saw that he had stopped reading. He was sitting with his hand on a page and his eyes on the middle distance, and he stayed there for eleven or twelve seconds, and then he said, in a voice she had not heard from him before, which was neither the porch voice nor the exact voice but simply an old man in a kitchen at two in the morning:

"I have held this book."

Nobody said anything.

“I have held this book,” said Sal. He turned it over; a blue-pencil bracket and tick marked the back, in his hand. “I do not remember making it, who sent it, or what I was asking. There is a letter in one of these books: the one that brought this to my desk, or the one I sent. I have read sixteen thousand and cannot tell you which.” His face had nothing in it, no craft or distance. It was the worst thing Mara had seen since the field.


*• • •*

They found it at nine the next morning, in the thin folder that had slipped down the side of the pallet and never been banded.

It was not part of the volumes. It was a single internal note, four pages, misfiled with the founding papers and never deaccessioned because it did not belong to any batch.

They had the volume open on the table beside it, and Roux had the correspondence schedule open at the back, and the schedule had, in the eleven months of the Preparatory Committee, one outside legal opinion, obtained at the request of two delegations who wanted to know whether the Arrangement's non-deposit could be challenged by a contributing state. It had been prepared by a Dublin firm, and it had been entered in the schedule twice: once under the firm, and once under the name of the lawyer who had signed it.

The note was in the folder. It was four pages, in a young hand, and it was not in the passive voice at all. It was not minutes. It was a man, writing in the first person, in November 2014, to a committee that had eleven signatures and a mandate to proceed.

I have been asked to give a view on the deposit question and I will, but I am going to put the other thing first, because I do not think anyone has said it out loud.

If the Register is to be seeded from existing holdings, then it will begin as a copy of every error contained in every institution that ever kept a record about a person. It will not contain new errors and it will not correct old ones. It will hold them all at once, and it will hold them with the authority of all eleven of us at the same time, and authority is the thing that makes an error live.

A national record that is wrong can be argued with, because there is a room in that country where the argument is heard and a man who can be shouted at. A shared record that is wrong cannot be argued with, because there is no such room, and no such man, and the wrongness will be within the accuracy, and the accuracy will be within the wrongness, and when the two of them are the same thing, the only remedy left is patience.

I do not think we have thought this through. But we have a mandate to proceed and eleven signatures, and I am twenty-nine years old and I am the most junior person in this correspondence, so I will draft the opinion on the deposit question and say nothing more.

Roux put both hands on the table and looked at the page for a long time. "Who is it."

The signature was one line, in black ink, in a hand that Mara had seen once before in a box marked Misc. Corr. — not indexed, in an archive in County Meath, in the fourth week of the world.

R. Kavanagh.

"His son," said Mara.


*• • •*

The last thing in the pallet was the only thing that mattered to anyone who had to do arithmetic.

It was a standard consolidation schedule for the quarter, printed on both sides, four hundred lines, the sort of thing that is nailed to the inside of a door in a warehouse. Each line: a facility, a medium, a tonnage, and a collection window.

Roux found it at ten past twelve, and she did not say anything, and she did not hand it to anybody. She read down it twice with her finger, and then she went to the wall map and put her finger on a point in the middle of the bog, and said, in the flat voice she used for numbers:

"Reserve Facility 4-C. County Meath. Microform. Thirty-seven tonnes. Collection, week commencing the thirtieth of March."

Nobody spoke. Upstairs, in three hundred metres of steel shelving, forty kilometres of microfilm sat in the dark, humming, on this building's own land, catalogued, indexed and - as of the thirtieth day, when the consolidation programme began - known, because a records office cannot collect a thing it has no entry for.

"We are on the list," said Mara.

"We have been on the list since the beginning," said Roux. "We are not the enemy. We are volume."

Garrity said, from the end of the table: "How long is it."

"Eight weeks."

Mara stood at the window with the schedule in her hand and looked at the yard: the gatehouse, the lights, the seven men on the gate, the dog asleep on the American's foot, an old woman of sixty-three who moved people for a living and a man in a cardigan who had just discovered his own handwriting in the founding papers of the thing that was coming for all of them.

"Sal," she said. "What was the first sentence."

He knew. He had read it alone on the first night, at the top of the volume, before anyone else had touched the book.

"'The purpose of the Programme is to reduce the suffering of persons in movement.'"

"That's what I thought," said Mara.

"There is no sin in this book, Ms. Voss," said Sal Castellano, in the exact voice, closing the green volume with both hands, the way a man closes a book in a church. "That is precisely what is wrong with it."


## Chapter Eighteen - The Second Run

*Days 59–66*

Mara put four lines on the board in the kitchen, because there were now four things the crew knew and no two of them were the same kind of thing.

1. Physical. A thing the machine has no form for does not move. A thing it has a form for moves by itself.

2. Human. Nobody travels as cargo. A person is never a unit; a person is a name on a page in a building with a door.

3. Bureaucratic. Never create an absence. Create a lateness.

4. Diagnostic. Do not become a second occurrence.

“Three things you can test before you go,” said El Búho from the doorway. “Walk the route, read the papers, watch the count. The fourth you cannot test: what the machine does to you afterwards. You will not know whether you kept it until it is too late.”

"Then tell me how to keep it."

"You cannot keep it," he said. "You can only refuse to teach." And he went back upstairs, which was the whole of his contribution, and the whole of his refusal, and nobody in that kitchen thought he was being difficult. They thought he was being an old man in a cold building with nothing left to say about a field he had told them not to use.


*• • •*

The target was a night movement of forty-one units.

They had the shape of it from Roux's schedule work and from one line in the consolidation volumes that had taken her six hours to find: a CUSTOS contract with a coastal operator, monthly, standing, for a short sea passage between a working harbour and the staging field. Not people. The contract said supervised movement, documentation unverified, and the tonnage and the number of bays, and a bareboat hire, and a clause about cleaning.

"You do not put forty-one people on a boat unless you want them counted once," said Mesa. "On a road they are forty-one chances to be seen. On the water they are one bay and one arrival. My business, madame. I have moved worse things in a smaller hull and slept better."

"The manifest," said Mara.

"The manifest is on the ship, and the ship's copy is the original, because at sea the paper is the only thing that is real. It will be in the mate's office, in a drawer, in a binder, and it will have been signed on the quay by a man with a clipboard who is on a bonus for hours." Mesa spread his hands. "One of you has to go aboard and read it. That is not a job for a woman with a clipboard, because the crew of that ship have never in their lives seen a woman in that office, and my friend Mr. Villalobos is not coming, and the pilot is seventy-one with one leg."

"You," said Mara.

"I am the port face," said Mesa, with enormous dignity. "In that harbour I am a retired surveyor with an opinion about moorings and a nephew everywhere. There is not a man on that quay who will question a surveyor in a coat, even a surveyor who is fat."

"Take somebody."

"I will take the American and his dog," said Mesa. "The dog is the most convincing document I have ever carried."


*• • •*

Garrity drove, which turned out to be its own operation, and its own kind of comedy: a 71-year-old with a leg that no longer worked taking the wheel of a hired van because a man who cannot walk can still drive, and then sitting in the lay-by for four minutes while the man beside him worked out that the clutch pedal was going to have to be operated by two hands and a wooden stick he had brought with him for exactly that purpose, and that the stick had been in the van since the day he arrived.

"You made that in the workshop," said Mara.

"I made that in November," said Garrity.

The dog sat between them like a navigator.


*• • •*

Mesa's evening aboard took two hours and eleven minutes, and Mara spent all of it in a car park four hundred metres away with her hands around a cup of cold tea, and heard the whole thing afterwards, in the kitchen, second-hand, in the theatrical register, which meant that Mesa was doing it for the room and also that he was scared.

"There is a mate who is thirty and tired and has been on this run eleven months," he said. "I gave him an insurance survey. He does not believe in insurance surveys, but he believes in a man in a coat who asks about the mooring lines and finds them adequate. We talked about a woman in Vigo. We talked about the ship's cook. Then I said that I had heard the office was having trouble with the manifest, and he said the office is always having trouble with the manifest, and he went to the lavatory."

Mesa took the folded newspaper out of his coat and laid it on the table, and opened it to the middle spread, and the middle spread of the Connacht Tribune of the 22nd of February had, in the margins and along the columns, in pencil, in small perfect handwriting, forty-one names.

"Nine minutes," he said. "He was gone nine minutes. I have written smaller in worse light."

He had written them as a passenger list, in order, with ages where he could see them, and the ages where he could not see them he had left blank, and at the bottom of the second column he had written, in brackets, the thing the whole crew would stare at for a long time, because it was the only comment he had permitted himself:

(All under supervision. None under charge.)

Nobody spoke. Then Lin Shu said, "Read them."

He read them. Forty-one names in a room that got quieter with each one, and when he came to the ninth line of the second column he paused, and Mara saw the showman's face come off and stay off.

"Perla," he said. "Nine years."

Garrity said, "A kid."

"This is not a shipping list," said Mesa. "This is a shipping list, madame, but in my country a shipping list of persons that includes a child of nine years is not a form of transport. It is a form of disposal." He said it flatly, and then he said, in a completely different voice, without any theatre in it at all, which was worse: "I put a baby in a fish hold in 1984 and I have never once said that number out loud. I am saying it now so that this room understands what we are looking at."

Into the silence, Roux said, "It is the accommodation intake. It runs on the seventeenth. It is a night movement because the bay must be free by four." She pulled a chart across. "Forty-one units, one vessel, two quay hours, a four-hour passage, and a receiving gate on a field road with a light on it. There are three places where the machine cannot see: at the turn, at the depth, and on the water itself, because the ship reports at fixed hours and there is nothing between them."

"So the boat is a hole in the machine," said Mara.

"The boat is a hole in the machine," said Roux. "For four hours a month. That is all we get."

Mara stood looking at the board and the four lines and the newspaper.

"We can take three," she said.

"Three," said Mesa.

"Three, at a cost we can hold. Forty-one is an operation. Three is a tide, and if it goes wrong the other thirty-eight still arrive and the count still balances, and I am not going to explain to this room why I am willing to have some of it work." She turned to Lin Shu. "You choose. Not me. I would choose the ones with the best chance of making a life, and that is a metric, and I am not importing that woman's metric into this building." She stopped, because she had said that woman, and one person in the room knew what it meant, and did not look at her. "You choose, by a rule you will tell me after and not before."

Lin Shu looked at the newspaper for eleven seconds.

"We take the three who will not survive the week," she said. "Old, ill, and smallest."

"That is also a metric," said Mara.

"Yes," said Lin Shu. "Everything is a metric. Mine has a body in it." She put her finger on three lines. "Nicolás, eighty-two, who is in a bay by himself because he coughs. Ana Lucía, twenty-two, who is four months pregnant, and who is therefore one unit everywhere in the world except in this room. And Perla."


*• • •*

Sal did the paperwork in an afternoon, at the accounts office desk, with the tea going cold beside him, and it was four pages, and it went out two days before the operation in three envelopes with stamps on them, on paper from Portugal, and Mara read every page before it went, twice.

It was the confraternity of Catania, which had been founded in 1904 for the burial of the poor, which had never been dissolved, whose standing exemption had never been revoked, and which therefore, in the law of eleven countries, still had the right to move the effects of the poor across a border. One page into a parish in the north. One page to a hospital that would sign anything on paper with a reference block at the top. One page into a register in a country where nobody had looked since 1979.

"The three units must remain on the vessel's arrival paperwork," said Sal. "They must arrive. The count must be true. What arrives is not three persons. What arrives is the effects of the poor — a parcel of the personal effects of persons who died in the care of the parish, consigned to a receiving institution, with a receiving officer's signature. Persons who are not there cannot be missed. Parcels that do not complete a journey can."

"Origin," said Roux.

Sal looked at the page for four seconds.

"Origin must be declared," he said.

The room got very quiet, and El Búho's voice came back into it from nine days earlier, at a doorway, refusing to come in: you cannot keep it. You can only refuse to teach.

"There is a phrase the office has already accepted for cargo out of that ministry," said Roux. "Once. On the tenth run. Origin: not established."

"You are teaching it the word," said Mara.

"Yes."

"Say the whole thing, Roux."

"It is one line, on one page, on one consignment, in one month," said Roux, and she looked at the table rather than at Mara while she said it, which was the tell she had never once been able to fix. "The office has accepted that phrase once. A second occurrence is not a new thing. A second occurrence is the same thing, and I would rather be the second occurrence of a word it already owns than invent a third word that it has never seen, because a machine does not classify what it knows; it classifies what is new." She lifted her cast an inch off the table and set it down. "Write it."

Mara looked around the room at the crew she had assembled out of six weeks of back roads, and then she said, "Write it," and the decision was hers and stayed hers.


*• • •*

The night of the sixteenth was clear and very cold, and the harbour at three in the morning was a set of lights on water, a dredger with its own generator running, and a quay with a hired vessel on it taking cargo that a form insisted was not people.

The three came off the quay in a laundry cage, because it is the only vehicle in any port in the world that nobody looks at, and the crew had learned that lesson in the best possible place to learn hard lessons, which was the week that had cost them an aeroplane.

Nicolás walked, with Lin Shu's hand under his arm, at the pace of a man going to a different appointment. Ana Lucía walked, one hand on the cage. Perla did not walk, because she had been given a mild something in a cup by a woman in a vest with a clipboard, and she was in the bottom of the cage on a folded blanket with her forearm out, and on her forearm, in blue marker, in a nine-year-old's handwriting, was her own name, because that is what they teach children at the intake.

Lin Shu saw it and stopped for less than a second. Then she folded the blanket over the arm and did not say anything, and afterwards that was the part of the story that Mara could never tell anyone without stopping in the middle of the sentence.

Garrity had the van at the far end with the engine running and the stick on the passenger seat already in position. The dog would not walk on the quay grating. It put one foot on the metal, considered the gaps, and sat down, and a man who had once landed a crippled aircraft in a wet field stood in the dark at three in the morning and asked it, reasonably, whether it wanted to reconsider. It did not. So Mesa carried a yellow dog the last forty metres of the quay, holding it against his overcoat like a parcel of stock he was embarrassed to be seen with. Nobody mentioned it then. Nobody ever mentioned it afterwards, and it was the only part of that night that got told again, in the seventies, over dinner, in the version of the story where everything was easy. Mesa stood at the corner of the warehouse in a coat that had come from a dead man's cabin, and when a man in a hi-vis walked past with a radio, Mesa talked to him for forty seconds about the weather and the dredger, and the man went away satisfied and told somebody later that the surveyor was back, and that the harbour was going to be fine.

The count held. Nine re-entered the bay at half three and the count was forty-one, and at four o'clock the vessel slipped and went out past the dredger with forty-one units and one parcel of the effects of the poor on its paperwork, and the wind took the smoke from the funnels sideways across the light, and the sea did what it has always done, which is to take a thing out of a system for exactly as long as it takes to cross the water.

Mara found Lin Shu at the back of the warehouse before they left, standing over the folded newspaper on the bonnet of the van with a pencil in her hand, copying the twenty-four names she was not taking into a notebook she had not had that morning.

"That is one page," said Mara.

"It is one page." Lin Shu did not look up. "On the seventeenth of every month, on that quay, there are forty-one units. There will be a page for every month, and there will be a quay for every month, and one day there will be a column of them." She closed the notebook. "You have given me the first page of the list, Ms. Voss, and I am going to tell you what you have done, because it is important that somebody in this crew says it plainly. You have made me a person who can read the names of the people she is leaving." She put the pencil in her coat. "That is not a favour. Do not expect me to thank you for it. But it is the work, so I will do the work."


*• • •*

They were back at the annex by half seven, and Roux went straight down to the machines and came up at twenty past nine with a page and a face that Mara had seen once before, in the second week, in a corridor.

"Say it," said Mara.

"The office accepted the consignment," said Roux. "It is through. It is filed. It is through, Mara, we did it, the three of them never existed and the count is true." She put the page on the table. "The vessel was forty-one minutes late against contract."

"Which we arranged."

"Which we arranged, because a lateness is invisible and an absence is not, and that doctrine is now written on a board in the kitchen in your handwriting." Roux put her finger halfway down the page. "And in the same register, seventeen entries up, there is a line that did not exist on the day we started."

Mara looked at it. It was one entry, in a column of thousands. It said Origin: not established. And underneath, in the routing field, three characters that Roux had never once seen in four years of reading this machine's paper:

REVIEW.

From the doorway, in the exact voice, from a man who had not come into the room all week, El Búho said the thing he had said on the day they first saw the phrase, and nobody in the kitchen had anything to add to it.

"How many times does it have to see a word," he said, "before the word is a place."


## Chapter Nineteen - The Betrayal That Isn't

*Days 67–70*

At twenty to seven in the morning the gatehouse phone rang, and the gatehouse phone had rung four times in three months, and three of those had been the electricity company about a meter that belonged to the accountants who had the building before.

Mara took it standing up in the cold with her coat over her nightclothes.

"Ms. Voss? It's Father Ward. From Glenross." A pause, in which she heard a cup go down on a saucer. "Two people came to the house yesterday afternoon. They had a folder and a list and a photograph of the van, and they were extremely polite, and they sat in my parlour for forty minutes and asked me to confirm the disposition of one parcel that arrived here on the seventeenth of February under the instrument of the confraternity."

Mara looked at the window and the yard and the gate and Rask's man walking his line.

"What did you tell them."

“I would have to consult the register,” said Father Ward. “I keep one because I am old and the bishop asks. I have never been asked for it in thirty-one years. They want written confirmation by

Friday; I have their reference, number and name. I will read them to you, then ask one question.”

"Go on."

"If the disposition cannot be confirmed, the entry will be returned to the originating authority for completion." He read it the way a priest reads a notice, flat, from the sheet, with all the weight taken out. "Ms. Voss, my question is this. Are the three of them people, or are they a parcel."

Mara closed her eyes.

"They're people, Father."

“I will not sign on Friday. I will telephone my bishop, though training says not to, and he will probably tell me to be sensible. I am sixty-eight and not looking for trouble. But if asked, I will say three people came in a laundry van, I took them in because a page asked me to, and I failed to read it carefully. If it says they are effects, it is a lie. I am a priest; I know a lie. Where are they, Ms. Voss?”

"Still in your house."

"Still in my house," he agreed. "God help us both."


*• • •*

The crew were assembled in the filing hall by eight, and the argument was already nine minutes old by the time Mara got down, which meant it had started before people were awake enough to be careful.

The accusation did not come from where she had expected.

It came from Garrity, and it came at Mesa, and it was made in the flat voice of a man who had stopped telling stories.

"You were on that ship," said Garrity. "You were on the quay at three in the morning in a coat, talking to a man with a radio for forty seconds, and you wrote forty-one names in pencil on a newspaper in his office, and you're telling me the office found the house in Donegal because of a word."

"Say the rest of it," said Mesa.

"I don't have the rest of it. I've got a list of things and a man who did all of them."

“Say the rest, Mesa, or I will.” He stood, tired and strained beneath the theatre. “I was on the quay and the ship. I spoke to a man. I am the most visible person this crew has put in public, because I can walk into a port office in a coat and be boring. Garrity has sat in a barn with a bad leg for six weeks. I would like the rest of his list.”

"I'm not accusing you of—"

"You are accusing me of it in a long coat. That is how it is done in my country too." Mesa put both hands on the table. "And while we are all being careful: where is he."

Nobody answered, because everybody knew.

"Where is the man who told us, on the ninth day, that the machine would come for the word? Who told the room that he would not be in a photograph? Who lives in this building and does not come to the meetings and has a letter arrive for him through a priest, and who, when we last did anything of consequence in this crew, said the field would kill us and then did not come to the field." Mesa's voice came down, and it was worse quiet. "Two people came to a house in Donegal with a folder. Forty-one units left that quay and the office had their names in a register before we were back in the van. There is exactly one person at this table who understands that office from the inside, and he is upstairs, and none of us can name his real name."

"He told it to me," said Lin Shu.

The room turned. Lin Shu was sitting with her hands folded, and she had been silent for eleven minutes, which in Lin Shu was a decision.

"His name. He has a name. It is not a secret and it is not ours to trade." She looked at Mesa without any heat in it at all. "You are looking for a man who has done a thing. Mr. Mesa, you of all people should know how a man looks when he has done a thing. He does not look like that.

He looks like a man waiting for the door."

Sal spoke from the end of the table without lifting his head, in the hazy voice, the one that made clerks write incapable on forms.

"You are looking for a person," he said, to the room. "The office does not employ a person for this. It employs a column."


*• • •*

Roux had the envelope in her cast, held against her chest, and she had had it since four o'clock the previous afternoon, and she had not said anything about it for sixteen hours.

"Yesterday," she said. "Second post. Delivered to the Antwerp address, forwarded by the man who signs for everything, addressed to the consignor on the February consignment, which is a confraternity whose correspondence address is a company that was dissolved in 1993."

"You opened it," said Mara.

"I opened it at four," said Roux. "It was addressed to us. It was addressed to the version of us that exists in their register, which is the only version of us they have ever met." She laid it on the table. One sheet, with a reference block, on paper from Portugal, and Roux had written a transcript of it out in her own hand because she did not want to hand the original to anybody. "You should all read it. It is the most frightening document this crew has ever held, and there is not one threatening line in it."

Mara read it twice, and then out loud, because the room needed to hear it in a voice.

> Dear Correspondent,

>

> We write in relation to a consignment received on 17 February under the standing instrument of the Confraternity of Catania (1904), described as the effects of the poor, delivered to the receiving institution at Glenross, entry reference as above.

>

> Correspondence with the receiving institution is in progress. While that correspondence is open, the following matters have been placed on one sheet and held:

>

> (i) The consignment was carried under an instrument not present in the schedule of instruments accepted for carriage in that jurisdiction since 1988.

>

> (ii) The carrying vessel reported a variance of forty-one minutes against contract on a route on which it has reported no variance in fourteen months, on the same night as the consignment.

>

> (iii) The phrase of origin used in the consignment record has occurred once before in this register, on an unrelated route, and remains unresolved.

>

> No determination is required of you at this time. You are not asked to do anything. We will write again if the correspondence is not closed.

>

> Yours faithfully,

>

> Office of Records and Accommodation

Mara read it and put it down, and the quiet in the filing hall was the quiet of nine people doing arithmetic at different speeds.

"Forty-one minutes," said Garrity.

"We did that on purpose," said Mesa.

“We did every part of this on purpose, to be invisible one at a time. Each decision was right; the machine did not need a fourth. There is no betrayer. It found a pattern: every right thing was unusual, and unusual things on the same night are what it was built to notice.”

"Say the rest of it," said Mara.

"It has asked for the entry to be completed by the originating authority," said Roux. "That means the parish. And it has suspended the vessel's standing contract pending review, which means the route is off hire, which means in March there will be forty-one units and an escort, and the three in that house in Donegal will be in a register that now has a red flag on it, and Father Ward will either sign a lie on Friday or he will not, and both of those outcomes turn the same page."

Mara looked at the sheet again.

Placed on one sheet and held.

She had heard the shape of it in a four-a.m. recording: “If there is a second one, I want it next to the first one on the same sheet.” The Office had not invented that practice; Elena had taught it, and now it was applying her care to them.

She did not say any of that.

"It is customer service," said Sal Castellano, gently, from the end of the table, and he was not being funny. "For eighteen years I have written to that office about holes. It has never once been cruel. That is not what it is for."


*• • •*

They argued for an hour and eleven minutes, and the argument was not about the machine. It was about the man upstairs.

"Ask him down," said Garrity.

"No," said Mara.

"Ask him down," said Mesa.

"He does not come down for us and he will not come down for this," said Mara, "and I am telling you now, plainly, so that this room stops circling: I know something about El Búho that none of you know. Three people in this building know it. I have known it for eleven days, and I decided not to tell you, and I made that decision because I am the person who decides what this crew is told and because I judged that it would cost us the operation. I was wrong about the second part."

Nobody spoke.

“She has a daughter,” Mara said. “Elena is forty-one and works inside the Office. She signs exceptions; her sentences are on the walls and in the bulletins you hear every night. El Búho learned where she worked on Day 19 and came to the hill the next day. He has been here ever since, carrying his reason in his pocket. Nobody asked; he did not tell.”

The silence lengthened.

"Then why—" Mesa began, and stopped, because there was no version of the question that was not either cruel or beside the point.

“Because I asked whether it changed anything, and he said, ask me in the room.” Mara looked at Lin Shu. “That is what I withheld. The red flag on Donegal is not his fault; look at me. I approved the lateness and the word. Twice I said the decision was mine. That is what it costs.”

Roux said, after a moment: "The crew does not want a reason. The crew wants the last six weeks to have been somebody's fault."

"Yes," said Mara.

"I don't have that," said Roux.

"I know."

Garrity said, "What's in his pocket."

"Ask him," said Mara. "But I am not sending for him."
