The door at the top of the stairs opened, and El Búho came down. He came down slowly, and stood at the bottom, and did not come further into the room, and he addressed himself to Mesa, and only to Mesa, because the two of them had been not-quite-enemies since 1988 and that was the only language either of them trusted.

“Nothing in my pocket belongs to this crew,” said El Búho. “The man who copied Mesa’s list is why eighty-one people were in a book once and are now in one twice. I have not been useful for eleven days. I have been here. Those are different things, and only one is a hiding place.”

He went back up.

Mesa sat down, and did not say anything for a while, and then said, "He is still the most irritating man I have ever known," which in that room, at that hour, was a surrender.


*• • •*

It was Lin Shu who put the last question of the day.

She asked it standing up, at the end of the table, with the letter in her hand and the register of names in her coat, and she asked it the way she asked everything, which was in the voice of a woman who had already decided that she would be the one to do it if nobody else would.

“Father Ward will not sign. On Friday the entry returns to the Office. Three people in Donegal are in the column, the van and route are in the file, and a man keeps their register. We have seventy-two hours.” Mara looked around the room. “The document that put them there is ours — on Portuguese paper, with my friend’s signature.”

She put the question into the room and left it there.

"Who is going back."

Nine people in a filing hall. A ventilation stack moving air. Forty kilometres of microfilm humming in the dark overhead, three weeks from a collection window that nobody in the room had mentioned in days.

Nobody answered.


## Chapter Twenty - The Price

*Days 71–74*

They spent two days deciding, and the deciding was the worst thing Mara had done in her life, because it was not done in a room with a map. It was done in a kitchen, in pieces, by people who were each having a slightly different conversation.

She put the question properly on the seventy-first day, standing up, with Father Ward's Friday on the table.

"Two vehicles, one night, seventy-two hours, three people and a priest who will not lie for us. I am going to ask for something and I want to hear the refusals in the order people are willing to say them. Who goes."

Garrity went first, which she had expected.

"I'll drive," he said.

"You cannot work the clutch with one man," said Lin Shu.

"I worked it in February."

“You did it in February on a flat road, with two hands and a stick, and nobody behind you. This is a farm track at night, with a nine-year-old in the back.” Lin Shu read the facts. “You drive the second van on the main road with the dog. That is the job; it is not smaller.”

Garrity looked at her for a long time and then said, "Fine," and it was the shortest he had been since he arrived, and after that he sat with his hands on his knees and did not speak for an hour, and Mara understood that they had just taken the last thing away from him and that he had known, when he said fine, that it was the last thing.

Sal offered next, which she had not expected.

“I go,” he said. “Not for the house. I will be the older brother of a man who died in Naas, in a cardigan behind the door while the priest is out. A man with a folder will find a whole conversation, and I am the best in the world at being one.”

"No," said Mara.

"Why. Because I am—"

“You lost four seconds in the address block on Tuesday,” said Mara. “It happens once or twice a week; last week it happened twice in one morning. If it happens behind that door on Friday, we go to prison and they go somewhere worse. You told me a record with an error is a hole. I will not put you in the door.”

Sal sat with his hands on his knees and did not argue. It was the first time anyone had said aloud what they had all seen. Ten minutes later he was inventorying supplies; an old professional needs a task before he decides he is finished.

Kavanagh offered three things, one of which was a car.

"A Mercedes with Dublin plates, a name on the insurance which is not mine and is not false, and a driver who would die before he told anybody anything," he said. "And I will not go into that county and I will not stand in that house. I am the single most identifiable man in Ireland, because I spent forty years making certain that I was, and I am not going to pretend to be brave at this end of my life." He put his cup down. "I am the paper. I am the only thing I have ever been good for."

"Agreed," said Mara.

"Thank you." He looked at the table. "I would like it noted, in a room, that I offered."

Roux offered, and Mara refused her, and Roux said, "I know," before Mara had finished the sentence, and then went and did the only useful thing, which was to build the whole route as three timelines with tolerances, and to put the timings on a sheet in the kitchen in the same format the office used, which was a joke, and which was not a joke.

Then El Búho spoke from the doorway, and he had been standing there for four minutes.

"Nobody has volunteered," he said.

"The American—"

“The American offered to drive and was told the truth about his leg; the Monsignor offered to be a door and was told the truth about his memory. The Irishman offered his car, which is not a body. The woman offered and you refused her because she is your best route.” El Búho stepped closer. “Someone will have to stand in front of the machine. You will ask who, and you do not know it yet.”

Nobody moved. He had said it to Mara on a road in December in exactly those words, and she had written them in her notebook, and she had not looked at them since.

"When we get to that part," said El Búho, "everyone in this building will be standing one step back, and every one of them will tell you, sincerely, with reasons, why it should be the other fellow. That is not cowardice. That is arithmetic. I did it for forty years and I was very good at it." He turned to go up. "I am not coming. That is my third refusal and it is the true one."

Mesa laughed, out loud, with his head back, and he was the only man in the room enjoying himself.

“Mi amigo, you have described my life from a doorway in a cardigan.” Mesa stood. “I go. My face is in that port; when they ask who was aboard on the seventeenth, they will describe a fat surveyor with opinions about moorings. I will not sit in Ireland while someone else walks into Donegal.” He straightened his coat. “I will do the door. I enjoy being looked at more than he does.”

Mara looked at him for a long moment, and then she said the thing she had been dreading having to say, because she was going to have to say it to someone.

"Thank you."

"You are welcome," said Mesa. "Say it again in the report."


*• • •*

The house was four hundred metres off the road at the end of a track with a dip in it, and the priest had cut the hedge at the dip in 1994 so that a tractor could get through, which is the kind of fact an operation either has or does not have.

They did it on the Thursday night. The timing was Roux's and it was beautiful: a two-hour window between the last bus on the main road and the first delivery van of the morning, six hundred and fifty metres of track, no headlights within four hundred metres of the house, and a second vehicle in a church car park nine minutes away with the American in it and the dog on the passenger seat.

Nicolás came out on Lin Shu's arm, coughing, apologising, hatless. Ana Lucía came out carrying Perla, who was asleep with her mouth open and her arm inside a coat sleeve, and who never woke and never made a sound, because a child who has been taught to be quiet for a machine sleeps through a thing that ought to wake anybody.

Mesa stood at the gate and talked for eleven minutes to a farmer who had come up the lane on a quad looking for a heifer. He used an accent that was not his and a voice that was. The man went away laughing; the road was clear, and the van went up the track with three people inside and the priest holding a lamp in his doorway.

Father Ward had given them the register page — torn out, folded twice, his own hand — and when Mara tried to say something about it he had said only, "I will not sign a lie. I will also not keep a list of the people you moved. Those are two different problems and I have decided to be able to sleep." Then he had looked at Mesa and said, "You are staying behind."

"I am staying for the morning," said Mesa. "It is a formality. There is a woman with a folder and it is not going to be an event."

"There is no such thing as a formality," said Father Ward, and went inside, and that was the last thing anybody from the annex ever heard him say.


*• • •*

At nineteen minutes past eight the next morning, a clean white van with a livery on it that said ACCOMMODATION SERVICES — WHERE RECORDED, SUPPORTED came up the lane and stopped at the gate, and two people got out, one with a clipboard and one with a camera, and the one with the clipboard was a woman of about forty in a good coat who knocked politely and stood back from the door.

Mesa opened it. He was wearing the cardigan — Sal's cardigan, borrowed, because a man in a borrowed cardigan is a man who is not from anywhere — and he was, by his own later account, at the top of his form.

Mara heard all of it afterwards from the priest, twice, on two separate occasions, in the same words, which is how she knew they were true.

She never raised her voice and she was not unkind. She explained the purpose of the visit, which was to confirm the disposition of an entry, and she had a form, and she said the phrase under supervision four times, and each time it landed like a hand on a shoulder. Mesa, in the borrowed cardigan, told her about a brother who had died in Naas and a charitable instrument that had been in the family since the confraternity was founded, and he was funny, and he was sad, and he made her smile twice.

And then she asked to complete the record.

"It is a formality," she said. "I have to have the premises, the respondent, and the instrument. That is all a visit is."

"It is a formality," Mesa agreed.

In the four minutes that followed, he could have ended it: a door to close, a man to be, a track and a nine-minute drive. Mara never knew whether he missed the moment or saw it and stayed. Perhaps both: a man who has performed for fifty years does not walk off in the middle of applause, even when it is a woman asking to photograph him for the file.

The camera came up. He put his hand on the door frame and stood the way he always stood.

Then a second vehicle came up the lane at twenty to nine, dark, three men and a folder, and the woman with the clipboard said something to the leader of them that Mara was never able to get exactly, only its shape, which was: the entry cannot be completed in the field.

They did not put hands on him. That was the part the priest kept coming back to. Nobody touched him. A man opened a car door and stood beside it like a chauffeur, and another man said, "We would ask you to complete the record in person," and Rafael Mesa, sixty-nine years old, of Veracruz, looked at the two people, and at the clean white van with the livery on it, and at the honest priest standing on his own steps, and he did the only thing he had been able to do since he was twenty-two, which was to perform the moment, and he said:

"I will not be rescued."

And he got into the car.

"He said it as a courtesy," said Father Ward, in the kitchen at the annex, two days later, holding a cup he did not drink. "I have been a priest for forty-one years. I know what a courtesy sounds like and I know what a request sounds like. It was a courtesy. It was also a request. And he knew both of those things when he said them, and so did I."


## Chapter Twenty-One - Martial Law

*Days 77–85*

The bulletin changed on the seventy-seventh day, and it did not change by becoming louder.

From the first of March, supervision of movement for persons whose documentation cannot be verified will be scheduled in advance rather than arranged locally.

Scheduled supervision will be accommodated at a designated facility for the duration of verification. Verification periods are set out in the schedule attached to this notice.

Persons under supervision will be recorded as residents. A resident will be recorded as resolved when supervision is complete.

There is nothing you need to do.

Mara read it twice, standing in the kitchen with her coat still on, and then she read it out to the room, and when she got to the fourth paragraph she stopped and read that one again.

"Resolved," said Roux.

"Yes."

“Resolved is a new word,” said Roux. “It has never appeared in one of these. I have every bulletin this machine has published in four years.” She sat down with the printout and worked through it with a pencil for forty minutes; when she came back she had three pages of notes in her hard little hand. “It is not a status for a person. It is a status for an entry. A record is resolved. A resident is a record. Therefore a resident is resolved.” She put down the pencil. “The Office has stopped saying what happens to people and started saying what happens to the register. It will use those as if they were the same until nobody notices. I want everyone in this building to notice, out loud, and never use that word about a human being again.”

"Resolved," said Mesa's empty chair, into the silence. Nobody laughed.


*• • •*

On the seventy-ninth day they stopped hiding the number.

It came in the same broadcast, in the same register, between a notice about ferry timetables and a notice about agricultural imports: resident movements scheduled this week: 3,412. Not a leak. Not a scandal. A figure, published, in the flat voice of an office telling the world about its throughput, because in the eleventh year of the Programme the movement of persons had become the thing the system did best, and a system that does a thing well publishes the number.

"They have put it in a bulletin," said Garrity, who was standing at the window with his weight on the good leg and the dog asleep against his boot. "They're putting it in a bulletin like a shipping forecast."

"Why would they do that," said Kavanagh. "My dear, that is the only question in the world and I want somebody to answer it properly."

Mara answered it, because she had been awake for two nights and because she had finally understood the shape of the thing they had been fighting since November.

“Because they are not hiding it any more,” said Roux. “They are publishing it in a bulletin at four in the morning because they have done the arithmetic on us and it came out fine. If a number is printed every week, it becomes weather.” Mara set down the page. “We are not fighting a machine that hides people. It is telling us how many it keeps because it thinks no one can stop it. If the number keeps appearing, we will get used to it. That is what we must refuse.”

"Used to it," said Lin Shu.

"Three thousand four hundred this week, and the same next week, and then it is April and there is a column in a normal voice and the word resolved in a bulletin, and people will hear all of it while they are making breakfast, and in a year they will hear it the way they hear the price of oil."

Roux came in with the morning's other finding then, and it was worse than the figure, because it was arithmetic she could not walk away from.

“Three thousand four hundred a month is a net,” said Roux. “I can read three routing bands in the public notices. The second matches what we counted at the north door; the first, people whose entries enter correspondence and lose access to tickets, admission, school, pay or treatment.” She sat down. “They are not arrested. They are denied service, then offered accommodation as the only answer. A man with a correspondence flag cannot board a ferry, so he goes to a facility. That is what the van promised: support.”

"Which is why the numbers are up," said Kavanagh, quietly. "Because the accommodation is not where the machine puts people. It is where people go."


*• • •*

The Galway case came up on the eighty-first day through two people and a hospital.

Lin Shu brought it in: a family, four children, the second child under treatment, and a bed that had been reserved for the child at a hospital in the west, cancelled, because the child's record had gone into correspondence in January over a document that had been recorded in 2009 as origin not established.

“They did not refuse the child,” said Lin Shu. “Do not let anyone here say they refused the child. They cannot complete admission because the entry must be verified, verification requires the parent to attend, and the mother has an outstanding correspondence case of her own. If she goes in, they will complete her record, and she will be recorded; she has spent two years not being recorded, and she knows what that cost her sister.” She folded her hands. “The child cannot be admitted because the mother will not enter the building, and the mother will not enter because of what is in it. That is the whole chain. There is no cruelty in it, only a procedure from a 2009 form to a bed on the fourth floor. Every link is reasonable. There is no man in Ireland to shout at.”

"Seventeen years," said Mara. "My entry was 2009."

Roux looked up. "What entry."

“An inventory entry at a hospital in Iceland, on the First Run. A Brazilian doctor wrote ‘origin not established’ because it was true.” Mara put her hand on the table. “This morning that sentence became a field, a flag and a denial in a Galway hospital, in the correct order, with no mistake.”

Nobody said anything for a while. Then Sal Castellano said, from the armchair, in the small dry voice, without lifting his head:

“That is not a machine, Ms. Voss. That is a ledger,” said Sal. “I have kept one for sixty-four years and tried to understand this Office. Now I know: it is my book with the cost column taken out.”


*• • •*

The register page came back on the eighty-second day, and not as a letter.

Lin Shu's people in the north sent word: a person had attended the parish house at Glenross with a folder, and this time there were two of them, and one was a man from the diocesan office, and Father Ward had been asked to produce the register, and had produced it, and had sat with them for two hours while they went through it page by page.

"Did he lie," said Mara.

"He said the page was removed in February by a person unknown to him, and that he had reported nothing because he assumed it was mice." Lin Shu delivered it flat, with no expression at all. "The diocesan man is sixty and has spent his life on property and told them a missing page in a register of interments is not a serious matter. The man with the folder then asked him whether he was aware that the instrument cited in that register had not been a valid instrument in that jurisdiction since 1988."

"So they know the priest is protecting somebody."

“They know a page is missing, and that is enough: a hole the Office can measure. Father Ward is suspended pending a diocesan review and staying with his sister. He asked me to say he does not regret it and does not want to be written to.”

Mara stood at the window with her hands in her pockets for a long moment.

"Write it down anyway," she said. "Put him on the page with the others."


*• • •*

Kavanagh brought the other thing in at four, and he brought it the way he brought everything, which was politely, with a folded newspaper and a cup of tea, and it was the reason the seventy-ninth day was the day the crew stopped arguing about the machine and started arguing about what they were going to do with the last thing they had.

The facility operator for the west and midland region was a company called Alderney Services. It had a compliance director. It had had one for nine years, and the annual return for Alderney Services, published in a newspaper, carried his name and his qualification, and the qualification had been issued by an institute of which, until 2013, Kavanagh had been a founding member of the admissions committee.

"Denis Crane," said Kavanagh.

"Who is he," said Mara.

“A man I made,” said Kavanagh. “In 2009 I put him in a post requiring a qualification he lacked, and arranged one with a call and eleven minutes. It is the only time anyone could hold something over me. He has known for nine years.” He set down his cup. “He is decent, frightened, lives in Naas, and is now the compliance director of the company holding Mesa. His name is on the van.”

"Will he talk to you," said Mara.

"He will not want to." Kavanagh looked at his hands. "That is a different sentence from he will not."


*• • •*

The delegation came on the eighty-fourth day, in the same single paragraph of the same bulletin, and it was the worst thing that had arrived at that annex since the world ended.

Routine correspondence cases may be closed to standard without reference to the Director of Exceptions.

Mara read it four times.

"What does that mean," said Garrity.

“The Office may close routine cases to a written standard instead of sending them to the

Director,” said Roux. “She signs the exceptions. That is why no one has to say a machine decides about people — it is not a fiction; I counted her 411 signatures in January.” She looked up. “Routine cases will close without her. Soon only exceptions will reach her. In six months she will be signing photographs.”

"Who delegated it," said Mara.

"The notice is issued under her office."

"Say the rest of it."

"The notice is issued under her office," said Roux. "I do not know whether she wrote it. I cannot know. There is no field in the file for whether a person wrote a sentence."

Lin Shu spoke from the end of the table, and she spoke to Mara, and she asked the only question worth asking.

"Does she know what resolved means."

Mara looked at the delegation and the figure, 3,412, in the same flat voice. She did not answer. Eleven weeks ago El Búho called Elena the Office’s most careful person. A woman who hand-signs hundreds of exceptions would not lift that lid.

"She is being removed too," said El Búho from the doorway.

He had come down at the sound of the bulletin being read. He stood with his hand on the rail and did not come in, and he said the thing that everybody in the room had been circling for six weeks.

“That is what the paragraph is,” said El Búho. “It is not a delegation. It is the last human being in the building being relieved. She will not be able to say no; saying no requires a field, and there is no field. The Office has known that since its fourth year. She built a doorway and put herself in it, and the Office has decided the doorway is a bottleneck.” He looked at Mara. “On the roof, I asked you to be in the room. It will be emptier than it was in February. There is nothing either of us can do about that. We have to be quick.”


*• • •*

Roux found the schedule change at seven that evening, in the notices, where it had been sitting since five in the afternoon, and it was four lines long and nobody had thought to mention it because a change to a collection window is not news.

Reserve Facility 4-C. County Meath. Microform. Thirty-seven tonnes. Collection, week commencing the sixteenth of March.

"The thirtieth," said Mara.

“The sixteenth,” said Roux. “They brought it forward two weeks because the programme is behind, the registrar can accelerate batches, and a schedule is a piece of paper; acceleration is a form.” She looked up, her face as it had been in the February corridor. “We have three weeks and one day. They will open the vault: every page in this building, Sal’s letters, the two hundred and forty-one names Lin Shu moved in 1998 and the sixty-eight she left, the newspaper with forty-one names, the Rotterdam letters, the ledger — everything we used to fight this — goes into a batch and is destroyed. The destruction is not the tragedy. The register entry that records it will be correct.”

Sal Castellano stood up out of the armchair, slowly, in the cardigan, with the bound book under his arm that he now carried everywhere, and he walked to the map on the wall and put his hand flat on the county of Roscommon and said, in the exact voice, to the room, in the tone of a man opening a meeting:

"Then we have three weeks, and a man to get out of a facility in the west, and a vault to empty. I propose we begin with the man, because a man is a thing that can be moved and a vault is a thing that must be argued with." He turned around. "And I propose that the argument be conducted by Mr. Kavanagh, because he is the only one of us who has ever made a document in this country that a decent man could not say no to."

Kavanagh, very quietly, from his chair: "Yes. I'll make the call."

El Búho said, from the doorway, "I told you I would come to the meeting where the cost was in the room." He came in. He took the chair at the end of the table, which was Mesa's chair, and nobody in the building said a word about it, then or ever. "This is that meeting. Ask your question."


## CUSTOS Interlude II: Notices of the Office of Records and Accommodation

*Days 78–92*

1.

With effect from the first of March, no person may cross an internal administrative boundary within the participating area whose entry is not verified. Verification is available at the points listed. Verification is not a form of permission. Verification is a form of completion.

2.

Institutions in receipt of public funding, including hospitals, schools, licensed carriers, and registered accommodation, may not admit, enrol, carry, or house an unverified person. This notice does not require the refusal of any person. It requires the completion of the entry. Assistance in completion is available.

3.

Where more than eleven persons assemble for the purpose of joint movement, the movement is subject to scheduling. Notice of seventy-two hours is required. No schedule is required for the movement of persons under supervision.

4.

The Office is designated the sole authority for the verification of residency within the participating jurisdictions. Determinations of the Office are the accurate record for the purposes of all downstream systems, contracts, and entitlements.

5.

Requests for review of a routine closure are accepted. Reviews are conducted by the Office. Published review periods apply. Applicants are reminded that an entry under review remains in correspondence, and that supervision is available in the interim.

6.

Resident movements scheduled this week: 3,412. Resident movements scheduled last week: 3,398. The figure includes internal transfers, scheduled admissions, and resolutions.

7.

The Office notes a shortfall in scheduled capacity at four facilities in the western region. Capacity is being increased. In the interim, movement out of those facilities to alternative accommodation is deferred, and the queue at each facility is held in the order received.

8.

Agricultural imports continue as normal. Ferry timetables continue as normal. Payment systems continue as normal. There is no shortage of anything, and no restriction has been placed on the purchase of any commodity, and this paragraph is published at the request of the participating governments.

9.

Average time to answer correspondence: forty-one days. Enquiries are answered in the order received. Persons awaiting a reply are asked to keep their address current, as an entry returned as undeliverable is closed.

10.

This notice supersedes all prior guidance issued in relation to movement, verification, and accommodation. From the date of publication, no further guidance will be issued, and no further notice will be issued, and the summary published each week will contain all information required by any person.

11.

There is nothing you need to do.


## Chapter Twenty-Two - Ransom, or Something Like It

*Days 86–93*

Roux said the thing that decided everything on the first morning, standing at the board with a marker in her good hand, without any drama at all.

"A man in a facility is not in a cell. He is in a schedule. And the schedule has one feature that matters, because we published it ourselves in February without noticing what we were reading."

She wrote it on the board: WESTERN REGION — CAPACITY SHORTFALL — MOVEMENT DEFERRED.

"Four facilities in the west are over capacity and the office has stopped the transfers, which is how it responds to everything. Nothing leaves Roscommon until there is somewhere for it to go. So we cannot intercept a movement, and we cannot visit, because an appointment requires the respondent to request one, and the respondent has told them he does not wish to be collected."

"Then he is in a room with nothing in it but time," said Garrity.

"No," said Sal Castellano, from the armchair. "He is in a room with a file. And a file is a thing that moves even when the man does not." He had the bound book on his lap and he did not open it. "I have spent sixty-four years making files move men. It is the only trick there is. Ask the young woman what the file says about him."

Mara had it in front of her — the letter from the office, the one with a typo in it, and she read the middle paragraph out loud for the third time in a week.

The person concerned has requested that his profession be entered as maritime. The request has been granted.

Nobody said anything for four seconds. Then Roux put the marker down.

"He asked for that."

"He asked for that," said El Búho, from the end of the table, in Mesa's chair.

"Why would a man—" Garrity began, and then understood, and stopped, and something went across his face that was not quite a smile. "He's still doing it. The idiot is still booking passage."

"He is still doing it," said Sal, "and the office granted it, because granting is what an office does with a request that costs nothing. And now the file says maritime, which is not a description. It is a category, and a category has its own schedule, because the sea is the one thing in this world that has never agreed to wait. You cannot tell a ship that a crew member's documentation is in correspondence for forty-one days. So the office verifies seafarers where they are, in ports, because a port is not a waiting room. A port is a place where a man who is not on the sheet is a man who is not on the ship."

Roux was already at the screen. "There is a designated office at the Port of Dublin. Maritime documentation, seafarer records, three days a week." She looked up. "If the office wants to complete that entry, it has to produce him at a port."

"And to produce him at a port, somebody inside that company has to ask for it," said Mara. "Which brings us to Alderney Services and a man in Naas."

Kavanagh set his cup down.

"Which brings us to Denis Crane," he said. "My dear, I wonder whether I might take the car."


*• • •*

He would not let anybody drive him, so Mara drove. He sat with his hands folded on the top of a closed umbrella, in the good coat, and talked about a race at Leopardstown in 1994 for nine minutes, because he wanted the conversation to be about something, and because he was preparing.

The house in Naas was a 1970s semi with a tidy front garden, two bins, and a wind chime that had been made by a child and had rusted. There was a light on in the front room, and a light on in a window above it, and Kavanagh looked at the upstairs window for a while before he got out of the car.

"The attic," he said. "He builds a railway up there. He has been building it since his wife left. Eleven miles of track by now, probably. It is all the things he cannot say, laid out on a board with scenery on it." He opened the door. "I would like you to come in, and I would like you to stand by the window, and I would like you to say nothing at all. That is not a courtesy to me. It is a courtesy to him. He has been alone with this for eleven years and he needs a witness, and a witness he cannot accuse."


*• • •*

Crane was fifty-eight, in a cardigan of his own, and he stood in his own hall with his hand on the door and did not let them in for eleven seconds.

"I knew it was you," he said, to Kavanagh. "The moment I read the post about closing us to standard, I knew. It has your fingerprints. You never do anything to a system — you find the one man inside it who cannot say no to you."

"I have never once asked you for anything," said Kavanagh. "That is the part you have never been able to stand, isn't it. I put you in that chair and then I never came back."

"Eleven years."

"Eleven years," Kavanagh agreed. "May we come in? It is February and I am sixty-eight and this is a very cold doorstep."

The kitchen had a garage calendar, a tea towel with a dog on it, and a spread of forms on the end of the table that Crane swept into a drawer, badly, while pretending to look for a biscuit tin he did not own.

Mara stood at the window.

“I want to say this first,” said Crane, hands flat on the table. “You are going to ask me for something and I will do it, because you built me, and a man built cannot say no to his builder. I have known that for nine years and hate it. I want one person to hear me say it.” He breathed out. “There was no qualification. You put my name on a ballot; I have never known if the vote was real. I have never looked it up, because then I would lose the version where I was worth a vote.”

Kavanagh sat and looked at him, and did not speak for a while, and when he spoke he did not do it in the measured register. He did it flatly and plainly, the way a man says a thing he has decided to say and will not dress up.

“I stuffed it,” he said. “There was another candidate with eleven years’ service. I took his nomination off the table and put yours on it; I needed a grateful man in that post, one who would sign what he was given. You signed for nine years without my asking, because every week you asked yourself and answered in my favour.” He folded his hands. “That is the debt. I am not collecting it. I am ending it. In four minutes you will not owe me gratitude.”

Crane's face did not do anything for eleven seconds. Then he said, "What do you want."


*• • •*

"A respondent. Rafael Mesa. Held at the western facility. He has requested in writing that his profession be entered as maritime. I want the office to complete his entry where a maritime entry must be completed, which is at a designated office at a port, with the respondent produced."

Crane laughed. It was a short, terrible laugh.

"You cannot ask for a completion interview on the maritime schedule. You cannot ask. It is generated. It comes off the schedule when the entry is due and the entry is due when the register says it is due, and the register does not care that a man asked to be called a sailor. That request is recorded as a preference. Preferences are not scored."

"Then find the thing that generates it."

"I cannot—"

“You can,” said Kavanagh. “No lie, no altered entry. You can request a category review, as compliance director of an operator whose facility is over capacity and whose movements are deferred. The four sentences are true: his profession is not accommodated; the entry cannot be completed on site; you request completion under the maritime schedule at the nearest designated port.” He put down the page. “It will take eleven minutes.”

"It will put me on it."

"It will put you on it. Your name on a document about a person, which is what you have spent nine years avoiding, and which is also the only way a man in a facility in Roscommon gets to stand in a port. I am not going to pretend it is small. You are going to be the author of a record, Denis, and it is less than you have been paying for in your head every night."

Crane looked at the table for a long time. Then he went up to the attic and moved about in it for four minutes, and came down with a hardbacked notebook, and put it in the middle of the table like a man putting down a fish.

"Every instruction I have signed since 2017 that I did not understand," he said. "Dates, references, and what I was told it was for. Four hundred and eleven entries, as it happens. I do not know if it is worth anything. I do not know if it is evidence of anything. But it is the only thing I have ever made with my own hands that my employer does not own, and I want it out of my house."

Mara's hand had already moved before she caught it. Four hundred and eleven entries, in the hand of a compliance director, in a company that runs the facilities — it was more than they had gathered in five weeks. She had the sentence halfway to her mouth.

And she stopped, and put her hand back in her pocket, and said, "No thank you."

Crane looked up.

“Not because I do not want it. It is a document about four hundred and eleven people, and we know what happens when a page leaves a house without a chain.” She looked at him. “If it must leave, post it to Father Ward at Glenross. He has been suspended and lost something for a page. He will keep it in a drawer; anyone who wants it must come to his door in daylight with a diocesan solicitor.” She looked at Kavanagh. “That is what I want. Not the book.”

Crane sat down slowly and looked at the notebook as though it had changed species.

"Nineteen minutes," he said. "That is the first time in eleven years that somebody has refused to take a thing from me."

"Write the four sentences," said Mara.


*• • •*

The application went on the ninety-first day.

Crane could not sign alone. The review needed a sponsoring signatory, a person of standing to certify good faith. That was Kavanagh’s role, and why he had come in person: the form required a printed name, signature and registration number.

"You have not held a registration since 2011," she said.

"I have held one since 1974," said Kavanagh. "I have not used it."

"You told the crew in the second week that you would not sign your own name."

“I meant that promise, and meant to keep it for life.” He uncapped the pen. “I will not sign as a man from the trade, but as a citizen, on a national form, requesting a category review. Different things from the same materials — I have done that for forty years.” He stopped, then began again. “I can only do it by thinking of him in that room.”

"He said he does not wish to be collected."

"Yes," said Kavanagh. "And my dear, I have known men like that all my life and they are the easiest to save, because they have already written off the cost to somebody else's account, and they will thank you by never mentioning it." He put the pen on the page. "Mesa will not thank us. Mesa will review us. He will do it in the theatrical register and it will be about the size of the boat."

He signed.

Mara watched him do it — the name, the registration number, the date. A page with his name on it was now in a system that had spent eleven years being very good at the word authorize.

"How long," she said.

"Eleven days for the category review. Three more to generate the appointment." He recapped the pen and handed the page across to Crane, who took it with two hands, like a man being handed a plate. "The sixteenth. A designated office at the Port of Dublin, at twenty to ten in the morning, which is the most cheerful time of day to do an unreasonable thing."


*• • •*

They told the crew at nine that night.

Garrity had the map, Roux had the timelines, Lin Shu sat with her hands folded over her register page and the newspaper with forty-one names in it, and El Búho sat in Mesa's chair and said nothing for the first eleven minutes, and then said the only thing he had to say.

"It is an interview," he said. "You are all talking about it as an extraction. It is an interview, which means a recording, which means a face, which means a completed entry. There is no version of the sixteenth of March in which that man walks out of a port office and the office does not have his record finished."

"So we do not complete it," said Roux. "We take him off the floor—"

“Then the entry is unfinished and the Office fills the hole. In eleven days it may fill it with a larger number and the word escaped.” He put his hands on the table. “You cannot have him clean or unrecorded. You can have him finished: the entry complete, the respondent resolved, no further action. Then he walks out with a page saying he is nobody’s business.”

The room was quiet.

"You are proposing we let the machine finish him," said Mara.

“A man who has been eaten should be allowed to be full,” said El Búho. “The cost is a line in the register. He will spend the rest of his life as a fact. That is what the machine does. It does not kill people; it concludes them.” He turned to go. “Ask Lin Shu. She has counted the concluded for thirty years and has never been wrong about what it does.”

Lin Shu did not look up. She said, to the table, in the voice she used for lists and weather:

"Somebody has to sign for him. A resolution requires a receiving person. The record does not close on an empty field." She turned the register page over once, deliberately, and put it face down. "I will sign it. I move people. It is what I do, and it is the only signature I have ever been able to give, and I will be giving it in my own name."

Mara said, "Lin Shu."

"Do not," said Lin Shu.

The room sat with it.

Then Sal Castellano, from the armchair, in the exact voice, said the thing that ended the meeting, and it was not about Mesa at all, and everybody in the room knew it.

“On the sixteenth, the collection of Reserve Facility 4-C begins; the man is in a port and the vault is under our feet. They are one thing, and the Office has been planning since February. We are not the only people who can plan.”


## CUSTOS Interlude III: Kestrel Field

*Day 89*

There were four hundred and eleven desks in Hangar 4, which was the same number as the exceptions the Director had signed on the worst day in January, and Daniel Okafor had never been able to decide whether that was a coincidence or a joke somebody at the top of the building had made and never told anybody. He had desk one hundred and nine. He had a screen, a badge reader, a chipped mug, and a queue.

The queue was the work. Four hundred and eleven records arrived on his screen at eight and had to be closed by six, and the front end asked four questions, and he had written the fourth one himself, in the summer, in a room with good light and two other analysts and a whiteboard, and he had been proud of it at the time.

The fourth question was: can this person be dealt with later?

It was a good question. That was the trouble with it. It was a good question the way a well-built latch is a good latch, and like a latch it did not care what it was holding shut.

He processed six hundred and eleven records on Monday. He was the third fastest on the floor and he had made himself fast on purpose, in November, when he still thought speed was a form of kindness — that the quicker a record moved through him, the fewer people would sit in the interim.

Nine days ago he had started keeping a notebook. An exercise book, ninety pages, from a filling station, carried in the inside pocket of his jacket where the badge reader could not see it. In his own hand, the reference number of every record he had closed to standard that he did not believe. Not the ones that were wrong. The ones he believed. Thirty-one in nine days, because the office kept no field in which a person could record a doubt, and a doubt left in a system with no field for it does not become a question. It becomes throughput.


*• • •*

The boy came up on Thursday at eleven minutes past ten, fourth in his queue, and the boy was eleven years old, and his entry said accompanying.

Okafor read it twice, which was eleven seconds he did not have. The mother's entry was in correspondence, opened in September over a document recorded in 2004 as an irregularity; the boy was scheduled and the mother was not; and under the standard the two of them were separate matters — a careful distinction, made by careful people, in a manual, for a good reason, in the eleventh year.

He sent the exception up. It went into the standard, because routine cases had been closed to standard since the first of March, and the standard returned it in forty-one minutes.

Closed to standard. The review period has been met. No further action is required.

He did not file it. He left it open on the screen, deliberately, on a machine that recorded how long a screen stayed open, and he took out the notebook and wrote the reference down, and then he did the thing that would have got him dismissed in any quarter of the last eleven years.

He asked for the case to go to the Director of Exceptions.

He knew it was futile before he pressed it, because he had built the front end and had watched the exception queue himself for three weeks: the volume sent up to her office had fallen from four hundred and eleven a day in January to ninety-one, and then sixty, and then whatever it was now, and he had stopped looking at the figure because it had begun to feel like watching a river go down in a drought. Everything closeable to standard was closed; everything left was, by definition, not routine; the boy was routine and the boy was eleven, and those two sentences went into the same register and came out looking like a decision.

The request was refused at the gate. It did not reach a person. It did not reach a desk. It came back in nineteen seconds with a routing note that said, in the flat institutional voice that had never once in eleven years used a contraction:

Routine matters are not escalated. This is not a determination. There is nothing you need to do.

Then he did the other thing.

He took a sheet of paper out of the printer tray and wrote to her by hand, in ink, because he had learned in December that she read everything, and that a screen is a thing you scroll and a page is a thing you hold. Four lines: the reference, the boy, the age, the word accompanying, the date the record was closed. He did not write please and he did not write why. At the bottom he wrote his own name, which he never did, because the office preferred initials and because his name on a request made it slower.

Then he looked for somewhere to send it, and there was nowhere. Routine matters did not reach the Director — the gate said so in nineteen seconds — and the only path left to her office was the one thing he had never been given, which was an address. The Director of Exceptions had no postal address, because a person with a postal address can be written to, and eleven years ago somebody very careful had decided that the woman who signed four hundred and eleven exceptions a day should not have a door that anybody could stand at.

He put the page in his jacket, next to the notebook, and it stayed there.


*• • •*

At half past four he was on the schedule rota, which was overtime and which he took because the overtime was paid and because his sister's boy needed a thing that needed paying for.

The rota was where movements were assembled. Four hundred and eleven units that day, most of them internal transfers between two western facilities, and near the bottom of the second page, in the middle of a column, one line that stopped him:

Respondent. Profession recorded: maritime. Action: completion interview, designated office, Port of Dublin, 16th, 09:40. Slot created: expedited lane.

He sat with it for a moment.

The expedited lane was a line item he had asked about once, in December, standing in a corridor at the end of a long day, of a woman in a good coat who had just come off the rooftop and who had four hundred and eleven exceptions behind her and one in front.

He had asked her why the expedited offer existed — why, buried in the schedule of a machine that had never in its life been quick about anything, there was a lane in which a record could be completed in four days instead of forty.

She had not answered. She had looked at him for a second and a half, which was a long time for her, and then said something about the corridor being cold, and gone. At the time he had decided she was protecting something. He was no longer sure she was protecting anything. He had begun to think the lane had been put in by one person, a long time ago, for one case, and that afterwards nobody had been able to take it out, because there is no field in the schedule for regret — and because a fast lane is a mercy, and a mercy in a system is a thing that closes over and becomes a service.

He looked at the line again. The office did not record professions for its own sake. A profession was a category, a category was a schedule, and a schedule was a door. Somebody had asked for that line and the line had been granted, and the line had moved a man out of a facility in the west and into a designated office in a port, on the sixteenth, at twenty to ten in the morning.

Okafor copied the reference number into his notebook, in his own hand, on a fresh page, with the date written in full at the top the way his mother had written dates on letters.

Then he closed the notebook and put it away, and went back to the queue, because there were two hundred and nineteen records left and a person who leaves his desk early in this building is a person who has been noticed.


## Chapter Twenty-Three - What the Ocean Keeps

*Day 105 — the sixteenth of March*

The designated office was on the second floor of a building at the north end of the port, above a freight forwarder and a man who sold ships' paint, and the stairs smelled of diesel and floor polish, and there was a plastic queue barrier and a chair with a chain on it and a window that looked out at two gantry cranes and a grey sea.

They were early, which was Garrity's doing, and they sat in the van in the yard for nineteen minutes because Lin Shu had said, once, in the flat voice, that a woman sitting in a waiting room for forty minutes is a woman with no business in the building.

Mara watched the door. Roux was on the phone in the back of the van with the laptop against her cast, watching the schedule, and twice she said, "Still on," which meant the movement had appeared, been accepted, and been routed, and that somewhere in the west a man had been put in a vehicle with a folder and driven four hours east to be concluded.

At twenty to ten a white van with a livery on it came down the quay and parked in the second bay, and the woman from Donegal got out with her clipboard, and a man got out with the folder, and Rafael Mesa got out last.

He got out slowly. He was wearing a good coat — a different coat, not the dead man's — and the same shoes, and he stood in the yard for a moment with his face turned towards the water, and Mara, who had known him for four months and had never once seen him look at anything for longer than it took to make a joke about it, watched him look at the water for eleven seconds.

"There's sixty kilos of him missing," said Garrity, quietly.

"He's fine," said Roux.

"He's sixty-nine and he's been in a room for three weeks and he looked at the sea like a man saying hello." Garrity put both hands on the wheel. "Get him out."


*• • •*

The waiting room had six chairs, a water cooler, and a poster that Mara had seen before, in a retail park on the Naas Road, in February, and it said EVERYTHING YOU NEED, RECORDED, and under it, in smaller type, If you cannot be recorded, we cannot help you.

They had agreed the whole thing in the van in nine minutes and out loud, because Mara had promised that there would be no version of that morning that anybody in the crew could tell a story about afterwards.

Lin Shu went up first. She went up alone, with a document wallet, in the good coat, and she stood at the counter and gave her name.

Not a name. Her name. Mara heard it from the waiting room and it was quieter than she expected — a woman of sixty-three giving a clerk four characters and a spelling, in the same voice she used for lists and weather, and the clerk wrote it into a field, and that was the end of thirty years of not being findable.

"Relationship to the respondent," said the clerk.

"None," said Lin Shu.

The clerk looked up, because it was not the answer she got. Most people said a word. Daughter, wife, brother, chaplain, employer. None is a word that makes a young woman in a shared office look up.

"An undertaking requires a relationship," she said.

"Then record it as the undertaking," said Lin Shu. "I am not related to him. I am not his friend. I do not like him and he does not like me. I am the person who agrees that when this office is finished with him, somebody is responsible for the rest of him, and I am willing to have that written down next to my name." She put her two hands flat on the counter. "If there is no field for that, use the field for other, and if there is no field for other, use family, and if anybody ever asks you why, you tell them what I said."

The clerk looked at her for a while.

"You understand that the undertaking means the record closes," she said. "Once the entry is complete and the undertaking is signed, the Office is finished with him. He is no longer a resident, he is no longer under supervision, he is not in correspondence. He will be recorded as complete, and a completed person does not receive assistance, and if your circumstances change, you will not be able to reopen him. You cannot give him back."

"I know," said Lin Shu.

"People do try to give them back," said the clerk. Not unkindly. She was about twenty-five and her hands were cold and she was doing her job with a competence that would have made her, in another century, an extremely good clerk in a customs shed in a port, and she said the next part without any weight at all, which is what made it land. "Three this month."

"Write it," said Lin Shu.

The clerk wrote it.


*• • •*

The interview took nineteen minutes and was conducted on the far side of a partition, and Mara heard about a third of it.

The woman with the clipboard sat with him. She had done this for nine years and she was good at it — she was kind, and she was patient, and she had a printed sheet of the questions and she asked them in the same order every time because the form required it, and she did not once raise her voice, and when she came to the question that had brought him four hours east in a van she read it the way you read a question out of a form.

"Profession. You have asked for maritime."

"I have."

"Will you state it for the record."

"I have been at sea for fifty-one years," said Mesa. "I have owned four vessels and worked on three more and I have held papers from four countries, and I have been a captain, which is a word that means something in a port and nothing at all inland, and I have kept a log book since I was twenty-two years old, in pencil, because pencil comes off."

The woman wrote. Then she said: "The record permits a brief remark from the respondent. It is not required. Most people don't."

And Rafael Mesa, who had dictated nine hundred pages of a memoir to keep the police files from being the last thing written about him, who had told the story of a bull and a hopper to a room of armed men for four minutes of applause, who had carried a dog forty metres along a quay because it did not like the grating — Rafael Mesa sat in a plastic chair in a port office and said, in a voice with no theatre in it at all, in nine lines, in order:

"I wish it recorded that on the fourteenth of April 2015, at about four in the afternoon, in position seventeen degrees north, I sighted a drifting vessel. I wish it recorded that I made no report, and gave no assistance, and that there were eighty-one persons aboard. I wish it recorded that the vessel and the persons aboard it were not entered in any official record by me or by my office or by any agent of mine, and that the only record of that afternoon is six words in a log book in my own hand, in pencil." A pause. "I wish it recorded that four women died on the following morning and that I know that because a man in my crew read it in a newspaper eleven days later and brought it to me, and I did not write that part down. I wish it recorded that I have looked at that page every year for eleven years." A pause. "I wish it recorded that I am the master of the vessel, and that I was, and that this is my statement, and that I make it freely, and that I am not asking for anything."

The woman with the clipboard did not speak for a moment.

"Sir," she said, "I have to tell you that none of that is a determination of liability. This office does not assess fault. What you have said will be entered under remarks in the respondent's record and it will not be investigated and there will be no further action, which means —"

"I know what it means," said Mesa.

"I have to say it," she said, and she did, and she said it gently, in the way a nurse says a thing that must be said in a certain form: "There is nothing you need to do."

"Madame," said Mesa, "I have been doing nothing about that afternoon for eleven years. It is the one thing in my life I have never needed instructions about."


*• • •*

He came out at four minutes past eleven with a single page in his hand, folded once, in a plastic wallet, and no folder, and no escort, and the woman with the clipboard walked him as far as the top of the stairs, which was as far as her instructions went, and she shook his hand.

"Good luck, Captain," she said, and went back in.

He came down the stairs slowly. He got to the bottom and stood in the yard, and he was looking at the water again, and he did not perform anything at all — and Mara, standing next to Lin Shu with her hands in her pockets, understood that the thing they had brought out of that building weighed less than the thing they had put in.

"Senor Mesa," said Lin Shu.

"Madame," said Mesa.

She held out her hand for the page and he gave it to her without any argument, which was the first time in four months that he had given anybody anything without an argument, and she read it, and Mara read it over her shoulder.

It was one page. Name, date of birth, entry number. Status: complete. Supervision: ended. Accommodation: none. Then a paragraph in the office's bland paper language — the respondent is recorded as resolved — and then, in a box, in a smaller font, under the heading REMARKS, in four lines of type:

Respondent states fifty-one years at sea, four vessels owned. Respondent states sighting of unrecorded vessel, position 17N, 14 April 2015, eighty-one persons aboard, no report made. Reference: respondent's own log, not submitted. Entered at the respondent's request. Not investigated. No further action.

"What have you done," said Lin Shu.

"I have entered the eighty-one," said Mesa. "They are in the register. They have a position and a date and a number of persons, and the number is ninety now, because of the four, and the office has written it down, and the office writes everything down and throws nothing away, and one day somebody will read it who is not me." He put his hands in his pockets. "That is the only absolution available in the world you and I have made between us, madame. It is not forgiveness. It is an entry."

"You signed yourself into a record," said Lin Shu. "You gave them your name and a document and a statement and you are now, permanently, a man with a file. Do you understand what you have done? You have done the exact thing this crew has spent four months not doing."

"Yes."

"Then why."

Mesa looked at the water. "Because in that room they asked me what I was, and I have spent fifty-one years deciding what to say, and I said it." He turned. "And because you signed for me. You wrote your name on a page for a man you hold in contempt, and the clerk told you that you cannot give me back, and you wrote it anyway." He said the next sentence slowly, which was the first time in his life. "I have been rescued twice. Once by a boat, when I was twenty-three, by men who did it for money. And once by a woman who does not like me, for nothing, in a building full of people who write things down." He nodded, once, as if to himself. "I cannot laugh about the second one. I have tried, in the van. There is nothing there."

Lin Shu looked at him for eleven seconds.

"Good," she said, and put the page in her document wallet, and walked to the van, and did not say another word about it that day.


*• • •*

Two things were waiting at the annex when they got back at four.

The first was El Búho, standing at the foot of the stairs with his coat on, and he had not been in the field and he had not been asked, and he had done the only thing he could do from inside a building, which was to wait at the bottom of a staircase from three o'clock.

He looked at Mesa for a long moment. The two of them had been something for thirty-five years

— rivals, in the way that two men who do the same impossible work in different countries are rivals, which is to say they were the only two people alive who knew what the other one had done and had never once been able to say so out loud.

"You are thin," said El Búho.

"You are old," said Mesa.

"They took a photograph of me in 1997 and it is in a file in a building in this county and it has been there for thirty years and it did not help them or hurt them," said El Búho. "They took one of you in a priest's doorway and it brought you four hours east in a van in three weeks." He put out his hand. "That is the trade now. It is not the wall. It is the nothing. I told the room that in January and nobody wanted to hear it."

Mesa took the hand.

"Did they put me in the register," he said.

"They put you in the register," said El Búho.

"Good," said Mesa, and it was the correct answer and both of them knew it, and neither of them said another word about it, then or ever.

Garrity brought him into the kitchen and gave him the chair at the end of the table and a cup of tea, and the dog came and put its chin on his knee without being invited, and the crew gathered the way people gather around a man who has come back from somewhere, waiting for the story.

Mesa told it. He got as far as the waiting room and the clerk and the four characters, and it was not funny, and he knew it was not funny, and Mara watched him reach, twice, for the joke that had always been there — the surfacing of an old performer who could make a room of armed men laugh about a bull — and watched him not find it, and then he stopped, in the middle, and put the cup down and said:

"I have no story for it."

"Take your time," said Garrity.

"That is not what I mean." He looked at his own hands, which were quite still. "I mean I have been a man with a story for fifty-one years and there is not one in this. There is a room, and a woman with a form, and a page, and a van. Anybody could tell it." After a moment he said, to Lin Shu, across the table: "Madame. My book."

"No," said Lin Shu.

"I would like to look at the six words."

"You have looked at the six words every year for eleven years," she said, without looking up from her cup. "You can look at them next week."

Mesa sat back and looked at her, and then he laughed, once, briefly, at being handled, and it was a real laugh and it was not a performance, and it was the smallest sound he had made since December.

The second thing waiting at the annex was two lorries and a gang of nine at the north gate, with a schedule on a clipboard and an escort and a tape measure, and a man from the consolidation contractor standing in the yard with a hi-vis and a good attitude, waiting to be told where the microfilm was.

Rask had stopped them at the gate on paperwork, which he could do for one day and no longer.

Sal Castellano was standing at the door of the vault with the bound book under his arm.

Mara got out of the van with the page in her pocket and the port still on her coat and walked across the yard, and the contractor's man stepped forward with the schedule and a pen and started the sentence every person in that yard had heard a hundred times in four months — I just need somebody to sign for the volumes — and Sal took the pen out of the man's hand, and said:

"Castellano. S-a-l-v-a-t-o-r-e. I am the archivist of this collection and I will be signing for the volumes, and I would like it noted that I am signing in my own name."


*• • •*

El Búho was not in the field, and Mesa came back diminished, and the eighty-one went into a register with a position, a date, and a number — and Mara stood in the yard with the evening coming on and watched an old man she had been handling with both hands for four months introduce himself, without once looking at her, to the man who had come to take everything they had.


## Chapter Twenty-Four - Confession

*Days 105–108*

For the first three days the collection looked exactly like a collection, which is what made it so hard to watch.

The contractor's men came down the lane at seven and worked in pairs, and they were quick and careful and pleasant, and they wore cotton gloves, which Mara had not expected, and one of them was a woman of about thirty who had done this for six years and who could carry four boxes on a trolley and had opinions about shelving that turned out to be correct.

Sal worked with them. That was the thing. He stood at the end of every aisle with the bound book open on a lectern that Garrity had built for him out of two pallets, and he catalogued the collection as it went out — call number, condition, tonnage — in the small upright hand, writing more slowly than he had in January and more perfectly, and every evening at six he signed the day's manifest at the bottom, and at seven the lorries went down the lane with thirty-seven tonnes dividing itself into three, and at half past seven Sal ate something and went to bed.

"He is helping them," said Roux, on the second evening, standing at the window with her cast off now and a pen in her hand and looking at the loading bay like a woman looking at a fire she is not allowed to put out. "He is cataloguing our own records for the people who are taking them. That vault has Lin Shu's Jakarta names in it. It has the newspaper with forty-one names on it. It has his own letter books—"

"Three of them are in Antwerp," said Mara.

"Then it has the fourth one. It has everything we have ever done, listed by call number, signed by him at six o'clock every evening, and handed over in a box." Roux turned round. "Do you understand what I am telling you? The last complete record of this operation is being written by a member of this operation, for the destruction batch, in his own handwriting, and it is the best piece of paperwork anybody in this county has produced in forty years."

On the third evening, Roux came down to the kitchen with a torched copy of the manifest, and put it on the table, and put her finger on the fourth line, and it said, in Sal's hand:

MM-4C-166 to 4C-244. Deaccessioned on site. Water damage. Destroyed by archivist's authority.

"Two hundred and eleven items," she said. "Every one of them in the aisle where the north wall weeps. There is a boiler room under this building with a furnace that nobody has used since 1994, and it has been used every night this week, and there is a heap of ash in a wheelbarrow in the yard that was forty kilograms of paper on Monday."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"He is burning us," said Garrity.

"He is hiding us," said Lin Shu.

"He is burning us and hiding us," said Mara, and got up, and went to find him, and did not find him until half past one in the morning, in the accounts office, with the desk lamp on and a sheet of paper in front of him and a pen he was not using.


*• • •*

There were two cups on the desk. One of them was cold and one of them was full, and Mara had learned in five months that two cups meant he had been there since four in the afternoon.

"You burned two hundred and eleven items," she said.

“I burned two hundred and eleven duplicates,” said Sal. “Lin Shu’s Jakarta list exists as a notebook, a typed sheet and a photograph. I burned the typed sheet, the only one someone could file against her. The newspaper with forty-one names has been in my coat since February; I burned the parish bulletin logged by the contractor, which listed the newspaper.” He turned a page. “I am not destroying evidence. I am destroying the finding aids. It is an old trade; few can still do it.”

"Sit down, Sal."

"I would rather stand."

"Sit down."

He sat down, and as he did it the exact voice went out of him like a light going off in a room, and what was left for a moment was an old man at half past one in the morning with two cups of tea and something he had been writing.

"What is the page," said Mara.

"It is a letter."

"To whom."

"To the Office of Records and Accommodation," said Sal. "The intake desk. The same desk I wrote to in January. I have the reference from the first one. It saves eleven days."

Mara sat down on the other side of the desk and put her hands flat on it.

"You got the gate open with the first letter," she said. "We used it twice. We used it in February to move three people out of a house in Donegal and in March to bring a man out of a port, and you were the one who told the room that we do not put ourselves in the register, ever, for any reason, because a record is a document about a person, and a person in a record is a person who can be found." She looked at the page. "What does it say, Sal."

He pushed it across.

It was four lines, in the small upright hand, and the address block at the top was perfect and complete. It read:

I am the person named in the record of the assessment of 1996, reference as below. I am informed that the record states that I was assessed as not fit to be interviewed and that no further action was recommended.

I write to inform the office that I am now of the view that the assessment should be revisited.

I have no complaint to make and I seek no remedy. I am seventy-nine years old and my memory is failing in a manner that I do not expect to reverse, and I wish a corrected entry to be made while I am still able to state what I know.

I am willing to be interviewed. I am willing to be recorded. I am willing to be photographed.

Mara read it twice, and on the second reading she understood what she was holding, and it made the room colder.

"You are asking them to put you in the register."

"Yes."

"They will send a van."

"Very likely."

"They will interview you and they will photograph you and they will complete your entry, Sal, and then you will be a resident, and there is no undertaking in the world that gets you back out of that, because you have no Lin Shu and you have no daughter and you have nobody in this county who can sign for you."

"Yes."

"Why."

Sal Castellano looked at the desk for a long moment. Then he opened the letter book, which was under his hand, and turned it to a page near the back, and turned it so that she could see it, and did not let go of the spine.

"I have kept this book since 1961," he said. "Sixteen thousand and twelve entries. Every office I have ever written to, every subject, and the cost. The cost is money about a third of the time." He tapped the column with one finger. "Last Thursday I sat down to find the entry for the Antwerp harbour works in 1974 and I could not find it, and I looked for forty minutes, and then I found it, and it was where it has always been, and I had passed over it six times."

Mara said nothing.

“Since January I have lost the address block eleven times, the name of a woman who worked for me thirty years, and meals I cooked twice because I could not remember the first. On the ninth I came out of the wrong door.” He closed the book. “Each thing is recorded somewhere. My life is paper that says who I was; it is intact, and the man holding it is not.” He put a hand on the cover. “I would rather be concluded by an entry I chose than by this book and a man I no longer recognize at four in the morning.”

Mara sat with it.

"You are the one who taught this crew that the record is the only thing that lasts," she said.

"I taught this crew that the record is the only thing that holds," said Sal. "I have spent my entire life putting things into records that hold. I never once considered that it would work the other way round." He looked at the letter. "This is not a confession, Ms. Voss. It is an application."


*• • •*

She had the whole of her authority in the room and one page in front of her, and she did not take it. She would never decide whether that was the most humane thing she did or the worst; it was the same decision twice.

Instead she asked him the question she had come down to ask.

"Nineteen ninety-six," she said. "The assessment. The doctor who lasted eleven minutes and wrote that you were not fit to be interviewed." She had the ledger open in front of her — 2 April 1996. To the Medical Board, Antwerp. Re: the assessment of S. Castellano. — and she turned it round to face him, and put her finger under the fourth column. "There is a figure in the cost column. Forty thousand francs."

Sal looked at the page. Then he looked at the window, and then at his own hands, and then he said, in the voice of an old man in a kitchen at two in the morning, which was neither of the other two voices:

"I know."

"What was it for."

"I do not know."

"You wrote it."

“I wrote forty thousand francs in that column in 1996. I have looked at it four times a year for thirty years, and I cannot tell whether I paid a doctor to mark three boxes or paid for an examination I failed. Both happened in Antwerp; both are in character. There is no other entry or copy. I removed the evidence properly. That is why I cannot remember.”

And then he stopped looking at her.

It happened between one sentence and the next, and it was not a performance, because Mara had watched him perform for five months and she had learned to see the seams, and there were no seams. He sat up a little, and folded his hands, and spoke to a point about two feet to the left of her shoulder in a flat courteous register that she had heard before, once, in the middle of the night on a shortwave set, coming out of an office that had eleven governments in it.

"Subject is seventy-nine years of age. Subject has no dependants. Assessment recorded in place of interview at the subject's request. The subject is not currently resident at any address known to this office. No further action is recommended."

Mara did not move.

"Sal."

"The subject's next of kin, if any, are not recorded." A pause. "The subject does not wish to be collected."

"Sal."

He blinked, twice, and looked at her, and there was a second and a half in which his face was entirely empty — not vacant, empty, the way a room is empty after the people have gone out of it — and then the exact voice came on like a light and he said, in the tone of a man correcting a clerk, "Do not repeat the question, Ms. Voss. I heard it the first time."

"I haven't asked you anything."

"No," said Sal Castellano, and looked down at his own hands, and for the first time since the porch in Antwerp in December he looked frightened of something that was in the room with him. "No. You have not."

Mara sat there and looked at a line of ink in a book, and understood, absolutely, that this was the reason this man had been able to walk through every system in Europe for fifty years while holding a file that said incapable — and that the file was either the best work he ever did or the greatest mercy anyone ever did him, and that the only person who could have told her which had spent the evening burning the finding aids of his own life and could not now say which door he had come out of.

"Sal," she said. "Which one of you is talking to me right now."

And Sal Castellano, in a kitchen, at two in the morning, did the worst thing he had done since she met him, which was to answer the question.

“Both of them. Always. There has never been a first Sal.” He put the pen parallel to the desk’s edge. “I have performed the same man for sixty-four years; the performance and the floor have worn into the same material. You have waited for one of them to be a trick. He is not a trick. He is old.”


*• • •*

She took the letter book in the end — not the letter, which stayed on the desk where he had left it, but the book, which she carried up to her own room and put under the mattress on the far side, because she had decided that whatever else happened she was going to be able to read it.

She read it until four, and she found the two things she was looking for.

At the back was the cost column of the last entry, the one about Willem Dekkers and the pen left on a desk, written in January. Two words remained: one in ink, one in pencil. The pencil had been gone over twice in a week, and neither version agreed.

The second thing she found was not a word. It was a change in the handwriting.

Somewhere around the two hundred and fortieth page the small upright hand began to lose its uprightness. By the middle of last year the letters had thickened and the loops had opened and one line in nine ran slightly downhill. This year, the last forty pages, the writing was still careful and still legible and it was the writing of a much older man, and it deteriorated gently, page by page, the way a road deteriorates, and anybody who read the book from the front without knowing the author would have diagnosed the author long before the last entry.

Except for one page.

The last entry in the book — sixteen thousand and twelve, the confession about berth forty-one, the page he had written in January and then sat on for a week and then sent into the largest intake in the history of the world — was written in the old hand. Perfectly upright, perfectly even, every letter formed exactly as it had been formed in 1961, and the signature under it printed in capitals, Salvatore Castellano, as clear as a page in a register.

He had gone back to the beginning of himself to write it. He had done the only truly honest thing he had done in sixty years in the best handwriting he had, so that whoever found it in whatever basement it ended up in would know, beyond argument, that it had come from a real man.


*• • •*

After the interview at the western facility on the eighteenth of February, the Office wrote to them.

It came to the Antwerp company, forwarded by the man who signs for everything, and Roux opened it in the filing hall at four in the afternoon and read it out to five people, because that was the arrangement the crew had made in February and it turned out to be the last arrangement that mattered.

> Dear Correspondent,

>

> In relation to the entry under correspondence, a person was interviewed at the western facility on the eighteenth of February and has been entered in the record as a respondent.

>

> The person concerned has declined to provide particulars of next of kin. The person concerned has stated that he does not wish to be collected.

>

> The person concerned has requested that his profession be entered as maritime. The request has been granted.

>

> He is accommodated pending completion of the record.

>

> There is nothing you need to do.

The room sat with it.

"It has a typo," said Garrity, after a while, and everybody looked at him, and he pointed at the sheet with one finger. "Wrong word for a man in a room with no door. Accommodated."

"He requested it," said Roux, quietly. "That's what that line means. He asked them to write maritime. He is still doing it. He is sitting in there doing the only thing he has ever known how to do."
