She put the page down. "And he told them he does not wish to be collected, so that whatever we decide to do about it, we decide it against him and not with him. That is the most expensive sentence he has ever written and he wrote it in there."

Then the argument started.

It was not the argument Mara had expected. Nobody shouted. It was worse than shouting; it was a room full of people taking turns being exactly, unanswerably accurate.

Garrity said: "I should have driven. I could have driven the track in reverse and had him out at the lane before the second car got there, and we both know it, and I let a woman tell me the truth about my leg in a kitchen and I sat down and took it."

Kavanagh said: "I am the only man at this table who could have walked into that house in Dublin shoes and been believed. I have spent forty years being believed. I did not spend one hour of it on him."

Sal said: "I should have been the door. He was a surveyor with an opinion about moorings, which is a story. I am a fact." And then he stopped in the middle of a sentence for eleven seconds, with everybody watching, and finished it somewhere else entirely, and it was the cruellest thing that had happened to Sal Castellano since 1996: "You see. There it is. That is the reason and not the excuse."

Roux said: "It is my letter. I wrote the origin line on the schedule and I argued for it in this room, and the office found the house with the document I made."

Lin Shu said nothing. She got up and went to the door, and then she stopped, and she turned around, and she said the only sentence she had been saving, and she said it to Mara, and it was not forgiveness and it was not accusation and it was much worse than either.

"I bought a page out of a priest's register this morning, Ms. Voss, and now I have to find where to put it," she said, and went out of the room, and it was nineteen minutes before anybody said anything else.

Then they all looked at Mara.

El Búho was in the doorway. He had been standing there for the whole of it, and he had not said a word, and he was not going to.

"It was me," said Mara.

"Say it," said Garrity.

“I asked him to stand in the open, in the morning, in front of a camera, because it was the cheapest way to be invisible. He said yes; the arithmetic was mine.” Mara lifted the letter. “I will not call him a hero or say he volunteered. I asked him to stand where the light was. I will say that in every room until we are finished.”

The room was quiet.

"Good," said El Búho, from the doorway, at last. "Somebody has finally said a true sentence out loud in a building. It has taken eleven weeks." He looked at Mara, and then at all of them, and he did not come in and he did not sit down and he did not offer to help, because his refusals were real and this was the third of them and he had meant every word. "Now you know what it costs. Ask your next question with that number in the room, and I will come to that meeting."


## Chapter Twenty-Five - The Dublin Rules

*Days 108–110*

The two of them had been in the same building for four months and had said perhaps forty sentences to each other, and every one of the forty had been about somebody else.

Mara watched the two men in the van for an hour and a half. For forty minutes neither spoke except to ask about the weather, twice, in different ways, and give the same answer. Neither closed the loop; a man who closes it has admitted a conversation has begun.

"They've sent a letter to the farm," said Kavanagh, eventually. "Dessie telephoned. It is addressed to me by name, with a registration number after it, which is a thing that has not happened in eleven years, and Niamh has put it in a drawer and told nobody, which is exactly what I would have done at thirteen." He turned a page of a newspaper he had already read. "I find I am looking forward to reading it."

"That is because you have not read it," said El Búho.

"No," Kavanagh agreed. "That is the whole pleasure of letters."

Then they were quiet again, and Mara drove, and the two of them fell asleep within a minute of each other somewhere past Kells, and woke at the same moment on the far side of Drogheda as though somebody had rung a bell, and neither of them mentioned it.


*• • •*

She had asked the question before they left, standing in the yard with the keys in her hand, because she had decided in December that she would not be a person who drove men to things without knowing what the thing was.

"Where in Dublin."

"A flat in Ranelagh. Number fourteen, top floor."

"Whose."

"My son's," said Kavanagh, and then, in the plainest voice he had used with her since the first day, in the rain, on a farm: "Ruairí. He was thirty-seven when he died. It was four years ago in October. I pay the rent on it and I have been in it eleven times since, and I have never once been able to sit in the chair."

So that was the day's business, and Mara understood that it had been the day's business since August, since before the beginning, since the man in the yard with the bad hip and the one reason to leave had put his name at the top of a list of six and then spent four months arranging to be taken to a room he could not enter alone.

The stairs took eleven minutes: three flights, a hip and a cane. Neither man would stop first. One described the building, the other the light; at the second landing they stopped together to look out. “It is a good stair,” said El Búho. “A very good stair,” Kavanagh agreed. Neither looked at Mara.

At the top Kavanagh put the key in the lock and did not turn it. He stood there with his hand on the door like a man checking a horse's leg.

"May I say the thing first," he said. "There are rules about this room, and they are mine, and I would like them observed. Nobody is to be kind. Nobody is to say he was young or that he had time or that it was a shame. He would have found any of those sentences obscene and he would have said so in writing, and he was better with a sentence than either of you, and that is not grief, my dear, that is fact."

He turned the key and let them in.


*• • •*

The flat was four rooms and a bathroom and it had not been lived in for four years and it was clean, because a woman came on Thursdays, and Mara stood for a moment in the middle of the front room and did the thing she had been trained to do, which was to read a life off surfaces.

A street window, a desk, two shelves of worn law books, a photograph of Kavanagh and a small boy on a pony, undated, in a cheap frame from 1993. The chair beside the desk faced whoever stood at the window; Mara recognized the flat’s only furniture placed on purpose.

And on the wall at the end of the desk, at the height of a reader's eye, in a plain frame with no mount, a piece of card.

Kavanagh saw her look at it.

"You may not read that," he said. "Not out loud. Not in this room."

"Why not," said El Búho.

“Because I have never read it,” said Kavanagh. “I have held it four years. It hung there while he was alive. I looked at the frame eleven times a year and never took it down; a father who does that has decided to have an opinion.” He sat, hands on his knees. “In sixty-eight years, that is the thing I have never done.”

El Búho crossed the room and took it off the nail.

"Rinaldo," said Mara.

“It is a card in a frame. The writer is dead; the man who will not read it is in a room being watched by a chair.” El Búho held it to the light. “You brought me here. I did not ask or want to come, and I have not been in Dublin since 1994. I will read it: it is the one thing I am still useful for, saying a true sentence about a dead man before his father.”

He read it.

It was a rectangle of card, handwritten, ruled with a pencil and a straight edge — the boy or the man had ruled the lines himself — and across the top, in capitals, it said THE DUBLIN RULES. Underneath, in two hands: the rules in a firm, quick script, and after each one, in a smaller, newer, later hand, an amendment.

"One," said El Búho. "Never be the man on the page." A pause. "He is right. It works. Nobody has looked for him in forty years. Amendment: be on the page, and make the page undeniable."

Kavanagh sat very still.

"Two. Never need anything in a room." El Búho's voice did not change at all, and it was the first time Mara had ever heard him do what he did with weather, with dogs, with children. "Amendment: need something, out loud, once a day, or you will end up needing nothing at all and calling it strength."

"Stop," said Kavanagh.

"Three. Give every man the version of himself he is looking for. Amendment: give him the version he is afraid of, on paper, signed, dated, delivered. Four. Never lie where a true sentence will do. Truth costs less and lasts longer." El Búho paused longer over that one than the others. "Amendment: agreed, wholly. This one he got right. There is an underlined line and two exclamation marks, which is what a serious man does when he agrees with his father."

"Rinaldo."

"Five. Keep the price out of the room. Amendment: the price is the only thing in the room that belongs to the client. Put it on the table first and let him hold it. Six. Never bring the work home."

El Búho stopped.

Under rule six there was no amendment. There was a space of about three inches, and then, in a different ink, in a hand that had thickened the way any hand thickens, dated in the corner — Sept. 2021 — one line.

I was born in the house the work was brought home to.

Nobody in the room said anything for a long time. Down in the street a bin lorry went along Ranelagh, and a seagull set up its complaint on a roof, and Kavanagh sat in the armchair with his hands on his knees and looked at the wall like a man watching a race he had already lost money on.

“He wrote that in September,” he said at last. “He put a date on it. He was thirty-six and he dated a sentence on a card, the habit of a man raised in a house where everything was written down.” He stood slowly, took the card from El Búho’s hands, looked at it, put it back on the nail and squared it. “I have a daughter in Cork who does not speak to me, a granddaughter who prices me at forty paces, and a son who has been on that wall for four years telling me what I was. I kept the rent on this flat because the alternative was a landlord in Ranelagh putting his furniture in a skip, and that is the entire content of my fatherhood.” He sat again. “You have an envelope in your coat. I have watched you check it twice a day for four months like a man checking a wound. I have not asked, because I have spent my life not asking, and this is the room in which I stop.”


*• • •*

El Búho took it out and put it on the desk, face down, in the light, with his hand flat on it, and did not open it, and told him everything: Nuria and the eleven days; the house outside Morelia; the hatch under the washing, and a girl of nine asking how far the tunnel went; the captain and six men in 1992 and a seven-year-old who held a room of armed men with one word; and a car park in Querétaro in 2014, nine minutes, and a sentence in a coat better than anything he had ever owned — I am going to spend the rest of my life taking apart men like you — and a man who said nothing at all.

He told it in eleven minutes, in the flat, in the particular flat register that this room had established, and when he had finished, Kavanagh said the thing that had been in his mouth since the stairs.

"You have a daughter who is alive."

"Yes."

“Permit me the ordinary arithmetic,” said Kavanagh. “You have a daughter in a building with a door, and you have spent twelve years forty kilometres from the road that leads to her. You are not in hell; you are refusing to leave it. I paid for my son with everything I had. That is tragedy. You have paid for your daughter with nothing. That is a decision.”

El Búho looked at the envelope under his hand.

“When I was thirty,” he said, “I wrote eleven rules in a notebook in a room in Morelia. Not six. Eleven. The first was: never be seen by a man who can describe you; the second, never be owed; the fourth, the boy must not know; and the ninth was—” He stopped. “If they come for the house, close the door and count what you lose.” He looked up. “You read your son’s card in my voice, and I heard my own notebook. That is why I was the one who took it off the wall. You think it was cruelty. It was recognition, and I would like that recorded, because I have never been offered a true sentence about myself, and I am too old to pretend it felt like anything other than being paid.”

"It was not paid," said Kavanagh. "You have not finished the transaction. You have a letter in your hand and a daughter who is alive, and the only absolution available to either of us in the world we made is a delivery." He nodded at the envelope. "Open it. In this room. Now. I will sit here, and the young woman will sit on that chair, and you will read to us what your wife wrote to your daughter in 1996, and then one of us will say something ordinary, and we will go downstairs and have a cup of tea."

"No," said El Búho.

"Why not."

"Because that letter is the last door I have," said El Búho, "and a man who opens every door in the world behind him does not arrive anywhere. He is just a draught." He put the envelope back inside his coat. "And because it is not addressed to you. It is addressed to her, and I have not decided whether she should have it, and I will not decide in a room that belongs to your dead son."

Kavanagh looked at him for eleven seconds. Then he laughed — once, briefly, without any humour in it at all, a sound like something being set down carefully.

"That," he said, "is the best answer either of us has given today, and it is also a lie dressed as an answer, and I am going to let it stand, because I am sixty-eight and I have a hip and I have never once been able to afford to be right about a man who agrees with me."


*• • •*

They had the tea in a place on the corner, and it was raining, and Kavanagh told El Búho about the 1974 filly and El Búho told Kavanagh about a drainage cut in Michoacán, and Mara sat between them and understood that they were two men in their sixties and seventies performing a conversation so that neither of them would have to say the actual last thing, and that the actual last thing had already been said upstairs, by both of them, in the same room, within four minutes of each other.

The letter was waiting for them at the annex when they got back at eight o'clock, because Niamh had not kept it in the drawer after all — she had put it in a fresh envelope, copied out Kavanagh's address in her own round hand, and posted it, with a note of her own on a separate sheet that said: Dessie says you should see it. I read it. It is only a form. N.

It was one page. On the office's paper, with a reference block, and it was addressed to him by name and registration number, and it said that his request of the second of March had been recorded, that his interest in the matter was noted and held, that the outcome would be communicated to him in the ordinary course, and that no further action was required of him at this time.

"It's a receipt," said Kavanagh.

"It's worse than a receipt," said Roux, from the doorway, reading over his shoulder with her arms crossed. "A receipt says you bought something. This says they know you were interested. In eleven years, that office has never once been interested in a person's interest. It does not track people. It tracks behaviour. And you have just taught it that a man in this county has a behaviour about a category of person."

"I taught it nothing."

"You signed a page, Mr. Kavanagh," said Roux. "You told the crew in November that you would not sign your own name, and you said it in front of all of us, and you held it for four months, and then you signed your name on a form to get a man out of a room, and the form worked, and it was the best thing anybody in this crew has done, and it cost us the only thing you had." She took the page off the table and read it again. "You are in the register now. Congratulations. You are a person with an interest. That is how everybody in the world gets found."

Kavanagh took the page back and folded it once and put it in the inside pocket of the good coat, on the left, where a man keeps a document he intends to have opinions about.

"I find," he said, "that I do not mind at all."

And Mara, going up the stairs an hour later with the ledger under her arm and Sal’s unsent letter under that, thought about the two of them — an old man with a card on a wall in Ranelagh, and an old man with a sealed envelope in his coat — and about the fact that both of them had asked her, in the last four days, in different rooms, to hold something, and that neither of them had actually handed it over. There is no such thing as a certainty. There are only prices, and she had begun to notice that in this crew everybody paid in the same currency, and that the currency was a piece of paper a man keeps in a pocket instead of doing the thing that the paper is for.


## Chapter Twenty-Six - The Plan

*Days 110–115*

They held it in the filing hall because it was the only room in the building where nine people could sit around one table, and because half the shelves behind them were empty now, which was its own kind of briefing.

Mara put the four lines on the board in her own handwriting, the same four she had written in February before the Second Run. She had not intended to. She wrote them because the room needed to see that nothing had changed, and because she had spent four months learning that a crew is not held together by hope, it is held together by a format.

1. Physical. A thing the machine has no form for does not move.

2. Human. Nobody travels as cargo. A person is a name on a page in a building with a door.

3. Bureaucratic. Never create an absence. Create a lateness.

4. Diagnostic. Do not become a second occurrence.

Then she turned the board over, and on the back she wrote the thing that had been sitting in her chest since the seventy-ninth day, and she wrote it before anybody could say a word about the machine.

5. Her.

“Every plan for six months has been for CUSTOS. It is not the only thing at the end. On 22 January a woman signed 411 exceptions; last week routine cases were taken off her desk without asking. We could win against the machine and hand her a perfect file about her father in the building where she works.” She capped the pen. “The plan has five parts. The fifth is the point.”


*• • •*

Roux did the shape of it, because Roux did shapes, and she did it in eleven minutes with three colours.

“The objective is not to break CUSTOS. We cannot break it; anyone who says otherwise has not listened for four months. The objective is to make it do a thing it has never done, in front of a person, so the person can say it aloud.” She raised the sheet. “Staging Facility Nine: two hundred and eleven aircraft, eleven thousand movements a month, the north-door register. The beds have no published number; this is the only thing the system has kept from itself. El Búho said in January that a man who becomes a second occurrence becomes a place. We have spent four months learning he was right.”

"The intake is on the south," said Garrity.

“The intake is south, the movement schedule east, the accommodation register north, and the correspondence archive under the block. Three objectives, three physical facts. The uncounted beds are the only thing the system has been ashamed of.”

Mara looked at the three lines for a while.

"You want to put the beds on the record," she said.

“Thirty-eight hundred and forty-one residents are under the north door tonight,” said Roux. “The Office does not publish that number. The weekly 3,412 is movements, a different figure. The difference is a small town. You cannot govern a system that counts its beds out loud; that is why it has not.”

"And the archive is the leverage," said Mara.

"The archive is the voice," said Roux. "Every letter that office ever wrote, including the ones to the confraternity, including the one about a dead tally clerk, including the one to Kavanagh's granddaughter with a child's writing on the envelope. If the letters exist somewhere, then the system can be shown, on paper, to have decided things, in a way that a court — or a bulletin — or a person — can read."

"And the movement schedule."

“The movement schedule gives us the timing,” said Roux. “At dawn on 2 April, 411 units leave the north block for the west as it is re-racked for spring intake. The routing notices are public; nobody has hidden anything by putting it in one.” She put down the pen. “Four hundred and eleven. I know.”


*• • •*

El Búho did the doctrine, because he had asked for it, and because it was the last piece of teaching he had in him, and it was the only time in six months that he had spoken standing up with the whole crew in the room.

"Four doors and four kinds of invisibility. You have known them since February. I am going to say the fourth one again, because you have all forgotten it."

"Say it," said Mara.

“Roux is right about the intake,” said El Búho. “It has no published number. But we cannot make that number public by taking it. If a crew reads it out of a stolen file, it is a leak; a leak becomes scandal; a scandal is absorbed. In eleven weeks it is a controversy, in eleven months a documentary, in eleven years a respectable argument about the past.” He rested his hands on Mesa’s chair. “The number must be read by a person the system cannot call a source, in the Office’s own voice and register, because a route requires it. Not leaked. Not stolen. Required. A record made because a procedure ran cannot be called a scandal. It is the kind of record that lasts.”

"So we don't steal it," said Roux slowly.

“We cause it. A movement is already wrong; the Office must solve it with the register. At four in the morning, the register will print its own number on its own page, and that page will be in the archive — your third door.”

Garrity said, "That's the whole plan. He's just described the whole plan."

“It is a plan by a man who will not be there,” said El Búho. “The four doors are three; the fourth is a person. Any plan that treats her as a door will fail. I have watched it fail nine times.”

Nobody looked at Mara. Everybody knew what he meant.


*• • •*

Garrity reported the aeroplane, because somebody had to, and it was the shortest report in the history of the crew.

"The Tractor is in the barn. It hasn't flown since February and it will not fly again, and I am not going to sit here and describe a way to put four hundred kilos of anything into the air, because I did that in December and it cost us a wrist and a leg and it very nearly cost us the fat man's life." He had his stick across his knees and the dog's chin on the stick. "So here is what I have that is worth something. I have forty years of flying freight into places that did not have a runway. And I will tell you now, for nothing, the thing that no one in this room has said: the only aircraft at Kestrel Field on the twenty-third will be parked, empty, uncrewed, and counted. Two hundred and eleven of them."

"Counted by what," said Roux.

"Racked, numbered, and on a sheet," said Garrity. "Every one of them is a line in the movement schedule, and every line is a tail number, and every tail number is a thing that somebody has to look at and tick. That building does not count people. It counts frames. That is what an airfield office is for."

Roux looked up.

"If the frames are the schedule," she said, "then the frames are the only thing in that building that anybody is ever going to look at twice."

"And there is a maintenance hangar at the west end," said Garrity, "because there is always a maintenance hangar at the west end, and it will have a crew office and a locker room and a door that opens onto the apron, and it will be full of equipment that a man of my age can describe in his sleep."


*• • •*

Lin Shu did the human door, and she did it last, and she did it standing.

"The person we take in goes in as staff. Not as a document, not as a parcel. As a woman with a clipboard who has a work order that says she must inspect the accommodation block's fire doors before the re-rack, and who will be on the schedule at the gate because her employer is a contractor that has a real registration and therefore a real entry, and whose name will be mine."

"Your name is in a register," said Kavanagh, quietly. "You signed an undertaking in a port."

“My name is in a register because someone needed a receiving person. Now it will be there because a building needs a fire officer. I am a contractor, the invisible person everyone thinks is somebody else’s problem.” Lin Shu put down a form. “The company dates to 2011; it has a tax reference, a bank account and a safety file. The Office will see a woman from Ballinasloe who does fire doors.”

"And the four hundred and eleven," said Mara.

"They move at dawn," said Lin Shu. "I will be in the block two hours before them, with a torch and a clipboard and a work order, and I will walk every bed space in that building and write down what is in it, and the torch and the clipboard are the whole of my invisibility, and the reason nobody will stop me is that in every institution on earth the person who is supposed to be there is the person who is never asked."


*• • •*

They argued for two hours about the fourth door, and nobody won, which was itself the answer.

Rask wanted to take the archive physically in a van; Roux said a removed page is a stolen page and the hole will be filled with a number. Garrity wanted the Tractor, though even he knew that was sentiment. Kavanagh wanted to walk into the front office until Lin Shu reminded him, “You are a person with an interest. The Office has noticed you.” Mesa offered the port face; Mara said no. They had two plans and no sensible bridge between them. Then Lin Shu asked why they could not tie it to a route, because a number produced by the route would have to be made by the Office. Mesa did not fight her. It was the most eloquent thing in the room.

And it was Mara who finally said it, standing at the back of the board with the pen in her hand, at about half past ten, with the room tired and the tea cold and the ventilation going.

“There is a fifth line I have avoided since January,” she said. “Every plan in this room ends with a page in a register, and a page in a register changes nothing until somebody with authority reads it and decides. Someone must stand before the woman who signs the exceptions, with the number and the file. Not a courier, not a message or letter she can bury. A person, in a room, with nothing left to gain.” She looked around the table. “The last part is not technical.”

"Then it is you," said Roux.

"No," said Mara.

"Who—"

"Ask her father," said Mara, "because I am not going to be the one who decides that for him. But whichever of us goes into that room, that is the plan, and I want every one of you to say out loud tonight that you know it, because I have spent four months watching people in this crew arrive at the last minute and discover, with enormous sincerity, that they thought somebody else was going to do it."

The room was quiet for a moment.

Then Sal Castellano said, from the end of the table, in the exact voice, with the letter book closed in front of him:

"There is one more item and I would like it recorded, and then I am going to bed."


*• • •*

He put the sheet of paper on the table. Four lines, in the small upright hand, the address block perfect and complete, and Mara had carried it up the stairs four nights earlier and put it under the mattress on the far side and had not touched it since, which meant that he had gone into her room and taken it back, and that was a sentence that nobody in the hall said out loud.

“Two weeks,” said Sal. “My application has been under a mattress since the nineteenth of March. I will send it myself on 2 April, with the movement. If I am wrong about the ending, the register will say a man here asked to be revisited while he could still speak.”

"Sal—" Mara began.

“I am not asking permission,” said Sal politely. “I am making a disclosure. I have spent sixty-four years being the man who writes letters. I have never announced one to a room before sending it, and I find I would like to try.” He counted them with his eyes. “On 2 April there will be an annex with two hundred and eleven items of ash in a wheelbarrow, a vault of shelf dust and a collection contract that ends that day. A complication is a man who has to be interviewed at an address.” He lifted the letter book. “I know what happens to a complication. It is not taken away. It is accommodated.”

Mara looked at him for a long moment and then did the thing she had been learning to do since February, which was to take a person at his word.

"Say the rest of it," she said.

“I have two hundred and eleven items of ash, and I know every call number; I wrote them. When the contractor ticks an empty shelf on the manifest, he will be ticking against my hand.” Sal looked around the room. “If the plan needs a building confused about its contents, I am the only one who can confuse it usefully.”

The ventilation stack moved air over nine people in a filing hall.

Then El Búho said, "Ask your question, Ms. Voss."

And Mara, standing at the board with four lines in her handwriting and a fifth on the back, asked the question that was the whole plan, and asked it to the room, and got the only answer that any of them had left to give.


## Chapter Twenty-Seven - The Port

*Days 118–121*

They left the annex on the twenty-ninth, and the leaving took four hours and was almost entirely a matter of paper.

The vault was shelf dust. Forty kilometres of microfilm had gone out of that room in forty boxes a day for two weeks, and what was left was the smell — the particular sweet chemical smell that a records room keeps for years after the records have gone — and two hundred and eleven items of ash in a wheelbarrow that Sal had covered with a tarpaulin and weighted with a brick and would not let anybody move, because, he said, a wheelbarrow is a position, and a position is a thing you hold until the trucks come.

Roux took the last sheet off the wall of the filing hall, which was the schedule she had drawn in February with three colours, and folded it and put it in her coat, and Mara watched her do it and did not say anything.

Garrity loaded the dog and the stick and a crate of aviation manuals that nobody had asked him to bring. Mesa carried one small bag out to the van and stood beside it for four minutes with his hand on the roof, and then got in and did not look back at the building, and Mara thought about the fact that she had never once seen him look at anything he was leaving.

Lin Shu locked the north gate herself. Kavanagh stood in the yard in the good coat with his hands in his pockets and looked at the place the way a man looks at a house he has sold.

Sal Castellano was the last one in. He came out of the accounts office at ten past five with the bound book under his arm and a letter in his hand and set both of them on the seat beside him, and as the van went down the lane he said, in the exact voice, to nobody in particular:

"I have been in that building for five months and I have never once learned where the gas meter is. I would like that recorded. It is the only piece of information about this operation that anybody ever failed to obtain."


*• • •*

The port was four hours west and it arrived the way ports arrive, which is as a smell and then as a horizon and then as a thing that is much bigger than it looked from the road.

Mara had read the file on it in December, in nineteen pages, and the file was accurate and had nothing in it. A deepwater container terminal, decommissioned in the fourth year of the Programme when the world's freight moved to two hubs and did not come back. Eleven gantry cranes, parked. Forty hectares of yard, empty. And then, slowly, over eight years, the other thing: an airfield two kilometres inland that had been a maintenance base and was now Staging Facility Nine, and a terminal that had been nothing and was now the office's western accommodation block and archive, and a fence, and a road, and a schedule.

They came in along the coast road at dusk and Roux made Garrity stop the van on the hard shoulder, and they all got out into the wind and looked at it, and nobody said anything for a while because there was nothing to say that the thing did not say better than they could.

"It's a fortress," said Garrity.

"It's a records office," said Roux, "with a sea in front of it and a fence around it and two hundred and eleven aeroplanes parked in the yard, and if you cannot tell the difference between those two sentences then you have not been paying attention for four months."

Mara looked at it the way she looked at buildings, which was from the exits. There was one gate on the coast road, with a booth, and a barrier, and two men, and behind the barrier a long straight road running between the container yard and the water to the terminal, and then the airfield road turning east, and the whole thing ringed by the fence that the fence contractors put up in the seventh year when the office consolidated. Eleven gantry cranes standing over an empty yard like the ribs of something.

"A machine built to move people," she said, "living in a place built to move freight, and neither of them moving anything, and eleven cranes standing over it like a cathedral."

Kavanagh, from beside her, in the wind: "You have been in this trade for five months, my dear, and you have just said the truest sentence anybody has said about the century. Let us get out of the wind."


*• • •*

The house was in a village nine kilometres back up the coast: three bedrooms, a kitchen, a stove, and a woman called Bríd who had let it to a company that Lin Shu had created in 2011 and who was delighted to have it let in March.

They worked the last two days the way they had worked the first two weeks, which is to say badly and then well. Roux rebuilt the timings on the kitchen table with one sheet of paper and no colours. Lin Shu went in on the thirty-first for the reconnaissance, as a fire-door contractor, and came back at four in the afternoon with four pages of notes in a hand that got smaller and smaller as it went.

“Four hundred and fifty bed spaces on the ground floor, six hundred and eleven on the first. The upper floors are racked and empty. I walked them with a torch and a work order; nobody asked a question. The archive is in the basement, unlocked and unguarded. Its fire notice now bears my signature. That is the most illegal thing I have done, and nobody will notice.”

"Where is the movement schedule office," said Roux.

“East side of the terminal, ground floor: four desks and a printer,” said Lin Shu. “Contractors run them. The office is a rota. Two of four desks for the movement on the second will be staffed by outsiders. One is a man from Hangar 4 at Kestrel, on the schedule desk at four with a paper notebook. I could not read him; I have never seen a clerk do what he did.”

"What."

"He was reading the sheet before he ticked it," said Lin Shu.

Mara stood at the window with her hands in her pockets for eleven seconds.

"Leave him," she said. "If he reads the sheet, he reads the sheet. We are not going to be invisible to one tired man with a notebook. We are going to be ordinary to two hundred."


*• • •*

On the last afternoon, Mara walked to the fence with El Búho, because he had asked her to, and because she had known since January that there was a version of this week in which he would ask her for something and she had decided she would say yes to anything reasonable.

He stopped four hundred metres short of the gate, on the path along the water, where the ground turns from shingle to grass and a person standing still is a person watching the sea.

"That is where she works," he said. "Not the terminal. The airfield. Two kilometres inland, in a building I have never seen, at a desk I cannot describe, and there is a window and there is probably a plant on it, because she was the sort of child who kept things alive."

Mara said nothing, because she had learned that with this man the silence did the work.

“Twelve years,” said El Búho. “I have been within four hundred metres of my daughter for two days and have not gone through that gate. In January I said I was finished, old, invisible. All true, none the reason. A man in Ranelagh told me so, and I had no answer.” He gripped the cane. “I have spent my life arriving after the damage, knowing what to do with the pieces. It is the only thing I am good at, and it is no use to a woman who was nine in Morelia and is now forty-one, running a province of this machine.” He looked at the fence. “You will tell her I was here?”

"If she asks," said Mara. "I am not going to volunteer it as a plea."

"No," said El Búho, and then, after a moment, in a voice Mara had never heard him use: "Thank you."

They stood there until the light went. The gantry cranes came up as black shapes against a sky the colour of a water tank, and somewhere behind them on the airfield a movement board turned over, and a van went along the inside road with its headlights on, and the two men on the gate went on not looking at anything, which is the single most professional thing anybody in that complex was doing.

“You asked me in January to be in the room,” said El Búho. “I will say this plainly, so you do not discover it in a corridor tomorrow. I will not go into that building. That is a fact, true since 1994. Whatever happens at dawn — in the basement, on the apron, at the fourth door — it happens to people younger than me. One is my daughter. She will be in a room I will never enter, and the last sentence I have for her has been in my pocket since 1996.”

"Then it is not finished," said Mara.

"No," said El Búho. "It has not even started."


*• • •*

They ate in the kitchen at nine, all seven of them, and it was a good hour, and it was the only hour in five months when nobody in the crew talked about the operation.

Garrity told a story about a horse. Mesa argued with him about the horse, at length, with both hands, in the theatrical register, and won, and Garrity conceded it the way a man concedes a horse race, and the whole table was laughing at the two of them and it was not a performance at all — and Mara, going for the tea, stopped in the doorway and looked back at all of them and thought: this is the last time.

Lin Shu went up at half past ten, because she was going in at four. Sal Castellano went up next, and on the stairs he stopped and came back down three steps and said, in the hazy voice, to the room, pleasantly, that he had enjoyed the evening, and that if anybody wanted the ledger it was in the green bag by the door.

Nobody said anything for a second, and then Roux said, "Why would anybody want the ledger, Sal?" and he looked at her with mild surprise and said, "For the record," and went up to bed.

Mara sat on the stairs for a while after that with her hands around a cup.

Then Roux came and sat down on the step below her with the schedule on her knee, and put the last of it in front of her in the flat voice she used for numbers, because that was the only thing she had ever been able to offer anybody.

“Four, Lin Shu enters with the work order and torch. Five fifteen, the movement starts. Five forty, the schedule desk is busiest. Dawn, 2 April.” She tapped the sheet. “One page, one number, one room, one woman. Everything this crew has been for six months depends on a leaf of paper that does not exist yet.”

"Say the number," said Mara.

"Three thousand eight hundred and forty-one," said Roux. "Counted in bed spaces by a woman who does fire doors, at half past two on the afternoon of the thirty-first of March, in a building with no published figure."

Mara looked at the sheet for a long time.

"Then that is what we put in the room," she said.


*• • •*

At eleven that night El Búho came down and went out into the yard of the rented house and stood looking east, and did not notice for a while that Mara had come out and was standing at the corner of the wall, and when he did notice he did not tell her to go in.

"There is a thing I want you to do tomorrow that has nothing to do with the operation," he said.

"Name it."

“If it goes wrong, and one of us is at the fence at seven with nothing to do, I want it to be me,” said El Búho. “Not because I am old or finished or get to be sorry. I have no part in the morning, and I have been that man in nine countries. I know how it is done. Sit. Look at the water. Do not telephone anyone.”

"That is a strange thing to ask."

"It is not a favour," said El Búho. "It is a job description." He turned and looked at her in the dark, and it was the closest thing to warmth that she had ever seen on his face. "You have spent five months thinking that I came to the hill because Kavanagh wrote to me. That is true, and it is not the whole of it, and you will find out the rest of it in a building tomorrow, and when you do I want you to remember that I asked you for one small thing the night before."

Mara looked at him.

"Which is?"

"Walk in front of me," said El Búho. "Whatever room we end up in, whatever door, whoever is on the other side. Walk in front of me, and do not look back, and do not wait."

And Mara Quinn Voss, standing in a yard in a village nine kilometres up the coast from a fortress full of paper, in the last hour of the last quiet day, said, "I will."


*• • •*

Roux found the last thing on the table in the small hours, because Roux found everything last, and she found it in the letters.

They had the correspondence in three bags by then — the office's own paper, the confraternity's, and the annex's — and she was going through the third bag because she could not sleep and because a woman with a cast off and a schedule finished has nothing to do with her hands.

The letter was at the bottom, under a hotel bill and two pages of a safety file, and it had a child's round hand on the envelope and a Dublin postmark and a name in the corner.

"Kavanagh."

He came down in his shirt with the good coat over it, because that was how he slept in a strange house, and he stood at the table and read it twice and put it down.

"Ah," he said.

“It went through the office,” said Roux. “Your granddaughter’s note went to the annex, was forwarded, and came back here. A nine-line note from a thirteen-year-old passed through three organizations. The office read the envelope and added a routing block to the back.” She looked at Kavanagh. “Do you understand what that means?”

Kavanagh turned the envelope over. There was a bar of characters in the corner, small, neat, identical in family to the bar on the bottom of a letter about a dead tally clerk in January.

"They do not know who she is," said Roux.

“They know somebody wrote to a person with an interest, from County Meath, in a child’s hand.” Kavanagh put the envelope in his coat. “For eleven years the machine had no reason to model a thirteen-year-old. Now it has a paper trail saying a child in that county wrote to a man it is tracking.” He sat carefully. “In November I said there are only prices. I was being clever about a system I had never met. Every price is paid by someone who was not in the room when it was agreed.”

He went back up at four, and Roux sat with the bags until the sky over the water started doing the thing it does at the end of a night, and then she went and stood in the doorway of the room where Mara was pretending to sleep and said the only thing anybody said in that house before dawn.

"It is the second of April," said Roux.

"I know," said Mara.


## Chapter Twenty-Eight - Four Doors

*Day 122 — the second of April*

Mara was awake at twenty to four with the light on and her shoes already on, which is what a person does on the day: get dressed in the dark, then sit in a lit kitchen waiting to be allowed to leave.

Mesa had made coffee at three and left it on the ring, which was either a kindness or a superstition and was probably both. Mara drank a cup standing up and looked at the four lines she had copied out of the filing hall onto the back of a receipt, in a hand that was too small for the paper.

Physical. Human. Bureaucratic. Diagnostic.

Roux came down at ten to four with a bag of paper and a face like a woman going to a meeting, and Kavanagh came down at four minutes to, in the good coat, with the car keys already in his hand, and did not sit down.

"Four o'clock," he said. "She will be at the gate."

Mara put the cup in the sink, and the four of them went out into the yard, and the last thing she saw from the door was El Búho sitting at the kitchen table with his hands around a cup he had not touched, in his coat, with the cane across his knees, not saying anything, because he was not coming.


*• • •*

1. Physical — a thing the machine has no form for does not move.

The west maintenance hangar at Kestrel Field had a parts door, a line shack, a compressor that had not run since the seventh year, and a laminated sheet on the wall listing the two hundred and eleven frames and the parts they were waiting for — which Garrity had spent two evenings reconstructing from memory, from a photograph, and from what a maintenance base needs and therefore has.

He went in at half four in the passenger seat of a van with a magnetic sign on the door that said AERO PARTS — MIDLANDS — 24HR, which was a real company with a real number that answered to a man in Athlone who had been paid four hundred euros to have a bad week with his phone off.

Mesa drove. That was the arrangement, and it had taken an hour to agree: Rafael Mesa, sixty-nine years old, of Veracruz, had spent fifty years walking into offices he had no business in, and he had no paperwork for this one beyond a delivery note for a nose-wheel assembly that did not exist, for a frame that did exist, on a sheet a bored storeman would look at for four seconds before writing a time on it and going back to his phone.

They were inside nineteen minutes. Mara never saw it; she heard it twice afterwards, in Mesa's voice, and both times he told it as nothing, and both times he stopped at the same place — a storeman of about twenty-four looking at the note, looking at the two of them, and saying, "You're the lads for the eleven," which was a sentence about a different van and a different day. Garrity said, "We are the lads for the eleven." The man wrote a time on the sheet and went back to his phone.

The Tractor stayed in the barn in Meath, under the cover, and nobody mentioned it.

They came out with two things. The first was that the west gate — the parts gate, the one nobody counted — had a barrier raised at 5:10 every morning for a fuel lorry and lowered again at 5:25, and that for fifteen minutes the service road was the emptiest road in the county.

The second was a laminated sheet off a wall, which Garrity took because a sheet on a wall in a line shack is not a document. It is a wall. It went into the van under the dog.


*• • •*

2. Human — a person is never a unit; a person is a name on a page in a building with a door.

Lin Shu went in at four o'clock exactly, at the contractor gate, in a hi-vis, with a torch, a clipboard, a work order, and her own name, and the guard on the contractor gate did not look up from his phone for more than two seconds, because a woman in a hi-vis at four in the morning is not a person. She is a shift.

She did the work first, and she was insistent about that afterwards, and it was the thing that made Mara unable to speak for a while.

She walked the ground floor’s four hundred and fifty bed spaces with a torch, ticking every fire door on the work order. People slept in the beds. Lin Shu said it was the first building full of people recorded as sleeping there that she had entered; the quiet was different, full of people told everything was all right.

At ten past five she came out of the block and stood in the lee of the wall with her clipboard and did the thing she had come for.

The fire declaration was one page on her own company's paper, with a reference block Roux had made in February and never used, addressed to the building's facilities office: a periodic inspection, a statement of the means of escape, a list of defects, and — because a fire officer cannot certify a means of escape without stating the number of persons the building is arranged to accommodate — an occupancy figure.

3,841 persons, first and second floors, as arranged.

She put it in the facilities mailbox beside the loading bay, a metal box with a sticker on it that said FACILITIES — FORMS IN, FORMS OUT, which was checked at eight. Then she went out of the contractor gate at 5:22 with her torch and her clipboard and her name, and the guard looked up that time and said, "Busy one?" and Lin Shu said, "Fire doors, first and second," and the guard said, "Rather you than me," and signed her off.


*• • •*

3. Bureaucratic — never create an absence. Create a lateness.

The schedule office was four desks and a printer on the east side of the terminal, and by half past five it was the loudest room in the port.

Roux was in it, and she had argued her way in with the only line nobody could beat: "I am a woman with a cast on her wrist who comes to fix the rota software, which is exactly the sort of person every institution fails to notice, because the institution is always waiting for a woman with a cast on her wrist to fix the rota software." The cast had come off in February. She had put it back on for the morning.

She sat at the corner desk with a laptop and a lanyard and a face she had spent four years learning to have, and for two hours she did nothing at all, which was the hardest part, and she watched a man from Hangar 4 read a sheet before he ticked it.

The movement went at five fifteen, and by twenty to six the desk was doing what a schedule desk does at its busiest hour, which is not verification but repair. Two escort vans had gone to the wrong road. A driver had called in about a barrier. A manifest had been printed with a block reference that did not exist — the crew's work, arranged two days earlier through a man in Ballinasloe, and the most expensive part of the operation, and the least dramatic: it cost eleven euros.

And at twenty to six, in the only room in the building that was never allowed to be wrong, somebody at the second desk said, without looking up, "We've got a facilities form saying three eight four one in the north block."

The desk went quiet in the particular way desks go quiet when a number is wrong.

"That's the accommodation figure. That's not our sheet. We move units."

"It's on a fire declaration. They've certified the building for three eight four one and our block record says two thousand nine hundred and sixty-one."

"Then the fire lot counted the racked floors."

"Racked floors are empty."

The man from Hangar 4 — Okafor, and Roux had known his name since the first week of December, and had never once said it out loud, because a name is a thing you give away — stopped reading the sheet he was ticking and looked up.

"Say the two numbers again," he said.

"Two nine six one on the block record. Three eight four one on the fire declaration."

He wrote something in a notebook. He did it without any drama at all, the way a man writes down a bus timetable, and then he said the sentence that decided the morning, and Roux, sitting six feet away with a laptop on her knees, had to keep her face absolutely still.

"Then one of them is a record of a building and one of them is a record of people," said Okafor. "The block record counts rack positions. The fire declaration counts persons." He looked at the sheet for a second and a half. "Has anybody ever reconciled those two numbers?"

Nobody had. In eleven years, nobody had, because the two records lived in different systems and neither had a reason to speak to the other, and reconcile was a word that cost money, produced an argument, and solved nothing on a Tuesday.

Then the supervisor came over and did what any supervisor in the world does when two numbers disagree at twenty to six in the morning: opened a routing entry, typed both figures into it, attached the fire declaration, and sent it to the office with a note that said, in full:

Discrepancy: block record vs fire declaration. Movement unaffected. For reconciliation. No action required of this desk.

And the register took it, because that is what a register does with a discrepancy, and Roux watched a small grey line appear at the bottom of a screen that nobody in the room would ever look at again, and the line said, in the office's own language and the office's own font:

Accommodation figure declared: 3,841. Reconciliation opened. Reference as below.

Roux said, out loud, to nobody in particular, in the voice of a woman fixing a rota: "That's not going to be quick." The supervisor said, "Nothing is," and went for a cigarette, and the desk went back to the escort vans.

It was in the register at eleven minutes to six. It could not be taken out. It was a procedure.


*• • •*

4. Diagnostic — do not become a second occurrence.

Mara's door was the fourth one, and her job was to be nobody.

She sat in a lay-by two kilometres up the coast road in a hired car with Kavanagh and the dog, and her job was to watch the west gate and the contractor gate and the coast road and to notice the thing nobody else in the crew could see — because she was the only person in the operation not doing anything, and a person not doing anything is a person who can see.

At 5:22 Lin Shu left the contractor gate without looking at the lay-by. At 5:26 the fuel lorry passed the raised barrier, which came down four minutes early. At 5:40 the sky turned grey into day; two gannets went along the water. “I have never been this early for anything,” Kavanagh said. “Don’t,” said Mara.

She saw the van at 5:51.

A plain white van came up the coast road from the south, the wrong direction. It passed the gate and lay-by without slowing. Mara watched it in the wing mirror and wrote on the back of the receipt: White van, no markings, southbound→north, 05:51. Two occupants.

At 6:04 her phone buzzed once. It was Roux, from inside, from the rota desk, with the only message she would send all morning, and it was three words long.

It's in. Clear.

Mara sat in a lay-by with a dog’s chin on her knee. They had put a true number into a system with no field for it, at the busiest hour of its week, on a document a stranger had to reconcile. The office entered it in its own register; removing 3,841 now required somebody to sign.

"Do we go," said Kavanagh.

"Wait for the second message."

There was no second message.

At twenty past six Lin Shu came up the coast road on foot, because she had walked out of the village to meet them, and got into the back of the car, and sat with her hands in her lap for a moment.

"There is a man with a notebook," she said.

"At the desk."

"At the desk. He read the sheet before he ticked it and he wrote the two numbers on the inside cover of an exercise book, and then he asked the room whether the two figures had ever been reconciled, and a woman typed it into a screen." She looked out at the fence. "And when I was signing out, he was standing at the contractor gate."

Mara turned round in her seat.

“He was at the gate,” said Lin Shu. “Not on duty. He watched me hand in the clipboard and read my name upside down when the guard wrote it down. I have seen that in Jakarta; clerks do not do it by accident.” She put her hands on her knees. “He said nothing, but he was the only person there with a notebook. I think he wrote my name down.”

The three of them sat in the lay-by for a moment. The dog put its head on Kavanagh's arm.

"Then we have the diagnostic door open," said Mara.

"He has my name."

"He has your name, Lin Shu, which is on a work order for fire doors in a company that has been filing safety returns since 2011, and which he can check, and which will check out, because it is real." Mara looked at the fence, at the two gannets, at the white light going along the top of the water. "The diagnostic door is the fourth one and it is the only one we cannot close. We always knew that. El Búho told us in February that the fourth one is a person."

"So what do we do."

"We finish," said Mara, "and we are quick, and we do not do anything else in that building ever again." She looked at her phone. "Roux is still inside. She stays until eleven, doing the rota software, doing it properly, because a woman who leaves early is a woman who was never there."


*• • •*

At ten past seven, El Búho was standing at the fence.

He had walked the nine kilometres, which nobody had asked him to do and which took an hour and forty minutes, and he was at the place where the shingle turns to grass, four hundred metres short of the gate, hands folded on the cane, doing the job he had asked for: watching the water, not telephoning anybody, and looking in the morning light exactly like a man of seventy-four taking the air on a coast road, which is the most invisible thing in Europe.

Mara did not go to him. That was the arrangement, and it was his arrangement, and she honoured it, and she watched him from the lay-by for about four minutes through a wing mirror, and then Kavanagh drove them north to the house, and the day began, and everything in the operation was in place.

Except the room.

The number was in the register, the page in a basement under sleeping people, and every part of the plan had worked. Still, two kilometres inland, a woman who had signed four hundred and eleven exceptions in January was at her desk, doing what she had been taught: making the system kind.

Sal had posted his letter at five fifteen, at the post box in the village, at the same hour as the movement, exactly as he had told the room he would, and nobody had watched him do it, because he had asked nobody to watch, and when Mara got back to the house at half past seven the green bag was gone from beside the door and Sal Castellano was sitting at the kitchen table in the cardigan with his hands wrapped around a cup, and he looked up and said, in the exact voice, entirely present:

"Good morning. I have done the thing I said I would do, and I have had a cup of tea, and I find that I am extremely tired, and I would like to sit in that chair for the rest of the day."

Which he did.


## Chapter Twenty-Nine - Casualties

*Days 122–131*

By nine in the morning Roux had established that the green bag was not in the house, the car, the shed, the coal store, or anywhere within four hundred metres — and that the only person who had left the property between one and five was Sal Castellano, who had walked to the post box at a quarter past five and been gone nineteen minutes.

"Four minutes each way," she said. "Eleven minutes unaccounted for, in a village of nine buildings, one of which is closed."

"I sat on the wall by the harbour. It is a good wall. I recommend it."

"The bag weighs eleven kilograms, Sal, and it cannot be concealed under a cardigan."

"No," Sal agreed. "It cannot."

Mara looked at him for a long moment. He was in the chair with the cup and had not moved out of it since half past seven, and he was, she noticed for the first time in five months, unhurried. Not calm. Unhurried. There is a difference, and it is the difference between a man who has decided something and a man who has not.

"Say it," she said.

"I placed it," said Sal Castellano, in the exact voice.

"Where."

"In the last consignment."

The room went quiet in a way that had nothing to do with volume.

"On the twenty-ninth. The manifest was four hundred lines long and every line had to be signed by an archivist. I entered the ledger as an item — one volume, personal papers, deposited at the owner's request, condition fair — gave it a call number in the four-C sequence, and put it in the last pallet of the last load. I signed for it at six o'clock with my own hand. It went down the lane with two hundred and ninety kilos of parish registers."

Roux sat down slowly, which was the first time Mara had ever seen her do that in a crisis.

"You put the one document in Europe with every name in this operation on it into the collection batch of a records office." said Roux, sitting down slowly, which was the first time Mara had ever seen her do that in a crisis.

“It was the collection batch, not the destruction batch. I have kept that distinction for sixty-four years.” He squared the cup. “The Office cannot deaccession its own formation archive. My ledger is now the personal papers of a Catania confraternity correspondent, on a shelf in a basement four hundred metres from here. Nothing in it can be used against you: the archive belongs to the office that would have to prosecute, and it concludes people.”

"You cannot know that."

"I can know it precisely." Sal put the cup down. "I have read every letter that office has ever written to me."


*• • •*

Mara took the car to the headland and stood there nineteen minutes, because there was nowhere else, and came back to find El Búho sitting on the garden wall in his coat, waiting for her, which he had never once done in five months.

"He is right," said El Búho. "And he is lying. Both of those are true and I would like you to stop trying to decide which one matters."

"Explain."

“He is right about the archive. Give a document to the institution that cannot admit it kept it. He is lying about the reason: there are other ways to protect us. He put it there because it is the only book he ever kept and he wanted it kept.” El Búho rested his hands on his knees. “It is the first honest thing he has done for himself, dressed as a professional decision. I will not tell him how it looked.”


*• • •*

The van came on the fourth of April at eleven minutes past nine in the morning.

It was white and clean and had a livery on the side — ACCOMMODATION SERVICES — WHERE RECORDED, SUPPORTED — and it came up the lane and turned into the yard like a vehicle that had been given an address and expected to be expected. Two people got out: a woman of about forty with a clipboard, who was the same woman who had taken Mesa out of a parish doorway in Donegal, and a man of about thirty who carried a folder and looked at the ground, which is what the second man always does.

Garrity got to the door first. He got to it on the stick, with the dog behind him, and he stood in it with his shoulder against the frame and his body across the opening, and he did not move.

"Mr. Garrity. We are here at the request of Mr. Castellano. He wrote to the office on the second and asked to be interviewed. There is an appointment. We are the appointment."

"Show me the appointment."

“It is not a document you can be shown,” she said. The office produced papers about people, not for them. “The interview is voluntary. If he declines, we record it and attend again in eleven weeks; a voluntary application does not lapse.” She looked past Garrity at the old man in the chair. “There is nothing you need to do.”

"He is not going anywhere," said Garrity.

And Sal Castellano got up out of the chair.

He had shaved. Mara had not noticed until that moment, and it was the detail she remembered longest: he had shaved in the previous two hours, in a house with one bathroom and five people in it, and nobody had seen him do it. He was in the cardigan and a clean shirt and the good shoes, with the letter book held flat against his chest under one arm — the last bound volume from the Antwerp house, the one that had never been in the green bag.

He stopped beside Garrity and put his hand on the old man's arm, and said the thing that everybody in that house repeated afterwards for years, each of them repeating it differently.

"You cannot keep a man from an appointment, Mr. Garrity. You can only keep him from the door, and a man kept from a door becomes a rescue, and I have watched you be rescued in a field, and it is a thing I would not do to anyone I liked." He took his hand off the arm. "Also, I would like it recorded that I am going willingly, that I have had a good week, and that I intend to be extremely interesting."

Then he went down the two steps into the yard and stood in front of the woman with the clipboard, and he was, Mara saw, taller than he looked in a chair.

"Salvatore Castellano," he said.

"Yes, Mr. Castellano. We have your letter." The woman consulted the clipboard once, briefly, out of professional habit rather than need. "You have asked to be interviewed in relation to the assessment of 1996, and you have stated that you are willing to be recorded and photographed."

"I have."

"May I ask why, before we begin? People usually have a reason."

"Because I am the only man I know who has spent sixty-four years making sure that nothing about him was ever on a page," said Sal Castellano, "and I find, at the end of it, that I would rather be on one. There is no more to it than that. I have looked at the choice from every side and it is a question of whether a man is a fact or a story, and I have decided to be a fact."

The woman wrote something down.

"That is an unusual answer," she said. "Most people say they want to be recorded so that they are not forgotten."

"That is not the same thing at all," said Sal, "and I would be grateful if you would write that down as well."


*• • •*

The goodbyes took nine minutes and were businesslike, which was worse.

Roux asked what he wanted done about the three in Donegal and he said, "Nothing. They are already a success," which was the truth and also a dismissal. Mesa came out and said something nobody heard, and Sal laughed once and said, "In the only language you have ever respected, Captain: it was a good crossing." Garrity stayed inside with the dog, and after the van had gone he sat in the shed for an hour and a half.

Lin Shu shook his hand. Two people who had spent five months in the same building and never once been alone in a room: she held his hand four seconds and said, "You will be a record," and

Sal said, "Yes," and that was all of it. They had understood each other completely since the second week of December and had found nothing to add.

Then Sal came to El Búho.

The two of them stood at the corner of the wall where Mara had stood with El Búho on the last night. They did not shake hands. El Búho said nothing for a while, and then: "You wrote to her."

"Six times, over nine years." He reached inside the cardigan and took out a folded page, worn soft, and held it out without saying anything about it. El Búho took it and did not unfold it. "The last was the certificate. She apologised for the trouble and enclosed money and I refused it and she wrote back and was annoyed with me for four paragraphs and then asked how one addressed a letter to a company that does not exist." A pause. "That letter is dated the seventh of May 1996. It is the last one I have."

El Búho looked at the folded page in his hand.

"Why are you giving it to me now," he said.

"Because in five minutes I will be a resident of the Office of Records and Accommodation, and residents do not own papers," said Sal Castellano. "Everything in my pockets is about to become a record. That page is not about me. It was never about me, and I have carried it for thirty years for no purpose that I have ever been able to justify to myself, and I have decided this morning that the correct action is to hand it to the only other person who was ever in the room."

"What is on the back," said El Búho.

"Ask the young woman," said Sal. "I am not the one who found it."

He went to the van and got in on the far side, the side away from the house, briskly, like a man boarding. The woman with the clipboard came round, got in behind him, closed the door properly and did up her own belt. The van reversed out of the yard at ten past nine, turned south on the coast road, and was gone.

Mara went and stood beside El Búho. On the back of the page, in a hand that sloped and ran a little and had been written with a fountain pen that was running out, in May 1996, four words were written, in the place where a person writes the thing they cannot say to the person they are writing to.

He is not to know.

Neither of them said anything for a while. El Búho folded the page along its old folds and put it in the inside pocket, and left his hand flat over it.

“She paid a forger to have me declared dead because a woman with a child and no papers needed a certificate. In the year she was dying, she wrote on the receipt: he is not to know. Then she wrote a stranger six times asking how to address a company that did not exist.” He took his hand from his pocket. “I had eleven days and came on the eleventh. She wrote to a stranger about company addresses while I was four hundred kilometres away being a legend.”

"Rinaldo—"

"Do not," said El Búho, and Mara did not, and he stood at the wall for a long time after that, and neither of them went inside.


*• • •*

The office wrote twice.

The first came on the seventh, forwarded to the annex, which Kavanagh still collected from, and which Roux drove to Kildare to pick up: the application had been received and entered, a person had been interviewed at the address on the second, the entry was under completion, and no further action was required of the respondent's correspondents. Four sentences, signed by an office and not a person.

The second came on the eleventh, four lines long, and Roux read it in the porch with her coat on and would not come in, so the rest of them went out and read it in the wind, in a semicircle, like a family around a telegram.

The entry in respect of the above respondent has been completed. The respondent is recorded as resolved. Correspondence in this matter is now closed. There is nothing you need to do.

Nobody said anything for four seconds.

"Resolved," said Roux.

"Yes," said Mara.

“That is the word we agreed never to use about a person.” Roux checked the back of the page; it was blank. “He asked to be interviewed on the second. On the eleventh he is resolved. Mesa came back with a page saying his supervision ended and his accommodation was none; this letter does not distinguish. It does not say released or dead. There is no field for the difference between a man let out of a building and a man who died inside one. The difference exists only in the world, where we cannot look.”

She folded the letter.

"So no," she said. "We will never know. That is not a mystery. A mystery has an answer somewhere. This is a system with no field for the question."

Garrity said, out in the wind, in a cracked voice that had nothing to do with the cold: "He did it for us."

“He did it for himself,” said El Búho. “He wanted to be a fact, and the ledger kept, and the last word his. If you turn him into a man who paid for you, you make him a story — the one thing he spent sixty-four years refusing.” He looked around the table. “He was a forger, a liar, and a man who could not remember his own door. He was also the only one who understood that a page lasts. Let him be that, not a sacrifice. He would find it in poor taste.”


*• • •*

The other casualty came on the same afternoon and it was not a person.

Roux found the post at the fire-door company Lin Shu had formed in 2011, registered to a solicitor in Ballinasloe. In its Tuesday mailbox was a letter from the port facilities office: a general inspection return dated 1 April had been accepted, and the Office requested the inspector’s registration number and certification for its file.

"Somebody is reading the reconciliation," said Roux.

"Okafor's reconciliation," said Mara.

"A man with a notebook read a sheet before he ticked it, and typed two numbers into a routing entry, and now an office has asked for the certification of a fire officer called Lin Shu — who exists, whose paperwork is perfect, and every document in it is real — for a registration number that belongs to a woman who has never been registered in her life." Roux looked up. "The diagnostic door has opened, and it opened properly, through a procedure. It will take them eleven days and no more."

Mara stood in the yard with the wind off the water and the two letters in her hand — one about a man who was now a word, one about a woman who was now a question — and said the only thing left to say, on the eleventh of April, with the plan in pieces at her feet.

"We have the number in the register and the vault is empty and one of us is a record and another of us is a lookup," she said. "So there is one room left and it has a woman in it, and there is nothing left to do but walk into it." She looked at El Búho. "Which means it is time for you to tell us what you have been keeping in your pocket since the nineteenth of January, because I am not going to stand in front of your daughter and be surprised."


## Chapter Thirty - The Core

*Days 133–141*

He came down at twenty past eight with his coat over his arm and laid it flat on the kitchen table, and then he went through the pockets in front of all of them, which was a thing nobody in that house had ever seen him do.

The inside left first. He put the envelope down in the middle of the table: small, gone soft at the corner, with a name and an address on it in a hand that was not his, and the flap still closed.

Then the folded page, worn thin along the folds, in a hand nobody in the room could read and everybody in the room had watched him read once, in February, without a word.

Then, out of the pocket on the right, a single ruled page torn out of an exercise book, with a neat small hand on it that was unmistakably his, and no date at the top, and no salutation.

He did not sit down. He put one hand on the back of the chair and read the page aloud in the flat voice he used for a manifest.

Three lines.

I will not go into their buildings.

I will not ask her for anything.

I will not tell her about the eleven days as though it were a reason.

And under the three lines, crossed out with two strokes of the same pen, so that the words were still legible if a person wanted them to be:

I would like to be forgiven.

“I struck it out on 21 January and have read the page every day since. It is on the table because Ms Voss asked me a question on 11 April. The page is a man talking to himself; it is not the answer.” He looked around the room. “The office has a file with my name on it.”

Mesa said, "Everybody's name is in that office."

“Mine is not in it as a name,” said El Búho. “Mine is in it as a conclusion. On 12 June 1996 a letter went from a house in Antwerp to the Registro Civil in Morelia about the death of Nuria Villalobos, accompanied by a second document declaring her husband presumed dead. It was a good document. It was made by a man better at paper than anyone in this room, for the reason he made everything: a woman with a house and a child could not eat without it.” He set down the coat. “I have known that for thirty years. It has been the safest thing about me, and I understood it only as a door kept shut. On 19 January I found out who signs the exceptions. On the twentieth I went up the hill. Somewhere in those two days, a man carrying his life in his hands understood the sentence: there is nowhere in the world that says I am alive.”

Garrity said, "That is not a plan. That is a leak in a roof."

“It is a hole,” said El Búho, “and the only thing I own the machine has no shape for. It counts movement; a finished person does not move. No one it has concluded has walked back into a room to be entered. There is no field for a man who shows up. It can only ask who decides.”

"And that is her," said Roux.

"That is her."

“You propose to present yourself as the subject of a closed 1996 assessment, on the day the office discovers that the woman who certified 3,841 people has never been registered to inspect anything, at a facility already under reconciliation. All four facts are true, and every one goes up. You are not applying to be recorded. You are applying to be the reason.”

“Yes,” said El Búho. “That is the plan. It is not suicide because there is exactly one thing I want from that Office, and it is nothing. No request, accommodation, transport, category, appeal or complaint. I have a name and a date and a document that says I am dead; I will sit in a room and let them look at it.” He turned the page blank-side up and squared it with the edge of the table. “Every man that machine has eaten wanted something. It does not take people; it accommodates them. Mesa wanted a voyage; it gave him a van and a clipboard and a polite woman. Sal wanted a page; it gave him four lines and closed the correspondence. I have watched you both be concluded. A man who wants nothing cannot be paid.”

Mesa had not moved. He said, in the flat voice, "They will take you the way they took me. Kindly, with a form. And there will be a rescue and it will cost somebody a wrist, and if I am asked to come on that rescue I will come, and if you tell me now not to come then I will have to decide in the morning whether I am the kind of man who listens."

“There will be no rescue,” said El Búho. “If a grey car comes and a woman with a clipboard gets out, I go with her; you do nothing. Do not follow, telephone or wait at the gate.” He looked at Mara. “You will hold this. I will be the only casualty, by choice.”

Mara looked at him for a long moment. Then she took him at his word and put it on the record. "If it is a van and not her, we do nothing, and I am the one who says it to the room. Nobody argues tomorrow. Nobody improvises. You will have that in writing before you leave this kitchen, because a crew that promises something at night drinks at dawn."

"That is acceptable," said El Búho.

Kavanagh had taken out his pen. He had it out, uncapped, held in the two fingers, before anybody asked him for anything, and he was looking at the table with the expression of a man in a queue at a bank.

"How many sentences," he said.

"Four," said El Búho. "A letter is four sentences. Five is an application, and an application invites a reply, and I am not inviting anything. I am announcing a man."

“Good,” said Kavanagh. “I will sign the letter, and I want four sentences, each true.” He wrote in the margin. “The Office cannot receive an identity claim from someone it has concluded. It can accept a certification from a person of standing that the applicant is the man in the 1996 assessment. I certify that; it will be the third time in eleven weeks my name has gone into that system.” He recapped the pen. “A man is allowed three mistakes that are decisions. The third decides what he was.”

The letter went on the fifteenth, at the post box in the village, and he walked it down himself, at a quarter past five in the morning, because the man who posts his own letter cannot afterwards claim he was persuaded.

Mara watched him go from the gate. It took nineteen minutes, the same as it had taken Sal, because it was the same walk and the same wall by the harbour, and when he came back he told Roux, in the voice of a man reporting a delivery, that he had sat on the wall for four minutes and that the wall was, in his professional opinion, overrated.
