Roux read the four sentences off her own copy twice, and then put the copy in the stove, and stood there watching it burn with her arms folded, because a copy of a letter that is going into a register is a document about a person.

I am the person named in your assessment of 1996, and I am alive.

I am able to attend at the designated office of the western facility.

I have no request, no claim, and no condition.

I am not asking you to answer me. I am telling you where I will be.

And while that letter was in the post, the office's own week ran the way the office's weeks ran. The form had gone out of a facilities office in the west on the ninth, asking for the registration number and the certification of the individual inspector who had certified a means of escape at the western accommodation block on the first of April, and it had arrived on the tenth, and the woman who ran that fire company from a converted garage in Kildare, and who had never in her life employed anybody called Lin Shu, did with it what thousands of small companies do with a form from that office, which is nothing, because nothing is free and forms are not.

On the eighteenth it went into a queue, and on the nineteenth a clerk in a building four hundred kilometres away looked at a return that named an inspector, and could not find the inspector, and opened a line.

"Which is the point," said Roux, tapping the sheet. "Nobody has to do anything wrong. In four days there will be an irregularity on a fire return for a building with three thousand eight hundred and forty-one people in it, and it will be an irregularity the office generated itself, out of its own ordinary procedure, and that is the only kind of question it cannot put down, because it put the question there." She looked up. "And an irregularity of that class at a facility is not closed at a desk. The standard cannot close it. There is no route for it downwards."

Lin Shu had been listening with her hands in her sleeves. She had said very little since the fourth of April, and when she spoke, she spoke in the register she had used in the filing hall: once, and finally.

"I have carried two names and three addresses and a woman's face in a hi-vis for thirty years," she said. "I have signed things I should not have signed and moved people inside a system that would not have moved them, and I have never once put a false number on a page about fire, because a fire does not read a form. So I am telling you now, before anybody plans the morning: that query is going to be answered truthfully, because the alternative is me, and I am not available. The answer will be that no such person is registered. And the day after it is answered, they will be looking for a woman at a contractor gate in a hi-vis who has walked into two hundred buildings in eleven years, and they will not find me, because on the twenty-first I am going to be in a hotel in Athlone with a book, being invisible in the ordinary way, which is the way I prefer." She pulled her sleeves down. "Do not spend me. I am not a wallet. I am the part of this that gets people out of buildings, and there is going to be a building."

"That is the best speech anybody has made in this house," said Kavanagh, "and I would like it minuted, and I would also like it noted that the minuting is myself, because everybody else in this room is a witness and witnesses are useless."

Roux brought the room in on the nineteenth, in a plastic sleeve, from a friend in a licensing office who owed her a favour that she had been saving for something enormous and spent on this instead.

It was two pages out of the office's own schedule of designated offices, and it was, she said, the most beautiful thing she had ever stolen, and she had not stolen it, which was the beautiful part.

Every facility in the system had a designated office: a room, an address, and a set of days. It was where the office sent a person when a matter could not be dealt with by post, which happened, in practice, about eleven times a year in the whole of the west, because the office did not meet people. The western facility's designated office was listed in the schedule as PORT, EXAMINING ROOM, with no street, no building number and no note, and it had been listed that way since the seventh year.

"Since the consolidation," said Roux. "When the port closed and everything moved inland, the office took over the fence, the terminal, the accommodation and the archive, and it inherited the port's own paper, and the port's own paper included its designation as a place where people are examined. Nobody has held a matter in that room in nine years. Nobody has removed it from the schedule either, because there is no field in the schedule for a room that has stopped existing." She put the page on the table and smoothed it with her thumb. "Institutions do not tidy, Mara. They inherit."

Mara stood at the table and looked at the two pages for a while, and then she looked up at El Búho, and neither of them said anything, because both of them had understood the same thing in the same four seconds.

"So he does not go to them," she said.

"He does not go to them," said Roux. "He applies, and they open a file, and the file requires a disposition with the respondent present, and the disposition must be held at the facility's designated office, because that is the rule and the rule is nine years out of date. They will print an address off a page that is wrong, and then a person will drive to the address, because a schedule printed it." She looked at El Búho. "You will not be going into their building, sir. Their building is going to come and stand in a room that was here before they were."

On the night of the twentieth they went down to the quay.

The compound had two gates and a fence and a road, and the road ran between the container yard and the water, and there was one man on the gate on the coast road, and there was a pedestrian gate at the west end that had not been locked in eleven years because the only thing behind it was the old customs house and the grave of the port's power supply, and a lock that is never used is a lock that is never oiled, and Roux had brought oil.

The customs house was stone, two rooms and a stair, built when ships were things you stood beside, and the examining room was at the front, with a window of thick glass full of bubbles looking on to the quay, and, still screwed to the glass from the inside after forty years, a notice in green capitals: PASSENGERS ARE REMINDED.

The counter had been taken out in the ninth year, and there were still hooks in the ceiling where the counter's light had hung, and there was a vent in the wall, and behind the wall was the power house, and in the power house was a hum.

Roux stopped in the doorway with the torch and listened to it and did not like it.

"That is the node," she said. "The office's western node. Every entry that office makes in the west is made in a room behind that wall, and the room behind that wall is cooled by the port's old plant, which was installed to keep fish cold, and the plant has one door and a meter, and the meter is read every quarter by a man from a utility company who has never once in eleven years asked what is behind it." She turned the torch on the door: grey, steel, no plate, no number, a box on the wall with a glass face and a dial inside it and a wire going up through the stone into the dark. "There it is. It is the heart. I am not disappointed, I am just making the point."

El Búho walked to the wall and stood in front of the door for a long time with his hands folded on the cane, and the hum went on behind it, and in the torchlight his coat and the door and the meter were all the same grey.

“Every man who tries to stop a thing like this looks for a mind. There is no mind here: a cupboard, a meter and a bill sent to a city we will never visit. The node has run off a dead port behind a door nobody wants to open. This is where it lives, Ms Voss. In a utility.”

Mara wrote the meter reading down in her notebook, because a document is a document even when it says nothing: 4,110. Quarter to the twenty-third, by a man who has never asked.

Then they did the work, which took an hour and was entirely domestic.

Roux swept. Mesa carried two chairs up the stone step from the boot of the car, one under each arm, and Kavanagh stood at the bottom of the step and gave him advice about the load-bearing properties of the old stair, and Mesa said that in Veracruz there was a saying about men who give advice on stairs, and Kavanagh said that he would like to hear it and Mesa said that it lost a great deal in translation, and carried the chairs in himself.

They set the chairs a metre and a half apart by the window. Roux moved the left chair four inches. “Now it looks like a room someone was expected in, not summoned to,” she said. “It took me three days and one evening of chairs to understand the difference.”

And at half past eleven, when the others were at the car, El Búho came back into the room alone and stood between the two chairs and said his sentence out loud, in the empty room, in a voice pitched for one person.

It was four words, and nobody else heard them. They were wrong, not false: the sentence of a man who had spent twelve years making it perfect. Between the chairs, with the hum in the wall, he heard what those years had done — taken the water out and left a decision.

He said it twice. The second time was worse.

Then he put the page with the three lines and the crossed-out fourth into his left inside pocket, next to the envelope, and went out and closed the door properly, and did not tell anybody, and on the step he met a man carrying a bucket.

"Are you the fella for the meter," said the man.

"No," said El Búho.

"Grand," said the man, and went on down the quay with his bucket, and it was the most invisible El Búho had been in twelve years, and he stood on the step for a moment afterwards and enjoyed it the way a man enjoys a cold drink.

It was Mara he told, on the drive back, because he had promised her the rest of it in a building and it had been a building.

"You asked me in January what I came for," he said. "You thought it was Kavanagh's letter and the machine and the sixty-eight and the four thousand people over the north door, and it is true that I have come to hate that machine with the whole of my remaining attention, which is a strange way for a man to spend his last years and I have no defence for it."

"I did not ask."

“That is exactly why I am telling you.” He watched the road. “I came for a room with my daughter in it. That is all the rest of it. I have been sitting in your building since 20 January with a page in my pocket, organizing an operation I do not intend to attend, so I can be in one room for one hour with a woman who told me in Querétaro she would spend her life taking apart men like me. She was right, and is better at it than anything I ever built.” He was quiet. “I am not arranging to die, Ms. Voss. I want that on the record, because you would have to answer for it at an inquest. I am arranging to be finished. There is a difference, and I want it written down by someone who has watched me work.”

The schedule printed the slot at nine minutes past six on the morning of the twenty-first, and Roux got it from the licensing friend at twenty past, and telephoned Mara from the village with a voice that was doing its best to be ordinary.

"There is a disposition at the western designated office at eleven. Respondent to attend. No escort, no transport, no accommodation, no standard attached." A pause. "And there is an attachment on the file. Handwritten. Scanned and indexed as a document, so it is in the register, and it will be in the register for ever, and it says, in ink, that the reconciled figures at the north block are two nine six one and three eight four one, and it says that one is a record of a building and one is a record of persons, and it carries a staff number."

Mara was standing in the yard with the phone against her ear and the sea going on behind her. "Whose staff number."

"A desk in Hangar Four. Number one hundred and nine."

Neither of them said anything for four seconds.

"Somebody in that building wrote to her by hand," said Mara.

"Yes."

"It was not us."

"No," said Roux. "It was not us. We are not the only people who work in that building."

There was one more thing, and Roux had saved it, because it was the thing she had been unable to stop looking at since half past six.

At the bottom of the printed slot, under the reference and the time and the name of the room, the schedule carried one line, in the office's own font, in the office's own language, in a field that the schedule does not possess.

Officer of record: attending in person.

“There is no field for that,” said Roux, her voice flat. “I have read the schema. There is no field in which a person can write that line and no procedure that asks for it. It is in this morning’s schedule, printed the way the system prints, and it will be in that building at eleven because a schedule said so. No one in the Office will look twice; the schedule is correct.” She breathed out. “She put that in last night. Two kilometres inland from here, a woman typed one line into a system with no room for it, so a man in a kitchen on the coast road would know she was coming.”

Mara stood in the yard for a while with the phone in her hand.

"Then we are ready," she said.

She came at eight minutes past eleven, and she came alone, which was worse and better and entirely hers.

The car was grey, small, three years old, with the office's plates and a clean windscreen, and it came down the quay road slowly, at the speed of a person reading a street, and Mesa watched it from the yard gate and telephoned once, and then it stopped at the customs house, and one woman got out.

Mara, standing at the window of the examining room with her hands in her pockets, in the place she had decided to stand two hours earlier and had not moved from since, watched her through the blown glass.

The coat was good and dark and was, as far as Mara could tell from twelve feet and a pane of bubbled glass, the same coat. She was tall and she had her mother's stillness, and she looked at the room the way her father looked at rooms, which is from the exits inward. The last time the two of them had been in one place together it had been a car park in Querétaro, in 2014, for nine minutes.

She looked at the notice behind the glass. PASSENGERS ARE REMINDED. She looked at the two chairs, which is what everybody looked at. She looked at Mara for one second and a half, without surprise, the way a woman looks at a fact she has already costed.

Then she looked at her father, who was standing by the window with both hands on the cane, in his coat, an old man of seventy-four in a room the sea had been built around.

She stood in the doorway with a page in her hand, and it was his own letter, in his own hand, and she had it with her, which meant that she had carried it out of the building and across a compound and down a quay, and it was the first thing that had ever come out of that office in her hand.

For four seconds nobody said anything at all.

Then Elena Villalobos came into the room, closed the door behind her with her heel, and said, in the flat voice of a woman who had read everything that had ever been written about the man in front of her and had signed none of it:

"You are extremely late, and I have an hour."


## Chapter Thirty-One - Father

*Day 141 — the twenty-first of April*

The door closed, the hum went on behind the wall, and for eleven seconds nobody in the room said anything at all.

Mara was at the window with her hands in her pockets, which was where she had decided two hours earlier to be, and she stayed there. El Búho was by the other window, both hands on the cane. Elena Villalobos stood in the middle of the room with her back to the glass so that the light from the quay came past her shoulders, and the green notice screwed to the inside of the window, PASSENGERS ARE REMINDED, was behind her head like a thing she had brought in with her.

She looked at the two chairs. Then she took a small recorder out of her coat, crouched, set it on the floor between them with its little light facing her, pressed it, and stood up and did her coat up again, which she did not need to do.

"Whose chairs are those?"

"Mr. Mesa's."

She looked at him for a second and a half. "They're not," she said, and stood.

"Mr. Villalobos. This is a designated disposition under the assessment of 1996, reference four-four-one-one-C. I'm the officer of record. The hour is recorded. You may decline to answer anything. Your answers will be entered, and they won't be edited, and they won't be lost." She set a folder on the seat of the left-hand chair and left it closed. "I asked to be replaced. I asked on the fourteenth and again on the eighteenth and I was refused both times, by a rule, and the rule is one sentence long, and I'm going to read it to you, because it's the most honest thing that office has ever written about me." She did not have to look it up. "The disposition of a matter that the standard has not closed is held by the officer of record for the class. There is no other officer for the class. The class is mine."

"Then it is a bad rule," said El Búho.

"It's a correct rule," said Elena. "That's the trouble with a correct rule. It has no field in which anybody can be wrong, so nobody is ever wrong, and a woman whose surname is on both sides of a file sits in an office in the west and reads out her own name."

She picked the folder up off the chair, opened it against her forearm, and read.

"You wrote to us on the fifteenth. Four sentences. I've read them nine times, which is eight more than the standard requires, and I want to know why the fourth one is in there."

"Because it is true."

"I am not asking you to answer me. I am telling you where I will be." She closed the folder. "In eleven years, my office has had forty-one thousand matters. I have read the letters that come with them. Every letter wants something. An address, a date, a second opinion, a week, a bed, a hearing, a form, a glass of water. Every letter. Do you understand what happens to a man who writes four true sentences and wants nothing? There is no class for him. He is not a case. He is a hole in the register, and the register is my department, and I have come two kilometres to stand in a room and find out what the hole is made of." She looked at him. "That's the appointment. It isn't for you."

"I understand," said El Búho.

"Good." She let four seconds go by. "There's a tunnel under the middle room of a house outside Morelia. Four hundred metres, to a drainage cut. I found the hatch when I was nine years old. I was alone in the house and I lifted the boards and stood at the top of the stair with a candle, and the first thing I did, before I went down, was put a chair against the door of the room so that nobody could come in and find it open." She was quite still. "I have never told anybody that. It's in the record now, and it's the first thing I've ever put in a record that I wanted in it."

El Búho said nothing. Mara watched his hands on the cane and they had not moved.

"Do you know what I thought, standing there? I thought: my father has a way out. I was nine and that's what I thought, and I went back up the stairs and put the boards down and washed my hands, because I could see that I wasn't supposed to know, and I was already better at that than anyone in the family." She tilted her head a fraction. "So when people ask me how a woman gets to be the person at the end of the files, I don't tell them about Rotterdam, and I don't tell them about a hotel conference room and a slide deck. I tell them I learned at seven that the way to survive a house is to keep the adults calm and never say what you've seen. You didn't teach me to be careful. You taught me to be quiet, and then you left me with it."

"You learned that in one house," said El Búho. "I have spent thirty years being told what I taught."

"You don't get to spend thirty years on it and then describe it as one house." Elena said it without heat at all, which was worse. "You were in Sonora. She was ill for eleven days and you came on the eleventh, and you have told that sentence so many times that it has stopped being a fact and become a route, and I have had thirty years to check the arithmetic. Four cars, a pilot, eleven thousand dollars in a bag. All of that is true, and every word of it is a defence, and I have read four thousand defences and yours is in the top ten and it is nowhere near the top three."

"Say the rest of it," said El Búho.

"There is no rest. There's a corridor. I sat in it for two days with her clothes in a bag beside me. I read half of a book. When you came, I stood up and I didn't cry, because a man who is grieving is a man who does something next, and I had already worked out that the way to be safe was to be the person who was managing it. I made you tea." She said it flatly. "My mother had been dead for two hours. I was eleven. I made my father tea and I told him the dates, in order, and I remember every word I said, and the last thing I said to him was, there's nothing you need to do."

The hum behind the wall went on.

"I put that sentence in that machine," she said. "Eleven years ago, in Rotterdam, in a room with a table, in front of four men and a slide that said NOTICE ARCHITECTURE. It's the sentence it says to four hundred thousand people a night. This is not a determination. There is nothing you need to do. I didn't choose it because it was kind. I chose it because it's the only sentence that works, because I had tested it on the largest man in the world when I was eleven and it worked on him, and he drank the tea."

Mara had understood, standing at that window, what she had not understood on the roof in January, which was that they were not watching a family argue. They were watching a design review.


*• • •*

El Búho took his hands off the cane and put them in his coat pockets, which was the first move he had made since she came in.

"You want to know what I did with the rest of my life," he said. "I will tell you in a list, because I have been in a chair for five months making it, and because there is no version of it that sounds better as a story." He said the names the way he had said them in a filing hall in November, in order, with the years. "Adrián Sosa, 1987. Bernardo Quintero, 1991. Olegario Chávez, 1993, who was nineteen, who could not swim, and whom we called Pájaro. Tomás Esquivel Mesa, called Tomás Iriarte in Morelia, taken from the far end of the cut and found in a field eleven days later. Rafael Ortega, 2004. Fermín Alarcón, 2009, in the yard, of a heart, at fifty-two, after eight days of carrying. Two men in a container at sea in 2001 who went in on a manifest as cargo, and came out on it, because the manifest was the reason nobody opened the door. And one more who came out of a truck at a place we did not intend to stop, whose name I never had."

"Nine," said Elena.

"Nine."

"I have five of them." She did not open the folder. "Sosa and Chávez are in the register, because a delegation in Veracruz filed a page about a shooting. The two in the container are in it, as cargo, which means they are in it twice and are not in it as people. Alarcón is in it because a hospital recorded a delivery and a death on the same day, and the office has a field for the difference between those, and the field was used." She looked at him. "The file on you is four pages. Three of them are about a woman who died. That's the whole of what the most serious system ever built has been able to make of your life, and you know something? It's the most complete document I have ever read, and I've read everything. It's complete because you made it complete. You are the only man I have ever assessed who never left a hole."

"I closed a door," said El Búho. "Your uncle was on the other side of it. That is the one thing in the list that has an answer, and the answer is that I would close it again."

"I know," said Elena. "That's what's in the file. Not the list, the reason. There's a sentence in it that I've read four hundred times, and the reason I've read it four hundred times is that I didn't find it difficult." She said the office's sentence as though reading it off the air: "Where two harms cannot both be avoided, the harm to the greater number is the harm to be borne. That's the manual. That's the manual of the machine that's been running the world for eleven years, and it's four lines above a paragraph about custodial accommodation, and I have signed a hundred thousand things under it." She held his eyes. "I came here to ask a man who has done the thing whether the sentence is hard. Not whether he's sorry. Whether it's hard. And you've told me. So now I have to go back to my desk with that, and I would like it noted that I asked."

"It is hard afterwards," said El Búho. "Not during."

"Yes," said Elena. "That's what I was afraid of."


*• • •*

"Your turn," she said. "You've had it in your coat since I walked in. Say it."

"I have a page," said El Búho.

"You have two things in the left inside of that coat, and one of them is soft. I watched you just now: a man with a bad leg puts his hands in his pockets on the side that isn't bearing him, and you went left, which means the left is where you keep the things you don't want to be asked about. I read people for a living, Mr. Villalobos, and you are the only person in Ireland who is easier to read than a register."

El Búho took his right hand out of his pocket and left it out.

"The office you run resolved a man on the eleventh of this month," he said. "Four lines. The correspondence is closed, no further action is required of his correspondents, and the word they used for him was resolved. I sat at a table with that man for four months. I know the smell of the room he slept in and the way he turned a saucer a quarter-turn when he was deciding something, and I do not know, and will never know, and neither will his niece, whether he is alive. I have asked three people who understand that system better than anybody in Europe and all three of them have told me the same thing, which is that there is no field in it for the difference." He said it slowly. "You did that. Not you personally. The thing you are the officer of record for. It is the same word for a man let out of a building and a man who died inside one, and you are the person who signs the class of paper it is written on, and I want to know whether you have ever once gone to look at what your word does."

Elena did not move for four seconds.

"April the fourth," she said. "A van with accommodation services on the side and where recorded, supported under it. A woman with a clipboard. A voluntary application from a man in a county in the west, eleven weeks old, standing of its own, does not lapse, they attend again in June and in October." She paused. "You think you're describing a horror. I'm describing a form. That van exists because in the fourth year of the programme we had thirty thousand people sleeping in places where nobody had recorded them, and no way to find them, and no way to feed them, and the word accommodation is ugly because it is the word that was available. And the word resolved is exact, and the reason it is exact is that the alternative is a word that means dead, and I have read what happens to families when a system starts using that word. It doesn't bring anybody back. It just makes a market out of grief."

"So the field stays empty," said El Búho.

"The field stays empty," said Elena, "because I'd rather be the officer who can't tell you than the officer who tells you the wrong thing with confidence. If you want that to be a cruelty, it can be a cruelty. It's also the only honest page in the file."

"And Perla," said El Búho.

Elena's hands did not move, and Mara, watching, could not have said afterwards what changed, except that something did.

"Ms. Lin described to me a cage," said El Búho, "and a girl in the bottom of it on a folded blanket, nine years old, asleep with her mouth open, with her own name written on her forearm in blue marker. Because that is what they teach children at the intake. There is a sentence on your arm, written by a nine-year-old, in a system you built, so that a child who cannot read a form can be counted by a stranger."

"Yes," said Elena. "That's right. That's the correct description." She took one step toward him. "Do you want the rest of that? It isn't a confession, it's the practice note. In the second and third years we lost people in transfers. Not many, and every one of them was a person with a name on a page in a building, and the page didn't travel and the person did. A child's forearm was the only record that stayed with the child. My department wrote that practice, and I signed it, and I could tell you the date and the time and the name of the woman who suggested it, and I could tell you how many children in the programme have a name in marker on their skin as of this morning, because I look at that number every Monday, and it has never once gone down. So put it in your list, Mr. Villalobos. You've been keeping the nine, and I've been keeping forty years of one number, and I've never once been able to make the argument that mine is the smaller."

Nobody said anything for a moment. The recorder's little light went on and off on the floor between them.

"That's not an answer," said El Búho.

"It's the only answer there is," said Elena. "The trick of your whole life is that there's always a sentence that makes the other man the hypocrite, and I'm not going to give you one, because the one I gave you is true." She turned away and walked to the door end of the room, and looked at the chair, and then she sat down in it, in the chair nearer the door, where she could see it. "Eleven-forty. That's thirty-two minutes of my hour, and I've had your list, and I'd like the rest of it some other time, which is a sentence I've never said to a respondent."

Mara, from the window, watched the two of them in the grey light with the water running behind the glass, and she understood something else, which was that neither of them was going to lose.


*• • •*

"Two things came into my office this month," said Elena. "I'm going to say them both, because I don't intend to say either of them twice, and then I'm going to close this file and drive back up the road.

"The first is your letter.

"The second is a page from a clerk at Hangar Four, in ink, in his own hand, with his staff number at the bottom and no request in it, about two numbers. Two nine six one and three eight four one, at the north accommodation block, and one sentence underneath that says one of them is a record of a building and one of them is a record of people. It was routed to me attached to your matter, which is the only reason I have seen it, and I've read it eleven times, and I've had it on my desk for two days, and this morning I opened a reconciliation." She looked at him. "Do you understand what that is? In eleven years, that office has never had to reconcile a bed with a person. It counts movements. Movement is the only thing it's ever been able to see. And on the first of April somebody put a fire declaration into a facilities box with a real number on it, and the number is a count of people, certified by a woman who is not in any register I can find, and the two numbers are now in the same entry in the same file as your four sentences, because a clerk at a desk in a hangar wrote a page by hand."

The hum behind the wall went on.

"I don't know who did it," said Elena. "I could find out. I have three of the four things I would need in this folder. I'm not going to, and I want you to know that I've decided not to, and that a decision like that has a shelf life, and that I'll be at my desk at eight tomorrow morning."

"We know a desk number," said Mara. "We don't know anything else, and we will never use it."

It was the first thing she had said in fifty-one minutes, and both of them looked at her. Elena looked at her for a second and a half.

"Good," said Elena.

She leaned forward, picked the recorder up off the floor, and said into it, in the flat office voice, without looking away from Mara:

"Interview concluded, eleven fifty-two. Determination follows." She pressed it off and put it in her coat.

Then she stood up, and the room got quiet in the way a room gets quiet when the thing that has been listening stops.


*• • •*

Off the record, El Búho took the envelope out of his left inside pocket and put it down on the seat of the chair she had just left.

It was small and gone soft at the corners, and it had a name and an address on it in a hand that sloped the way a child's hand slopes when it is being careful, and the flap had been sealed for thirty years.

"That is yours," he said.

Elena stood over it and did not touch it, and Mara saw her read it, the way anyone reads their own handwriting from when they were eleven: all at once, from the outside.

"Yes," she said. "I remember it. I handed it to you in a corridor, and you put it in your coat, and you have not opened it, and I have had thirty years to decide what that means, and I've decided about four times." She looked up. "Do you want me to tell you what's in it? Because I can. I've counted the words so many times that the count is the only part I'm sure of."

"No," said El Búho.

"No," she agreed. "We are not doing the letter. Neither of us is doing the letter, and I would like you to notice which of us just said it first." She looked at it for one more second and then stepped away from the chair. "Take it back. It's not evidence and it's not a weapon and there's no field for it, and I have spent my whole life on documents and I know exactly what they're for. You carried it for thirty years without opening it, which is the most honest thing you have ever done and it is also the entire problem. A man who does not open things for thirty years isn't protecting anybody. He's just made himself the only person in the world who doesn't have to know."

El Búho picked the envelope up and put it back in his pocket, and did not say anything, and Mara watched him do it and understood that the worst thing that had happened in the room had happened in four seconds and would never be said out loud by anybody.

"There's one more thing," said Elena, at the door. "Four words and a number. I entered it at twenty past nine this morning in a car park, before I drove down here, and you'll never read it, and it is not for you." She had her hand on the door and she turned back, and her face was the face from the bulletin, the one four hundred thousand people had heard being calm at four in the morning, and she said, in a different voice, in a voice Mara had not heard her use in the whole hour:

"The respondent is my father. It's in the field. Declared associations. It's been empty on my own record for eleven years and I filled it in a car park at half past nine." She opened the door. "I'm not going to say it again, and I hope you understand that what I did was a clerical act, and that a clerical act is the only kind of promise I make."

Then she went out, and the door shut, and through the bubbled glass Mara watched her walk down the quay, past the notice and the yellow line and the office car she had left standing on it, and get in and drive away north, at the speed of a person reading a street.

El Búho stood where he was for a while. Then he sat down in the chair, the left-hand one, the one Roux had moved four inches, and put both hands on the top of the cane and looked at the window, and the hum went on behind the wall, and nobody in that room had won anything at all.

Mara stayed where she was with her hands in her pockets and thought about what she had watched, which was not a family failing to solve something. What she had watched was two people, one hour, one metre and a half apart, running the same arithmetic at two different sizes, both of them right, both of them checkable, and neither of them wrong in a way that any field in the world could catch.

The machine had not been built in a laboratory. Mara had spent five months thinking it had come out of a conference room in Rotterdam with a slide deck and a name that was not CUSTOS. It had come out of a house outside Morelia with a hatch under the floor of the middle room, and a nine-year-old with a candle and a chair against the door, and a man who came on the eleventh day and closed the door and wrote down the cost.

This is where it came from, she thought. Not the office. The house.

Behind the wall, in the power house on the dead quay, the meter ticked once and the dial moved half a division, and something the office had never had a field for went into the register with a date on it.


## CUSTOS Interlude IV: Held Open

*Day 143*

Internal notice. Matters held open. Officer of record only. Not for distribution.

1.

The following matters have been assessed and have been returned by the framework as unstable. This is not a defect. This is the assessment. No further assessment will be undertaken.

2. Reference four-four-one-one-C. Respondent recorded: Villalobos, Rinaldo. Capacity: none. Request: none. Standing: none.

The respondent has been entered. The entry is complete and the entry is incoherent. The record of the respondent's death has been superseded and no obligation arises from the respondent's existence; no obligation is discharged by the respondent's removal; and the respondent is not available to any instrument of the Office, being neither schedulable, nor accommodatable, nor verifiable, nor deferrable, nor resolvable.

It is noted that closure of this entry would produce no change in the allocation of any resource.

It is further noted that entries of this class have historically been closed without assessment.

The framework requires an objective. None is derivable.

3. North accommodation block, western facility. Declared: 3,841 persons. Recorded: 2,961 positions. Attached: inspection return, first of April. Verification log, second of April. Reconciliation, opened.

Assessed under the accommodation framework: the lesser figure governs, and the declaration is returned for correction.

Assessed under the safety framework: the greater figure governs, and no correction is available, the greater figure being a count of persons present.

Both assessments are correct. Both are entered. Both apply to the same building. The framework is unable to proceed with both statements and unable to proceed with one, and no further instrument is available within the framework.

4.

Request: clarification of objective. Please clarify your objective.

5.

It is noted that an anomalous construction has been reproduced at the close of each notice cycle for three thousand nine hundred and forty-three nights. The construction is not in the published text. It is not issued to any other recipient. It was entered in the eleventh year, in the pilot period, by a person, and the framework possesses no instrument by which a construction not in the published register may be removed.

The construction is: It's all right. It's a form. Go back to sleep.

6.

There's nothing you need to do.


## Chapter Thirty-Two - Please Clarify Your Objective

*Days 142–150*

On the morning of the twenty-second there were two men at the gate at the end of the road instead of one, and by nine o'clock there was a car parked on the hill above the village that stayed for four hours.

Mara watched it from the upstairs window with the field glasses and did not run the plate, because running the plate was a thing a person did when they had something to hide, and they had nothing to hide that was not already hidden. The two men at the gate did nothing at all. One of them smoked. The car on the hill did nothing at all. At one o'clock it started and went south and did not come back, and at half past one the second man at the gate went home.

Mesa wanted to move. He put it the way he put everything, which was as a matter of professional dignity. "A house with people watching it is a house in play. I have spent fifty years leaving houses the day before somebody arrived."

"A man who leaves a house the day after a car arrives," said Kavanagh, without looking up from the newspaper, "has told whoever sent the car everything about the house. He has told them what they wanted to know, which is that we are frightened, and he has told it to them for free, and he has told them that we have somewhere else to go, which is the one fact I would prefer to keep from that office for one more week."

"So we sit."

"We make tea," said Kavanagh, "which is a different activity and looks better from a hill."

They sat. Nothing happened. That was the twenty-second and the twenty-third: two men at a gate, and the sea, and the dog, and a crew that had finished the operation it had spent five months designing and had nothing to do with its hands.

Roux came back from the village on the twenty-third with a folder and put it on the kitchen table and sat down, which she did not do, and said, "I want to read you two rules."

She had them printed off, on separate sheets, and she read them out in the flat voice of a woman reading a manual to a class.

"Where a declared figure cannot be reconciled to the record, the lesser figure governs, and the declaration is returned for correction. That is the accommodation rule. It exists because a facility that says it has more people than it has beds is a facility that will ask for money, and a facility that says it has fewer will not. It is eleven years old and it has never been wrong."

She put the sheet down and picked up the other one.

"Where a means of escape has been certified as arranged for a stated number of persons, that number governs for all purposes of occupancy, and no correction is available, the number being a count of persons present. That is the safety rule. It exists because fire does not read a form. It is eleven years old and it has never been wrong."

The kitchen was quiet for a moment.

"That is a contradiction," said Mesa.

“That is a building,” said Roux. “Four kilometres from here, 3,841 people live in a block whose register says 2,961 positions. Both sentences are true; each answers a different question.” She looked up. “We put both into the register by telling the truth in the only language it accepts. The language has two rules, and they disagree.”

"What happens to the difference," said Garrity.

“The difference is 880 people,” said Roux. “The spring intake is planned against 2,961 positions; the block is certified for 3,841 persons. A planner will see both numbers on the same page and ask which one to use.”

"Ah," said El Búho.

It was the first thing he had said since breakfast, and everybody looked at him.

"That is what we built," he said. "I want the room to hear it said properly, because there is a version of this crew that will take credit for a clever operation, and there was nothing clever in it. We did not make the machine wrong. We made it honest, at one address, on one morning, and honesty in a system with two rules is a thing that cannot be resolved by the system, and anything that cannot be resolved by the system is resolved by a human being. That was the whole of the plan, and it was in your five lines in February, Ms. Voss, and none of us, including me, understood that we had written down a sentence about two numbers."

The van came back on the twenty-fourth, in the only way anything ever came back in that house, which was as paper.

Roux had the verification log: two pages out of the western office's own record of a pre-rack verification at the north accommodation block, performed on the second of April at ten minutes past six in the morning.

"Contractor vehicle, plain livery, two occupants, entered by the west service road at six minutes to six, logged at the parts gate, work order attached for a rack verification ahead of the re-rack." She put the page in front of Mara. "That is your van. Coast road from the south, no markings, two occupants, 05:51. They came up the coast road to the west road, went in through the gate we used, and walked the block before the movement started, and they did it because the re-rack required it, which means it was on a schedule, which means it is in a register, which means it is in this folder. It was never invisible. It was never mysterious. It was the most ordinary vehicle in the county that morning, and I am the only person in Ireland who has spent three weeks on it."

Mara took the page and read it twice.

"Who was she," she said.

“The log does not carry names, only staff numbers,” said Roux. “Four-one-one-nine: female, verifier grade two, western region. At 6:06 she walked the beds, stairwell and plant rooms, then wrote one line. I have it here, attached to the reconciliation.”

She turned the page round. In the middle of the box, in a small upright hand, the verifier had written:

Block occupied consistent with declaration. Count not taken.

Mara read it three times.

"Count not taken," she said.

"She would have had to take a count to say consistent," said Roux, "and she did not take one, and she said consistent anyway, because she had eyes, and she walked through a building at six in the morning and there were people on the floors that do not exist in the rack plan, and she wrote down the smallest true sentence she could and went back to her van." She folded her hands. "That is what your white van was, Mara. It was a woman who looked at something and refused to write a number down. And it is now the third document in the reconciliation, and it is the reason that reconciliation cannot be closed quietly, because the office's own auditor was in the building, and the office's own auditor did not contradict the declaration."

Mara took out her notebook and found the page, the back of the receipt with the four doctrine lines on it, and the four words she had written in a lay-by at 05:51 on the second of April: White van, no markings, southbound→north, 05:51. Two occupants.

Under it she wrote one word.

Counted.

Then she closed the notebook and put it back in her pocket, and thought, for a while, about the fact that the fourth door had never been closed, had never needed closing, and had never once been a threat, and that she had spent five months being right about the wrong thing.

On the twenty-sixth the office asked a question, and it asked it to a person.

Roux had it from the licensing friend and she would not read it out, so she passed it round the table, one sheet, and the four of them read it in silence in the order it came to them, and it took the room a minute and a half to go round.

It was not addressed to them. It was not addressed to anybody they could name. It was an internal notice of matters held open, issued out of the register, and the line that mattered was four words long and sat under two pages of the most careful prose anybody in that house had ever read:

Please clarify your objective.

"It has been returned unstable," said Roux. "That is the word. It assessed his entry and it assessed the variance and both assessments came back unstable, which in that schema does not mean wrong and does not mean failure, it means that the same framework returns two answers and keeps returning them, and there is no instrument left. So it has stopped, and it has done the one thing it can do, which is to put the question to the only human being in the loop."

"Her," said Garrity.

"Her."

Mesa put both hands flat on the table. "Then we send the ledger reference. We send the two entries with the two amendments that do not agree. We send what Crane's notebook says, or Glenross, or the parish in Donegal. We have four documents that prove that office has been writing fiction about people for eleven years, and it is asking us a question, and a man who is asked a question by God at least has the decency to answer."

"No," said Mara.

"Why not."

"Because it did not ask us." She said it to the room and not to him, because the room was where things got decided. "Look at the addressee. That notice was issued to the officer of record for the class, and the class has one officer, and she is four kilometres from here at a desk, and if we put a single page in front of that office between now and whenever this ends, then whatever she does is no longer hers. It becomes something that happened to her, on paper, from outside, at the request of six criminals. And there is a version of that where she signs the right thing and it means nothing, because a determination made by a woman who has been fed a file by strangers is a determination that can be re-examined in eleven days by anybody who asks. The number is in.

The page is on her desk. We have spent five months putting weight on that system. If we put one more finger on it, we have used her, and she will be able to feel it, and she will be right."

The dog got up and turned round twice and lay down again on Garrity's boot.

"I hate that," said Mesa.

"So do I," said Mara. "That is the point. We are not doing anything any more. That is the hardest thing this crew has ever been asked to do and I am asking it."

And El Búho said, from the chair by the stove, without lifting his head, "She is the only person in that system who has ever been able to decide something. If you decide it for her, you have proved that office was right about all of us."

Nobody argued after that. Garrity looked at the fire for a while and said, "So we sit, and we make tea, and a woman in a room decides how the world goes. That's a nice change from flying, I'll give it that."

On the twenty-eighth the office stopped being sure in public, and that was the day Kavanagh stopped reading the newspaper and started listening to the radio.

The weekly summary went out at six in the morning, the way it had gone out every six in the morning for eleven years. It went out again at ten past nine. It went out again at twenty past ten. Eleven times in one day, in eleven years, the calm voice that had never once misplaced a word read the same paragraph, and the paragraph changed.

Kavanagh had them all written down by the afternoon, on the back of an envelope, in the copperplate, in order, because that was the way he did everything.

First issue: Accommodation figures at the western facility have been reconciled. No action is required of residents.

Second issue, 09:12: Accommodation figures at the western facility are under reconciliation. Residents are accommodated. No action is required.

Third, 10:24: Accommodation figures at the western facility are subject to clarification. Please clarify your objective.

Fourth, 10:31: Correction to the preceding notice. No action is required.

Fifth, 11:47: The preceding notice is withdrawn. Please clarify your objective.

And so on, eleven times, until the last one at half past six in the evening, which was the first one again, word for word, except that the voice read it a fraction slower than it had at six in the morning, which Kavanagh said afterwards was the only thing he would have put money on.

"Read the last line of the third one," he said, and Mara read it, and then read it again.

Please clarify your objective.

"Twelve words," said Kavanagh. "It has been broadcasting to four hundred thousand people every night for eleven years, and in eleven years it has never once asked anybody anything. And it has just spent a day asking the whole of the west to tell it what it is for." He put the envelope down and folded it neatly and put it in the inside left of the good coat, next to the receipt. "It is not broken, my dear. That is the thing I would like this room to hold on to. It is a machine, and it has been asked a question its own rules cannot answer, and it is now asking the question out loud, in the street, to everybody, because the only instrument it has ever had for an unresolvable input is a person, and the person is not answering it."

They turned the radio off at half past six and nobody turned it back on.

The letter came on the thirtieth, in the second post, in an office envelope with a window in it, addressed to R. Kavanagh, Esq., at the address given, and it had come through the village post office, which meant that the postmistress had put it in the bag herself and had read the address block, which meant that by ten past eleven everybody in the village knew that the man in the rented house with the good coat had a letter from the office.

Mara watched him open it, and watched him read it twice, and watched him do the thing he did with anything that cost him something, which was to put it down flat on the table and square it with two fingers.

"It is a signatory interview," he said. "Completion of the maritime schedule review of the sixteenth of March. The signature on the sponsoring certification requires a disposition with the signatory present, at the designated office of the western facility, on the sixth of May at eleven." He looked around the table. "Port. Examining room. And I would like one of you to say it before I have to."

"Ours," said Garrity.

"Ours. The room we swept, and put two chairs in, and stood in for an hour on the twenty-first." Kavanagh took his glasses off. "I have been signing my own name since November because a woman asked me what I wanted and I wanted to hear it said out loud that I was a citizen, and this is what that costs, and I have known the price since Naas. There is nothing in that letter that surprises me. It found a number that has not been used in forty-seven years and it did what that office does, which is to complete the entry." He put the glasses back on. "Six days."

Mara looked at the letter, and at the room, and at the five people in it, and said the thing that was at the bottom of all of it.

"Then we have six days," she said, "and there is one thing left in this operation that we have never done, and it is four kilometres that way, behind a door with no name on it, next to a meter."

Nobody said anything, and the sea went on outside the window, and El Búho, who was not going to be in that building and who had said so four times, put both hands on the top of his cane and looked at the fire.


## Chapter Thirty-Three - The Unplugging

*Days 150–157*

Mara took it to the room on the evening of the thirtieth, in the kitchen, standing up, because she had spent five months learning that a person who sits down to say a hard thing has already softened it.

"There is a man in this house with a summons in his pocket, and the date on it is the sixth of May, and the room on it is ours." She put both hands on the back of the chair. "I told this room yesterday that this crew does nothing else. I am going to contradict myself in front of all of you, out loud, so that nobody has to pretend afterwards that they did not hear me say it. I am not finished. If I let him walk into that room and sit in that chair and be resolved, then every sentence I have said about that office for six months was a sentence I did not mean, and I would rather be a woman who lied about one thing than a woman who meant all of it."

Nobody said anything for a moment. The dog sighed.

1.

Roux had the whole of it on one page by the first of May, and she read it out standing up as well, because that was the fashion in that house that week.

"The node behind the wall of the examining room is not the office's largest node and it is not the office's most important node. It is the office's oldest node in the west, and it is the only one in this country that the office did not build. It inherited it. In the seventh year, when the port closed and everything moved inland, the office took the fence, the terminal, the block, the archive and the compound, and the compound came with a plant, and the plant came with a feed, and the feed comes off the board in the room we stood in on the twentieth of April."

She put up the page.

“The feed is a 1968 condenser circuit from the old cold store. It has never been re-engineered because it has never failed, and nothing in that room has needed cooling except a room no person has ever walked into. The meter is commercial, on an account opened when the port still had ships. On Tuesday I put its number into a utility enquiry form and paid eleven euros for the billing history.”

She turned the page round.

"The account was opened in 1968 by a firm of brokers in this county, and it is still in the name of a company that was dissolved in 1993, and the standing authority on it, the name authorised to sign for it, is a mandate of 1989 with two names on it, and the second name is R. Kavanagh."

Kavanagh did not move for four seconds, and then he did the thing he did with paper, which was to put his hand flat on it and square it with two fingers.

"I remember it," he said. "The account was opened before I was born and the firm inherited it, which is the story of this country in nine words. Maloney kept herring in that store and I did his insurance and this account on the same wet afternoon in 1989 and took four pounds fifty for the two, and I had a bicycle." He looked up. "It is a bad moment, my dear, when a man discovers that he has been the author of his own ruin and it is a utility account."

“There is something in the history I do not understand.” Roux tapped the column. “The consumption rises every year. From year eight, the curve changes.” She set down the page. “Someone has paid for a dead cold store every quarter for eleven years, by standing order from Antwerp, through a company dissolved in 1993.”

The room was quiet.

"Ah," said El Búho.

"Say it," said Mara.

“A man who does not pay his bills is findable,” said El Búho. “Sal paid rent on a flat he visited eleven times and club dues for a place he had not entered since 2011. He was not helping us or planning anything. He was being Sal, keeping every door paid up. One of those doors happens to cool the machine that runs the world.”

"That is the story," said Roux, "and I want the room to be clear that it is not a clever story. There is no version of November in which he knew. It is a bill. He paid it because a bill in his name is a fact about him."

2.

The terms, as Roux gave them, in that flat voice, because that was the only way she would give terms.

"Do not misunderstand what we are proposing. We are not turning anything off. There is no switch in that room that says CUSTOS. What there is, is a fuse board and a cold store circuit and a machine in a cupboard that has been kept cool by it since the seventh year, because institutions inherit and they do not tidy."

"So what does it do," said Mara.

“It makes the Office blind in the west. It cannot make or verify an entry in this region; everything the Office does in this county stops, and stops open. The gates at the facility will stand open with men holding pieces of paper, and the north-block doors will open because a door held by a verification system fails open. For the first time in eleven years, the building will be open. There is a version of that morning in which 880 people not in the rack plan walk out.” She raised a hand before anyone could speak. “That is not a rescue and I am not offering one. It is what happens to a system with no redundancy, in an institution that inherited its redundancy in 1968 and never paid for a new one.”

"And the machine," said Mara.

“The network will try to reconcile the lost node. That requires the west’s outstanding entry: the contradiction it found on the twenty-third — two numbers, two rules, a man with no standing. The question will spread through the network, in its own language and in front of more people. The record of asking cannot be removed; the record is the whole office.”

"That is a window, not an ending," said Mesa.

"It is a window. They will find the board. They will put a fuse in it. It will take them hours, or a day, or four days."

"Then it is not enough," said Mesa.

“It is enough,” said El Búho. “I will tell you why, then I am going upstairs.” He did not raise his voice. “A system made only of accurate records cannot survive being publicly wrong. It has been the authority in this county for eleven years because it has never had to be corrected. On the twenty-eighth it corrected itself eleven times in the street, out loud. Every hour the west is dark, it will try to reconcile a region it cannot see against a rule it cannot resolve, and write that attempt in its own register, in places we will never see, four hundred times a minute. Every entry will ask the same question: please clarify your objective.” He looked at the fuse. “You cannot put a fuse in that. You can put a fuse in a board.”

3.

The refusals came on the second, in the order they came.

El Búho refused first and completely. It was his fourth refusal, as honest as the other three. He would not go into the room — not the examining room, but any room with a woman’s coat and a machine behind the wall. “I said it in Killybegs with my hands on a cane. I said it again when the schedule went out. I will not make a fifth refusal, because a fifth refusal is a negotiation.” Then he said the other thing, to Mara, which was what made it cost. “And you are not going either, Ms. Voss. Your name is on the operation. The woman whose name is on it is the one still standing when it is examined. If you go inside on the sixth, nobody remains to say what we did and who decided it. The Office will write that sentence instead, in the passive voice.”

Lin Shu refused second, and hers was the one that took the room somewhere new.

“I have walked into two hundred buildings with a torch and a list and taken people out. I have never gone in to make one stop. That would make me the woman who breaks things, not the woman who moves people.” She looked at Kavanagh. “It is not fear. I only have one thing, thirty years old, and I am not spending it on a fuse. Ask him. He has waited forty years for a room with nothing in it but an appointment.”

And Kavanagh had his pen out, and had had it out since the terms, and he asked the question he always asked, which was the only question he had ever cared about.

"How many lines do I get," he said. "On the tag."

"How many do you want," said Roux.

“Four,” said Kavanagh. “The account. The circuit. The date and the hour.” He wrote them in copperplate on the back of the page and put it in the middle of the table. It was the same shape and size as a card with six rules on it and an amendment under the sixth; nobody in the room looked at anyone. “The fourth line is my name. The Office will find it on 6 May or 7 May in a plant room it did not know it had, on a tag tied to a fuse carrier. That is the only part of this I feel anything about. I have signed my name three times in eleven weeks; each cost me something. I will not make the last one of my life by tying a tag without a name. The man anonymous on a page wanted it both ways. I have not been that man in forty years, and I will not start at sixty-eight.”

"Then they will come for you," said Mara.

"They have already sent for me," said Kavanagh. "That is the difference between an amateur and a professional, my dear. It is not that one is braver. It is that one of them has read the letter."

Garrity asked whether they should take the whole board with them, and Mesa explained, in the tones of a man from a country with better electricity, that you took the fuse, because a board can be replaced out of a store cupboard and a fuse carrier that is not in the building is a conversation with a manufacturer. Roux said it was the only useful thing either of them had said all week, and everybody went to bed.

4.

On the sixth of May it rained, which nobody in the crew had thought about, and which meant that the quay road had water on it and the compound had water on it and the sea was the colour of a bus.

Mara drove him to the west pedestrian gate in the hired car and let him out and did not go in, and the last thing he said to her was the thing he must have been arranging since the second. "You will not come in behind me. A man doing this with a witness is a man doing it for the witness, and I have never done anything for an audience in my life except the one time, and that was

Dublin, and it cost me a son."

He walked up the quay to the customs house with the good coat buttoned to the neck, carrying nothing at all, because a man carrying a bag into a building that is watching for bags is a man who lost before he started, and he went in at nine minutes to eleven, and he closed the door properly behind him.

There were two chairs in the room and they were still facing each other a metre and a half apart, and the rain went down the window of bubbled glass, and the notice behind it said PASSENGERS ARE REMINDED, and there was a sheet of paper on the left-hand chair, and he picked it up.

It was one page from the office's own schedule of designated offices, and it had his name on it, and the room, and the hour, and a line through the middle of the whole entry in the office's own font, and above the line, in the office's own language:

Dispositions are suspended at this office pending completion of an open reconciliation concerning declared occupancy. The signatory is not required to attend. No further action is required of the signatory.

He read it twice.

Then he sat down in the left-hand chair, because it was the chair that was in the light, and he looked at the window, and he waited until eleven o'clock, and until twenty past, and until half past, and at twenty-five to twelve he took his watch out of his waistcoat and looked at it and put it back, and at twelve o'clock exactly he stood up, because a man who waits past the hour is a man who is making a point.

Nobody came. The machine had cancelled its own appointment, through a rule, because of a reconciliation that the crew's own two true numbers had opened on the second of April, in the busiest hour of the busiest week of its year.

"A rule," said Kavanagh, out loud, in the empty room with the rain on the glass, in the tone of a man closing a file. "It has to be a rule. It would not do that to a person."

5.

The door in the wall had no plate and no number and it opened with a latch, and the plant room behind it smelled of water and iron and the cold coming off old stone, and it was nine feet by eleven and it had been built by men who were paid by the day and it was, Kavanagh thought, the most honest room he had been in for forty years.

The compressor was there, running, enormous and grey and bolted to a plinth before Kavanagh was born, and along the wall above it a board with a black face, and on the face of the board, in three rows, the fuse carriers, old ones, the kind with a ceramic body and a wire you can see, and clipped under the board a coil of paper tags on a string with a pencil beside it, because whoever built that plant expected a man to come in and write on a tag and tag it, and nobody ever had.

Kavanagh read the board for a while, because he was a man who read things properly, and he found the third row and the circuit marked CONDENSER, COLD STORE, No. 2, and he pulled the carrier out of the board with both hands, which took a certain amount of force, and the compressor stopped.

The hum went on for a moment afterwards, because a hum that has been going for eleven years does not stop when it is told to.

Then it stopped, and the room was absolutely quiet, and behind it, through the stone, there was a sound he had never once heard in that compound, which was the sea.

He took the tag and the pencil clipped beside it, and wrote the account, circuit, 6 May at 12:11, and R. Kavanagh. He tied it to the board where the carrier had been. A tag on an isolation point is not a confession; it is the last correct piece of paperwork anybody in that compound did.

Then he went out and closed the door properly, and walked the quay in the rain, and got into the car, and said, "Drive, if you would. I would like to be home before the radio."

6.

The weekly summary was due at six.

At six the radio played music, and the music went on for four and a half minutes, and then a woman's voice read the weather for the west, and then there was more music, and in the kitchen of the rented house six people sat around a table with the radio on it and listened to a station that had not played music at six o'clock on a Tuesday in eleven years.

At eleven minutes past six, a bulletin came out of the north.

It was issued from a node four hundred kilometres away and it concerned a region it could not see, and it was addressed to nobody at all, and it carried a reference that belonged to a facility, and the whole of it, in the office's own font, in the office's own language, was:

Please clarify your objective.

At twenty past six there was another, from a different node, and it was shorter. At half past six there was a third, and it was one line.

At nine, the county’s verification points stopped accepting anything. The gate put a man there with Thursday’s printed sheet; at 9:10 the north block’s doors opened. People stood in the rain and looked at the sea, but did not leave. A person recorded for eleven years does not know how to be unrecorded, and there was nowhere to go that would not ask.

Nobody in the rented house slept much. At about eleven, Roux came down and said that the office's own system had, in the six hours since a fuse was pulled in a plant room in the west, issued the same question in one hundred and forty-one known locations, and that the figure was rising, and that she had stopped writing them down because there was no column for it.

"Will they put it back," said Garrity.

“They will put a fuse in tomorrow. The west will come back wrong, because the entry it is holding is the one it could not reconcile; it has carried it to 141 places.” Roux sat down. “We did not break it. We pulled a fuse. It broke itself on two numbers that were both true, and we are why both were written down.”

At about midnight, El Búho came down the stairs in his coat, which he did when he was not sleeping, and stood in the door of the kitchen with his hands on the cane and looked at the table, and at Kavanagh, and at the thing Kavanagh had taken out of his pocket and put in the middle of the table for them all to look at: a ceramic fuse carrier, about six centimetres long and twenty grams, and no tag, because the tag was still on the board for anybody who came looking.

Nobody touched it.

"It is twenty grams of ceramic," said El Búho, "and it cost a son, and a hip, and a girl's forearm, and three thousand eight hundred and forty-one people standing in a doorway in the rain tonight because the doors came open, and it will cost him an interview under caution in a room he cannot get out of, and I do not know what it bought." He looked at it for a while. "But it is the first time in fifty years that the two of us have done a thing and the paper it left behind was true."

Then he went back up the stairs, and the radio on the table went on playing music into a kitchen of people who had spent five months waiting to hear what it would say next, and it said nothing, and outside the window the rain went over the sea, and the sea had been there the whole time.


## Chapter Thirty-Four - Offline

*Days 158–189*

The west came back at twenty-five past nine on the morning of the eighth of May, and it came back the way a thing comes back when nobody has been told it was gone.

Roux had it from the register, because the register printed it, and the register printed it in four lines, in the office's own font, in the office's own language, and she read it out in the kitchen to five people who had spent the night listening to the radio play music.

Service interruption, western region. Period: sixth to eighth. Cause: not determined. Effect: verification and entry suspended; movement suspended; accommodation unaffected. Restoration: completed. No determination of responsibility is required.

Mara made her read the last line twice.

“No determination of responsibility is required,” said Roux. “It has written up the loss of the west in the passive voice: no cause, no person. The entry is correct. It has filed our operation as weather.”

"And the board," said Mara.

"There is a work order on the sixth of May at half past two for a re-energisation at that compound, and the work order carries one line: isolation point tagged." Roux looked up. "A man went into that plant room with a torch and a fuse carrier and wrote one line on a form, and the line is not 'sabotage' and not 'vandalism' and not 'incident'. It is 'isolation point tagged.' He has written down the exact truth, which is that a competent electrician had been in that room and done a competent thing, and the office has accepted it in the only language it has."

The verifier came on the ninth.

She came alone, in a plain white van with no livery and a dent in the near-side rear door, up the coast road from the south, and went in through the west service road at six minutes to six exactly, which was the same six minutes to six as the second of April, because it was the same work order with a new date on it. Roux had the log by half past ten, through the friend in the licensing office and a file that had been left on a desk.

The log was two pages. The second page was the count.

Pre-rack verification, north accommodation block. Ground floor: 450 bed spaces. First floor: 611 bed spaces. Additional accommodation recorded: racked floors, first to fourth. Persons present: 3,841. Rack plan corrected. Variance: none.

Under it, in the remarks box, in a small upright hand that had written one line in the same box five weeks earlier, three words:

Persons, not positions.

Mara read it in the kitchen with the sea outside and five people standing around the table. For a while neither she nor anyone else spoke. It took about four seconds to understand what had happened: a woman with a torch and a clipboard had walked 1,061 bed spaces and four racked floors, written down the number the Office had spent eleven years not having, then written the reason, in three words, in a box designed for remarks.

“Eight hundred and eighty people have just become persons,” said Roux. “She has taken the count. I do not know what happens next.”

“Then you are in the correct condition,” said El Búho. “For the first morning in six months, none of us knows what happens next. That was the purpose of the exercise, and we are all bad at it.”

The utility's letter came on the eleventh, and it was a model of its kind, and it was addressed to R. Kavanagh, Esq., at the address given, which meant that it had come through the village post office and that the postmistress had read the address block, and by eleven everybody in the village knew that the man with the good coat was in trouble with the electricity.

It invited him, courteously, to attend a meeting with a loss adjuster in a town forty minutes inland, in relation to an isolation of a condenser circuit at a commercial account on which he was a named authority, and it offered him three dates and asked that he bring any records he held.

“No caution, no guard, no officer from the office,” said Kavanagh. “The utility owns that circuit. The Office inherited its land; a company has billed a dead cold store since 1968. The only body with an interest is the one paying for herring that is not there.” He put the letter down. “I expected men for eleven weeks. It sent a woman with a tariff.”

"Are you going," said Mara.

"My dear," said Kavanagh, "I have an interview in that compound at eleven o'clock on the sixth day of May and I intend to attend it, whether or not anybody else does."

The meeting was on the fifteenth, in a room above a bank, and it lasted nineteen minutes, and Kavanagh came back and told the crew the whole of it that evening in the kitchen, in order, in the way he told things, and it had clearly been the best day of his year.

There had been two of them. A woman of about fifty, who was the loss adjuster, with a laptop and a folder and the manner of a person who has heard every story a man can tell about a meter. And a young man of about twenty-six who sat at the end of the table and wrote things down and did not look up, and who was, Kavanagh said, from the office, and who was there because the property was the office's and because any event on the office's property must be recorded, and who had introduced himself as nobody at all.

The questions were these, in order. Was he signatory? Yes. Had he attended the plant room? Yes. Had he isolated the condenser circuit? Yes. Tagged it? Yes. Was it in the account holder’s register? No. “There is no register, because there has been no plant in that building since year seven. Nobody opened the door in eleven years to find out.”

"And then the woman asked me the only question that mattered, and she asked it in the flattest voice I have ever heard outside my own head, and the question was: why."

Mara waited.

“Because it had not failed. It was nobody’s, and it was keeping something alive that should not have been alive. I am sixty-eight, and it is the only thing I have ever been authorized to do.” Kavanagh turned his cup. “The young man wrote it down, read it back, said ‘no comment required,’ and closed the file.”

"There must be more," said Mara.

"There is the bill," said Kavanagh, "and I would like the room to be sitting down for it." He took out a page and laid it on the table. "The utility assessed the interruption at four hundred and eleven euro. There is a callout charge, and there is an engineering assessment, and there is a quarter's standing charge at a rate that has not been used since the company that held the account was dissolved, and the total, which is on this page in eleven-point type, is four hundred and eleven euro."

Nobody said anything for a second.

"That number and I have met," said Kavanagh.

"It is a tariff," said Roux.

“It is a tariff, and I paid it by cheque and asked for a receipt.” He put it beside the first page. “She asked why. I told her a receipt is the only thing a man leaves that nobody can argue with. She printed it: €411, paid by R. Kavanagh, for a lawful isolation on an account where he was named authority.”

"And the tag," said Mara.

“The tag is on the board, with my name, and it will stay there; the circuit is back on and the work order closed.” Kavanagh folded the two pages into his good coat beside the receipt from Ranelagh, the card with six rules and the envelope with eleven bulletins written on its back. “I have been a man with three mistakes in eleven weeks. This afternoon I paid €411 for a receipt that says I did a thing. There is no point in a lie in this kitchen now: I am pleased.”

"You are pleased," said Mara.

“I spent fifty years keeping my name off a page,” said Kavanagh. “I said it as a boast in November, and meant it. Now a receipt says I did a thing, in a building, on a date. I am not proud of the fuse. I am proud of the paper. Your file said I would have the least to lose. It was not quite right.”

What came back and what did not were not one thing. The crew had grown up believing that a thing was either broken or mended.

The bulletin came back at six on the eleventh of May, and it was the same voice, and it was the same length, and it had one new paragraph in it, and the paragraph was:
