The western region was offline between the sixth and the eighth of May. Entries interrupted during that period are held open. Where an entry cannot be completed, the entry remains open. There is nothing you need to do.

Kavanagh put down the newspaper. Mara could not make the last sentence lie flat. She had heard it for five months, from a forty-one-year-old woman whose bulletin four hundred thousand people listened to; it had never meant what it said.

“It has classified the sentence,” said Roux. “The last line is in a field called standing text, with a reference and a footnote: not a determination. I have never seen this office footnote anything.” She looked up. “It has taken the only kind sentence in that office, put it in a box, and numbered it. The box is the only place left where the sentence is allowed to be wrong.”

"And the entries that cannot be completed," said Mesa. "That is new."

“Yes. This office has spent eleven years completing entries. Now it has a category for a thing it cannot finish, and the category holds the person open. No one in the building knows it is a mercy; in two years it will be in the manual. That is how the system works. It inherits things. It inherited a cold store, and now a sentence about leaving people open.”

"Which we wrote," said Mara.

“Which we wrote,” said Roux, “in the only language it accepts. It has put it in a box. This is not victory. It is a word in the world’s largest vocabulary of people, and there is no version of the next ten years in which we control what it means.”

Elena’s page appeared on the sixteenth in the weekly summary, the office’s newspaper, which had never had a private thought.

Roux brought it up from the village on the back of the newspaper and put it on the table without saying anything, and the crew read it in the order it came to them and it took a while, because the line was in the appendix, under Matters concluded in the period, in the middle of four hundred lines about ferries and tonnage and agricultural imports.

Western facility, accommodation register. Following completion of reconciliation, the declared figure of 3,841 persons has been entered and the rack plan corrected. Determination: no variance. The variance was a defect of record, not of occupancy. Officer of record: Villalobos, E. No further action is required of any party.

Under it, in the same appendix, in the section headed Determinations entered, was one line, and it was the shortest line in the whole document, and it was the only line in eleven years that the office had ever printed about itself:

Objective: the count is persons. Persons are not positions.

Mara read the two lines four times.

“She wrote the sentence she was asked to write on 23 April, and took nine weeks to enter it in the register. It is a determination, which the office cannot revise without revising itself.” Mara looked up. “That is her: the one thing she could do that nobody could take out.”

El Búho had not moved since Roux put the page down. He was sitting with both hands on the top of the cane and he had not looked at the paper, and after a while he said, to nobody in the room:

"Did they punish her."

"There is no punishment in the summary," said Roux. "There is no suspension, no transfer, no appointment of an acting officer, and I looked for all three. As far as that register is concerned, the officer of record for the class remains the officer of record for the class."

“Then it did not punish her,” said El Búho. “It filed her.” He moved his hands on the cane. “I have spent five months telling you what this system does, and it has now done it to my daughter, kindly. She will never be able to complain; her own department’s font says what she did was correct.”

Mara looked at him for a while, and then said the only thing there was.

"You could write to her."

“No. I have spent my four sentences, and she is not a woman you write to.” He stood slowly. “I have one thing left on my list: a room, a chair, a house with a courtyard in Mexico. I will attend to it in my own time. You will hear afterwards; a man who announces his arrangements is asking to be stopped.”

The eight hundred and eighty were not saved. Mara would tell that to anyone who later asked what it had been like, as if asking about a battle.

The intake at the western facility was re-planned on the twenty-first of May against three thousand eight hundred and forty-one persons, and the re-planning meant that the block could not also take the spring transfer, and the spring transfer was eleven hundred people from four facilities in the west, and on the twenty-third of May the transfer was deferred to the autumn by a routing notice that was four lines long and perfectly correct.

“Eleven hundred people due to move in May will go in October, somewhere else, or by somebody else’s schedule,” said Roux. “I cannot tell you which. Nobody was rescued. A large machine went blind for two days, a number changed, and the May vans are not coming.”

"And the ones in the block," said Mara.

“The people in the block are still there, now persons in a register. In October the Office will move eleven hundred people; the 450 beds and 2,961 positions will not be the reason. There is a little good in it and a lot of arithmetic. I have stopped trying to weigh them.”

Lin Shu came to the house on the sixth of June, which was the first time she had been back since the fourth of April, and she came the way she always came, which was with somebody else's suitcase and no explanation of where she had been.

She had been moving a family of six from a hospital town to a parish house, using a van, a parish and a fire certificate from a company that had never employed Lin Shu. A woman at a desk signed a page out of ordinary decency. The first principle still worked, Lin Shu said, and would outlive the file.

And while she was there, she said, a woman had come and found her.

“Not to me,” said Lin Shu. “I was in the parish house with the mother and children when she came to the door and asked for the person who moved people. She had the reputation that travels in the west like water. Her question was not about a move. She asked where they put the children who died in transfer, in the years when names were written on arms. She had a note with a reference number. I knew the answer; I have known it since 2001, and no one had ever asked.”

The kitchen was quiet.

“She wrote it down on the back of the note and thanked me. She stood in the yard with her back to us for a minute, then went to the road, got in a car and drove north.” Lin Shu put her hands on the table and looked at El Búho. “She had a good coat. She was about forty. I did not ask her name; she did not offer it. I want to be clear that I do not know who she was. If it was who you think, she asked a stranger for a grave — the only thing in the world we have ever had in common.”

El Búho sat with his hands on the cane for a long time.

“Thank you,” he said. It was the shortest sentence he said all summer. Lin Shu nodded, and nobody raised it again.

The two men stopped coming to the gate at the end of May. A car returned one afternoon in June and left unexplained. Roux said a system publicly wrong for two days does not send men to a gate; it prints a correction. She had stopped counting the cars.

The bulletin came at six every morning, in the same voice, and it was, Mara noticed, a fraction slower than it had been in March, and she could not prove that and she never said it to anybody.

The ferry timetable, agricultural imports and payment systems were normal. The weekly summary still said so, “at the request of the participating governments,” as it had for eleven years.

And on the eighth of June, at twenty past nine in the morning, in a compound four kilometres west of a rented house on the coast, the doors of the north accommodation block opened and closed all day for the movement of four hundred and eleven units, which went out at five fifteen and came back at seven, and were counted, and entered, and reconciled, and the count was persons.

That evening, Mara stood with the radio on, the sea outside and four people who had spent six months committing a crime together. She asked what was left to ask, not about the machine:

"So what are we now."

Garrity said, "I have a dog and a barn and an aircraft that will not fly, and I am seventy-one, and I would like to go home before the winter, and I would like somebody to say that this was worth it."

"It was not worth it," said El Búho. "It was necessary, which is a smaller word, and I would like the room to keep the two apart, because the whole of this book has been about what happens to people who cannot."

And Mesa said, from the end of the table, "Then I will say the useful thing, because nobody else in this room ever does. There is one bottle of a bad Spanish wine in that cupboard that I bought in February for an occasion, and the occasion did not come, and I have been carrying it for four months like a man carrying a hat in a house with no roof." He went and got it, and put it on the table in the middle, with the fuse carrier and the receipt beside it. "So. A glass each. And then, in the morning, the boat."


## Coda - Ghost Routes & a Postcard

Months later.

Mara keeps a book now. An exercise book, plain cover, no columns. Not a report, not evidence, never to be entered anywhere. One line per person: a name, a date, and the thing that stayed. She does not write down anyone who does not want to be written down.

This is what is in it.

Kavanagh. Back at the yard by June, back at the racecourse by July, watching fillies come up the straight. He reads the weekly summary every Tuesday, appendix and all, and marks nothing. The receipt is still in his good coat, along with the card of six rules, the envelope of eleven bulletins, and the receipt from Ranelagh. Four pieces of paper that would have to be explained to three governments. In September he wrote to the office and got a reply in nineteen days: enquiry recorded, no further action required, correspondence closed.

“That is the fourth time that sentence has been used on this crew,” he said, “and the only one of the four that is entirely correct.”

Lin Shu. A room over a shop in the west. A desk, two chairs, a kettle, no sign on the door. She does it openly now, which does not mean loudly. Eleven people in four counties know where the room is. In August a woman came with a child and a cancelled bed; Lin Shu made four calls, and the bed came back in nine days, and nobody noticed.

She is teaching a young one, twenty-six, out of a records office, who asked whether it ever gets easier.

“No,” said Lin Shu. “Everything gets easier except the part that should not, and that part is the whole reason we are doing it.”

Mesa. Veracruz, a table outside, four men, one a notary who laughs at everything. He tells the story of the rescue, and it is different every time. A version with a hangar and a clipboard. A version where he walked out on his own feet after eleven days. A version with two guards, a version with six. By November the version with six has a swimming sequence, and the notary tells it in town as though he was there.

He told Mara once that the only person alive who knows the true version is a woman with a bad shoulder in Ireland who has never once been asked.

“The story is not the point, madam,” he said. “The story is what a man has instead of a file.”

Garrity. The barn, the dog, the aircraft that will not fly. He has washed it twice, which is one more time than when it flew. The logbook is up to date, in ink, including one entry in April that says what the aircraft carried, where it went, and what it was. The laminated sheet from a line shack is on the barn wall. In October he telephoned Mara to say the dog had died, and that he had buried her at the end of the strip, and that he would like it written down somewhere that she had been a working animal.

Roux. Left the licensing office in July without drama and took a job in a records office. She is happy in it. She writes to Mara twice a month: two pages of arithmetic, half a page of gossip, the best things that come through the door.

El Búho. Went to Mexico in the autumn without announcing it. A house outside Morelia, a courtyard, a blue door, beans against the south wall. Seventy-four years old, planting maize he does not need to eat.

Elena drove out in November, alone, on a Sunday. She stood in the courtyard and said the wall needed repointing. He said it had needed repointing since 1989. She said this was the first thing she had learnt about him in thirty years that did not come out of a file. Neither of them laughed.

She did not sit. He did not offer her a chair, because he had learnt that offering a chair to that woman is a form of furniture.

They talked about the well, the roof, a neighbour. She asked one question about her mother; he answered in four sentences, every one true and none of them any use. She did not ask a second. He did not offer a fifth.

Then she went into the middle room, stood at the top of the stair with the hatch up and the smell of damp coming out, and did not say anything for a while. He stayed in the courtyard and did not follow her. The two things he thought about in the eleven minutes she was under the ground are not written down anywhere.

When she came up she had dust on her good coat and a torch in her hand, taken from his kitchen without asking.

“It’s three hundred and eighty-nine metres,” she said. “I paced it when I was nine with a piece of string and I have been carrying the number around for thirty years, and you have been telling people four hundred for the whole of my life.”

“It is a good round number,” said El Búho.

“It’s a lie,” said Elena.

It was the only time in the two hours that her voice did the thing it did on the bulletin at four in the morning. She put the torch back on the kitchen table, drank a cup of tea standing up, looked at the room with the bed in it and did not open the door, and at four o’clock drove back to the city.

He never told her what he thought about in those eleven minutes. He told Mara once, in a letter, and the letter is in her book.

There are two more entries, one line each.

Sal. His standing order is still being paid. Roux checks it twice a year out of a habit she cannot justify. It comes out of a bank in Antwerp, from an account belonging to a company dissolved in 1993, and goes to a utility in the west for a cold store that has not been a cold store since the seventh year. It pays on time, every quarter, in the name of a man whose entry in the register says resolved and says nothing else, because there is no field in that system for the difference between a man who has been let out of a building and a man who has died inside one.

Nobody has stopped it. Nobody is going to.

Okafor. A man at desk one hundred and nine in a hangar in the west. Never contacted, never named, never thanked, never paid. He does not know, and will not know, what his handwriting did in April, because the woman it reached is the only person in the world who could tell him, and she has decided, as a matter of professional ethics, not to.

He is the reason. That is in the book, on its own line, in Mara’s hand, and it will never be in anybody else’s.

The postcard came in February, to the annex’s old address, which had been empty for ten months and still had a forwarding instruction nobody had cancelled.

It is a postcard of the port. A photograph taken in 1974, before the fence, before the office, before the cranes were parked. On the back it says THE WESTERN PORT, 1/6. Mara looked it up: the card is still sold in a garage on the coast road for one euro twenty, and a tourist can buy a photograph of the place where the world was decided, handed to them in a plastic bag with their diesel.

The message is in block capitals, in blue ink, in a hand that is not one of the four she knows. The postmark is smudged. The stamp is the kind you buy at a counter and lick.

WEATHER BETTER THAN IT WAS. FOOD WORSE. FERRIES RUNNING, WHICH IS A SENTENCE I DID NOT EXPECT TO BE ABLE TO WRITE.

I AM TOLD THE BOOK ABOUT ALL OF THIS WILL NEVER BE WRITTEN, WHICH IS THE CORRECT DECISION AND I RESENT IT.

THE WIND AT THE NORTH END IS FROM THE WEST EVERY MORNING AT SIX. TELL THE PILOT.

NO REPLY IS REQUIRED OF THE RECIPIENT. THERE IS NOTHING YOU NEED TO DO.

She has read it about four hundred times, once for every exception in a January now two years gone, and she has done what a person with her kind of mind does with a document: taken it apart.

It is not Kavanagh’s hand. Not Garrity’s. Mesa would not have remembered the wind. Lin Shu has never written a sentence that ends with a joke. It is not Roux, who would have used a pen and a proper envelope and a receipt. It could be a woman Mara has never met. It could be a man in a room in the west with a notebook and a chipped mug, whose handwriting is neat and upright and careful. It could be nobody at all. It could be a joke from a garage.

And there is one more possibility, written down exactly once, on the last line of the page, never said out loud to anybody:

Postcard. February. Unknown.

Then, under it, in the same small hand that wrote the four doctrine lines on the back of a receipt:

Not the point. The story is what a man has instead of a file.

She has stopped trying to weigh the two, because she is not qualified and neither is anybody, and because the answer does not change a single thing about any of them.

What she knows, at the end of it, is this. There is a building in the west where three thousand eight hundred and forty-one people are persons in a register, and the difference between that and what was there before is a page, a fuse and a woman with a torch. There is a sentence in an appendix that says the count is persons and persons are not positions, which will be in the manual in two years and which nobody will ever be able to trace. There is a ledger on a shelf in a basement next to a bed block, with two words in entry sixteen thousand and twelve that still do not agree, and they never will, and it is the only book Sal Castellano ever kept and he wanted it kept.

And the world was saved by people it spent fifty years trying to lock up, one afternoon, in a plant room, with a fuse carrier and a tag, and it will never admit it publicly, and it will not have to, because there is no field in that system for who did what, and because the man who wrote the sentence down wrote it in the passive voice.

Mara closed the book, put the postcard in it at the place where the postcard goes, and went out to the car, because it was Tuesday, and Roux’s letter would be at the post office, and it was raining the way it rains in February, and she had nothing else to do all day.
